They remain relevant to identifying what the enactment adds to the claimant’s circumstances. Requirements may not be insulated from review through artificial isolation when they operate as an integrated restriction.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not hold that a twenty-four-hour period is always valid or always invalid. Nor does cumulative analysis justify relief against a provision that contributes nothing to the combined constitutional burden.

**First controlling authority:** Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer, 18 F.3d 526, decided February 13, 1995, Operational burden and the remand record; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer, 18 F.3d 526, decided February 13, 1995, Operational burden and the remand record; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer, 18 F.3d 526, February 13, 1995.

### Usable medical-emergency routes in penal abortion regulation

**Governing question:** What medical-emergency protection a penal abortion regulation must provide.

**Current rule:** A penal abortion regulation must contain a usable medical-emergency route that permits a physician, acting in good faith and appropriate medical judgment, to protect a patient from death or grave medical harm without first proving the emergency to the prosecutor. The required postviability health protection includes a grave, medically established danger of severe, lasting, and potentially irreversible mental or emotional injury when appropriate medical judgment identifies that danger.

**First controlling authority:** Planned Parenthood v. Miller, 63 F.3d 1452, decided April 15, 1996, usable medical-emergency route; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Planned Parenthood v. Miller, 63 F.3d 1452, decided April 15, 1996; Women’s Medical Professional Corp. v. Voinovich, 130 F.3d 187, decided April 20, 1998, grave mental or emotional health consequences; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Women’s Medical Professional Corp. v. Voinovich, 130 F.3d 187, April 20, 1998.

**Development:**
- April 20, 1998, Women’s Medical Professional Corp. v. Voinovich: The existing grave-medical-harm rule expressly includes severe, lasting, and potentially irreversible mental or emotional injury established through appropriate medical judgment.

### State-created liberty in prison discipline

**Governing question:** When state prison law creates a protected liberty interest in avoiding disciplinary segregation.

**Current rule:** A State creates a protected prison liberty interest when substantive law meaningfully limits officials’ authority to impose a significant deprivation. Mandatory wording alone is insufficient, but a court may not disregard a genuine legal constraint on punitive segregation. The court examines both the substance of state law and the deprivation at issue. State law must genuinely constrain official authority rather than merely prescribe procedures or use mandatory drafting. The restraint must concern a meaningful loss of liberty rather than a routine transfer, brief inconvenience, or ordinary discretionary condition of confinement.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not constitutionalize every prison regulation, routine transfer, brief inconvenience, or discretionary management choice. Institutional security and lawful correctional judgment retain their ordinary weight.

**First controlling authority:** Sandin v. Conner, 515 U.S. 472, decided June 19, 1995, State-created liberty in prison discipline; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Sandin v. Conner, 515 U.S. 472, decided June 19, 1995, State-created liberty in prison discipline; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Sandin v. Conner, 515 U.S. 472, June 19, 1995.

### Full faith and credit for a settlement releasing exclusively federal claims

**Governing question:** When a federal court must give full faith and credit to a state settlement judgment releasing exclusively federal claims.

**Current rule:** The federal court first determines the effect the rendering State gives its judgment. It then applies that effect unless Congress expressly or by irreconcilable implication displaced § 1738, or the judgment is not entitled to recognition because the rendering court lacked jurisdiction over the action before it or the judgment failed constitutional due process.

**First controlling authority:** Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Epstein, 516 U.S. 367, decided February 27, 1996, Full faith and credit for a settlement releasing exclusively federal claims; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Epstein, 516 U.S. 367, decided February 27, 1996, Full faith and credit for a settlement releasing exclusively federal claims; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Epstein, 516 U.S. 367, February 27, 1996.

**Development:**
- February 27, 1996, Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Epstein, 516 U.S. 367: The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule for state class judgments releasing exclusively federal claims. Section 1738 remains the starting point; exclusive federal jurisdiction alone creates no exception. A constitutionally adequate class process remains a prerequisite to binding absent members.

### Parole information when parole remains legally possible

**Governing question:** Whether Simmons requires parole information when parole remains legally possible but release is practically unlikely.

**Current rule:** *Simmons* requires correction of a legally false release premise when the only noncapital sentence forecloses parole. It does not require courts to predict future parole-board decisions or convert practical estimates of release into constitutionally fixed sentencing facts.

**Exceptions and limits:** A State remains free to provide accurate, nonmisleading information about a legally fixed minimum period of confinement. The holding does not narrow *Simmons* when the life sentence legally eliminates parole.

**First controlling authority:** Allridge v. Scott, 41 F.3d 213, decided January 15, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Parole information when parole remains legally possible; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Allridge v. Scott, 41 F.3d 213, decided January 15, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Parole information when parole remains legally possible; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Allridge v. Scott, 41 F.3d 213, January 15, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- January 15, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Allridge v. Scott, 41 F.3d 213: *Simmons* is declined extension beyond legal parole ineligibility; practical estimates of release do not become constitutionally fixed sentencing facts while parole remains legally possible.

### Due-process limits on nonparty preclusion

**Governing question:** What representation and process are required before a judgment may bind a person who was not a party.

**Current rule:** Shared taxpayer status, a common public issue, and similar litigation interests do not themselves create representation. Binding representation requires a recognized legal relationship, genuinely aligned interests, responsibility to protect the absent person, and procedure adequate to make that responsibility effective. Notice is ordinarily required when practicable.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision leaves intact ordinary privity, actual control of prior litigation, adequately constituted class actions, fiduciary representation, and suits brought by legally authorized public representatives.

**First controlling authority:** Richards v. Jefferson County, 517 U.S. 793, decided June 10, 1996, Due-process limits on nonparty preclusion; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Richards v. Jefferson County, 517 U.S. 793, decided June 10, 1996, Due-process limits on nonparty preclusion; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Richards v. Jefferson County, 517 U.S. 793, June 10, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 10, 1996, Richards v. Jefferson County, 517 U.S. 793: *Matsushita* is applied and clarified. A judgment may bind absent persons only when the rendering process satisfies the constitutional prerequisites of notice and adequate, legally accountable representation. A generalized identity of public interest is insufficient.

### Child-specific process governing implementation of custody

**Governing question:** What process is required before a State abruptly transfers a child from a long-standing recognized placement to a fit parent with whom the child lacks an established relationship.

**Current rule:** When a child has lived for a substantial period in a state-recognized family placement and the State orders an abrupt transfer to a fit parent with whom the child has no established relationship, procedural due process requires notice to the child’s representative and a prompt, focused hearing on immediate safety, timing, continuity, professional support, and transition. The hearing does not authorize a veto over the fit parent’s custody, a new determination of permanent parenthood, or indefinite delay.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court recognizes no general best-interests override of a fit parent, no right to remain permanently with prospective adoptive parents, and no authority for transition planning to become indefinite custody litigation.

**First controlling authority:** Baby Richard, by his guardian ad litem, v. Kirchner, 164 Ill. 2d 468, 649 N.E.2d 324, decided June 21, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Child-specific process governing implementation of custody; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Baby Richard, by his guardian ad litem, v. Kirchner, 164 Ill. 2d 468, 649 N.E.2d 324, decided June 21, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Child-specific process governing implementation of custody; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Baby Richard, by his guardian ad litem, v. Kirchner, 164 Ill. 2d 468, 649 N.E.2d 324, June 21, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- June 21, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Baby Richard, by his guardian ad litem, v. Kirchner, 164 Ill. 2d 468, 649 N.E.2d 324: The decision adds a narrow child-specific procedural rule without altering the fit-parent judgment in *Doe*. It does not create a new substantive hierarchy among biological, adoptive, or psychological parents. It governs only the State’s manner of implementing a custody judgment under circumstances presenting a material risk from abrupt disruption.

### Prospective zoning changes applied to existing commercial uses

**Governing question:** Whether applying an adult-business zoning ordinance to businesses operating before enactment violates substantive due process.

**Current rule:** An existing commercial use has no substantive constitutional immunity from an otherwise lawful prospective zoning change. Applying a change to an existing use satisfies substantive due process when the measure bears a substantial relationship to legitimate land-use interests, regulates future location rather than imposing liability for past operation, and provides a substantial transition period together with a nonarbitrary mechanism for hardship relief.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not approve arbitrary confiscation, standardless denial of a hardship extension, selective enforcement, or a pretextual land-use measure.

**First controlling authority:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, decided March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date, Substantive due process; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, decided March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date, Substantive due process; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date.

### Threshold for selective-prosecution discovery

**Governing question:** What showing of differential treatment and factual similarity is required to obtain selective-prosecution discovery.

**Current rule:** The showing must address both differential treatment and factual similarity in legally relevant charging characteristics. Statistical concentration may contribute to the showing but does not alone establish entitlement when it fails to identify or reliably describe comparable conduct handled differently. The ultimate burden to prove discriminatory effect and purpose remains on the defendant.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not foreclose discovery on a properly supported claim, require a completed merits case before discovery, or decide the remedy for an ultimately proved constitutional violation.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Armstrong, 517 U.S. 456, decided May 13, 1996, Threshold for selective-prosecution discovery; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Armstrong, 517 U.S. 456, decided May 13, 1996, Threshold for selective-prosecution discovery; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Armstrong, 517 U.S. 456, May 13, 1996.

**Development:**
- May 13, 1996, United States v. Armstrong, 517 U.S. 456: The decision creates the first controlling simulated selective-prosecution discovery rule. It leaves the ultimate discriminatory-effect, purpose, causation, and persuasion requirements unchanged.

### Gross excessiveness of the remitted award

**Governing question:** Which guideposts determine whether a remitted punitive-damages award remains grossly excessive.

**Current rule:** Constitutional review considers three principal, nonexclusive guideposts: the reprehensibility of the defendant’s conduct; the relationship between punitive damages and actual or potential harm; and the difference between the award and authorized civil or criminal sanctions for comparable misconduct. No fixed numerical ratio governs every case.

**Exceptions and limits:** High ratios may remain permissible when actual damages are difficult to detect, the potential harm is substantially greater than the realized injury, or nominal damages conceal serious misconduct. The guideposts do not establish a mathematical cap.

**First controlling authority:** BMW of North America, Inc. v. Gore, 517 U.S. 559, decided May 20, 1996, Gross excessiveness of the remitted award; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** BMW of North America, Inc. v. Gore, 517 U.S. 559, decided May 20, 1996, Gross excessiveness of the remitted award; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** BMW of North America, Inc. v. Gore, 517 U.S. 559, May 20, 1996.

**Development:**
- May 20, 1996, BMW of North America, Inc. v. Gore, 517 U.S. 559: *Honda Motor Co. v. Oberg* is extended. Meaningful state judicial review remains necessary but is no longer always sufficient: a reviewed award may still violate the substantive constitutional ceiling when it is grossly excessive under the guideposts adopted here. *Honda*’s rejection of a fixed ratio remains unchanged.

### Proof required for a termination-like no-contact order

**Governing question:** What level of proof is required before a State may impose a complete, indefinite, or partly permanent parent-child no-contact order.

**Current rule:** Failure to satisfy the heightened proof requirement bars the long-term complete prohibition. Satisfaction permits the State to impose the protective order otherwise authorized by law. The rule does not govern temporary emergency protection, ordinary visitation scheduling, supervised-contact conditions, or short-term restrictions.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide the constitutionality of the treatment condition; the required form of notice or written findings; emergency procedures; the timing or form of periodic review; or whether and when a separate restoration procedure is required.

**First controlling authority:** A. St. P. C. v. B. C., No. 94-7810; 643 So. 2d 743, decided November 14, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Proof required for a termination-like no-contact order; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** A. St. P. C. v. B. C., No. 94-7810; 643 So. 2d 743, decided November 14, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Proof required for a termination-like no-contact order; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** A. St. P. C. v. B. C., No. 94-7810; 643 So. 2d 743, November 14, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- November 14, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, A. St. P. C. v. B. C., No. 94-7810; 643 So. 2d 743: The decision establishes a new procedural due-process proof rule for complete, indefinite, or partly permanent parent-child no-contact orders. *Hodge* is distinguished because confidential record retention did not itself alter family relations; Louisiana’s order directly and legally prohibits the relationship.

### Brady materiality of inadmissible information

**Governing question:** When suppressed inadmissible information is material under Brady because it identifies a concrete route to admissible evidence.

**Current rule:** Suppressed inadmissible information may satisfy *Brady* when its disclosed contents would have provided a concrete, record-supported route to admissible evidence concerning a key witness or disputed element. An unspecified hope that further investigation might uncover something useful remains insufficient.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not make every undisclosed polygraph result material, permit use of inadmissible results before the jury, or treat speculation about unidentified evidence as sufficient.

**First controlling authority:** Wood v. Bartholomew, 516 U.S. 1, decided October 10, 1995, Brady materiality of inadmissible information; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Wood v. Bartholomew, 516 U.S. 1, decided October 10, 1995, Brady materiality of inadmissible information; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Wood v. Bartholomew, 516 U.S. 1, October 10, 1995.

**Development:**
- October 10, 1995, Wood v. Bartholomew, 516 U.S. 1: *Kyles* is clarified rather than displaced. Its collective-confidence inquiry contains no categorical admissibility prerequisite. The clarification does not eliminate the requirement that the claimed downstream value be concrete and supported by the record.

### Bodily integrity and individual supervisory responsibility

**Governing question:** What knowledge, omission, and causation establish individual supervisory liability for a subordinate’s sexual abuse of students.

**Current rule:** Individual supervisory liability requires proof that the official had actual awareness, established directly or circumstantially, of facts or a pattern plainly pointing to a substantial risk that the subordinate was sexually abusing students; consciously failed to take an obviously necessary protective step within the official’s authority; and thereby caused the plaintiff’s injury.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not constitutionalize negligence, every inadequate investigation, or ordinary school-management error. Municipal liability, the teacher’s separate liability, damages, and the truth of disputed facts remain outside this decision.

**First controlling authority:** Doe v. Taylor Independent School District, 15 F.3d 443, decided October 16, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Bodily integrity and individual supervisory responsibility; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Doe v. Taylor Independent School District, 15 F.3d 443, decided October 16, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Bodily integrity and individual supervisory responsibility; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Doe v. Taylor Independent School District, 15 F.3d 443, October 16, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- October 16, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Doe v. Taylor Independent School District, 15 F.3d 443: The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule for individual supervisory liability arising from a subordinate public employee’s direct sexual invasion of a student’s bodily integrity. *Elder* and *Johnson v. Jones* are applied without alteration: the complete legal landscape governs clearly established law, while disputed factual sufficiency remains outside interlocutory review.

### Confidential retention of a ruled-out investigation

**Governing question:** Whether confidential retention of an accurate record of a ruled-out investigation deprives a family of liberty or property.

**Current rule:** Retention of a confidential record accurately stating that an investigation occurred and was ruled out does not deprive the investigated family of a federal liberty or property interest when the record is not publicly or materially disseminated, does not alter legal status, does not separate the family, and produces no denial of employment, custody, benefit, or other concrete entitlement.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not govern a registry disclosed to employers, licensing bodies, or custody decisionmakers; a record that omits the ruled-out disposition; an entry that automatically imposes a legal disability; or retention contrary to a substantive state-law entitlement to expungement.

**First controlling authority:** Hodge v. Jones, 31 F.3d 157, decided October 23, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Confidential retention of a ruled-out investigation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Hodge v. Jones, 31 F.3d 157, decided October 23, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Confidential retention of a ruled-out investigation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Hodge v. Jones, 31 F.3d 157, October 23, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- October 23, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Hodge v. Jones, 31 F.3d 157: *Doe* is distinguished. Its direct constitutional invasion and defendant-specific causal responsibility do not create a general expungement right. The decision establishes a narrow no-deprivation rule for confidential, accurate, nonconsequential retention while reserving materially disseminated or disability-producing registries.

### Indigent access to a first parental-termination appeal

**Governing question:** What access a State must provide when an indigent parent takes a first appeal from a permanent termination decree.

**Current rule:** A State that creates a first appeal from a judgment permanently and exclusively terminating the legal parent-child relationship may not deny meaningful review solely because the parent cannot prepay the record cost. It must furnish a transcript, settled narrative, agreed statement, or another adequate equivalent sufficient for review.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule creates no general right to a civil appeal, appointed appellate counsel, free discovery, or publicly financed litigation concerning money, property, or ordinary custody scheduling.

**First controlling authority:** M.L.B. v. S.L.J., 519 U.S. 102 (1996), decided December 16, 1996, Indigent access to the first termination appeal; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** M.L.B. v. S.L.J., 519 U.S. 102 (1996), decided December 16, 1996, Indigent access to the first termination appeal; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** M.L.B. v. S.L.J., 519 U.S. 102 (1996), December 16, 1996.

### Continued federal civil commitment under § 4246

**Governing question:** What current predicates and safeguards permit federal civil commitment to continue beyond the maximum sentence for a dismissed charge.

**Current rule:** Continued custody under § 4246 rests on current mental disease or defect causing current dangerousness, not punishment for the dismissed charge. It requires clear and convincing proof of the current statutory predicate, meaningful periodic review, practical routes to conditional or unconditional release, release when the predicate ends, and renewed reasonable efforts to transfer responsibility to an eligible State. Transfer becomes mandatory when a State accepts responsibility.

**Exceptions and limits:** Incompetence alone, dangerousness without qualifying mental disease, stale findings, nominal review, or retention after lawful state acceptance is insufficient.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Sahhar, 56 F.3d 1026, decided December 16, 1996, Duration and safeguards under § 4246 and state-placement obligation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Sahhar, 56 F.3d 1026, decided December 16, 1996, Duration and safeguards under § 4246 and state-placement obligation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Sahhar, 56 F.3d 1026, December 16, 1996.

### Incidental employment burdens on the right to marry

**Governing question:** What review governs a public-employer rule that affects spouses’ workplace assignments without prohibiting marriage.

**Current rule:** A governmental anti-nepotism rule that does not prohibit marriage, condition legal recognition, or impose a direct legal obstacle to marrying receives rational-basis review when it causes only incidental employment or economic consequences. It is valid when rationally related to legitimate concerns such as supervision, favoritism, conflicts, and workplace administration.

**Exceptions and limits:** A rule directly conditioning marriage, legal status, or access to a fundamental marital incident may require different review.

**First controlling authority:** Wright v. MetroHealth Medical Center, 58 F.3d 1130, decided March 3, 1997, Anti-nepotism policy and the right to marry; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Wright v. MetroHealth Medical Center, 58 F.3d 1130, decided March 3, 1997, Anti-nepotism policy and the right to marry; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Wright v. MetroHealth Medical Center, 58 F.3d 1130, March 3, 1997.

### Parole-like community release

**Governing question:** When a community-release program creates parole-type procedural protection.

**Current rule:** A release program equivalent to parole in operation creates a protected liberty interest even if the State uses a different label. When the participant lives and works in the community subject to conditions and faces return to prison, revocation requires the core notice, hearing, neutral decisionmaker, disclosure, opportunity to be heard, and written-reason protections associated with parole.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not govern ordinary institutional classifications or routine changes within prison.

**First controlling authority:** Young v. Harper, 520 U.S. 143 (1997), decided March 18, 1997, Parole-like community release; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Young v. Harper, 520 U.S. 143 (1997), decided March 18, 1997, Parole-like community release; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Young v. Harper, 520 U.S. 143 (1997), March 18, 1997.

### Governmental use and disclosure of sensitive identifying information

**Governing question:** What limits govern governmental acquisition, examination, use, and disclosure of highly sensitive identified information.

**Current rule:** Identifiable prescription and medical records, particularly records revealing HIV status, and a governmental compilation linking a named person to nonpublic current residential and identifying information receive limited Fourteenth Amendment protection against unjustified governmental acquisition, examination, use, and disclosure. Personally identifying access or dissemination must remain genuinely and proportionately connected to the actual governmental function. Relevant considerations include sensitivity, whether names or addresses rather than aggregate or coded data are necessary, who receives access, the use made of the information, the scope of disclosure, and the safeguards actually maintained.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Constitution creates no general right to conceal a public conviction or every fact obtainable from public sources. Aggregate or coded auditing, an identified inquiry prompted by concrete fraud or eligibility concerns, legally required disclosure, confidential retention, and targeted notification to persons with a concrete protective need may present different cases. Perfect prevention of every unauthorized disclosure is not required, but recipient eligibility, authorized use, confidentiality, and redistribution controls must be meaningfully fitted to the governmental function. The rule governs governmental, not private-employer, action.

**First controlling authority:** Doe v. Southeastern Pennsylvania Transportation Authority, 72 F.3d 1133, decided May 5, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, governmental use of identified medical information; established the medical-information component.

**Controlling authorities:** Doe v. Southeastern Pennsylvania Transportation Authority, 72 F.3d 1133, decided May 5, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, supplies the genuine-and-proportionate connection inquiry for identified medical information. Paul P. v. Verniero, 170 F.3d 396, decided February 7, 2000, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, extends limited protection to compiled nonpublic residential and identifying data and supplies the recipient, use, scope, confidentiality, and redistribution safeguards for targeted governmental disclosure.

**Present operation effective:** Paul P. v. Verniero, 170 F.3d 396, February 7, 2000, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Related current rules:** Process before higher-tier community notification. That rule governs the individualized classification procedure and proof; this rule separately governs the fit between the protective need and the information disseminated.

**Development:**
- February 7, 2000, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Paul P. v. Verniero, 170 F.3d 396: Limited informational-privacy protection, previously expressed only for identifiable medical information, now also reaches a governmental compilation of nonpublic current residential and identifying data. Targeted dissemination must be fitted to concrete protective need through meaningful recipient, use, scope, confidentiality, and redistribution controls. Former title: Governmental use of identifiable medical information.

### Immediate unpaid suspension after an independent arrest

**Governing question:** When a public employer may impose immediate unpaid suspension without a predeprivation hearing.

**Current rule:** An independent arrest and formal charge supplying reasonable grounds concerning conduct bearing on a sensitive public position may permit immediate temporary unpaid suspension without a prior opportunity to respond, provided the employee receives a constitutionally prompt and meaningful opportunity to contest the continuing consequence.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not authorize indefinite suspension, reliance on an arrest after contrary information emerges, or immediate action lacking an independent objective basis. A full evidentiary trial is not required before provisional action.

**First controlling authority:** Gilbert v. Homar, 520 U.S. 924 (1997), decided June 9, 1997, Immediate suspension without pay and prompt post-suspension process; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Gilbert v. Homar, 520 U.S. 924 (1997), decided June 9, 1997, Immediate suspension without pay and prompt post-suspension process; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Gilbert v. Homar, 520 U.S. 924 (1997), June 9, 1997.

### Fundamental-right identification and physician-assisted suicide

**Governing question:** How an asserted unenumerated liberty is identified and reviewed when the claimed entitlement is physician assistance in suicide.

**Current rule:** The asserted liberty must be identified as the legal entitlement actually sought rather than at the highest level of generality. The court examines constitutional text and structure, precedent, the function of the claimed protection, and relevant legal tradition. Those sources do not establish a fundamental right to obtain intentional physician assistance in suicide, so a prohibition receives rational-basis review.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not permit forced treatment, override competent refusal or advance directives, criminalize good-faith pain relief merely because death is foreseen, or deny lawful palliative care. A materially narrower application involving another independently protected interest remains open.

**First controlling authority:** Washington v. Glucksberg, 521 U.S. 702 (1997), decided June 26, 1997, Asserted fundamental right to physician-assisted suicide; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Washington v. Glucksberg, 521 U.S. 702 (1997), decided June 26, 1997, Asserted fundamental right to physician-assisted suicide; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Washington v. Glucksberg, 521 U.S. 702 (1997), June 26, 1997.

### Effective bypass authority in parental-notification laws

**Governing question:** What bypass authority a parental-notification law must provide for a minor seeking an abortion.

**Current rule:** A State may encourage parental consultation and require notice for a minor who cannot establish maturity or a contrary best interest. When notification is otherwise unavoidable, an effective independent decisionmaker must possess authority to authorize the abortion without notice under criteria addressing maturity and best interests. The Constitution does not require one verbal formulation of the best-interests inquiry; it requires genuine authority to dispense with notice when notification is contrary to the minor’s best interests.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decisionmaker need not be a court, and the rule does not prescribe a particular hearing format or decide separate penalty, information, and severability questions.

**First controlling authority:** Planned Parenthood v. Miller, 63 F.3d 1452, decided April 15, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Facial review and parental notification; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Planned Parenthood v. Miller, 63 F.3d 1452, decided April 15, 1996, supplies the effective bypass requirement. Lambert v. Wicklund, 520 U.S. 292 (1997), decided March 31, 1997, clarifies that no mandatory phrasing of the best-interests inquiry is required.

**Present operation effective:** Lambert v. Wicklund, 520 U.S. 292 (1997), March 31, 1997.

### Caregiving relationships and the rights of a fit parent

**Governing question:** What constitutional protection governs displacement of a fit parent’s authority over a child by a prospective adoptive placement or a third-party visitation order.

**Current rule:** The Due Process Clause protects a fit parent’s authority over the care, custody, and control of the parent’s child. Time accumulated in a prospective adoptive placement cannot itself transfer permanent parenthood when the adoption lacked the fit parent’s consent or a valid termination of parental rights. A court likewise may not sustain the third-party visitation order adjudicated in *Troxel* over a fit parent’s objection merely by substituting its preferred arrangement for the parent’s decision.

**Exceptions and limits:** Prospective adoptive parents remain entitled to fair adjudication of their own legal claims, but not to a comparative custody proceeding that treats possession accumulated during litigation as a constitutional source of superior title. The rule does not prevent termination of an unfit parent’s rights through adequate procedures, emergency protection of a child, or child-specific safeguards governing implementation of a custody judgment.

**Unresolved interaction or authority boundary:** *Troxel* supplies no controlling *Marks* rationale establishing a universal third-party-visitation test. No participating majority adopted a universal serious-harm predicate, strict scrutiny, the plurality’s special-weight formulation, or facial invalidation. The independently majority-supported fit-parent proposition and invalidity of the order adjudicated there coexist with the adoption-placement rule from *Doe v. Kirchner*; controlling law does not supply a single additional test governing every nonparent claim.

**First controlling authority:** *Doe v. Kirchner*, 159 Ill. 2d 347, 638 N.E.2d 181, decided June 21, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, fit-parent rights in a prospective adoptive placement; established the first component of the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Doe v. Kirchner*, 159 Ill. 2d 347, 638 N.E.2d 181, decided June 21, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, supplies the adoption-placement component. *Troxel v. Granville*, 530 U.S. 57 (2000), decided June 5, 2000, supplies the independently majority-supported protection of a fit parent’s authority over care, custody, and control, the invalidity of the visitation order adjudicated there, and the unresolved authority boundary.

**Present operation effective:** *Troxel v. Granville*, 530 U.S. 57 (2000), June 5, 2000.

**Related current rules:** `Child-specific process governing implementation of custody`, under Due Process, governs the manner of carrying an adjudicated custody judgment into effect.

**Development:**
- June 5, 2000, *Troxel v. Granville*: Before *Troxel*, the entry governed the attempt to displace a fit parent through time accumulated in a prospective adoptive placement. After *Troxel*, the fit-parent liberty protection also controls the third-party visitation order adjudicated there, while the absence of a majority rationale leaves the universal visitation mechanics unresolved. The adoption-placement and implementation limits remain unchanged.

### Affirmative official creation or intensification of a private danger

**Governing question:** When affirmative official conduct that creates or materially increases a specific private danger violates substantive due process.

**Current rule:** Substantive due process may be violated when an official affirmatively uses governmental authority to create or materially intensify a specific, immediate, and grave private danger, knows or should know the risk, acts with the culpability required by the available deliberation time, and causes the resulting exposure. A promise-and-reliance theory requires the inducement, altered self-protection, and causation components stated in Pinder. When officers directly force an incapacitated person into the danger after time to deliberate, separate inducement and reliance are unnecessary; deliberate indifference to the known risk is sufficient.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule creates no general police duty to protect and does not constitutionalize negligence or a broken assurance standing alone.

**First controlling authority:** Pinder v. Johnson, 54 F.3d 1169, decided May 21, 1996, affirmative creation or intensification of danger; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Pinder v. Johnson, 54 F.3d 1169, decided May 21, 1996, promise-and-reliance route; County of Sacramento v. Lewis, 523 U.S. 833 (1998), decided May 26, 1998, culpability calibrated to deliberation time; City of Florence v. Chipman, 126 F.3d 856, decided June 1, 1998, direct forced-exposure route; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** City of Florence v. Chipman, 126 F.3d 856, June 1, 1998.

**Development:**
- June 1, 1998, City of Florence v. Chipman: Pinder’s inducement and reliance mechanics remain required for the promise route but are not universal elements when police directly force an incapacitated person into a known grave danger.

### Extraterritorial enforcement of an injunction against nonparties

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs extraterritorial effect of the Michigan injunction.

**Current rule:** Full faith and credit does not require one State’s court to enforce an injunction as an evidentiary exclusion rule in a different State’s action involving parties who were neither parties nor privies to the original proceeding.

**First controlling authority:** Baker v. General Motors Corp., 522 U.S. 222 (1998), decided January 13, 1998, Extraterritorial effect of the Michigan injunction; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Baker v. General Motors Corp., 522 U.S. 222 (1998), decided January 13, 1998, Extraterritorial effect of the Michigan injunction; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Baker v. General Motors Corp., 522 U.S. 222 (1998), January 13, 1998.

### Fourteenth Amendment personhood before live birth

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs fourteenth Amendment personhood before live birth.

**Current rule:** A fetus that was not born alive is not a person within the Fourteenth Amendment. A State may use live birth as a prerequisite for fetal-estate, survival, and derivative wrongful-death remedies when that line is rationally related to administrable boundaries concerning legal personhood, estate creation, causation, and damages. The State’s interest in potential life remains distinct from constitutional personhood.

**First controlling authority:** Alexander v. Whitman, 114 F.3d 1392, decided February 2, 1998, Fourteenth Amendment personhood before live birth; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Alexander v. Whitman, 114 F.3d 1392, decided February 2, 1998, Fourteenth Amendment personhood before live birth; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Alexander v. Whitman, 114 F.3d 1392, February 2, 1998.

### Process before higher-tier community notification

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs process and proof before higher-tier notification.

**Current rule:** Before Tier Two or Tier Three notification, the State must provide timely notice, disclose the proposed factual basis and notification scope, permit a meaningful opportunity to contest the classification before a neutral decisionmaker, issue written reasons, and prove the material facts and risk classification by clear and convincing evidence.

**First controlling authority:** W.P. v. Verniero, 119 F.3d 1077, decided March 16, 1998, Process and proof before higher-tier notification; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** W.P. v. Verniero, 119 F.3d 1077, decided March 16, 1998, Process and proof before higher-tier notification; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** W.P. v. Verniero, 119 F.3d 1077, March 16, 1998.

**Related current rules:** Governmental use and disclosure of sensitive identifying information. This entry governs individualized classification procedure and proof; the related rule separately governs whether the recipients, information, use, scope, confidentiality, and redistribution controls are fitted to the protective need.

### Minimum access to state clemency procedures

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs minimal constitutional limits on clemency access.

**Current rule:** Discretionary clemency creates no substantive entitlement and does not require trial-type safeguards, but due process forbids wholly arbitrary state action that effectively denies access to the clemency process the State chose to provide.

**First controlling authority:** Ohio Adult Parole Authority v. Woodard, 523 U.S. 272 (1998), decided March 25, 1998, Minimal constitutional limits on clemency access; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Ohio Adult Parole Authority v. Woodard, 523 U.S. 272 (1998), decided March 25, 1998, Minimal constitutional limits on clemency access; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Ohio Adult Parole Authority v. Woodard, 523 U.S. 272 (1998), March 25, 1998.

### Clarity and culpability in penal abortion restrictions

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs scope, notice, and scienter of the D&X prohibition.

**Current rule:** A State may not prohibit the predominant previability abortion method through an enacted definition whose practical reach creates a substantial obstacle. A penal method prohibition must require sufficient culpability concerning the disputed clinical judgment separating lawful from unlawful conduct.

**First controlling authority:** Women’s Medical Professional Corp. v. Voinovich, 130 F.3d 187, decided April 20, 1998, Scope, notice, and scienter of the D&X prohibition; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Women’s Medical Professional Corp. v. Voinovich, 130 F.3d 187, decided April 20, 1998, Scope, notice, and scienter of the D&X prohibition; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Women’s Medical Professional Corp. v. Voinovich, 130 F.3d 187, April 20, 1998.

### Culpability during rapidly evolving police emergencies

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs culpability during a rapidly evolving pursuit.

**Current rule:** A high-speed pursuit requiring instantaneous operational judgment violates substantive due process only when the officer acts with a purpose to cause harm unrelated to legitimate apprehension. Deliberate indifference may remain sufficient in materially different settings allowing genuine deliberation.

**First controlling authority:** County of Sacramento v. Lewis, 523 U.S. 833 (1998), decided May 26, 1998, Culpability during a rapidly evolving pursuit; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** County of Sacramento v. Lewis, 523 U.S. 833 (1998), decided May 26, 1998, Culpability during a rapidly evolving pursuit; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** County of Sacramento v. Lewis, 523 U.S. 833 (1998), May 26, 1998.

### Post-seizure notice and published recovery procedures

**Governing question:** What notice due process requires after personal property is seized under a criminal warrant.

**Current rule:** When government gives timely notice identifying the seized property and the responsible custodian, due process does not additionally require personalized instructions describing a published, generally available state-law procedure for obtaining return of the property.

**Exceptions and limits:** Additional procedural information may be required when the available route is unpublished or inaccessible. The rule does not govern pre-seizure process for civil forfeiture of real property.

**First controlling authority:** City of West Covina v. Perkins, 525 U.S. 234 (1999), decided January 13, 1999, content of post-seizure notice; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** City of West Covina v. Perkins, 525 U.S. 234 (1999), decided January 13, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** City of West Covina v. Perkins, 525 U.S. 234 (1999), January 13, 1999.

### Statutory eligibility conditions and property interests

**Governing question:** Whether due process requires payment of a disputed statutory benefit before an enacted eligibility condition is resolved.

**Current rule:** A claimant has no protected entitlement to payment of a particular disputed bill before the treatment or service has been determined eligible under the governing statutory conditions. A distinct claim to fair adjudication of the underlying statutory entitlement must be preserved and analyzed separately.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not decide whether the underlying statutory claim itself is protected property or what process a governmental actor must provide when that theory is properly presented.

**First controlling authority:** American Manufacturers Mutual Insurance Co. v. Sullivan, 526 U.S. 40 (1999), decided March 3, 1999, payment before the statutory condition is resolved; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** American Manufacturers Mutual Insurance Co. v. Sullivan, 526 U.S. 40 (1999), decided March 3, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** American Manufacturers Mutual Insurance Co. v. Sullivan, 526 U.S. 40 (1999), March 3, 1999.

### Standardless antecedent triggers in penal laws

**Governing question:** When an undefined antecedent classification makes a criminal public-order law facially vague.

**Current rule:** A penal law is facially vague when compulsory police action and every resulting prosecution depend on an officer’s unexplained or subjective antecedent classification and the law supplies no objective criterion governing that judgment. A warning, dispersal order, later refusal to obey, or additional mental-state requirement does not cure the undefined trigger. When the trigger purports to preserve First Amendment activity through an undefined exception, the law must supply objective standards or a binding narrowing construction adequate to give the public and enforcement officers fair notice.

**Consequence or recurring remedy:** A law whose enforcement necessarily depends on the standardless trigger is facially invalid.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not decide the substantive validity of a precisely defined public-order restriction or a juvenile curfew containing objective standards and constitutionally adequate exceptions.

**First controlling authority:** City of Chicago v. Morales, 527 U.S. 41 (1999), decided June 10, 1999, standardless dispersal trigger; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** City of Chicago v. Morales, 527 U.S. 41 (1999), decided June 10, 1999, supplies the subjective-trigger and warning components. Schleifer v. City of Charlottesville, 159 F.3d 843, decided June 15, 1999, supplies the boundary for an undefined First Amendment exception in a penal curfew.

**Present operation effective:** Schleifer v. City of Charlottesville, 159 F.3d 843, June 15, 1999.

**Development:**
- June 15, 1999, Schleifer v. City of Charlottesville, 159 F.3d 843: The warning rule established five days earlier extends to an undefined First Amendment exception that controls whether lawful nighttime presence becomes subject to dispersal and criminal exposure. The substantive standards governing a precise juvenile curfew and fit-parent authority remain unresolved.

### Process before liability against a newly added party

**Governing question:** What process must precede imposition of personal liability on a person newly added as a party after an earlier judgment.

**Current rule:** A court may not add a person as an adverse party after judgment and simultaneously impose personal liability. The newly added party must receive service of the amended pleading and a meaningful opportunity to answer, assert defenses, obtain appropriate discovery, and contest liability before adjudication.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** After amendment adding the adverse party, serve the amended pleading; allow the response period, defenses, and appropriate discovery; then adjudicate the asserted basis of liability.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not immunize the new party from alter-ego, successor-liability, or another properly pleaded theory. It requires fair procedure before adjudication and does not predetermine the merits.

**First controlling authority:** *Nelson v. Adams USA, Inc.*, 529 U.S. 460, decided April 25, 2000, liability imposed simultaneously with postjudgment joinder; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Nelson v. Adams USA, Inc.*, 529 U.S. 460, decided April 25, 2000, supplies the service, response, defense, discovery, and preliability sequence.

**Present operation effective:** *Nelson v. Adams USA, Inc.*, April 25, 2000.

### Reincarceration after mistaken release

**Governing question:** What substantive-due-process standard governs an executive decision to reincarcerate a person after a good-faith mistaken release while a valid sentence remains unfinished.

**Current rule:** Reincarceration to complete a still-valid sentence after a good-faith mistaken release does not violate substantive due process merely because the person lived successfully at liberty for a substantial period. The executive correction is governed by the shocks-the-conscience standard, informed by history and tradition, the actor’s culpability, the continuing validity of the sentence, and the practical opportunity for deliberation. No fundamental right exists to retain liberty obtained through administrative error while the lawful sentence remains uncompleted.

**Exceptions and limits:** Bad-faith inducement, deliberate manipulation, an attempt to evade a legally completed sentence, or comparably egregious circumstances may present a different conscience-shocking case. The rule does not decide state-law sentence credit, estoppel, compensation, a defect in the retaking procedure, a materially longer state-caused delay, or custody sought after the sentence legally ended.

**First controlling authority:** *Hawkins v. Freeman*, citation and docket not supplied, decided August 14, 2000, reincarceration after good-faith mistaken release; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Hawkins v. Freeman*, citation and docket not supplied, decided August 14, 2000, by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies the shocks-the-conscience standard, no-fundamental-right conclusion, culpability and deliberation considerations, and stated egregious-circumstances limits.

**Present operation effective:** *Hawkins v. Freeman*, August 14, 2000.

## Equal Protection

### Sex discrimination in peremptory challenges

**Governing question:** Whether a party may use peremptory challenges to exclude prospective jurors because of sex.

**Current rule:** The Equal Protection Clause prohibits purposeful use of peremptory challenges to exclude prospective jurors because of sex, including in civil litigation conducted through state judicial process.

**Burdens and proof:** The ultimate burden of persuasion remains on the challenger.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** The challenger must establish circumstances supporting an inference of purposeful sex discrimination. The striking party must then articulate a genuine sex-neutral explanation, and the trial court determines whether purposeful discrimination occurred.

**Exceptions and limits:** Disproportionate impact alone does not prove purpose, and the rule does not bar strikes based on individualized answers, relationships, experiences, or demeanor that are not proxies for sex.

**First controlling authority:** J.E.B. v. Alabama ex rel. T.B., 511 U.S. 127 (1994), decided April 19, 1994, Sex discrimination in peremptory challenges; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** J.E.B. v. Alabama ex rel. T.B., 511 U.S. 127 (1994), decided April 19, 1994, Sex discrimination in peremptory challenges; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** J.E.B. v. Alabama ex rel. T.B., 511 U.S. 127 (1994), April 19, 1994.

### Sex-conscious remedies for demonstrated governmental inequality

**Governing question:** When a public entity may use sex-conscious measures to correct or avoid perpetuating demonstrated inequality within its own program or employment structure.

**Current rule:** Correcting or avoiding perpetuation of demonstrated sex inequality within a governmental system is an important objective. The public entity must establish an objectively grounded inequality connected to its own system and a substantial, nonstereotyped relationship between the sex-conscious measure and preservation or correction of the underrepresented sex’s opportunity. Numerical imbalance alone is insufficient. In public employment, evidence of historical exclusion, relevant workforce conditions, and their connection to the challenged internal promotion structure may establish the predicate; a temporary and bounded promotion measure operating among qualified candidates may satisfy the required fit without using sex as a permanent bar or discharging nonbeneficiaries.

**Exceptions and limits:** The principle applies symmetrically to whichever sex is demonstrably underrepresented. It does not decide classifications involving sexual orientation, gender identity, transgender status, or eligibility for an individual sex-designated team.

**First controlling authority:** Kelley v. Board of Trustees of the University of Illinois, 35 F.3d 265, decided May 22, 1995, Equal protection and sex-conscious restructuring; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Kelley v. Board of Trustees of the University of Illinois, 35 F.3d 265, decided May 22, 1995, supplies the demonstrated-inequality, substantial-relationship, and nonstereotype requirements. City of Dallas v. Dallas Fire Fighters Association, 150 F.3d 438, decided June 18, 1999, supplies the public-employment predicate and the constitutionality of a temporary, bounded promotion remedy among qualified candidates.

**Present operation effective:** City of Dallas v. Dallas Fire Fighters Association, 150 F.3d 438, June 18, 1999.

**Development:**
- June 18, 1999, City of Dallas v. Dallas Fire Fighters Association, 150 F.3d 438: The demonstrated-inequality rule formerly stated for athletic-program restructuring now also governs a public employer’s remedial promotions. Historical exclusion, relevant workforce evidence, and connection to the internal promotion system may establish the predicate, and a temporary bounded measure among qualified candidates may satisfy intermediate scrutiny. Former title: Equal protection and sex-conscious restructuring.

### Race-conscious public-employment remedies

**Governing question:** What predicate and fit permit a public employer to use race-conscious remedial promotions.

**Current rule:** A public employer has a compelling remedial interest when objectively grounded evidence connects its own relevant racial discrimination to continuing effects in the challenged employment structure. A final liability judgment concerning each beneficiary is not indispensable. The measure must be narrowly tailored to that source-linked injury. A temporary, rank-specific measure operating only among qualified candidates within genuine equivalence bands, following attempted race-neutral reforms, ending with the identified imbalance, limiting the share of affected vacancies, and imposing neither discharge nor an absolute barrier to later advancement satisfies that requirement on the record establishing those limits.

**Burdens and proof:** The public employer bears the burden of establishing both the source-linked remedial predicate and narrow tailoring.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not approve generalized societal remediation, permanent racial allocation, selection outside genuine qualification bands, the entire affirmative-action plan from which a challenged measure arose, or a measure unsupported by evidence connecting the employer’s own discrimination to the affected structure.

**First controlling authority:** City of Dallas v. Dallas Fire Fighters Association, 150 F.3d 438, decided June 18, 1999, racial remedial predicate and fit of race-conscious promotions; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** City of Dallas v. Dallas Fire Fighters Association, 150 F.3d 438, decided June 18, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** City of Dallas v. Dallas Fire Fighters Association, 150 F.3d 438, June 18, 1999.

### Violation-remedy nexus and interdistrict attraction

**Governing question:** How a school-desegregation court determines whether an intradistrict remedy remains tied to an identified vestige rather than an interdistrict attraction objective.

**Current rule:** A school-desegregation remedy may continue only insofar as it remains reasonably directed to eliminating an identified vestige of the constitutional violation to the extent practicable. An intradistrict violation does not authorize interdistrict attraction or reversal of demographic movement as an independent constitutional objective. For each challenged component, the district court must identify the constitutional violation or vestige addressed, determine whether the measure remains reasonably connected to eliminating it, and assess whether continued federal control is still necessary. Federal remedial authority is corrective rather than a general mandate to optimize a school system.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not categorically forbid salary measures, educational improvements, or other relief genuinely necessary to eliminate a proven vestige. It does not require premature termination or impose a fixed administrative checklist.

**First controlling authority:** Missouri v. Jenkins, 515 U.S. 70, decided June 12, 1995, Violation-remedy nexus and interdistrict attraction; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Missouri v. Jenkins, 515 U.S. 70, decided June 12, 1995, Violation-remedy nexus and interdistrict attraction; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Missouri v. Jenkins, 515 U.S. 70, June 12, 1995.

### Achievement benchmarks and incremental withdrawal of control

**Governing question:** Whether national achievement levels justify continued desegregation control and when control must end incrementally.

**Current rule:** National achievement levels are not freestanding constitutional benchmarks for determining whether desegregation remedies must continue. Federal control must be withdrawn incrementally from separable areas in which compliance is complete and the relevant vestige has been eliminated to the extent practicable. Academic performance may be relevant evidence when prior de jure segregation caused an identified educational deficit, but the constitutional question is whether that attributable vestige has been remedied to the practicable extent, not whether students have reached national averages or the district’s maximum potential.

**Exceptions and limits:** Test scores and other educational evidence remain relevant when tied to a proven vestige. The Court does not determine on the present record which particular programs must end or continue.

**First controlling authority:** Missouri v. Jenkins, 515 U.S. 70, decided June 12, 1995, Achievement benchmarks and incremental withdrawal of control; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Missouri v. Jenkins, 515 U.S. 70, decided June 12, 1995, Achievement benchmarks and incremental withdrawal of control; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Missouri v. Jenkins, 515 U.S. 70, June 12, 1995.

### Nonexclusive racial access and inclusion measures

**Governing question:** What scrutiny governs a race-conscious governmental measure that expands access or structures institutional integration without making race decisive for an identified person’s discrete benefit.

**Current rule:** A genuinely remedial federal measure that expands access without making race decisive for a discrete contract, payment, or comparable benefit must serve an important remedial objective and bear a substantial relationship to that objective. A K–12 school system not remedying its own adjudicated constitutional violation may pursue meaningful integration and prevention of serious racial isolation through a genuinely inclusionary structural measure under the same demanding intermediate scrutiny when the measure concerns the institution’s operation rather than allocation of an identified student’s benefit by race. A qualifying K–12 measure must expand or preserve meaningful interracial educational participation, avoid racial entitlement, stigma, and stereotype, reject racial percentages as ends in themselves, consider workable race-neutral means, and contain meaningful limits on scope, duration, review, and termination.

**Exceptions and limits:** A federal remedial measure must address identified exclusion rather than generalized societal discrimination. A race-determinative allocation of an identified contract, payment, school seat, or comparable discrete benefit falls outside this rule and is governed by strict scrutiny. Racial balance or proportional numbers cannot serve as an independent governmental end. Formal labels and nominal percentages do not control; the measure’s actual legal operation does.

**First controlling authority:** *Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Peña*, 515 U.S. 200, decided June 12, 1995, nonexclusive federal access measures; established the federal remedial component.

**Controlling authorities:** *Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Peña*, 515 U.S. 200, decided June 12, 1995, supplies the federal remedial-access component. *Eisenberg v. Montgomery County Public Schools*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 20, 2000, supplies the important integration objective, the institution-versus-individual-allocation boundary, and the fit, alternatives, duration, review, termination, stigma, entitlement, and racial-balance limits for inclusionary K–12 measures.

**Present operation effective:** *Eisenberg v. Montgomery County Public Schools*, June 20, 2000.

**Related current rules:** `Race-determinative governmental benefits`, under Equal Protection, governs an identified benefit for which race is decisive. `Scrutiny of race-predominant districts`, under Equal Protection, applies the remedial-access principle to district-specific redistricting remedies after racial predominance is established.

**Development:**
- June 20, 2000, *Eisenberg v. Montgomery County Public Schools*: Before *Eisenberg*, the entry governed nonexclusive federal remedial access measures addressing identified exclusion. After *Eisenberg*, a separate inclusionary K–12 branch permits a genuinely structural measure serving meaningful integration or prevention of serious racial isolation under demanding intermediate scrutiny and supplies the stated fit and anti-balance limits. Race-determinative individual allocations remain governed by strict scrutiny. Former title: `Federal nonexclusive racial access measures`.

### Race-determinative governmental benefits

**Governing question:** What scrutiny applies when government makes race decisive for an identified person’s receipt of a contract, payment, school seat, or comparable discrete benefit.

**Current rule:** Strict scrutiny governs when race determines or effectively determines who receives a particular governmental contract, payment, school seat, or comparable discrete benefit. Government must establish a compelling interest and narrow tailoring. The inquiry includes the identified remedial predicate, necessity, actual operation and duration, workable race-neutral alternatives, and whether the racial rule reaches materially beyond the demonstrated injury. A K–12 school system not remedying its own adjudicated constitutional violation may not make an identified student’s race determinative of access to a specific school or program unless that classification satisfies strict scrutiny.

**Exceptions and limits:** A genuinely inclusionary structural measure that does not allocate an identified benefit by race is governed by the separate nonexclusive-access rule. Meaningful integration and prevention of serious racial isolation are distinct from racial balance, but proportional racial numbers cannot serve as an independent end or as the decisional mechanism for an identified individual benefit.

**First controlling authority:** *Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Peña*, 515 U.S. 200, decided June 12, 1995, race-determinative federal governmental benefits; established the strict-scrutiny component.

**Controlling authorities:** *Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Peña*, 515 U.S. 200, decided June 12, 1995, supplies strict scrutiny for a race-determinative federal contract, payment, or comparable benefit. *Eisenberg v. Montgomery County Public Schools*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 20, 2000, supplies the K–12 school-access component and the boundary between individual allocation and inclusionary institutional structure.

**Present operation effective:** *Eisenberg v. Montgomery County Public Schools*, June 20, 2000.

**Related current rules:** `Nonexclusive racial access and inclusion measures`, under Equal Protection, governs a qualifying access-expanding or inclusionary structural measure that does not make race decisive for an identified discrete benefit. `Scrutiny of race-predominant districts`, under Equal Protection, governs the distinct districting context after racial predominance is established.

**Development:**
- June 20, 2000, *Eisenberg v. Montgomery County Public Schools*: Before *Eisenberg*, the entry expressly governed race-determinative federal contracts, payments, and comparable benefits. After *Eisenberg*, the same strict-scrutiny rule expressly governs an identified K–12 school seat or program access made determinative by race, while genuinely inclusionary institutional measures remain governed separately. Former title: `Race-determinative federal governmental benefits`.

### Racial predominance in districting

**Governing question:** When racial considerations predominate over traditional race-neutral principles in designing a particular district.

**Current rule:** Race is predominant when racial considerations materially subordinate traditional race-neutral districting principles in the particular district. Courts examine the actual design process and boundaries, including who was included or excluded, boundary movement, splitting or combining communities and political subdivisions, compactness, contiguity, communities of interest, ordinary political geography, and whether a racial target displaced those ordinary considerations. Geometric bizarreness is relevant evidence but not a threshold element.

**Burdens and proof:** At summary judgment, supported evidence permitting both a racial and a political explanation must be viewed in favor of the nonmoving State. Predominance may not be found for the challenger at that stage unless no reasonable factfinder could accept the political account.

**Exceptions and limits:** Racial awareness, demographic forecasting, or compliance analysis does not alone establish predominance. The inquiry remains district-specific and turns on whether race materially subordinated the principles that otherwise would have shaped the district.

**Related current rules:** Scrutiny of race-predominant districts, under Equal Protection, supplies the next analytical stage after predominance is established.

**First controlling authority:** Miller v. Johnson / Abrams v. Johnson / United States v. Johnson, 515 U.S. 900, decided June 29, 1995, Predominance threshold; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Miller v. Johnson / Abrams v. Johnson / United States v. Johnson, 515 U.S. 900, decided June 29, 1995, supplies the predominance threshold. Hunt v. Cromartie, 526 U.S. 541 (1999), decided May 17, 1999, supplies the summary-judgment treatment of supported racial and political explanations.

**Present operation effective:** Hunt v. Cromartie, 526 U.S. 541 (1999), May 17, 1999.

**Development:**
- May 17, 1999, Hunt v. Cromartie, 526 U.S. 541 (1999): The district-specific predominance standard remains unchanged. When race and politics plausibly explain correlated districting evidence, summary judgment for the challenger is unavailable unless the political account could not be accepted by a reasonable factfinder.

### Scrutiny of race-predominant districts

**Governing question:** Which scrutiny lane applies to a race-predominant district, and what remedial predicate and fit the State must establish.

**Current rule:** Once race predominates, a genuinely fitted, district-specific remedy for a demonstrated constitutional or Voting Rights Act violation receives intermediate scrutiny. Under that lane, the State must establish an important remedial objective grounded in the demonstrated violation and a substantial, district-specific fit between the racial design and the injury. A race-predominant district lacking that remedial predicate, or driven by racial maximization, unnecessary packing or separation, or unsupported racial allocation, receives strict scrutiny, requiring a compelling interest and narrow tailoring.

**Burdens and proof:** After predominance is shown, the State bears the burden of establishing the qualifying remedial lane and satisfying the corresponding scrutiny.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** First determine racial predominance. If predominance is established, classify the district as a genuinely fitted remedial measure or unsupported racial sorting, then apply intermediate or strict scrutiny accordingly.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court did not decide whether Georgia’s Eleventh District was constitutional or unconstitutional. The classification and applicable scrutiny remained for remand. Racial awareness, demographic forecasting, and compliance analysis alone do not establish predominance.

**Related current rules:** Racial predominance in districting supplies the threshold. Federal nonexclusive racial access measures and Race-determinative federal governmental benefits, under Equal Protection, supply the Adarand operational distinction applied in the districting setting.

**First controlling authority:** Miller v. Johnson / Abrams v. Johnson / United States v. Johnson, 515 U.S. 900, decided June 29, 1995, Two scrutiny lanes after race predominates; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Miller v. Johnson / Abrams v. Johnson / United States v. Johnson, 515 U.S. 900, decided June 29, 1995, Two scrutiny lanes after race predominates; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Miller v. Johnson / Abrams v. Johnson / United States v. Johnson, 515 U.S. 900, June 29, 1995.

### Comparator and scrutiny for sex-structured prison services

**Governing question:** How courts identify comparators and select scrutiny for sex-structured prison services.

**Current rule:** For each challenged service, the court must identify the relevant eligibility, need, custody, safety, scale, staffing, demand, and cost characteristics. An express allocation by sex receives intermediate scrutiny, requiring an important governmental objective and a substantial, nonstereotyped relationship between the classification and that objective. When a facially neutral rule produces a disparity, the plaintiffs must establish discriminatory purpose before sex-based scrutiny attaches. Legitimate correctional differences then operate within the comparator, purpose, and fit inquiries to which they are legally relevant.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not require identical prisons, equal per-capita spending, identical program menus, or disregard of legitimate differences in security, population, need, demand, staffing, and cost. Disparate impact alone does not establish discriminatory purpose.

**First controlling authority:** Klinger v. Department of Corrections, 31 F.3d 727, decided December 4, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Comparator and scrutiny for sex-structured prison services; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Klinger v. Department of Corrections, 31 F.3d 727, decided December 4, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Comparator and scrutiny for sex-structured prison services; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Klinger v. Department of Corrections, 31 F.3d 727, December 4, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- December 4, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Klinger v. Department of Corrections, 31 F.3d 727: *Kelley* is clarified for sex-segregated correctional institutions. Its intermediate scrutiny governs an express sex-based service allocation, while *Feeney*’s purpose requirement continues to govern facially neutral rules. The decision adds service-specific comparator analysis and confirms that correctional deference does not displace the scrutiny attached to an express sex classification.

### Adequacy of a replacement public-education remedy

**Governing question:** What operational equality a remedy must provide after women are excluded from a public educational opportunity.

**Current rule:** A remedy for exclusion from a public educational opportunity must place the excluded class, as nearly as practicable, in the position it would have occupied absent the violation. Comparison proceeds in operation and includes institutional benefits that materially affect the value of the education.

**Exceptions and limits:** A State may admit the excluded class to the original institution or adopt another lawful system supplying genuinely equal public opportunities and benefits. The Court does not prescribe every implementation detail.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Virginia; Virginia v. United States, 518 U.S. 515, decided June 26, 1996, Adequacy of VWIL; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Virginia; Virginia v. United States, 518 U.S. 515, decided June 26, 1996, Adequacy of VWIL; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Virginia; Virginia v. United States, 518 U.S. 515, June 26, 1996.

### Sex-based exclusion from a public military college

**Governing question:** What standard governs an express sex classification excluding women from a public military college.

**Current rule:** An express sex classification receives intermediate scrutiny. Government bears the burden of establishing that the classification serves an actual important objective and is substantially related to that objective through a genuine, nonstereotyped fit. The phrase “exceedingly persuasive justification” describes the rigor of that received standard; it does not create a separate tier of scrutiny.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not invalidate every public single-sex program or forbid tailored arrangements responding to genuine physical differences. Such a program must satisfy intermediate scrutiny and provide real rather than nominal equality.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Virginia; Virginia v. United States, 518 U.S. 515, decided June 26, 1996, VMI’s male-only admissions policy; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Virginia; Virginia v. United States, 518 U.S. 515, decided June 26, 1996, VMI’s male-only admissions policy; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Virginia; Virginia v. United States, 518 U.S. 515, June 26, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 26, 1996, United States v. Virginia; Virginia v. United States, 518 U.S. 515: *Kelley* is applied and clarified. An important objective must explain the actual classification and may not rest on group stereotypes. *Klinger*’s service-specific comparison principle is extended to public educational opportunities, including relevant intangible benefits.
