The decision does not create a categorical rule against single-sex public education.

### Status-based withdrawal of ordinary legal protection

**Governing question:** When a law broadly withdrawing ordinary legal protection from a status-defined class fails rational-basis review.

**Current rule:** A law that identifies a class by status and broadly withdraws access to ordinary legal protection must bear a rational relationship to a legitimate objective distinct from hostility, civic subordination, or a desire to make the class unequal. The breadth and operation of the classification may demonstrate that the asserted objectives do not explain the law.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not require a jurisdiction to enact any particular antidiscrimination law, create a right to preferred political outcomes, invalidate neutral generally applicable rules, decide a substantive-liberty claim, or assign sexual-orientation classifications to a heightened-scrutiny tier.

**First controlling authority:** Romer v. Evans, 517 U.S. 620, decided May 20, 1996, Status-based withdrawal of ordinary legal protection; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Romer v. Evans, 517 U.S. 620, decided May 20, 1996, Status-based withdrawal of ordinary legal protection; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Romer v. Evans, 517 U.S. 620, May 20, 1996.

### Sexual-orientation classifications in public employment

**Governing question:** What review governs a public-employment decision that intentionally classifies through sexual orientation or a proxy for it.

**Current rule:** A public-employment decision intentionally classifying through sexual orientation, including a same-sex commitment ceremony used as a proxy, receives intermediate scrutiny. Government bears persuasion that the classification serves an actual important objective through a substantial, nonstereotyped fit. Military judgment receives substantial evidentiary weight in military applications but does not lower the standard. Identity-based presumptions must permit genuine individualized rebuttal through evidence of conduct, propensity, and intent.

**First controlling authority:** Walmer v. United States Department of Defense, 52 F.3d 851, decided September 11, 1995, sexual-orientation classifications in military employment; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Walmer v. United States Department of Defense, 52 F.3d 851, decided September 11, 1995; Thomasson v. Perry, 80 F.3d 915, decided April 5, 1996; Richenberg v. Cohen, decided December 8, 1997; Shahar v. Bowers, 114 F.3d 1097, decided March 2, 1998; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Shahar v. Bowers, 114 F.3d 1097, March 2, 1998.

**Development:**
- March 2, 1998, Shahar v. Bowers: The intermediate-scrutiny rule formerly governing military discharge classifications extends to civilian public employment and proxy classifications; military-specific evidentiary weight remains limited to military applications. Former title: Military sexual-orientation classifications and statement-based presumptions.

### Treatment refusal and intentional assistance in suicide

**Governing question:** Whether a State must treat refusal of unwanted medical treatment and intentional assistance in suicide as the same conduct.

**Current rule:** A State does not violate equal protection by permitting a competent person to refuse unwanted medical treatment while prohibiting intentional assistance in suicide. Rational-basis review governs when the law contains no suspect or status-based classification and distinguishes the acts through bodily consent, legal causation, intent, and professional role.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not authorize forced treatment, override an advance directive, permit discriminatory denial of pain care, or make good-faith palliation unlawful merely because death is foreseen.

**First controlling authority:** Vacco v. Quill, 521 U.S. 793 (1997), decided June 26, 1997, Treatment refusal and intentional assistance in suicide; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Vacco v. Quill, 521 U.S. 793 (1997), decided June 26, 1997, Treatment refusal and intentional assistance in suicide; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Vacco v. Quill, 521 U.S. 793 (1997), June 26, 1997.

### Sex classifications in derivative-citizenship rules

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs sex and parentage classifications.

**Current rule:** Derivative-citizenship rules violate equal protection when they impose materially different acknowledgment and relationship requirements on citizen fathers and citizen mothers through sex stereotypes rather than a substantial, nonstereotyped fit to legitimate proof and connection interests.

**First controlling authority:** Miller v. Albright, 523 U.S. 420 (1998), decided April 22, 1998, Sex and parentage classifications; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Miller v. Albright, 523 U.S. 420 (1998), decided April 22, 1998, Sex and parentage classifications; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Miller v. Albright, 523 U.S. 420 (1998), April 22, 1998.

### Equality remedies in citizenship administration

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs equality remedy in citizenship administration.

**Current rule:** A court finding an unconstitutional citizenship classification must provide a real equality remedy while respecting Congress’s authority to prescribe neutral citizenship conditions. Automatic conferral of citizenship is not required when an equivalent lawful procedure can cure the injury.

**First controlling authority:** Miller v. Albright, 523 U.S. 420 (1998), decided April 22, 1998, Equality remedy in citizenship administration; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Miller v. Albright, 523 U.S. 420 (1998), decided April 22, 1998, Equality remedy in citizenship administration; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Miller v. Albright, 523 U.S. 420 (1998), April 22, 1998.

### Selective enforcement based on perceived sexual orientation

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs selective enforcement based on perceived sexual orientation.

**Current rule:** Probable cause does not excuse an enforcement decision made solely because officers perceive the person to be homosexual. Selective enforcement for that reason violates equal protection when similarly situated persons outside the classification are treated differently and no legitimate enforcement purpose supports the distinction.

**First controlling authority:** City of Florence v. Chipman, 126 F.3d 856, decided June 1, 1998, Selective enforcement based on perceived sexual orientation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** City of Florence v. Chipman, 126 F.3d 856, decided June 1, 1998, Selective enforcement based on perceived sexual orientation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** City of Florence v. Chipman, 126 F.3d 856, June 1, 1998.

### Equal state citizenship after interstate migration

**Governing question:** Whether a State may give a bona fide new resident lower generally available benefits solely because of recent arrival or the benefit level in the resident's former State.

**Current rule:** A United States citizen who becomes a bona fide resident of a new State is entitled to the same generally available benefits as other similarly situated citizens of that State. The State may not create inferior first-year citizenship by reducing benefits solely because of recent arrival or the benefit level in the citizen's former State. Congress may structure and condition a federal benefit program, but it may not authorize a State to violate this Fourteenth Amendment guarantee.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** Determine whether the claimant has established bona fide residence and satisfies the State's generally applicable eligibility requirements. If so, the State may not vary the generally available benefit by duration of that residence or the former State's benefit level.

**Exceptions and limits:** A State may verify bona fide residence and enforce equal prospective eligibility requirements. The rule does not decide whether Congress could adopt a materially different, nationally uniform federal-benefit rule.

**First controlling authority:** Saenz v. Roe, 526 U.S. 489 (1999), decided May 17, 1999, equal state citizenship after interstate migration; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Saenz v. Roe, 526 U.S. 489 (1999), decided May 17, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Saenz v. Roe, 526 U.S. 489 (1999), May 17, 1999.

### Class-of-one intentional differential treatment

**Governing question:** What a plaintiff must allege and prove to establish a class-of-one equal-protection claim.

**Current rule:** A plaintiff may proceed as a class of one by plausibly alleging, and ultimately proving, intentional differential treatment from materially similarly situated persons without a rational basis for the distinction. Subjective spite or ill will may be evidence of arbitrariness or pretext but is not an independent element.

**Burdens and proof:** The plaintiff must establish intentional treatment, material similarity, and the absence of a rational basis.

**Exceptions and limits:** Negligence, routine administrative error, discretionary disagreement, and treatment supported by a material factual difference do not satisfy the rule.

**First controlling authority:** Village of Willowbrook v. Olech, 528 U.S. 562 (2000), decided February 23, 2000, class-of-one equal protection; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Village of Willowbrook v. Olech, 528 U.S. 562 (2000), decided February 23, 2000, supplies the elements, pleading consequence, and the status of subjective ill will.

**Present operation effective:** Village of Willowbrook v. Olech, 528 U.S. 562 (2000), February 23, 2000.

### Racially motivated election-to-appointment restructuring

**Governing question:** Whether the absence of an independent constitutional right to elect a governing body defeats an equal-protection claim that election was replaced by appointment for a racially discriminatory purpose.

**Current rule:** A proper nonimmune defendant may not implement or maintain replacement of an elected governing body with an appointed body when racial discrimination materially motivated the restructuring, even though a racially neutral decision to use appointment rather than election would ordinarily be constitutional. Governmental authority to choose either structure does not include authority to select or maintain it to disadvantage citizens because of race.

**Burdens and proof:** The claimant must establish discriminatory purpose, material motivation, causation, and the other governing equal-protection elements. A defendant remains entitled to any controlling same-decision defense; the restructuring and surrounding circumstances do not create a presumption of discriminatory purpose.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule creates no free-standing constitutional right to elect the particular office, does not displace state sovereign immunity for a direct claim against the State, and does not make a genuinely race-neutral appointment system unconstitutional.

**First controlling authority:** *Mixon v. Ohio*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 27, 2000, racially motivated election-to-appointment restructuring and effect of no independent right to elect the body; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Mixon v. Ohio*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 27, 2000, Questions 3 and 5, by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies the discriminatory-purpose rule, neutral-appointment boundary, proper-nonimmune-defendant limit, and no-independent-right component.

**Present operation effective:** *Mixon v. Ohio*, June 27, 2000.

**Related current rules:** `One-person-one-vote and genuinely appointive governing bodies`, under Elections and Voting, governs the neutral appointive structure. `Election-to-appointment restructuring under Voting Rights Act § 2`, under Elections and Voting, supplies the separate statutory route and benchmark.

## Elections and Voting

### Objective comparator for governing-body-size claims under Voting Rights Act § 2

**Governing question:** When a challenge to the size of a governing body is cognizable under Voting Rights Act § 2.

**Current rule:** A governing-body-size claim is cognizable under § 2 when the plaintiff identifies a concrete and workable alternative structure grounded in objective law, governmental practice, history, or a closely comparable institution. Comparator sufficiency permits the statutory comparison but does not itself prove vote dilution.

**Burdens and proof:** The plaintiff retains the existing burden to establish the Gingles predicates and liability under the statutory totality.

**Exceptions and limits:** The comparator requirement is a comparative merits prerequisite, not an Article III bar, and it creates no right to proportional representation.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** Determine first whether the plaintiff has identified a concrete and workable comparator, then separately apply the Gingles preconditions and the statutory totality. Comparator sufficiency neither establishes nor defeats liability.

**Related current rules:** Election-to-appointment restructuring under Voting Rights Act § 2, under Elections and Voting, uses the State’s immediately preceding lawful electoral system as the objective benchmark when the challenged act directly eliminates an existing election; this entry instead governs a governing-body-size claim requiring a concrete and workable alternative structure.

**First controlling authority:** Holder v. Hall, 512 U.S. 874 (1994), decided June 30, 1994, Cognizability and the five-member comparator; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Holder v. Hall, 512 U.S. 874 (1994), decided June 30, 1994, Cognizability and the five-member comparator; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Holder v. Hall, 512 U.S. 874 (1994), June 30, 1994.

### Voting Rights Act § 2 totality, proportionality, and nonmaximization

**Governing question:** How *Gingles* preconditions, proportionality, and the ability to draw additional majority-minority districts bear on § 2 vote-dilution liability.

**Current rule:** The *Gingles* preconditions are necessary but not invariably sufficient to establish § 2 vote dilution. The court must determine under the full statutory totality whether protected voters have less opportunity than other members of the electorate to participate effectively and elect representatives of choice. Rough proportionality is relevant evidence but neither dispositive nor a safe harbor, and the ability to draw additional majority-minority districts does not by itself establish dilution because § 2 does not require maximization.

**Exceptions and limits:** Districts counted in a proportionality comparison must operate as genuine electoral opportunities. The rule creates no proportional-representation entitlement and leaves free-standing influence-district claims unresolved.

**First controlling authority:** *Johnson v. De Grandy*, 512 U.S. 997, Supreme Court simulation decided June 30, 1994, *Gingles*, proportionality, and nonmaximization; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Johnson v. De Grandy*, 512 U.S. 997, Supreme Court simulation decided June 30, 1994, supplies the current totality, proportionality, and nonmaximization rule.

**Present operation effective:** *Johnson v. De Grandy*, June 30, 1994.

**Related current rules:** *First Gingles precondition and demographic proof* governs the threshold practical-ability-to-elect and demographic showing before the remaining predicates and statutory totality are reached. *Objective comparator for governing-body-size claims under Voting Rights Act § 2* supplies an additional threshold when the challenge targets governing-body size.

### Anonymous political leaflets

**Governing question:** Whether a State may require an author’s identity on hand-distributed political leaflets.

**Current rule:** Government may not require every person distributing independent campaign literature to identify herself on the face of the communication when the rule broadly prohibits anonymous core political advocacy and is not narrowly fitted to fraud or corruption. A content-defined identification mandate imposed directly on independent political advocacy receives exacting review, requiring an overriding interest and narrow fit to the activity producing the asserted harm.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not invalidate disclosure rules governing candidates, political committees, coordinated expenditures, substantial campaign financing, or fraudulent impersonation. Those settings implicate interests and institutional relationships not present in McIntyre’s independent leafletting.

**First controlling authority:** McIntyre v. Ohio Elections Commission, 514 U.S. 334, decided April 19, 1995, Anonymous political leaflets; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** McIntyre v. Ohio Elections Commission, 514 U.S. 334, decided April 19, 1995, Anonymous political leaflets; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** McIntyre v. Ohio Elections Commission, 514 U.S. 334, April 19, 1995.

### Responsive financing and the independent speaker

**Governing question:** Whether responsive public financing burdens an independent speaker whose expenditure triggers resources for an opposing candidate.

**Current rule:** A public-financing system does not burden an independent expenditure merely because that expenditure triggers additional public funds or a higher voluntary spending limit for the candidate opposed, where the independent speaker remains legally free to spend and incurs no restriction, charge, delay, reporting duty, or other legal disability. The First Amendment protects an independent speaker against governmental suppression of the speaker’s own advocacy. It does not provide a right to prevent another electoral participant from receiving lawful resources with which to respond, or to preserve the original expenditure’s strategic value against responsive speech.

**Exceptions and limits:** A different question would arise if a trigger reduced the independent speaker’s rights, imposed a fee or new disclosure obligation because of the message, discriminated according to identity or viewpoint, or provided support so disproportionate that the program operated as a device for suppressing disfavored advocacy.

**First controlling authority:** Day v. Holahan, 34 F.3d 1356, decided May 15, 1995, Responsive financing and the independent speaker; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Day v. Holahan, 34 F.3d 1356, decided May 15, 1995, Responsive financing and the independent speaker; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Day v. Holahan, 34 F.3d 1356, May 15, 1995.

### Increased limits within a voluntary public-financing program

**Governing question:** When government may raise a participating candidate’s voluntary spending ceiling in response to specified electoral conditions.

**Current rule:** Government may increase a participating candidate’s voluntarily accepted spending ceiling when specified electoral conditions occur, including qualifying independent expenditures.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide a system in which participation is practically coercive or benefits are allocated through a viewpoint-based trigger.

**First controlling authority:** Day v. Holahan, 34 F.3d 1356, decided May 15, 1995, Increased limits within a voluntary public-financing program; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Day v. Holahan, 34 F.3d 1356, decided May 15, 1995, Increased limits within a voluntary public-financing program; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Day v. Holahan, 34 F.3d 1356, May 15, 1995.

### State-imposed qualifications for Congress

**Governing question:** Whether a State may condition congressional ballot access on a candidate characteristic beyond the Constitution’s qualifications.

**Current rule:** A State may not condition ordinary congressional ballot access on prior congressional service when the restriction operates as an additional substantive qualification for an otherwise constitutionally eligible candidate. States may regulate the time, place, and manner of congressional elections and may enforce neutral ballot-administration requirements. They may not use ballot access to impose a personal disability based on a candidate characteristic beyond the age, citizenship, and residency qualifications fixed by the Constitution.

**Exceptions and limits:** Genuine time, place, and manner rules, petition requirements, filing deadlines, anti-fraud protections, and neutral ballot-management measures remain valid when they do not add substantive qualifications for office.

**First controlling authority:** U.S. Term Limits, Inc. v. Thornton / Bryant v. Hill, 514 U.S. 779, decided May 22, 1995, State-imposed qualifications for Congress; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** U.S. Term Limits, Inc. v. Thornton / Bryant v. Hill, 514 U.S. 779, decided May 22, 1995, State-imposed qualifications for Congress; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** U.S. Term Limits, Inc. v. Thornton / Bryant v. Hill, 514 U.S. 779, May 22, 1995.

### Comparator and totality in judicial-election claims

**Governing question:** What comparator and totality analysis applies to a Voting Rights Act § 2 challenge involving judicial elections.

**Current rule:** For each challenged unit, the court must first identify a workable alternative grounded in objective law, established practice, history, or a closely comparable institution. Comparator sufficiency permits comparison but does not establish liability. The court must then evaluate cohesion, legally relevant bloc voting, and the complete statutory totality without presuming proportional officeholding or racial maximization.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not require election of judges of any particular race, guarantee proportional officeholding, approve cumulative voting or subdistricts, or disregard legitimate concerns about jurisdiction, linkage, geography, judicial administration, and state structure.

**First controlling authority:** Southern Christian Leadership Conference of Alabama v. Sessions, 56 F.3d 1281, decided December 18, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Comparator and totality in judicial-election claims; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Southern Christian Leadership Conference of Alabama v. Sessions, 56 F.3d 1281, decided December 18, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Comparator and totality in judicial-election claims; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Southern Christian Leadership Conference of Alabama v. Sessions, 56 F.3d 1281, December 18, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- December 18, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Southern Christian Leadership Conference of Alabama v. Sessions, 56 F.3d 1281: *Holder* is applied and clarified for judicial elections. Its comparator requirement remains a comparative merits prerequisite rather than an Article III bar. *De Grandy* is applied without alteration: proportionality is relevant evidence, not a safe harbor or entitlement, and § 2 does not require maximization.

### Federal registration duties administered through state offices

**Governing question:** Whether Congress may require state offices to administer federal voter-registration duties under the Elections Clause despite an anti-commandeering objection.

**Current rule:** Congress may alter state rules governing the times, places, and manner of congressional elections and may require state administrative machinery to carry out reasonably related federal-election procedures. Incidental operation on an integrated state registration system is permissible when inseparable from or reasonably necessary to federal compliance. The authority does not independently extend to severable state-only elections or state voter qualifications.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not authorize Congress to prescribe state voter qualifications, regulate an election having no federal component, or compel consolidation of otherwise severable state and federal rolls.

**First controlling authority:** Voting Rights Coalition v. Wilson, 60 F.3d 1411, decided February 26, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Federal registration duties administered through state offices; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Voting Rights Coalition v. Wilson, 60 F.3d 1411, decided February 26, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Federal registration duties administered through state offices; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Voting Rights Coalition v. Wilson, 60 F.3d 1411, February 26, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- February 26, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Voting Rights Coalition v. Wilson, 60 F.3d 1411: *U.S. Term Limits* is applied and clarified. Registration procedures are part of the manner of conducting federal elections, while candidate and voter qualifications remain distinct. The decision establishes that a general anti-commandeering objection does not override the Constitution’s express federal authority to alter state-administered congressional election machinery.

### District-specific justification for race-predominant remedial districts

**Governing question:** What district-specific showing a State must make to defend a race-predominant district as a Voting Rights Act remedy.

**Current rule:** A State bears the burden of identifying the actual district-specific constitutional or statutory injury, its corrective authority or obligation, and the fit of the racial design. A generalized desire to achieve racial targets or preserve percentages does not establish a fitted Voting Rights Act remedy.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not hold Districts 18 or 29 valid, require maximization, or immunize a remedial label from examination of the districts’ actual boundaries and operation.

**First controlling authority:** Bush v. Vera; Lawson v. Vera; United States v. Vera, 517 U.S. 952, decided June 13, 1996, Remedial classification of Districts 18 and 29; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Bush v. Vera; Lawson v. Vera; United States v. Vera, 517 U.S. 952, decided June 13, 1996, Remedial classification of Districts 18 and 29; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Bush v. Vera; Lawson v. Vera; United States v. Vera, 517 U.S. 952, June 13, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 13, 1996, Bush v. Vera; Lawson v. Vera; United States v. Vera, 517 U.S. 952: Simulated *Miller* is clarified in operation: predominance and remedial classification must be performed separately for each district, even when several districts form part of the same statewide plan.

### Independent political-party expenditure

**Governing question:** When a political party’s expenditure remains independent rather than becoming a contribution or coordinated candidate spending.

**Current rule:** A party expenditure is not converted into a contribution merely because the party and a candidate share political interests. Government must establish coordination, agreement, candidate control, or another legally sufficient connection showing that the spending functions as a candidate-controlled transfer or a means of circumventing contribution limits.

**First controlling authority:** Colorado Republican Federal Campaign Committee v. Federal Election Commission, 518 U.S. 604, decided June 26, 1996, Independent political-party expenditure; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Colorado Republican Federal Campaign Committee v. Federal Election Commission, 518 U.S. 604, decided June 26, 1996, Independent political-party expenditure; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Colorado Republican Federal Campaign Committee v. Federal Election Commission, 518 U.S. 604, June 26, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 26, 1996, Colorado Republican Federal Campaign Committee v. Federal Election Commission, 518 U.S. 604: *McIntyre* and *Day* are applied to independent party advocacy. The decision adds a recurring coordination boundary: organizational identity and shared political goals are insufficient without an operative connection to candidate control or contribution circumvention. The law governing actual coordinated party expenditures remains unresolved.

### Section 5 coverage

**Governing question:** When Section 5 covers a political-party rule governing participation in a state-authorized nomination process.

**Current rule:** Section 5 reaches a party rule when the party acts under authority granted by a covered jurisdiction to perform a legally operative nomination function and the rule alters participation in that function. Coverage does not extend to internal membership, platform, fundraising, or governance decisions lacking that electoral operation.

**First controlling authority:** Morse v. Republican Party of Virginia, 517 U.S. 186, decided March 27, 1996, Section 5 coverage; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Morse v. Republican Party of Virginia, 517 U.S. 186, decided March 27, 1996, Section 5 coverage; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Morse v. Republican Party of Virginia, 517 U.S. 186, March 27, 1996.

**Development:**
- March 27, 1996, Morse v. Republican Party of Virginia, 517 U.S. 186: The decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule for § 5 coverage of a state-authorized party nominating convention. *Allen* is applied without expanding the district court’s role into preclearance adjudication.

### Administration of state-mandated voting changes in covered jurisdictions

**Governing question:** Whether § 5 applies when a covered subdivision implements a postbaseline voting change mandated by a noncovered State.

**Current rule:** A covered political subdivision seeks to administer a voting change when its officials give the change operative effect within the subdivision. Section 5 therefore requires preclearance even when the subdivision did not author the policy and possesses no discretion over it. The noncovered State need not preclear the enactment in the abstract.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not impose statewide preclearance on a noncovered State or reach a law having no operative effect in a covered jurisdiction. Purpose, effect, and entitlement to preclearance remain for the designated forums.

**First controlling authority:** Lopez v. Monterey County, 519 U.S. 9 (1996), decided November 6, 1996, State-mandated voting changes; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Lopez v. Monterey County, 519 U.S. 9 (1996), decided November 6, 1996, State-mandated voting changes; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Lopez v. Monterey County, 519 U.S. 9 (1996), November 6, 1996.

### Section 5 coverage designations and prospective nonuse

**Governing question:** What effect designation, bailout, delay, and laches have in a § 5 enforcement action.

**Current rule:** A covered jurisdiction may not collaterally relitigate an operative § 4 designation in an enforcement action when Congress supplied bailout as the route out of coverage. Delay may affect retrospective relief, reliance, or transition timing, but laches cannot convert an unprecleared change into a lawful prospective practice.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not decide damages, completed elections, a factual bailout claim, or the precise effect of a particular administrative submission.

**First controlling authority:** Lopez v. Monterey County, 519 U.S. 9 (1996), decided November 6, 1996, Covered change, designation, and laches; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Lopez v. Monterey County, 519 U.S. 9 (1996), decided November 6, 1996, Covered change, designation, and laches; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Lopez v. Monterey County, 519 U.S. 9 (1996), November 6, 1996.

### Judicial interim plans under Section 5

**Governing question:** When a federal court may use a temporary election plan without separate § 5 preclearance.

**Current rule:** A § 5 enforcement court may not authorize elections under an unprecleared governmental plan, and a court order does not transform a State or local policy choice into an exempt judicial plan. A plan formulated wholly by the court may operate without separate preclearance only when independent remedial authority makes temporary judicial action necessary, the plan complies with other law, displaces state policy no further than necessary, and ends when a lawful precleared arrangement becomes available.

**Exceptions and limits:** A court-developed plan remains subject to all other constitutional and statutory constraints.

**First controlling authority:** Lopez v. Monterey County, 519 U.S. 9 (1996), decided November 6, 1996, Enforcement-court authority and temporary remedies; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Lopez v. Monterey County, 519 U.S. 9 (1996), decided November 6, 1996, Enforcement-court authority and temporary remedies; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Lopez v. Monterey County, 519 U.S. 9 (1996), November 6, 1996.

### Section 5 baseline and discretionary implementation choices

**Governing question:** How a court identifies the operative voting baseline and treats state policy choices made while complying with federal law.

**Current rule:** A brief provisional practice does not become the § 5 baseline unless it actually becomes the voting practice in force or effect. Discretionary state choices used to implement federal election law are changes requiring preclearance when they differ from the lawful baseline. A superior legal constraint does not preclear policy choices left to state or local officials.

**Exceptions and limits:** No fixed duration or numerical threshold determines when a provisional practice becomes operative. Direct compliance with an express federal command leaving no state policy choice remains distinct, and state-mandated changes remain governed by Lopez.

**First controlling authority:** Young v. Fordice, 520 U.S. 273 (1997), decided March 31, 1997, The operative baseline and discretionary NVRA implementation changes; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Young v. Fordice, 520 U.S. 273 (1997), decided March 31, 1997, The operative baseline and discretionary NVRA implementation changes; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Young v. Fordice, 520 U.S. 273 (1997), March 31, 1997.

### Section 5 retrogression and Section 2

**Governing question:** How § 5 effect relates to § 2 vote-dilution analysis.

**Current rule:** A § 2 violation does not independently establish prohibited effect under § 5. Section 5 effect compares the proposed change with the jurisdiction’s lawful operative baseline and asks whether the change is retrogressive. A nonretrogressive plan does not violate the effects prong merely because it preserves dilution, fails to create a majority-minority district, or is less favorable than an available alternative. Section 2 separately uses the statutory totality and an undiluted benchmark. Preclearance does not decide a separate § 2 claim, and possible § 2 liability does not substitute for the § 5 effect inquiry.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule creates no entitlement to proportional representation or maximization. Nonretrogression resolves only the effects prong and does not provide a safe harbor from the separate prohibition on discriminatory purpose.

**First controlling authority:** Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, 520 U.S. 471 (1997), decided May 12, 1997, relationship between § 5 effect and § 2; established the current effects rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, 520 U.S. 471 (1997), decided May 12, 1997, supplies the distinct § 5 and § 2 inquiries and the retrogression baseline. Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, 528 U.S. 320 (2000), decided January 24, 2000, supplies the nonretrogressive-plan boundary and the separation between effects and discriminatory purpose.

**Present operation effective:** Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, 520 U.S. 471 (1997), May 12, 1997.

**Current wording authority:** Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, 528 U.S. 320 (2000), January 24, 2000, supplies the present statement of the nonretrogression boundary and identifies the separate purpose rule without changing the effects mechanics.

**Related current rules:** Section 5 discriminatory purpose and identical-change proof; Dilution evidence in Section 5 purpose review. The first governs the independent purpose condition; the second identifies evidence relevant to that inquiry.

**Development:**
- January 24, 2000, Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, 528 U.S. 320: The effects rule remains a comparison with the lawful operative baseline and still does not import § 2. The previously reserved interaction with purpose is now resolved: nonretrogression defeats only the effects theory and is not a safe harbor from a discriminatory-purpose objection.

### Dilution evidence in Section 5 purpose review

**Governing question:** What evidence may be considered when determining discriminatory purpose in a § 5 preclearance proceeding.

**Current rule:** Evidence of dilutive impact, rejected alternatives, historical context, decision sequence, procedural departures, official statements, and pretext may be relevant to why a covered jurisdiction selected a plan. The evidence may not be excluded merely because it also supports a § 2 theory. Relevance does not transfer the legal conclusion from § 2 to § 5; dilutive effect and less dilutive alternatives are evidence, not automatic proof of prohibited purpose.

**Burdens and proof:** The covered jurisdiction retains the statutory burden governing preclearance, and purpose is evaluated through the complete circumstances.

**Exceptions and limits:** This evidentiary rule does not make dilution or the availability of a more favorable plan independently dispositive. The substantive scope of prohibited purpose and the identical-change same-decision route are governed separately.

**First controlling authority:** Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, 520 U.S. 471 (1997), decided May 12, 1997, dilution evidence in the purpose inquiry; established the current evidentiary rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, 520 U.S. 471 (1997), decided May 12, 1997, supplies the relevant evidence and the distinction between evidentiary relevance and automatic proof. Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, 528 U.S. 320 (2000), decided January 24, 2000, resolves the formerly open substantive-purpose and same-decision questions to which that evidence is directed.

**Present operation effective:** Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, 520 U.S. 471 (1997), May 12, 1997.

**Current wording authority:** Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, 528 U.S. 320 (2000), January 24, 2000, resolves the formerly open purpose and same-decision questions while leaving the evidentiary mechanics unchanged.

**Related current rules:** Section 5 discriminatory purpose and identical-change proof; Section 5 retrogression and Section 2. The first supplies the purpose rule and the jurisdiction’s possible same-decision showing; the second keeps effects analysis distinct from § 2.

**Development:**
- January 24, 2000, Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, 528 U.S. 320: The listed evidence and the covered jurisdiction’s burden remain unchanged. The former open questions about the scope of § 5 purpose and an identical-change same-decision showing are now governed by the separate current rule `Section 5 discriminatory purpose and identical-change proof`.

### Private enforcement of Section 5 preclearance

**Governing question:** Who may obtain prospective relief against implementation of an unprecleared covered voting change.

**Current rule:** Voters directly excluded or charged under an unprecleared covered change may seek an injunction enforcing § 5’s prohibition on implementation before preclearance.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not decide whether § 10 creates an independent private cause of action or authorizes repayment of a fee already collected.

**First controlling authority:** Morse v. Republican Party of Virginia, 517 U.S. 186, decided March 27, 1996, Private enforcement and statutory sequencing; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Morse v. Republican Party of Virginia, 517 U.S. 186, decided March 27, 1996, Private enforcement and statutory sequencing; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Morse v. Republican Party of Virginia, 517 U.S. 186, March 27, 1996.

### Uniform federal election day

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs uniform federal election day.

**Current rule:** A State conflicts with federal election-day law when its procedure makes a candidate’s earlier victory the legally operative election to federal office and no legally operative election occurs on the federally prescribed November day. States may use preliminary procedures before that day so long as the final legal selection occurs on the federal date.

**First controlling authority:** Foster v. Love, 522 U.S. 67 (1997), decided December 2, 1997, Uniform federal election day; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Foster v. Love, 522 U.S. 67 (1997), decided December 2, 1997, Uniform federal election day; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Foster v. Love, 522 U.S. 67 (1997), December 2, 1997.

### Candidate-contribution limits and anticorruption fit

**Governing question:** When a candidate-contribution limit is closely drawn to a constitutionally sufficient anticorruption interest.

**Current rule:** Government defending a candidate-contribution limit must identify an objectively grounded risk of actual or apparent quid-pro-quo corruption or circumvention and connect the selected ceiling closely to that risk without materially disabling meaningful association or effective campaign advocacy. Completed bribery, a changed official vote, concealment, and formal legislative findings are not indispensable. No universal dollar floor or automatic inflation formula controls; the court examines the limit’s practical operation in the affected elections, including whether contributors retain meaningful participation and candidates can assemble resources adequate for effective advocacy.

**Burdens and proof:** Government bears the burden of identifying the anticorruption or anticircumvention risk and establishing the limit’s close fit without material impairment of protected participation and advocacy.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not govern independent expenditures or establish that any fixed dollar amount is invariably constitutional or unconstitutional.

**First controlling authority:** Russell v. Burris, 146 F.3d 563, decided April 28, 1999, anticorruption predicate, practical operation, and constitutional fit of candidate-contribution limits; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Russell v. Burris, 146 F.3d 563, decided April 28, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Russell v. Burris, 146 F.3d 563, April 28, 1999.

### Guam gubernatorial majority denominator

**Governing question:** What votes form the majority denominator for election of Governor and Lieutenant Governor under Guam Organic Act § 1422.

**Current rule:** The statutory majority is calculated from votes cast for gubernatorial slates, not from every ballot submitted in the simultaneous general election. A slate receiving more than half of the votes cast for Governor and Lieutenant Governor has won without a runoff.

**Controlling construction:** In § 1422, votes cast in the gubernatorial election exclude blank, overmarked, and otherwise nonresponsive general-election ballots from the denominator.

**Exceptions and limits:** The construction does not govern materially different statutory language or disputes about fraud, invalid votes, or count accuracy.

**First controlling authority:** Gutierrez v. Ada, 528 U.S. 250 (2000), decided January 19, 2000, majority denominator under Guam Organic Act § 1422; established the current construction.

**Controlling authorities:** Gutierrez v. Ada, 528 U.S. 250 (2000), decided January 19, 2000, supplies the current construction.

**Present operation effective:** Gutierrez v. Ada, 528 U.S. 250 (2000), January 19, 2000.

### Continuing baseline consequence in a Section 5 controversy

**Governing question:** When anticipated replacement of a voting plan moots a controversy over that plan’s § 5 preclearance status.

**Current rule:** A § 5 preclearance controversy remains live when the challenged plan retains a concrete legal consequence by supplying or materially affecting the lawful operative baseline for a later covered change. The prospect that a new plan will govern a future election does not erase that baseline consequence.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not make every superseded voting plan reviewable indefinitely; a concrete continuing baseline consequence must remain.

**First controlling authority:** Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, 528 U.S. 320 (2000), decided January 24, 2000, continuing baseline consequence in a § 5 controversy; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, 528 U.S. 320 (2000), decided January 24, 2000, supplies the current rule and its limit.

**Present operation effective:** Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, 528 U.S. 320 (2000), January 24, 2000.

### Section 5 discriminatory purpose and identical-change proof

**Governing question:** What discriminatory purpose § 5 prohibits and how a covered jurisdiction may establish that the identical voting change would have resulted without the prohibited consideration.

**Current rule:** Section 5’s purpose condition is independent of its effects condition and is not limited to intent to cause retrogression. A covered jurisdiction may not obtain preclearance for a voting change selected with a racial purpose to deny or abridge voting rights merely because the change is nonretrogressive. When prohibited racial purpose materially influenced adoption, the jurisdiction may obtain preclearance only by proving that independently sufficient lawful reasons would have produced the identical voting change absent the prohibited consideration.

**Burdens and proof:** The covered jurisdiction retains the statutory burden of persuasion. An identical-change showing requires proof that lawful reasons sufficient in their own right would have generated the same change; general assertions that do not establish that causal proposition are insufficient.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** The preclearance court determines effect by comparison with the lawful operative baseline, separately determines purpose from the complete circumstances, and, if prohibited purpose materially influenced the choice, determines whether the jurisdiction has proved the identical-change same-decision showing.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not import § 2 liability into § 5, require maximization, or make the availability of a more favorable plan automatic proof of prohibited purpose.

**First controlling authority:** Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, 528 U.S. 320 (2000), decided January 24, 2000, discriminatory purpose and identical-change proof under § 5; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, 528 U.S. 320 (2000), decided January 24, 2000, supplies the independent purpose condition, the jurisdiction’s burden, and the identical-change same-decision route.

**Present operation effective:** Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, 528 U.S. 320 (2000), January 24, 2000.

**Related current rules:** Section 5 retrogression and Section 2; Dilution evidence in Section 5 purpose review; Continuing baseline consequence in a Section 5 controversy; Same-decision proof and retrospective relief for a discrete governmental decision, under Remedies. The first three rules respectively govern effects, purpose evidence, and continuing justiciability. The Remedies rule governs retrospective § 1983 liability; this entry instead places the preclearance burden on the covered jurisdiction to prove the identical voting change.

### Ancestry classifications in a public franchise

**Governing question:** When a State may use ancestry to restrict voting in an election for public officials.

**Current rule:** A State may not condition the franchise in an election for public officials on an ancestry rule operating as a racial classification when the election is not an internal election of a constitutionally sufficient political community. A statewide election for officials of a state agency is not converted into internal tribal governance merely because the agency administers interests associated with an indigenous population.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not govern membership criteria or internal elections of a federally recognized tribe. Exceptions associated with special-purpose districts do not authorize a racial restriction prohibited by the Fifteenth Amendment.

**First controlling authority:** Rice v. Cayetano, 528 U.S. 495 (2000), decided February 23, 2000, ancestry-based franchise in a state public election; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Rice v. Cayetano, 528 U.S. 495 (2000), decided February 23, 2000, supplies the ancestry-as-racial-proxy rule, the public-election boundary, and the limits concerning tribal governance and special-purpose districts.

**Present operation effective:** Rice v. Cayetano, 528 U.S. 495 (2000), February 23, 2000.

### Selective access to cross-nomination

**Governing question:** How a court reviews an election law that permits major parties to cross-nominate candidates while denying the same opportunity to minor parties.

**Current rule:** A State that permits cross-nomination may not make that electoral opportunity available to major parties while categorically denying it to minor parties without a constitutionally sufficient justification for the unequal treatment. *Anderson–Burdick* governs the practical voting and associational burden, while equal protection requires focused review of the facial party-status classification. The required fit increases with the burden and discrimination shown.

**Burdens and proof:** The State must justify the asymmetry itself; interests capable of supporting a neutral prohibition do not suffice unless they explain why minor-party cross-nomination presents a materially different regulatory danger.

**Exceptions and limits:** Minor parties are not a suspect class. A State may adopt an evenhanded anti-fusion rule applicable to all parties, subject to ordinary election-law review.

**First controlling authority:** *Reform Party of Allegheny County v. Allegheny County Department of Elections*, 174 F.3d 305 (3d Cir. 1999), Supreme Court simulation decided May 8, 2000, selective access to fusion; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Reform Party of Allegheny County v. Allegheny County Department of Elections*, 174 F.3d 305 (3d Cir. 1999), Supreme Court simulation decided May 8, 2000, supplies the combined burden/classification inquiry and the requirement to justify the disparity itself.

**Present operation effective:** *Reform Party of Allegheny County v. Allegheny County Department of Elections*, May 8, 2000.

### First Gingles precondition and demographic proof

**Governing question:** What electoral ability and demographic proof satisfy the first *Gingles* precondition for a Voting Rights Act § 2 districting claim.

**Current rule:** A protected group need not categorically exceed 50 percent of citizen voting-age population in the proposed district. A geographically compact and politically cohesive protected group satisfies the first precondition by proving that, in a reasonably configured district, it has a practical ability to elect its preferred candidates with dependable crossover support. The crossover support must be reliable and recurring, not speculative, episodic, or sufficient only to influence an election. Citizen voting-age population is the appropriate demographic measure when citizenship is material, and reliable current demographic evidence may displace decennial census figures.

**Burdens and proof:** The plaintiff must establish the compactness, cohesion, practical ability to elect, and any current demographic facts by a preponderance of the evidence. No clear-and-convincing standard applies to displacement of decennial data.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** Apply this first precondition before the remaining *Gingles* predicates and the statutory totality. Satisfaction of this threshold does not establish § 2 liability.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not recognize a free-standing influence-district claim or decide broader multigroup-coalition theories. It creates no right to proportional representation or maximization.

**First controlling authority:** *Thornburg v. Gingles*, 478 U.S. 30 (1986), first precondition; current below-majority and crossover-support operation supplied by *Valdespino v. Alamo Heights Independent School District*, 168 F.3d 848 (5th Cir. 1999), Supreme Court simulation decided May 16, 2000.

**Controlling authorities:** *Thornburg v. Gingles*, 478 U.S. 30 (1986), supplies the compactness and political-cohesion threshold. *Valdespino v. Alamo Heights Independent School District*, 168 F.3d 848 (5th Cir. 1999), Supreme Court simulation decided May 16, 2000, Souter’s Opinion of the Court joined by Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies the below-50-percent crossover route, reliability requirement, preponderance standard, citizen-voting-age-population measure, and current-evidence rule.

**Present operation effective:** *Valdespino v. Alamo Heights Independent School District*, May 16, 2000.

**Related current rules:** *Voting Rights Act § 2 totality, proportionality, and nonmaximization* governs the later liability inquiry after all threshold predicates are satisfied. *Objective comparator for governing-body-size claims under Voting Rights Act § 2* supplies the additional comparator prerequisite when the challenge targets governing-body size.

### Political-committee status and an organization’s major purpose

**Governing question:** When a State may subject an organization to the full, continuous registration, organization, accounting, reporting, and disclosure regime applicable to a political committee.

**Current rule:** A State may impose the full political-committee regime only on an organization whose major purpose is nominating, electing, or defeating candidates. Discussion of candidates, voting records, or public issues in some communications does not by itself permit comprehensive political-committee treatment of an organization whose major purpose is issue advocacy.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not bar transaction-specific sponsor disclosure, reporting of qualifying candidate or party activity, coordinated-spending rules, or full political-committee regulation of an organization whose major purpose is electoral.

**First controlling authority:** *North Carolina Right to Life, Inc. v. Bartlett*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 6, 2000, full political-committee status for an issue-advocacy organization; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *North Carolina Right to Life, Inc. v. Bartlett*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 6, 2000, supplies the major-purpose threshold and the distinction between continuous organizational burdens and transaction-specific regulation.

**Present operation effective:** *North Carolina Right to Life, Inc. v. Bartlett*, June 6, 2000.

**Related current rules:** `Definite electoral boundary for regulated campaign expenditures`, under Elections and Voting, governs whether a particular communication may be treated as a regulated electoral expenditure rather than whether the speaker may be placed under the full political-committee regime.

### Definite electoral boundary for regulated campaign expenditures

**Governing question:** What boundary a law must supply when it regulates campaign expenditures beyond communications containing a rigid list of express electoral words.

**Current rule:** A campaign-finance law may reach a communication beyond a rigid list of express electoral words only when it supplies an objective and sufficiently definite boundary identifying communications that are unmistakably electoral in operation. Open-ended language turning on whether issue discussion may “influence” an election or possess a general political purpose is impermissibly vague and overbroad when it leaves speakers uncertain whether candidate discussion, voting records, or voter guides trigger expenditure regulation.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not require one exclusive verbal formula or bar regulation of coordinated spending, communications objectively and unmistakably electoral in operation, or fitted sponsor disclosure supported by a definite trigger.

**First controlling authority:** *North Carolina Right to Life, Inc. v. Bartlett*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 6, 2000, definite boundary for regulated campaign expenditures; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *North Carolina Right to Life, Inc. v. Bartlett*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 6, 2000, Question 2, by the controlling coalition of Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies the objective, sufficiently definite, unmistakably-electoral boundary and the vagueness-and-overbreadth consequence.

**Present operation effective:** *North Carolina Right to Life, Inc. v. Bartlett*, June 6, 2000.

**Related current rules:** `Political-committee status and an organization’s major purpose`, under Elections and Voting, governs the separate decision to impose a full continuing organizational regime.

### Political-party control over nominee selection

**Governing question:** When a State may require voters outside a political party to participate in choosing the party’s official nominee.

**Current rule:** A State may not compel a political party to select its official nominee through a blanket primary in which voters unaffiliated with that party, including adherents of competing parties, participate on equal terms. Because selection of the party’s standard-bearer is a central associational act, a law transferring that choice to nonmembers receives the most demanding review and must be justified by interests and fit sufficient for that burden.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not bar neutral regulation of ballot access, timing, and election administration; a nonpartisan or top-two election that does not select a party’s official nominee; or a form of open primary the party itself authorizes.

**First controlling authority:** *California Democratic Party v. Jones*, 530 U.S. 567 (2000), decided June 26, 2000, compelled blanket-primary selection of a party nominee; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *California Democratic Party v. Jones*, 530 U.S. 567 (2000), decided June 26, 2000, supplies the nominee-selection trigger, most-demanding-review assignment, and neutral-administration and nonpartisan-election limits.

**Present operation effective:** *California Democratic Party v. Jones*, 530 U.S. 567 (2000), June 26, 2000.

### Election-to-appointment restructuring under Voting Rights Act § 2

**Governing question:** Whether Voting Rights Act § 2 reaches replacement of an established elected governing body with an appointed body and what objective benchmark governs that claim.

**Current rule:** Section 2 reaches a State’s replacement of an existing lawful elected governing body with an appointed body because the change removes an established electoral opportunity. The lawful electoral system operating immediately before the restructuring supplies the objective benchmark for comparing political opportunity.

**Burdens and proof:** Cognizability and identification of the preceding electoral benchmark do not establish liability. Protected voters must still prove under the statutory totality that the challenged restructuring leaves them with less opportunity than other citizens to participate in the political process and elect representatives of choice.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** Determine whether an established lawful election was replaced and use the immediately preceding system as the comparator; then apply the remaining § 2 predicates and statutory totality without treating benchmark sufficiency as liability.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not create a constitutional right to elect every local body, require restoration of the former structure, or govern an office that was genuinely appointive throughout the relevant period.

**First controlling authority:** *Mixon v. Ohio*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 27, 2000, § 2 coverage and benchmark for an election-to-appointment restructuring; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Mixon v. Ohio*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 27, 2000, Questions 1 and 2, by the controlling coalition of Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies § 2 coverage, the immediately preceding lawful electoral benchmark, and the cognizability-versus-liability boundary.

**Present operation effective:** *Mixon v. Ohio*, June 27, 2000.

**Related current rules:** `Objective comparator for governing-body-size claims under Voting Rights Act § 2`, under Elections and Voting, governs a claim requiring a workable alternative size or structure rather than direct removal of a State’s own immediately preceding election. `Voting Rights Act § 2 totality, proportionality, and nonmaximization`, under Elections and Voting, supplies the separate totality inquiry after the present benchmark is identified.

### One-person-one-vote and genuinely appointive governing bodies

**Governing question:** Whether one-person-one-vote applies to a multi-jurisdictional governing body because the official with appointment authority is elected by only part of the population the body serves.

**Current rule:** A genuinely appointive multi-jurisdictional governing body does not violate one-person-one-vote merely because the appointing official is elected only by residents of one participating jurisdiction. Once the body is genuinely appointive, residents served by it do not acquire a constitutional right to vote for every official participating in the appointment process.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not govern officials who are themselves selected through election, an appointment label used to conceal an electoral mechanism, or a restructuring materially motivated by racial discrimination.

**First controlling authority:** *Mixon v. Ohio*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 27, 2000, one-person-one-vote and appointment authority; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Mixon v. Ohio*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 27, 2000, Question 4, by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies the genuinely appointive-body rule and the narrower-electorate limit.

**Present operation effective:** *Mixon v. Ohio*, June 27, 2000.

**Related current rules:** `Racially motivated election-to-appointment restructuring`, under Equal Protection, governs discriminatory purpose even when a race-neutral appointment structure would ordinarily be constitutional.

## First Amendment

### Public-employee discipline based on a reasonable factual understanding

**Governing question:** Which factual version of disputed employee speech a public employer may use when applying the public-employee speech doctrine.

**Current rule:** A public employer may act on a version of disputed speech it genuinely believed when that factual understanding was objectively reasonable in light of information reasonably available to the decisionmaker. The inquiry into how the employer investigated is evidence of actual belief, reasonableness, and pretext, not an independent First Amendment hearing code. Pickering and Connick then apply to the speech as permissibly understood.

**Burdens and proof:** The employer must establish actual reliance on a genuinely held and objectively reasonable factual understanding.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not protect a fabricated reason, an asserted belief the employer did not hold, an objectively unsupported factual premise, or pretext.

**First controlling authority:** Waters v. Churchill, 511 U.S. 661 (1994), decided May 31, 1994, Permissible factual basis for public-employee discipline; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Waters v. Churchill, 511 U.S. 661 (1994), decided May 31, 1994, Permissible factual basis for public-employee discipline; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Waters v. Churchill, 511 U.S. 661 (1994), May 31, 1994.

### Near-total elimination of the residential-sign medium

**Governing question:** When a municipality may broadly prohibit signs displayed at a resident’s own home.

**Current rule:** A municipality violates the First Amendment when it nearly eliminates residential signs and leaves no realistic equivalent for the inexpensive, location-linked expression conveyed by a sign at one’s own home. Other media are not adequate substitutes when they cannot communicate the same personal message from the residence itself.

**Exceptions and limits:** A municipality may impose reasonable content-neutral rules governing size, number, placement, duration, illumination, and safety, and no one has a right to place a sign on another person’s property.

**First controlling authority:** City of Ladue v. Gilleo, 512 U.S. 43 (1994), decided June 13, 1994, Near-total elimination of residential signs; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** City of Ladue v. Gilleo, 512 U.S. 43 (1994), decided June 13, 1994, Near-total elimination of residential signs; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** City of Ladue v. Gilleo, 512 U.S. 43 (1994), June 13, 1994.

### Cable must-carry classification and intermediate scrutiny

**Governing question:** What scrutiny governs content-neutral cable must-carry obligations and what record the government must supply.

**Current rule:** Cable must-carry obligations keyed to broadcast status and transmission method rather than viewpoint or subject are content neutral and receive intermediate scrutiny. Government must establish important interests unrelated to suppressing expression and a reasonable fit that does not burden substantially more editorial discretion than necessary. Courts may respect adequately supported legislative predictions in a complex market, but must independently review whether the record supports the asserted threats to broadcasting and whether the actual distribution of compelled carriage is reasonably fitted to those interests.

**Burdens and proof:** Government bears the burden of establishing the substantial interests, record support, and reasonable fit.

**Exceptions and limits:** Least-restrictive-means analysis is not required. Legislative prediction does not substitute for record support, and a materially different as-applied editorial burden remains reviewable.

**First controlling authority:** Turner Broadcasting System, Inc. v. FCC, 512 U.S. 622 (1994), decided June 27, 1994, Classification and scrutiny of must-carry; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Turner Broadcasting System, Inc. v. FCC, 512 U.S. 622 (1994), decided June 27, 1994, supplies the classification, scrutiny, and burden allocation. Turner Broadcasting System, Inc. v. FCC, 520 U.S. 180 (1997), decided March 31, 1997, supplies the final record-support and editorial-fit requirements.

**Present operation effective:** Turner Broadcasting System, Inc. v. FCC, 520 U.S. 180 (1997), March 31, 1997.

**Development:**
- March 31, 1997, Turner Broadcasting System, Inc. v. FCC, 520 U.S. 180 (1997): The evidentiary gap that required remand was closed, and the rule now expressly requires independent review of record support and the actual distribution of editorial displacement.

### Leased-access operator discretion over patently offensive programming

**Governing question:** When federal law may permit a cable operator to decline patently offensive programming on a leased-access channel.

**Current rule:** Section 10(a) is not facially invalid. Federal law may permit a cable operator to decline programming within the statutory category on a federally created leased-access channel. The permission does not foreclose an as-applied challenge when the operator’s choice is produced by governmental coercion, administered jointly with government, selectively enforced, or applied outside the statutory category.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not establish a categorical forum or state-action rule for every cable-access channel, authorize exclusion outside the statutory category, or protect a choice fairly attributable to governmental coercion or joint administration.

**First controlling authority:** Denver Area Educational Telecommunications Consortium, Inc. v. FCC; Alliance for Community Media v. FCC, 518 U.S. 727, decided June 28, 1996, Section 10(a), leased-access operator discretion; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Denver Area Educational Telecommunications Consortium, Inc. v. FCC; Alliance for Community Media v. FCC, 518 U.S. 727, decided June 28, 1996, Section 10(a), leased-access operator discretion; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Denver Area Educational Telecommunications Consortium, Inc. v. FCC; Alliance for Community Media v. FCC, 518 U.S. 727, June 28, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 28, 1996, Denver Area Educational Telecommunications Consortium, Inc. v. FCC; Alliance for Community Media v. FCC, 518 U.S. 727: *Turner Broadcasting* is applied through provision-specific review rather than converted into a universal cable rule. Section 10(a) survives facial review, while governmental coercion, joint administration, selective enforcement, and materially different applications remain open.

### Mandatory cable segregation and written-request access

**Governing question:** What First Amendment limits govern mandatory segregation and written-request access for protected indecent cable programming.

**Current rule:** Government may channel protected indecent cable programming to protect children only through a measure that preserves realistic adult access and is reasonably fitted to the demonstrated harm. A regime requiring advance written identification, delay, and placement on a stigmatized blocked channel is invalid when prompt, confidential, subscriber-controlled blocking can address household exposure with materially less interference in adult access.

**Burdens and proof:** Government must establish both the child-protection interest and a record-supported fit that preserves practical adult access to protected programming.

**Exceptions and limits:** Government may prohibit obscenity, threats, exploitation, and other unprotected material and may employ lawful subscriber-controlled blocking or another precise channeling measure that preserves realistic adult access.

**First controlling authority:** Denver Area Educational Telecommunications Consortium, Inc. v. FCC; Alliance for Community Media v. FCC, 518 U.S. 727, decided June 28, 1996, Section 10(b), mandatory segregation and written-request access; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Denver Area Educational Telecommunications Consortium, Inc. v. FCC; Alliance for Community Media v. FCC, 518 U.S. 727, decided June 28, 1996, Section 10(b), mandatory segregation and written-request access; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Denver Area Educational Telecommunications Consortium, Inc. v. FCC; Alliance for Community Media v. FCC, 518 U.S. 727, June 28, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 28, 1996, Denver Area Educational Telecommunications Consortium, Inc. v. FCC; Alliance for Community Media v. FCC, 518 U.S. 727: *Turner Broadcasting*’s requirement of demonstrated burden and reasonable fit is applied to adult access. The decision does not import broadcast scarcity into cable or require one universal treatment of distinct cable-access arrangements.

### Public-access operator vetoes over protected programming

**Governing question:** When federal law may authorize a cable operator to exclude protected indecent programming from a public-access channel.

**Current rule:** Section 10(c)’s broad operator veto over protected indecent programming on government-created public-access channels is invalid on the present record. Government did not establish a sufficiently strong, record-supported justification and fit for the content-based veto when confidential subscriber-controlled blocking could address household exposure without suppressing programming at its source.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule is confined to the challenged provision and record. It does not classify every cable-access channel as a public forum, resolve every state-action question, restrict regulation of unprotected material, or foreclose lawful subscriber-controlled blocking and precise channeling rules.

**First controlling authority:** Denver Area Educational Telecommunications Consortium, Inc. v. FCC; Alliance for Community Media v. FCC, 518 U.S. 727, decided June 28, 1996, Section 10(c), public-access operator veto; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Denver Area Educational Telecommunications Consortium, Inc. v. FCC; Alliance for Community Media v. FCC, 518 U.S. 727, decided June 28, 1996, Section 10(c), public-access operator veto; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Denver Area Educational Telecommunications Consortium, Inc. v. FCC; Alliance for Community Media v. FCC, 518 U.S. 727, June 28, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 28, 1996, Denver Area Educational Telecommunications Consortium, Inc. v. FCC; Alliance for Community Media v. FCC, 518 U.S. 727: The decision invalidates § 10(c) on its developed record without establishing one categorical forum or state-action rule for every cable-access channel. *Turner Broadcasting* remains applicable to the need for record-supported burden and fit.

### Provision-specific review of content-neutral speech injunctions

**Governing question:** What fit is required for a content-neutral injunction directed at identified speakers and enforced by contempt.

**Current rule:** A content-neutral injunction directed at identified speakers must be reviewed provision by provision. Each restraint must be connected to demonstrated unlawful conduct and burden no more speech than necessary to provide complete lawful protection. Fixed geographic zones may rest on location-specific findings of obstruction, intimidation, or access interference. Mobile zones require separate precision and record support because they move with the listener and can suppress ordinary conversation. A narrow conduct-based restriction on persistent pursuit after a clear refusal may be imposed on adjudicated parties when the record establishes repeated conduct that fixed relief cannot prevent.

**Exceptions and limits:** Misconduct supporting one provision does not automatically justify another. The rule does not create a general listener’s veto, and fixed, floating, or personal-distance rules require findings tied to their own operation.

**First controlling authority:** Madsen v. Women’s Health Center, Inc., 512 U.S. 753 (1994), decided June 30, 1994, Standard for a content-neutral speech injunction; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Madsen v. Women’s Health Center, Inc., 512 U.S. 753 (1994), decided June 30, 1994, supplies provision-specific necessity review. Schenck v. Pro-Choice Network of Western New York, 519 U.S. 357 (1997), decided February 19, 1997, supplies the fixed-zone, floating-zone, and narrow post-refusal boundaries.

**Present operation effective:** Schenck v. Pro-Choice Network of Western New York, 519 U.S. 357 (1997), February 19, 1997.

**Development:**
- February 19, 1997, Schenck v. Pro-Choice Network of Western New York, 519 U.S. 357 (1997): Fixed zones may be sustained by location-specific obstruction findings; floating zones require distinct precision and support; and a confined post-refusal pursuit remedy may address repeated adjudicated conduct without creating a general listener’s veto.

### Binding nonparties through active concert in speech injunctions

**Governing question:** When a speech injunction may bind a person who was not named as a party.

**Current rule:** A speech injunction may bind a nonparty with actual notice who actively coordinates with, aids, or participates with a named enjoined person in conduct the injunction lawfully prohibits. It may not reach a person merely because of shared views, organizational affiliation, presence at the same event, or independently chosen parallel advocacy.

**Burdens and proof:** Enforcement against a nonparty requires proof of actual notice and coordinated participation in prohibited conduct.

**First controlling authority:** Madsen v. Women’s Health Center, Inc., 512 U.S. 753 (1994), decided June 30, 1994, Persons acting in active concert or participation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Madsen v. Women’s Health Center, Inc., 512 U.S. 753 (1994), decided June 30, 1994, Persons acting in active concert or participation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Madsen v. Women’s Health Center, Inc., 512 U.S. 753 (1994), June 30, 1994.
