**Related current rules:** Provision-specific review of content-neutral speech injunctions, under First Amendment, supplies the validity and fit requirements for the underlying injunction.

### Truthful professional credentials and potentially misleading advertising

**Governing question:** When a regulator may prohibit or qualify truthful use of a professional license or bona fide private credential.

**Current rule:** A regulator may not punish truthful use of an active professional license or bona fide private credential without proof that the representation is false, inherently misleading, actually deceptive, or presents a real risk of deception materially alleviated by the restriction. Unsupported speculation that a private certification implies state approval is insufficient. When a real potential for confusion is shown, a proportionate disclosure ordinarily must be considered before prohibition.

**Burdens and proof:** The regulator bears the burden of establishing the asserted deception risk and the material effectiveness and proportionality of the response.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not protect a revoked, inactive, falsely described, or fabricated credential, and it permits supported disclosure requirements identifying the certifying body or other material qualification.

**First controlling authority:** Ibanez v. Florida Department of Business and Professional Regulation, Board of Accountancy, 512 U.S. 136 (1994), decided June 13, 1994, Truthful use of the CFP designation; established the private-credential rule, together with the same decision’s unanimous active-license holding.

**Controlling authorities:** Ibanez v. Florida Department of Business and Professional Regulation, Board of Accountancy, 512 U.S. 136 (1994), decided June 13, 1994, Truthful use of the CFP designation; supplies the private-credential rule, together with the same decision’s unanimous active-license holding.

**Present operation effective:** Ibanez v. Florida Department of Business and Professional Regulation, Board of Accountancy, 512 U.S. 136 (1994), June 13, 1994.

### Religion-neutral allocation of civil governmental authority

**Governing question:** When government may allocate ordinary civil authority to a religiously homogeneous community.

**Current rule:** Government may accommodate religious needs and organize municipalities or school districts under neutral laws, but it may not specially allocate civil authority to a religiously identified community without neutral, generally applicable criteria available on equal terms to comparable religious and secular communities.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not invalidate a religiously homogeneous municipality formed under neutral incorporation law, bar religious citizens from ordinary political authority, deny equal public services, or forbid accommodations administered through neutral eligibility and secular public control.

**First controlling authority:** Board of Education of Kiryas Joel Village School District v. Grumet, 512 U.S. 687 (1994), decided June 27, 1994, Special allocation of public-school authority to a religious enclave; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Board of Education of Kiryas Joel Village School District v. Grumet, 512 U.S. 687 (1994), decided June 27, 1994, Special allocation of public-school authority to a religious enclave; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Board of Education of Kiryas Joel Village School District v. Grumet, 512 U.S. 687 (1994), June 27, 1994.

### Sectarian targeting through ostensibly general regulation

**Governing question:** When an ostensibly general regulation impermissibly targets a religion through its purpose or administration.

**Current rule:** Government may regulate charitable solicitation through neutral law, but it may not use an ostensibly general ordinance to supervise or disadvantage a named religion. Evidence that sustained official focus on a particular denomination materially shaped a measure’s design or administration is legally relevant, and a genuine dispute on that purpose or targeting question precludes summary judgment.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not find that every provision was enacted for a forbidden purpose and does not disable a neutral replacement ordinance aimed at fraud rather than religious identity.

**First controlling authority:** Church of Scientology Flag Service Organization, Inc. v. City of Clearwater, 2 F.3d 1514, decided December 5, 1994, Sectarian purpose and religious targeting; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Church of Scientology Flag Service Organization, Inc. v. City of Clearwater, 2 F.3d 1514, decided December 5, 1994, Sectarian purpose and religious targeting; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Church of Scientology Flag Service Organization, Inc. v. City of Clearwater, 2 F.3d 1514, December 5, 1994.

### Church autonomy and ministerial employment

**Governing question:** When church autonomy and the ministerial protection bar application of employment law to a religious organization’s selection or supervision of ministers.

**Current rule:** The Religion Clauses bar application of Title VII to a claim whose adjudication or requested relief would require a civil court to determine whether a religious body properly selected, ordained, promoted, assigned, supervised, disciplined, retained, or removed a minister. Ministerial status turns on the employee’s religious functions rather than title or ordination alone. Civil courts may not review the religious body’s reasons, test them for pretext, order reinstatement to ministry, or substitute civil criteria for the organization’s choice of religious leaders.

**Exceptions and limits:** Religious organizations remain subject to neutral civil law governing severable secular conduct. Salary, property, contract, tort, or comparable claims may proceed when independently pleaded and resolvable through neutral principles without deciding doctrine or ministerial fitness. Not every employee of a religious institution is a minister.

**First controlling authority:** Young v. Northern Illinois Conference of United Methodist Church, 21 F.3d 184, decided January 17, 1995, Church autonomy and ministerial employment; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Young v. Northern Illinois Conference of United Methodist Church, 21 F.3d 184, decided January 17, 1995, Church autonomy and ministerial employment; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Young v. Northern Illinois Conference of United Methodist Church, 21 F.3d 184, January 17, 1995.

### Prospective restrictions on unrelated speech by public employees

**Governing question:** What justification government must provide for a prospective restriction on public employees’ citizen speech unrelated to official duties.

**Current rule:** Section 501(b) violates the First Amendment as applied to the represented lower-level employees because it suppresses compensated citizen speech unrelated to official duties without an adequate connection to actual or apparent misuse of office. When government imposes a prospective restriction that deters a broad category of citizen speech by a massive number of employees before any particular workplace disruption occurs, it must show that the interests of employees and their audiences are outweighed by the expression’s necessary impact on governmental operations. The required justification increases with the breadth of the deterrent.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not invalidate restrictions on senior policymakers, honoraria tied to official duties or status, use of nonpublic information, actual conflicts of interest, or materially different statutory applications supported by an adequate record.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. National Treasury Employees Union, 513 U.S. 454, decided February 22, 1995, Prospective honoraria prohibition for lower-level employees; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. National Treasury Employees Union, 513 U.S. 454, decided February 22, 1995, Prospective honoraria prohibition for lower-level employees; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. National Treasury Employees Union, 513 U.S. 454, February 22, 1995.

### Neutral housing antidiscrimination laws and religious objections

**Governing question:** Whether religious objections excuse compliance with a neutral housing antidiscrimination law.

**Current rule:** The Free Exercise Clause does not require an exemption because the state and municipal provisions are neutral and generally applicable, prohibit the same commercial refusal without regard to religious motivation, and contain no system of comparable secular exemptions.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not govern religious teaching or advocacy, internal religious governance, or a scheme that permits comparable secular refusals while withholding religious accommodation.

**First controlling authority:** Swanner v. Anchorage Equal Rights Commission, 874 P.2d 274, decided March 27, 1995, Free Exercise Clause; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Swanner v. Anchorage Equal Rights Commission, 874 P.2d 274, decided March 27, 1995, Free Exercise Clause; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Swanner v. Anchorage Equal Rights Commission, 874 P.2d 274, March 27, 1995.

### Constitutional access to executive criminal-justice records

**Governing question:** Whether the First Amendment creates public access to executive-branch criminal-justice records.

**Current rule:** The First Amendment does not create a general right to inspect executive-branch criminal-justice records merely because the information could facilitate protected speech. Ordinary access to executive records begins with enacted disclosure law. A constitutional access right attaches only within a separately established category, such as a judicial proceeding or record satisfying the governing historical and structural requirements. A desire to speak using government-held information does not itself compel disclosure.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide access to a court filing, hearing, or other judicial record satisfying the experience-and-logic inquiry.

**First controlling authority:** Lanphere & Urbaniak v. Colorado, 21 F.3d 1508, decided April 18, 1995, Constitutional access to executive criminal-justice records; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Lanphere & Urbaniak v. Colorado, 21 F.3d 1508, decided April 18, 1995, Constitutional access to executive criminal-justice records; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Lanphere & Urbaniak v. Colorado, 21 F.3d 1508, April 18, 1995.

### Selective denial based on intended commercial speech

**Governing question:** When a State may deny otherwise available records because the requester intends truthful commercial solicitation.

**Current rule:** Once a State establishes an access regime, it may not categorically deny otherwise available records solely because the requester intends truthful, lawful commercial solicitation unless the use-based restriction satisfies the commercial-speech standard. Government must establish a substantial interest, show that the distinction materially advances that interest, and demonstrate a reasonable fit between the restriction and the proven harm. The absence of a freestanding access entitlement does not remove a discriminatory access condition from First Amendment review.

**Exceptions and limits:** Colorado need not disclose confidential records, eliminate neutral access qualifications, or tolerate false, deceptive, coercive, or harassing solicitation. A materially narrower use restriction supported by an adequate record remains open.

**First controlling authority:** Lanphere & Urbaniak v. Colorado, 21 F.3d 1508, decided April 18, 1995, Selective denial based on intended commercial speech; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Lanphere & Urbaniak v. Colorado, 21 F.3d 1508, decided April 18, 1995, Selective denial based on intended commercial speech; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Lanphere & Urbaniak v. Colorado, 21 F.3d 1508, April 18, 1995.

### Truthful commercial speech and contradictory regulatory exceptions

**Governing question:** What First Amendment scrutiny applies to a restriction on truthful, nonmisleading commercial information about lawful activity.

**Current rule:** A restriction on truthful, nonmisleading commercial information about lawful activity must materially advance a substantial governmental interest and have a reasonable fit with that interest. Internal statutory or regulatory contradictions and extensive exceptions leaving comparable conduct, incentives, or speech untouched may establish that the restriction does not directly and materially advance its asserted purpose. Governmental authority to regulate the underlying activity does not automatically authorize suppression of truthful speech about that activity.

**Exceptions and limits:** Government may prohibit false or misleading claims and may directly regulate lawful products and conduct within its substantive authority. A coherent restriction closely implementing a lawful interstate policy remains governed by its own record and fit.

**First controlling authority:** Rubin v. Coors Brewing Co., 514 U.S. 476, decided April 19, 1995, Alcohol-content information on beer labels; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Rubin v. Coors Brewing Co., 514 U.S. 476, decided April 19, 1995, supplies the material-advancement, reasonable-fit, and internal-contradiction components. Greater New Orleans Broadcasting Association, Inc. v. United States, 527 U.S. 173 (1999), decided June 14, 1999, supplies the extensive-exceptions and underlying-activity boundaries.

**Present operation effective:** Greater New Orleans Broadcasting Association, Inc. v. United States, 527 U.S. 173 (1999), June 14, 1999.

**Development:**
- June 14, 1999, Greater New Orleans Broadcasting Association, Inc. v. United States, 527 U.S. 173 (1999): The rule now expressly reaches contradictory schemes whose extensive comparable exceptions defeat direct and material advancement, and authority to regulate an underlying activity no longer supplies an automatic speech-suppression power. Former title: Alcohol-content information on beer labels.

### Compelled inclusion in a private parade

**Governing question:** Whether a public-accommodations law may compel a private parade organizer to include a message the organizer does not wish to convey.

**Current rule:** Applying a public-accommodations law to compel private parade organizers to include an identified contingent conveying a message the organizers do not choose violates the First Amendment. A parade is expressive even when it contains diverse messages, has no single precise theme, and admits participants through a broad selection policy. Selection of participating contingents forms part of the organizers’ expressive composition.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not invalidate public-accommodations laws generally, authorize exclusion from ordinary nonexpressive goods or services, or establish that a customer’s identity always constitutes a message.

**Related current rules:** Expressive-association nexus for compelled inclusion, under First Amendment, governs a neutral access law applied to a participant in an open civic organization and requires a proved connection among a defined organizational message, the participant’s role, and material expressive alteration.

**First controlling authority:** Hurley v. Irish-American Gay, Lesbian and Bisexual Group of Boston, 515 U.S. 557, decided June 19, 1995, Compelled inclusion in a private parade; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Hurley v. Irish-American Gay, Lesbian and Bisexual Group of Boston, 515 U.S. 557, decided June 19, 1995, Compelled inclusion in a private parade; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Hurley v. Irish-American Gay, Lesbian and Bisexual Group of Boston, 515 U.S. 557, June 19, 1995.

### Truthful targeted direct-mail solicitation

**Governing question:** What restrictions government may place on truthful targeted direct-mail solicitation by attorneys.

**Current rule:** Government may not categorically prohibit truthful, nonmisleading targeted direct-mail solicitation for a fixed post-event period merely because recipients or the public may regard prompt solicitation as offensive or undignified. It must identify a substantial interest, establish that the restriction directly and materially advances that interest, and demonstrate a reasonable fit to proven deception, coercion, harassment, or another concrete harm. Least-restrictive-means analysis is not required, but offense, poor taste, and generalized concern for professional image do not substitute for evidence.

**Exceptions and limits:** Supported restrictions on in-person solicitation, deception, coercion, repeated unwanted contact, confidential-record misuse, and proportionate disclosures remain lawful. A materially different evidentiary record may present a different application.

**First controlling authority:** Florida Bar v. Went For It, Inc., 515 U.S. 618, decided June 21, 1995, Truthful targeted direct-mail solicitation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Florida Bar v. Went For It, Inc., 515 U.S. 618, decided June 21, 1995, Truthful targeted direct-mail solicitation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Florida Bar v. Went For It, Inc., 515 U.S. 618, June 21, 1995.

### Equal civic access and direct financing of devotional activity

**Governing question:** When equal civic access or neutral allocation becomes unconstitutional direct support for devotional or inseparably sectarian activity.

**Current rule:** The Free Speech Clause protects religious speakers against exclusion from genuinely opened civic and expressive channels, but government may not use public money, a public monetary equivalent, or fungible publicly purchased instructional resources placed under a religious institution’s control to pay for devotional instruction, evangelism, religious formation, integrated sectarian schooling, or an institutional expense the recipient otherwise would bear. A court must distinguish access to a neutral forum, a genuinely severable civic service, a genuinely student-directed secular benefit, or a publicly controlled service from transfer of restricted public value that supports the provider’s own religious instructional program. Routing a voucher, credit, reimbursement, tax mechanism, or redeemable instrument through an independent chooser does not by itself convert restricted public financing into private expenditure.

**Exceptions and limits:** Religious organizations are not categorically ineligible for public programs. They may receive neutral civic services and compete for genuinely secular benefits on equal terms. Publicly staffed services, tightly controlled short-term loans, genuinely student-directed benefits, and programs retaining effective public control remain distinct from school-controlled fungible instructional support. The rule concerns the designated religious use and institutional substitution, not religious identity alone.

**First controlling authority:** *Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia*, 515 U.S. 819, decided June 29, 1995, equal civic access and direct financing of devotional activity; established the access-and-financing distinction.

**Controlling authorities:** *Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia*, 515 U.S. 819, decided June 29, 1995, supplies the forum, civic-service, and direct-financing distinction. *Jackson v. Benson*, 218 Wis. 2d 835, 578 N.W.2d 602, decided March 16, 1999, supplies the restricted-public-payment rule and the limit on routing public monetary value through a private chooser. *Strout v. Albanese*, citation and docket not supplied, decided April 3, 2000, supplies the restricted-public-value boundary for sectarian schooling. *Mitchell v. Helms*, 530 U.S. 793 (2000), decided June 28, 2000, supplies the fungible instructional-resource, institutional-control, substitution, student-direction, and public-control components.

**Present operation effective:** *Mitchell v. Helms*, 530 U.S. 793 (2000), June 28, 2000.

**Development:**
- March 16, 1999, *Jackson v. Benson*: Before *Jackson*, the entry distinguished neutral civic access from direct public financing of devotional activity. After *Jackson*, that distinction expressly governed restricted public monetary benefits routed through private choice; private selection does not cleanse public value used to satisfy a religious provider’s charge.
- June 28, 2000, *Mitchell v. Helms*: Before *Mitchell*, the entry governed public money and monetary equivalents. After *Mitchell*, it also reaches fungible publicly purchased instructional resources controlled by a religious school and substituting for institutional expense, while preserving genuinely student-directed benefits and publicly controlled services.

### Private religious expression in an open government forum

**Governing question:** When private religious expression in an open government forum may be excluded under the Establishment Clause.

**Current rule:** When government genuinely opens a forum on equal terms, it may not exclude otherwise eligible private expression because the viewpoint is religious. The expression remains private when forum history, neutral access rules, private ownership and sponsorship, absence of public financing or editorial control, and available identification measures make private attribution clear. Neutral identification rules may apply, but religious speech may not alone be forced to carry a special disclaimer.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not approve a government-sponsored religious display, selective religious favoritism, captive-audience worship, public financing of the religious message, or circumstances in which the forum’s operation reasonably attributes the expression to the State.

**First controlling authority:** Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette, 515 U.S. 753, decided June 29, 1995, Private religious expression in an open public forum; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette, 515 U.S. 753, decided June 29, 1995, Private religious expression in an open public forum; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette, 515 U.S. 753, June 29, 1995.

### Attribution of private religious displays inside government buildings

**Governing question:** When a private religious display inside a government building is attributable to the government.

**Current rule:** Neutral access for a clearly identified private religious display does not violate the Establishment Clause merely because the forum is inside a government building. Attribution depends on the forum’s established character, neutral access rules, private ownership and sponsorship, governmental control or financing, location, and contextual measures making private authorship clear.

**Exceptions and limits:** A display selected, financed, designed, controlled, or presented by the State as its own may be attributed to government. The Court also reserves a pretextual forum, obscured sponsorship, or circumstances in which the State affirmatively adopts the message.

**First controlling authority:** Chabad-Lubavitch of Georgia v. Miller, 5 F.3d 1383, decided June 29, 1995, Establishment Clause attribution inside the Capitol; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Chabad-Lubavitch of Georgia v. Miller, 5 F.3d 1383, decided June 29, 1995, Establishment Clause attribution inside the Capitol; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Chabad-Lubavitch of Georgia v. Miller, 5 F.3d 1383, June 29, 1995.

### Attribution and the Establishment Clause

**Governing question:** What facts determine whether private religious activity in a public-school access program is attributable to the school.

**Current rule:** In a public-school access program, neutral access for private religious activity is not attributable to the school when the meetings are voluntary, privately run, outside instructional time, dependent on parental consent, unsupported by public financing, accompanied by a clear private-sponsorship disclaimer, and neither selected nor led by school personnel. Attribution remains a contextual inquiry into sponsorship, control, timing, funding, consent, and the presentation of the activity to students and parents.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not require access during instruction, authorize teacher-led recruitment or devotion, excuse disruption or coercion, or bar neutral rules governing supervision and safety. It also does not require the District to retain the forum indefinitely if it closes or redefines it through genuinely viewpoint-neutral rules.

**First controlling authority:** Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue, 28 F.3d 1501, decided December 11, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Attribution and the Establishment Clause; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue, 28 F.3d 1501, decided December 11, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Attribution and the Establishment Clause; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue, 28 F.3d 1501, December 11, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- December 11, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue, 28 F.3d 1501: *Rosenberger* is distinguished because no public money is transferred for devotional activity. *Pinette* and *Chabad-Lubavitch* are applied to an after-school facility program, with parental consent, private sponsorship, timing, public control, and disclaimers supplying the attribution inquiry. The decision adds a recurring school-access application without changing those rules.

### Religious viewpoint discrimination in an after-school access program

**Governing question:** Whether a school may exclude religious viewpoints from an after-school access program open to comparable secular activity.

**Current rule:** Once a school opens a private-access channel for a defined subject, it may impose reasonable subject-matter and operational restrictions, but it may not identify a religious perspective on the permitted subject as the forbidden viewpoint.

**First controlling authority:** Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue, 28 F.3d 1501, decided December 11, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Religious viewpoint discrimination in an after-school access program; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue, 28 F.3d 1501, decided December 11, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Religious viewpoint discrimination in an after-school access program; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue, 28 F.3d 1501, December 11, 1995, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

### Voluntary school-led Pledge containing “under God”

**Governing question:** Whether a voluntary school-led Pledge containing “under God” remains a civic observance rather than a devotional exercise.

**Current rule:** A school-led civic observance may include a brief, historically embedded acknowledgment of religion when its object and operation remain patriotic rather than devotional and students retain a complete, practical right of nonparticipation. The government may not use that principle to conduct prayer, religious instruction, profession of faith, or an exercise that penalizes or formally identifies dissenters.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not permit punishment, compelled standing, compelled departure, teacher-led prayer, devotional elaboration on the Pledge, or selective treatment of students who decline to join. It does not govern a materially different exercise whose language or administration converts civic acknowledgment into religious profession.

**First controlling authority:** Sherman v. Community Consolidated School District 21, 980 F.2d 437, decided February 12, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Voluntary school-led Pledge containing “under God”; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Sherman v. Community Consolidated School District 21, 980 F.2d 437, decided February 12, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Voluntary school-led Pledge containing “under God”; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Sherman v. Community Consolidated School District 21, 980 F.2d 437, February 12, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- February 12, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Sherman v. Community Consolidated School District 21, 980 F.2d 437: *Good News/Good Sports Club* is distinguished. Its protected expression was privately sponsored and admitted through a neutral access program; the Pledge is government selected and government led. The present decision creates a narrow rule for a voluntary, nondevotional civic observance and does not expand the private-forum attribution rules.

### Facial First Amendment challenge

**Governing question:** What showing permits facial invalidation of an access-protection statute that also reaches plainly regulable obstruction.

**Current rule:** Peaceful advocacy, prayer, leafletting, picketing, persuasion, and association remain protected. The access-related intent element identifies the conduct Congress regulated; it does not make opposition to abortion an element of liability. Difficult boundary applications must be addressed on their facts rather than through facial invalidation of the statute’s plainly legitimate sweep.

**Exceptions and limits:** A prosecution resting on ambiguous rhetoric, passive presence, immaterial delay, protected counseling, or viewpoint-selective enforcement remains open to as-applied review. RFRA and other issues outside the controlled grant are not decided.

**First controlling authority:** American Life League v. Reno, 47 F.3d 642, decided January 29, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Facial First Amendment challenge; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** American Life League v. Reno, 47 F.3d 642, decided January 29, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Facial First Amendment challenge; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** American Life League v. Reno, 47 F.3d 642, January 29, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

### Patronage retaliation against a regular service provider

**Governing question:** What First Amendment framework applies when government ends a regular service-provider relationship for political affiliation or speech.

**Current rule:** A claim based principally on compelled political allegiance or affiliation is governed by *Elrod* and *Branti*. A claim based on independent speech is governed by the *Pickering* balance adapted to the contractual setting.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not govern first-time applicants or competitive bidders, decide whether political affiliation was appropriate for any particular service, or determine qualified immunity, reinstatement, or the mayor’s final policymaking authority. The separate same-decision rule governs whether conclusive proof of a lawful identical decision defeats retrospective liability.

**First controlling authority:** O’Hare Truck Service, Inc. v. City of Northlake, 518 U.S. 712, decided June 28, 1996, patronage retaliation against a regular service provider; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** O’Hare Truck Service, Inc. v. City of Northlake, 518 U.S. 712, decided June 28, 1996, supplies the extension of the Elrod, Branti, and Pickering frameworks to established service-provider relationships.

**Present operation effective:** O’Hare Truck Service, Inc. v. City of Northlake, 518 U.S. 712, June 28, 1996.

**Related current rules:** Same-decision proof and retrospective relief for a discrete governmental decision, under Remedies. That rule supplies the effect of conclusive proof that the same action would have occurred for lawful reasons.

**Development:**
- June 28, 1996, O’Hare Truck Service, Inc. v. City of Northlake, 518 U.S. 712: The decision extends *Elrod*, *Branti*, and *Pickering* beyond formal public employment to an established service-provider relationship. Together with *Umbehr*, it distinguishes constitutionally forbidden retaliation from the absence of a property entitlement and from lawful contracting discretion.

### Alternative avenues and application to existing businesses

**Governing question:** How courts evaluate alternative avenues when adult-business zoning applies to businesses already operating at enactment.

**Current rule:** A municipality need not guarantee an adult business its current address, preferred neighborhood, least expensive parcel, or commercially optimal site. It must leave a meaningful field of lawful locations in practical operation. Relocation expense and economic conditions matter when they demonstrate that nominally available sites are functionally unavailable, but ordinary commercial inconvenience does not establish a First Amendment violation.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court establishes no universally sufficient acreage, number of sites, or amortization period. A site count fails when the listed parcels are unavailable in practice.

**First controlling authority:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, decided March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date, Alternative avenues and application to existing businesses; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, decided March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date, Alternative avenues and application to existing businesses; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date.

**Development:**
- March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date, Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858: *City of Renton* and *City of Ladue* are clarified for alternative avenues. Practical availability, rather than a nominal parcel count, controls; relocation cost matters when it demonstrates functional unavailability, but the Court creates no fixed acreage, site-count, or amortization safe harbor.

### Official sectarian symbols used as governmental identity

**Governing question:** When a religious symbol incorporated into a government seal violates the Establishment Clause.

**Current rule:** Government violates the Establishment Clause when it selects an unmistakably sectarian symbol and pervasively uses it as the government’s own official identifying mark, unless the complete design, context, history, and official use establish a genuinely secular governmental meaning rather than religious endorsement. Private-expression attribution rules do not apply to a symbol chosen and used by government as its own.

**Exceptions and limits:** Historical significance, longevity, and secular images are relevant but not independently controlling. The rule does not govern private religious expression in a neutral forum.

**First controlling authority:** Robinson v. City of Edmond, 68 F.3d 1226, decided March 31, 1997, Christian cross in the official city seal; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Robinson v. City of Edmond, 68 F.3d 1226, decided March 31, 1997, Christian cross in the official city seal; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Robinson v. City of Edmond, 68 F.3d 1226, March 31, 1997.

### Private religious viewpoints in limited public access programs

**Governing question:** When government may exclude private religious teaching or worship from a program opened to comparable private subjects.

**Current rule:** When government opens a limited access program to private educational, cultural, philosophical, or religious subjects, it may define the permitted subject matter through reasonable, viewpoint-neutral rules. It may not exclude an otherwise eligible presentation because it affirmatively teaches, advocates, or practices a religious viewpoint on a permitted subject. Avoiding an Establishment Clause violation does not justify exclusion when neutral access, private sponsorship, and attribution safeguards keep the expression private.

**Exceptions and limits:** Government need not sponsor worship, finance devotional materials, allow disruption, or admit expression outside the defined program. It may enforce neutral rules against obstruction, coercion, and interference.

**First controlling authority:** Church on the Rock v. City of Albuquerque, 84 F.3d 1273, decided May 27, 1997, Religious viewpoint exclusion from the senior-center program; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Church on the Rock v. City of Albuquerque, 84 F.3d 1273, decided May 27, 1997, Religious viewpoint exclusion from the senior-center program; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Church on the Rock v. City of Albuquerque, 84 F.3d 1273, May 27, 1997.

### Public-employee religious expression and government attribution

**Governing question:** What distinctions govern a public employer’s regulation of an employee’s private religious expression at work.

**Current rule:** A public employer may regulate use of subordinate labor, official resources, coercion, harassment, and expression reasonably attributable to government. It may not impose a religion-specific ban on voluntary private prayer, witnessing, counseling, or religious items merely because coworkers object, when comparable private expression is allowed and the conduct does not use public authority or resources. Any asserted Establishment Clause justification must be objectively grounded in attribution or coercion rather than offense alone.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not protect official worship, compelled participation, use of subordinates or government resources for personal religious activity, harassment, or disruption of public operations.

**First controlling authority:** Brown v. Polk County, 61 F.3d 650, decided March 17, 1997, Religion-specific directives and attribution; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Brown v. Polk County, 61 F.3d 650, decided March 17, 1997, Religion-specific directives and attribution; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Brown v. Polk County, 61 F.3d 650, March 17, 1997.

### Content-based criminal regulation of Internet indecency

**Governing question:** What scrutiny governs content-based criminal restrictions on protected indecent speech over a general-purpose communications network.

**Current rule:** A content-based criminal restriction on Internet expression must define prohibited content with adequate precision, satisfy strict scrutiny, and preserve realistic adult access. Severe penalties, uncertain content categories, nationwide distribution, community-standard uncertainty, and speaker-side access controls that exclude lawful adults all enter facial vagueness, overbreadth, and tailoring review. Scienter concerning transmission does not cure uncertainty about what speech the law makes criminal.

**Burdens and proof:** Government bears the burden of establishing narrow tailoring and the inadequacy of practical, materially less speech-restrictive means of limiting minors’ access.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not protect obscenity, child sexual-abuse material, true threats, fraud, unlawful solicitation, or direct harmful contact with minors, and it does not classify every Internet service identically.

**First controlling authority:** Reno v. American Civil Liberties Union, 521 U.S. 844 (1997), decided June 26, 1997, Content-based criminal suppression of protected Internet speech; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Reno v. American Civil Liberties Union, 521 U.S. 844 (1997), decided June 26, 1997, Content-based criminal suppression of protected Internet speech; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Reno v. American Civil Liberties Union, 521 U.S. 844 (1997), June 26, 1997.

### RFRA strict scrutiny when valid federal authority makes RFRA applicable

**Governing question:** What substantive standard governs a RFRA claim when a valid source of federal authority makes the statute applicable.

**Current rule:** The claimant must establish a substantial burden on religious exercise. Government then bears production and persuasion that application of the particular burden furthers a compelling governmental interest through the least restrictive means. Review is provision specific and application specific. This substantive formulation operates only when RFRA validly applies under a constitutional source of federal authority.

**Burdens and proof:** Claimant bears the substantial-burden showing; government bears production and persuasion on compelling interest and least restrictive means.

**Exceptions and limits:** City of Boerne forecloses universal application to state and local government under Fourteenth Amendment § 5. RFRA’s application to the Federal Government and other asserted sources was not decided in OT1996.

**First controlling authority:** Church of Scientology Flag Service Organization, Inc. v. City of Clearwater, 2 F.3d 1514, decided December 5, 1994, RFRA substantial-burden and strict-scrutiny formulation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Church of Scientology Flag Service Organization, Inc. v. City of Clearwater, 2 F.3d 1514, decided December 5, 1994, supplies the provision-specific formulation. Swanner v. Anchorage Equal Rights Commission, 874 P.2d 274, decided March 27, 1995, supplies the third-party-protection application. City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997), decided June 25, 1997, preserves those mechanics only where a valid source makes RFRA applicable.

**Present operation effective:** City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997), June 25, 1997.

**Development:**
- June 25, 1997, City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997): The substantive RFRA mechanics survive only for applications resting on a valid source of federal authority; universal state and local application under § 5 ceased.

### Secondary effects, governmental purpose, and content neutrality

**Governing question:** When an adult-business zoning ordinance qualifies for content-neutral review under the secondary-effects doctrine.

**Current rule:** An adult-business zoning ordinance receives content-neutral treatment only when its enacted design and actual justification are genuinely directed at adverse secondary effects. Courts consider the text, enactment record, evidence before the decisionmakers, alternatives selected or rejected, and practical operation. Evidence that suppression of protected expression materially controlled the governmental decision may defeat secondary-effects classification. An improper statement by one participant does not establish that conclusion by itself.

**Exceptions and limits:** Government may not convert censorship into a content-neutral regulation by reciting secondary effects, commissioning post hoc studies, or disguising a practical prohibition as zoning. A materially different record showing pretext, discriminatory administration, deliberate expressive suppression, or a practical ban remains subject to content-based review.

**First controlling authority:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, decided March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date, Secondary effects, governmental purpose, and content neutrality; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, decided March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date, Secondary effects, governmental purpose, and content neutrality; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date.

### Off-duty public-employee speech and reaction-generated disruption

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs capacity and protection of the Albany speech.

**Current rule:** Speech delivered off campus is citizen speech when it was not assigned, commissioned, controlled, authorized, or delivered as part of the employee’s teaching or administrative duties. A public university may not punish such speech solely because of controversy, audience hostility, reputational harm, or predicted disruption generated by reaction to its viewpoint.

**First controlling authority:** Jeffries v. Harleston, 52 F.3d 9, decided November 17, 1997, Capacity and protection of the Albany speech; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Jeffries v. Harleston, 52 F.3d 9, decided November 17, 1997, Capacity and protection of the Albany speech; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Jeffries v. Harleston, 52 F.3d 9, November 17, 1997.

### Petitions invoking an established benefits process

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs retaliation for filing unemployment-benefit claims.

**Current rule:** A public employee’s lawful, non-sham invocation of an established administrative process to claim benefits is protected by the Petition Clause and need not concern a matter of public concern. A public employer may not refuse reemployment solely because the petition required payment of benefits prescribed by law.

**First controlling authority:** Valot v. Southeast Local School District Board of Education, 107 F.3d 1220, decided January 5, 1998, Retaliation for filing unemployment-benefit claims; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Valot v. Southeast Local School District Board of Education, 107 F.3d 1220, decided January 5, 1998, Retaliation for filing unemployment-benefit claims; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Valot v. Southeast Local School District Board of Education, 107 F.3d 1220, January 5, 1998.

### Official public-university prayer and neutral silence

**Governing question:** When prayer or silence at an official public-university event constitutes governmental religious sponsorship.

**Current rule:** A public university violates the Establishment Clause when it selects clergy or another speaker and conducts an invocation or benediction as part of an official university function. Adult status and formal voluntariness do not cure governmental sponsorship, and practical employment pressure adds to the concern. A genuinely neutral moment of silence that neither directs, endorses, nor prescribes religious exercise is permissible.

**First controlling authority:** Tanford v. Brand, 104 F.3d 982, decided December 22, 1997, official public-university prayer; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Tanford v. Brand, 104 F.3d 982, decided December 22, 1997, official public-university prayer; Chaudhuri v. Tennessee, 130 F.3d 232, decided April 27, 1998, official prayer and neutral silence; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Chaudhuri v. Tennessee, 130 F.3d 232, April 27, 1998.

**Development:**
- April 27, 1998, Chaudhuri v. Tennessee: The official-prayer rule extends beyond commencement to official university functions and adds the neutral-moment-of-silence boundary.

### Fixed eligibility standards in public-broadcast candidate debates

**Governing question:** How a state broadcaster must administer eligibility standards for a candidate debate.

**Current rule:** A state broadcaster may adopt reasonable, viewpoint-neutral eligibility standards for a candidate debate. Once it establishes those standards, it may not exclude a ballot-qualified candidate who satisfies them through unpublished, candidate-specific judgments of viability, newsworthiness, or public support. The broadcaster may revise standards prospectively and may exclude candidates who do not meet them, but it must apply the operative standards evenhandedly and may not use editorial discretion as a pretext for political favoritism or viewpoint discrimination.

**Exceptions and limits:** Perry Education Association and Cornelius remain applicable to genuinely selective nonpublic forums, but do not authorize departure from fixed eligibility criteria.

**First controlling authority:** Arkansas Educational Television Commission v. Forbes, 523 U.S. 666 (1998), decided May 18, 1998, candidate access under an announced debate standard; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Arkansas Educational Television Commission v. Forbes, 523 U.S. 666 (1998), decided May 18, 1998, candidate access under an announced debate standard; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Arkansas Educational Television Commission v. Forbes, 523 U.S. 666 (1998), May 18, 1998.

### Competitive discretionary arts-grant criteria

**Governing question:** When noncategorical qualitative considerations in a competitive public arts-grant program are facially constitutional.

**Current rule:** A statute is not facially unconstitutional when it adds noncategorical considerations to a competitive, discretionary arts-grant process without requiring denial of work considered indecent, disrespectful, controversial, or offensive. Qualitative and content-sensitive selection is permissible in such a discretionary program, but viewpoint discrimination, categorical exclusion, retaliation, and unconstitutional conditions remain independently forbidden.

**Exceptions and limits:** Arkansas Educational Television Commission v. Forbes is distinguished because the grant process contains no fixed access standard and remains competitive and discretionary; Forbes governs exclusion of a candidate who satisfies an announced debate-eligibility standard.

**First controlling authority:** National Endowment for the Arts v. Finley, 524 U.S. 569 (1998), decided June 25, 1998, competitive discretionary arts-grant criteria; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** National Endowment for the Arts v. Finley, 524 U.S. 569 (1998), decided June 25, 1998, competitive discretionary arts-grant criteria; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** National Endowment for the Arts v. Finley, 524 U.S. 569 (1998), June 25, 1998.

### Expressive sales and speaker-status distinctions

**Governing question:** When sales of message-bearing goods are protected expression and whether government may distinguish affiliated from unaffiliated speakers.

**Current rule:** Sales of recordings, literature, buttons, clothing, and other message-bearing items remain protected expression when integrated with political, religious, or ideological communication. Government may not condition comparable expressive solicitation or sales on nonprofit organizational status. A speaker-status distinction is not narrowly tailored to pedestrian-flow or unfair-competition interests when affiliated and unaffiliated speakers engage in physically identical conduct.

**First controlling authority:** Perry v. Los Angeles Police Department, 121 F.3d 1365, decided May 4, 1998, expressive sales and speaker-status distinctions; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Perry v. Los Angeles Police Department, 121 F.3d 1365, decided May 4, 1998, expressive sales and speaker-status distinctions; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Perry v. Los Angeles Police Department, 121 F.3d 1365, May 4, 1998.

### Intentional operational assistance to criminal conduct

**Governing question:** When the First Amendment permits generally applicable liability for publication used to facilitate a crime.

**Current rule:** The First Amendment does not categorically bar generally applicable tort liability when a publisher specifically intends to facilitate criminal use, supplies operationally concrete assistance, and that assistance is materially used in the crime. Liability requires the intent, assistance, material use, causation, and other elements of governing tort law. Negligent publication, generalized dangerous ideas, abstract advocacy, and remote foreseeability are insufficient.

**First controlling authority:** Rice v. Paladin Enterprises, Inc., 128 F.3d 233, decided May 14, 1998, intentional operational assistance to crime; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Rice v. Paladin Enterprises, Inc., 128 F.3d 233, decided May 14, 1998, intentional operational assistance to crime; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Rice v. Paladin Enterprises, Inc., 128 F.3d 233, May 14, 1998.

### Retaliation for prisoner litigation and grievances

**Governing question:** When an adverse prison or parole decision unlawfully retaliates for protected litigation or grievances.

**Current rule:** A prison or parole official may not impose a discretionary adverse consequence because a prisoner pursued protected, nonfrivolous litigation or filed or threatened a protected grievance. The claimant must show that protected activity substantially motivated the responsible official’s act; the official may establish that the same action would have occurred for an independently lawful reason. Knowingly filing a materially false disciplinary charge is actionable. Some evidence supporting the ultimate disciplinary judgment does not defeat the separate retaliation claim, and actual confinement or loss of privileges is adverse action without a separate subjective-chill showing.

**First controlling authority:** Johnson v. Rodriguez, 110 F.3d 299, decided February 17, 1998, retaliation for protected prisoner litigation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Johnson v. Rodriguez, 110 F.3d 299, decided February 17, 1998, retaliation for protected prisoner litigation; Pearson v. Hines, 108 F.3d 265, decided June 22, 1998, retaliation for protected grievances; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Pearson v. Hines, 108 F.3d 265, June 22, 1998.

**Development:**
- June 22, 1998, Pearson v. Hines: The parole-retaliation rule extends to a knowingly false disciplinary charge; a some-evidence ruling does not resolve retaliatory motive, and actual confinement or privilege loss supplies adverse action.

### Religious material in a neutral public-school curriculum

**Governing question:** When a public school may use religious music and venues for secular curricular instruction and performance.

**Current rule:** The Establishment Clause does not prohibit a public-school choir from selecting, rehearsing, and performing religious music, including in religious buildings, when the choices serve genuine secular artistic and pedagogical purposes and the school does not conduct or sponsor worship. Requiring study or performance as part of a neutral curriculum, subject to a genuine grade-neutral opt-out, does not violate free exercise or compelled-expression principles when the student is required neither to profess belief nor participate in worship.

**First controlling authority:** Bauchman v. West High School, 132 F.3d 542, decided June 26, 1998, religious material in a neutral school curriculum; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Bauchman v. West High School, 132 F.3d 542, decided June 26, 1998, religious material in a neutral school curriculum; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Bauchman v. West High School, 132 F.3d 542, June 26, 1998.

### Petition-circulator eligibility

**Governing question:** When government may condition participation as an initiative-petition circulator on voter registration.

**Current rule:** Government may not exclude otherwise qualified resident-adult petition circulators solely because they are unregistered voters when retained identification, affidavits, service requirements, verification, and fraud-enforcement mechanisms address accountability more directly. A registration rule that substantially reduces the available pool of petition speakers materially burdens core political circulation.

**Exceptions and limits:** Government may impose neutral residency, age, identification, service, verification, and fraud-prevention requirements that are constitutionally fitted to their purposes.

**First controlling authority:** Buckley v. American Constitutional Law Foundation, Inc., 525 U.S. 182 (1999), decided January 12, 1999, registered-voter qualification for petition circulators; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Buckley v. American Constitutional Law Foundation, Inc., 525 U.S. 182 (1999), decided January 12, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Buckley v. American Constitutional Law Foundation, Inc., 525 U.S. 182 (1999), January 12, 1999.

### Personal identification and disclosure in petition advocacy

**Governing question:** What exacting review requires when government compels personal identification during petition advocacy or publishes detailed information about individual circulators.

**Current rule:** Government may not compel a petition circulator to display the circulator’s personal name during the expressive encounter when post-circulation identification serves enforcement with materially less interference in political advocacy. Sponsor identity and aggregate expenditure disclosure may remain permissible, but publication of each paid circulator’s address and individual compensation requires a demonstrated substantial incremental relationship between those details and an additional important governmental need.

**Exceptions and limits:** Post-circulation identification, affidavits, service information, sponsor disclosure, aggregate expenditure reporting, and fitted fraud-enforcement measures remain available.

**First controlling authority:** Buckley v. American Constitutional Law Foundation, Inc., 525 U.S. 182 (1999), decided January 12, 1999, live name badges and detailed circulator disclosure; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Buckley v. American Constitutional Law Foundation, Inc., 525 U.S. 182 (1999), decided January 12, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Buckley v. American Constitutional Law Foundation, Inc., 525 U.S. 182 (1999), January 12, 1999.

### Institutional curricular expression by public-school teachers

**Governing question:** Whether a public-school teacher’s selection and production of material through assigned teaching duties is the teacher’s personal First Amendment speech.

**Current rule:** Selection, production, and direction of material pursuant to assigned teaching responsibilities are institutional curricular expression subject to the school system’s lawful control, not the teacher’s personal speech protected from that control by the First Amendment. School authorities may control school-sponsored curriculum for genuine pedagogical reasons.

**Exceptions and limits:** Speech outside assigned duties and curricular control remains distinct. The rule does not authorize retaliation for protected personal speech or control unrelated to lawful pedagogical administration.

**First controlling authority:** Boring v. Buncombe County Board of Education, 136 F.3d 364, decided February 17, 1999, status of assigned curricular expression; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Boring v. Buncombe County Board of Education, 136 F.3d 364, decided February 17, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Boring v. Buncombe County Board of Education, 136 F.3d 364, February 17, 1999.

### Viewpoint-neutral allocation of mandatory public-university student fees

**Governing question:** Whether a public university may require students to fund a broad program of private extracurricular expression through a generally applicable activity fee.

**Current rule:** A public university may use a generally applicable student fee to support a broad program of private extracurricular speech without granting individual ideological opt-outs when the allocation criteria and administration are viewpoint neutral. A majority vote or referendum may not allocate support according to a speaker’s popularity or viewpoint.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule governs a program facilitating diverse private student expression. It does not decide direct governmental financing of worship or devotional instruction, speech attributable to the university itself, or a program administered with viewpoint discrimination.

**First controlling authority:** *Board of Regents of the University of Wisconsin System v. Southworth*, 529 U.S. 217, decided March 22, 2000, mandatory student-fee support for a private-speech program; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Board of Regents of the University of Wisconsin System v. Southworth*, 529 U.S. 217, decided March 22, 2000, supplies the no-ideological-opt-out rule and the viewpoint-neutral allocation requirement.

**Present operation effective:** *Board of Regents of the University of Wisconsin System v. Southworth*, March 22, 2000.

**Related current rules:** *Equal civic access and direct financing of devotional activity*, under First Amendment, governs the distinct boundary between neutral access for private religious expression and direct payment for integrated devotional production or instruction.

### Public-nudity laws applied to expressive dancing

**Governing question:** When a generally applicable public-nudity law may be applied to expressive nude dancing.

**Current rule:** A generally applicable public-nudity law directed at crime or other independently nonexpressive secondary effects may be applied to nude dancing when it satisfies the incidental-burden requirements of *United States v. O’Brien* and leaves the erotic message substantially available through minimally clothed performance.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not permit suppression of an erotic viewpoint, discriminatory enforcement, a pretextual nonexpressive justification, or a restriction that fails the governing incidental-burden fit. A materially different law or evidentiary record must be assessed on its own terms.

**First controlling authority:** *City of Erie v. Pap’s A.M.*, 529 U.S. 277, decided March 29, 2000, controlling *Marks* rule for application of a general public-nudity ordinance to expressive dancing; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *City of Erie v. Pap’s A.M.*, 529 U.S. 277, decided March 29, 2000, supplies the genuine logical subset requiring an independently nonexpressive objective, *O’Brien* fit, and continued availability of the message through minimal clothing.

**Present operation effective:** *City of Erie v. Pap’s A.M.*, March 29, 2000.

### Content-based transmission restrictions on protected cable programming

**Governing question:** What review governs a content-based requirement that protected sexually explicit cable channels either prevent all signal bleed or cease transmission for much of the day.

**Current rule:** A content-based transmission restriction on protected cable programming is valid only if the Government proves that it is narrowly tailored to a compelling interest and that no less restrictive effective alternative is available. Protecting children from unwanted exposure is compelling, but broad time channeling fails when targeted, confidential household blocking can address the exposure with materially less suppression of protected adult access.

**Burdens and proof:** The Government bears the burden of proving narrow tailoring and the inadequacy of identified less restrictive alternatives. It cannot shift that burden to the speaker merely because complete scrambling is technologically imperfect.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not prevent targeted household blocking, subscriber notice, improved scrambling, or regulation of obscenity. It governs content-based suppression of protected programming, not every technical cable rule.

**First controlling authority:** *United States v. Playboy Entertainment Group, Inc.*, 529 U.S. 803, decided May 22, 2000, content-based time channeling of protected cable programming; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *United States v. Playboy Entertainment Group, Inc.*, 529 U.S. 803, decided May 22, 2000, supplies strict scrutiny, the Government’s burden, and the targeted-household-blocking alternative.

**Present operation effective:** *United States v. Playboy Entertainment Group, Inc.*, May 22, 2000.

**Related current rules:** *Mandatory cable segregation and written-request access* governs a distinct cable-access regime and its burdens on prompt, confidential adult access.

### School-attributed prayer selected through student elections

**Governing question:** Whether a public school may use student elections to authorize or select an invocation delivered at a school event.

**Current rule:** A public school may not use student elections to authorize or select an invocation that is institutionally structured, supervised, and delivered as part of a school event. A majority vote does not convert the resulting religious exercise into independent private speech when school policy determines whether the exercise will occur, the school selects or channels the speaker, and the message is presented through the school event and its facilities.

**Exceptions and limits:** Students remain free to pray privately, speak spontaneously, and participate in genuine neutral-access arrangements not selected, controlled, financed, or attributed to the school. The rule does not treat every student statement at a school event as government speech.

**First controlling authority:** *Santa Fe Independent School District v. Doe*, 530 U.S. 290 (2000), decided June 19, 2000, student-election authorization and selection of a school-event invocation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Santa Fe Independent School District v. Doe*, 530 U.S. 290 (2000), decided June 19, 2000, supplies the student-election, institutional-structure, supervision, school-event attribution, and private-speech limits.

**Present operation effective:** *Santa Fe Independent School District v. Doe*, 530 U.S. 290 (2000), June 19, 2000.

**Related current rules:** `Attribution and the Establishment Clause`, under First Amendment, governs voluntary private religious activity admitted through a neutral public-school access program. `Official public-university prayer and neutral silence`, under First Amendment, governs prayer selected and conducted as part of an official university function.

### Expressive-association nexus for compelled inclusion

**Governing question:** When application of a public-accommodations law requiring an open civic organization to retain a participant materially alters the organization’s protected expression.

**Current rule:** A generally applicable public-accommodations law may require an open civic organization to retain an otherwise qualified participant unless the organization proves that the particular role carries a defined organizational message and that inclusion would materially alter expression attributable to the group. Protected expressive activity by the organization does not make every membership or leadership decision constitutionally immune from neutral access law.

**Burdens and proof:** The organization must identify the expression at issue, connect the particular role to communication of that expression, and establish that the compelled inclusion would materially alter expression attributable to the organization.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not govern an intimate association, prevent an organization from advocating its views or selecting genuine spokespersons, or authorize government to compel a participant whose role is proved to carry the disputed organizational message. A participant’s identity alone does not automatically establish expressive alteration.

**First controlling authority:** *Boy Scouts of America v. Dale*, 530 U.S. 640 (2000), decided June 28, 2000, public-accommodations law and an open civic organization’s claimed expressive-association right; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Boy Scouts of America v. Dale*, 530 U.S. 640 (2000), decided June 28, 2000, supplies the defined-message, role nexus, group attribution, material-alteration, and burden components.

**Present operation effective:** *Boy Scouts of America v. Dale*, 530 U.S. 640 (2000), June 28, 2000.

**Related current rules:** `Compelled inclusion in a private parade`, under First Amendment, governs compelled alteration of the organizer’s expressive composition when selection of an identified message-bearing contingent is itself part of the parade.

### Nonconsensual approach limits near health-care entrances

**Governing question:** When a generally applicable law may restrict a speaker’s nonconsensual close approach to another person near a health-care entrance.

**Current rule:** A content-neutral law may prohibit a person within 100 feet of a health-care entrance from knowingly approaching within eight feet of another person, without consent, for leafletting, sign display, protest, education, or counseling when the restriction serves significant interests in safe access, avoidance of unwanted close pursuit, and administrable enforcement and leaves stationary advocacy, consensual conversation, signs, audible communication, and communication with willing listeners realistically available.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not authorize a broad speech-free zone, require a stationary speaker to retreat, restrict consensual communication, dispense with knowledge, or foreclose a discriminatory-enforcement or materially different as-applied challenge.

**First controlling authority:** *Hill v. Colorado*, 530 U.S. 703 (2000), decided June 28, 2000, knowing eight-foot nonconsensual approach limit within 100 feet of a health-care entrance; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Hill v. Colorado*, 530 U.S. 703 (2000), decided June 28, 2000, supplies content neutrality, the knowing approach trigger, the eight-foot and 100-foot boundaries, significant interests, alternative channels, and stated limits.

**Present operation effective:** *Hill v. Colorado*, 530 U.S. 703 (2000), June 28, 2000.

### School-sponsored student-election speech

**Governing question:** What First Amendment rule governs speech within a school-created and school-administered student-government election and enforcement of campaign rules.

**Current rule:** Speech within a student-government election created by a public school, conducted under school rules, and presented as part of the school’s civic program is governed by the school-sponsored-speech rule rather than the substantial-disruption rule for independent student speech. The school may enforce clear, viewpoint-neutral campaign rules reasonably related to fair procedure, equal campaign access, responsible administration, and the educational purposes of student government, including disqualification for a material violation.

**Exceptions and limits:** Independent student advocacy outside the school-sponsored campaign remains governed by ordinary student-speech doctrine. The school-sponsored-speech rule does not permit viewpoint discrimination, a pretextual restriction unrelated to legitimate pedagogical administration, punishment based solely on audience hostility, or control of an election the school has genuinely opened as a public forum.

**First controlling authority:** *Henerey v. City of St. Charles, School District*, citation and docket not supplied, decided September 25, 2000, school-sponsored student-government election speech and viewpoint-neutral campaign rules; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Henerey v. City of St. Charles, School District*, citation and docket not supplied, decided September 25, 2000, supplies the school-created-election trigger, school-sponsored-speech standard, independent-advocacy boundary, legitimate campaign-rule objectives, and disqualification consequence.

**Present operation effective:** *Henerey v. City of St. Charles, School District*, September 25, 2000.

**Related current rules:** `Institutional curricular expression by public-school teachers`, under First Amendment, governs assigned teacher expression rather than student speech in a sponsored election.

## Second Amendment

### Danger-linked firearm disability after a violent domestic-abuse conviction

**Governing question:** Whether the individual right to keep and bear arms permits a firearm-possession disability based on a legally operative misdemeanor domestic-abuse conviction involving violence.

**Current rule:** A constitutionally adequate conviction for domestic abuse involving actual or attempted physical force, or the threatened use of a deadly weapon, supplies an adjudicated danger predicate sufficient to prohibit firearm possession while the conviction remains legally operative, including possession of a government-issued duty firearm. The Constitution does not require a new present-danger hearing, a fixed expiration date, or a general restoration entitlement.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not establish a disability based on a nonviolent predicate, a civil restraining order, an unadjudicated accusation, or a conviction that has ceased to be legally operative. It does not foreclose relief supplied by an applicable pardon, expungement, restoration statute, or successful attack on the predicate conviction.

**First controlling authority:** *Fraternal Order of Police v. United States*, 173 F.3d 898 (D.C. Cir. 1999), Supreme Court simulation decided March 13, 2000, danger-linked firearm disability based on a qualifying domestic-abuse conviction; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Fraternal Order of Police v. United States*, 173 F.3d 898 (D.C. Cir. 1999), Supreme Court simulation decided March 13, 2000, supplies the adjudicated-danger predicate, duration, and no-new-hearing components.

**Present operation effective:** *Fraternal Order of Police v. United States*, March 13, 2000.

## Fourth Amendment

### Deadly force and asserted less-deadly alternatives

**Governing question:** Whether otherwise reasonable deadly force becomes excessive because an officer might have used a less-deadly alternative.

**Current rule:** The Fourth Amendment does not require an officer to exhaust every feasible less-deadly tactic before using otherwise reasonable deadly force. Earlier police conduct may bear on the totality when officers unreasonably create or materially intensify the need for force. Reasonableness is assessed without hindsight from the circumstances confronting the officer. Availability of another tactic is relevant evidence only insofar as it bears on whether the force actually used was objectively unreasonable. It does not create a least-deadly-means requirement or authorize courts to direct tactical sequencing after the event.

**Exceptions and limits:** The holding does not protect an officer who needlessly manufactures a confrontation, uses force after the threat has ended, or relies on earlier unlawful conduct that proximately creates the asserted necessity.

**First controlling authority:** Plakas v. Drinski, 19 F.3d 1143, decided January 9, 1995, Deadly force and asserted less-deadly alternatives; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Plakas v. Drinski, 19 F.3d 1143, decided January 9, 1995, Deadly force and asserted less-deadly alternatives; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Plakas v. Drinski, 19 F.3d 1143, January 9, 1995.

### Objectively reasonable reliance on a court-maintained warrant record

**Governing question:** When the exclusionary rule applies to an officer’s objectively reasonable reliance on an erroneous court-maintained warrant record.

**Current rule:** The exclusionary rule does not require suppression when an officer makes an arrest in objectively reasonable reliance on a court-maintained record that incorrectly reports an outstanding warrant, where the record identifies an isolated court-clerical failure and shows no police participation or recurring systemic unreliability. The court considers whether exclusion could materially deter the governmental actors responsible for the error and whether the officer executing the apparent warrant acted objectively reasonably. The holding does not create a general exception for inaccurate databases.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide errors generated or maintained by police, recurring inaccuracies known to law enforcement, reckless recordkeeping, or a system whose unreliability makes officer reliance objectively unreasonable.

**First controlling authority:** Arizona v. Evans, 514 U.S. 1, decided March 1, 1995, Objectively reasonable reliance on a court-maintained warrant record; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Arizona v. Evans, 514 U.S. 1, decided March 1, 1995, Objectively reasonable reliance on a court-maintained warrant record; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Arizona v. Evans, 514 U.S. 1, March 1, 1995.

### Deliberate acquisition of otherwise unavailable home-interior information

**Governing question:** When deliberate acquisition of otherwise unavailable information about the interior of a substantially screened home is a search.

**Current rule:** Law enforcement conducts a search when officers deliberately obtain otherwise unavailable information about activity inside a substantially screened home, whether by sense-enhancing technology from outside or by entering a fenced, nonpublic vantage without lawful access to look inside. Ordinary observation from a lawful public vantage remains unaffected.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Pinson, 24 F.3d 1056, decided March 27, 1995, technological acquisition of home-interior information; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Pinson, 24 F.3d 1056, decided March 27, 1995; United States v. Fields, 113 F.3d 313, decided February 9, 1998; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Fields, 113 F.3d 313, February 9, 1998.

**Development:**
- February 9, 1998, United States v. Fields: The rule formerly limited to sense-enhancing technology now also reaches deliberate observation obtained from an unlawfully entered restricted physical vantage. Lawful public observation remains unchanged. Former title: Technological acquisition of information from inside a home.
