### Knock and announce as a component of reasonableness

**Governing question:** What threshold governs an unannounced entry and how execution-related property damage is reviewed.

**Current rule:** The Fourth Amendment’s reasonableness inquiry ordinarily includes announcement before forcible residential entry. Officers may dispense with announcement on case-specific reasonable suspicion that notice would create danger, futility, or a risk of evidence destruction. Property damage does not create a heightened threshold for dispensing with announcement. Destruction during execution is reviewed independently for objective reasonableness. Section 3109 incorporates traditional common-law exigency exceptions.

**First controlling authority:** Wilson v. Arkansas, 514 U.S. 927, decided May 22, 1995, knock and announce as part of reasonableness; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Wilson v. Arkansas, 514 U.S. 927, decided May 22, 1995; Richards v. Wisconsin, 520 U.S. 385 (1997), decided April 28, 1997; United States v. Ramirez, 523 U.S. 65 (1998), decided March 4, 1998; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Ramirez, 523 U.S. 65 (1998), March 4, 1998.

**Development:**
- March 4, 1998, United States v. Ramirez: Property damage does not alter the reasonable-suspicion threshold; execution-related destruction remains independently reviewable for objective reasonableness.

### Privacy in a rental vehicle

**Governing question:** When a driver who is not listed on a rental agreement has a reasonable expectation of privacy in the vehicle.

**Current rule:** A driver’s absence from a rental agreement does not by itself defeat a legitimate expectation of privacy when the driver possesses and controls the vehicle with the authorized renter’s permission and without theft, fraud, or comparable wrongful possession. The inquiry considers the defendant’s actual relationship to the vehicle, including permission, possession of the keys, practical control, ability to exclude strangers, the owner’s retained rights, and whether possession was obtained or continued wrongfully. A private contractual driver restriction is relevant but not conclusive.

**Exceptions and limits:** Permission from the renter does not automatically establish privacy under every circumstance. The Court does not protect a thief, carjacker, fraudulent renter, or person continuing possession after an effective repossession.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Wellons, 32 F.3d 117, decided May 30, 1995, Privacy in a rental vehicle; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Wellons, 32 F.3d 117, decided May 30, 1995, Privacy in a rental vehicle; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Wellons, 32 F.3d 117, May 30, 1995.

### Privacy in and investigative manipulation of closed luggage

**Governing question:** Whether a person retains privacy in closed luggage placed in another person’s vehicle and when physical handling of the luggage becomes a search.

**Current rule:** A person’s lack of a legitimate expectation of privacy in a vehicle does not by itself eliminate a legitimate expectation of privacy in that person’s closed luggage found within the vehicle. Privacy in a container depends on ownership, possession, control, and steps taken to preserve its contents as private. Deliberately squeezing or manipulating closed soft luggage to discover concealed contents is a search; exposure to routine handling, lifting, repositioning, bumps, or accidental contact is not consent to exploratory tactile examination. The Government may nevertheless search the container when the automobile exception, consent, a valid inventory, or another lawful authority reaches it.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not decide ownership of each bag, disclaimer, abandonment, the scope of probable cause, or the validity of an inventory procedure. Ordinary handling necessary to transportation remains distinct from calculated investigative manipulation.

**First controlling authority:** *United States v. Wellons*, 32 F.3d 117, Supreme Court simulation decided May 30, 1995, independent privacy in closed luggage; established the original rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *United States v. Wellons*, 32 F.3d 117, Supreme Court simulation decided May 30, 1995, supplies the independent-container-privacy rule and lawful-search qualifications. *Bond v. United States*, 529 U.S. 334, decided April 17, 2000, supplies the distinction between routine handling and exploratory tactile manipulation.

**Present operation effective:** *Bond v. United States*, April 17, 2000.

**Development:**
- April 17, 2000, *Bond v. United States*: The prior rule protected closed luggage independently of privacy in the surrounding vehicle but did not state when handling the container itself becomes a search. *Bond* adds that calculated tactile manipulation to discover contents is a search while routine transportation handling remains outside that rule. Former title: Independent privacy in closed luggage.

### Suspicionless drug testing of public-school athletes

**Governing question:** When a public school may require suspicionless drug testing of student athletes.

**Current rule:** A public school may conduct suspicionless drug testing of interscholastic athletes when the students’ reduced privacy expectations in the athletic program, the minimally intrusive collection and restricted use of results, and a documented drug problem together make the search reasonable. Participation does not create a general waiver of Fourth Amendment protection.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision concerns a non-law-enforcement program limited to interscholastic athletes and restricted drug information. It does not approve disclosure to police, testing for unrelated medical conditions, or suspicionless testing of every student regardless of context.

**First controlling authority:** Vernonia School District 47J v. Acton, 515 U.S. 646, decided June 26, 1995, Random urinalysis of interscholastic athletes; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Vernonia School District 47J v. Acton, 515 U.S. 646, decided June 26, 1995, Random urinalysis of interscholastic athletes; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Vernonia School District 47J v. Acton, 515 U.S. 646, June 26, 1995.

### Objective reasonableness of a traffic stop

**Governing question:** Whether a traffic stop supported by objective probable cause is invalid because of an officer’s subjective motive.

**Current rule:** The constitutional inquiry ordinarily asks whether the facts objectively authorized the seizure actually performed. A court does not suppress evidence because an officer also possessed an unexpressed investigative motive or because another officer might have exercised enforcement discretion differently.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not authorize a stop lacking objective legal support, fabricated violations, prolongation beyond the traffic mission without independent justification, or discriminatory enforcement. A claim that officers selected motorists because of race remains governed by equal protection and its own proof and remedial rules.

**First controlling authority:** Whren v. United States, 517 U.S. 806, decided June 10, 1996, Objective reasonableness of a traffic stop; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Whren v. United States, 517 U.S. 806, decided June 10, 1996, Objective reasonableness of a traffic stop; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Whren v. United States, 517 U.S. 806, June 10, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 10, 1996, Whren v. United States, 517 U.S. 806: *Ornelas* is applied without alteration. That decision governs appellate review of the objective constitutional determination; *Whren* establishes that subjective enforcement purpose ordinarily does not enter the Fourth Amendment merits. *Armstrong* remains a separate equal-protection and discovery rule.

### Appellate review of reasonable suspicion and probable cause

**Governing question:** What standard of appellate review applies to determinations of reasonable suspicion and probable cause.

**Current rule:** The trial court determines what occurred, resolves credibility, and identifies the immediate factual setting. The appellate court independently applies the constitutional standard to those facts. Practical consideration of local knowledge and police experience does not transfer final authority over the constitutional boundary.

**Exceptions and limits:** Independent review does not authorize appellate factfinding, disregard of supported credibility determinations, or use of a mechanical checklist of suspicious circumstances. The Court does not decide whether suppression is ultimately required.

**First controlling authority:** Ornelas v. United States, 517 U.S. 690, decided May 28, 1996, Appellate review of reasonable suspicion and probable cause; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Ornelas v. United States, 517 U.S. 690, decided May 28, 1996, Appellate review of reasonable suspicion and probable cause; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Ornelas v. United States, 517 U.S. 690, May 28, 1996.

**Development:**
- May 28, 1996, Ornelas v. United States, 517 U.S. 690: *Thompson v. Keohane* is applied and clarified. Its distinction between historical facts and independent application of an objective constitutional rule now governs reasonable suspicion and probable cause.

### Interception through another participant’s vulnerable endpoint

**Governing question:** Whether a caller loses Fourth Amendment protection because the other participant unknowingly uses a vulnerable communications device.

**Current rule:** A person using a protected landline or cellular telephone does not lose Fourth Amendment protection merely because the other participant, without the caller’s knowledge, uses a vulnerable cordless handset. Participant consent, a willing informant, knowing exposure, or acceptance of the retransmission presents a different case. Technical accessibility alone does not establish voluntary exposure of the unaware caller’s own words.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not determine the other participant’s privacy interest or disturb participant-consent cases.

**First controlling authority:** In re Askin, 47 F.3d 100, decided December 9, 1996, Fourth Amendment protection of the noncordless caller; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** In re Askin, 47 F.3d 100, decided December 9, 1996, Fourth Amendment protection of the noncordless caller; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** In re Askin, 47 F.3d 100, December 9, 1996.

### Passenger-exit orders during lawful traffic stops

**Governing question:** Whether an officer may order a passenger out of a vehicle during a lawful traffic stop.

**Current rule:** During a lawful traffic stop, an officer may categorically order passengers to exit the vehicle while the stop remains in progress. The initial stop must itself be objectively valid, and the authority controls passenger location only for the duration and purposes of that stop.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not authorize prolongation of the stop, frisk without the required suspicion, or unrelated detention after the traffic mission ends.

**First controlling authority:** Maryland v. Wilson, 519 U.S. 408 (1997), decided February 19, 1997, Passenger-exit authority; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Maryland v. Wilson, 519 U.S. 408 (1997), decided February 19, 1997, Passenger-exit authority; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Maryland v. Wilson, 519 U.S. 408 (1997), February 19, 1997.

### Suspicionless testing requires a concrete special need

**Governing question:** What threshold government must satisfy before imposing suspicionless drug testing outside ordinary law enforcement.

**Current rule:** Reduced test intrusiveness does not itself justify departure from individualized suspicion. Government must identify a substantial and concrete special need connected to the actual duties or risks of the covered class, beyond ordinary law enforcement or symbolic reassurance, and then establish context-specific privacy, efficacy, and fit. A blanket test for candidates for public office fails when no real operational risk is shown.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not govern individualized suspicion, medical fitness examinations, private testing, financial disclosure, or security screening tied to a real and immediate safety risk.

**First controlling authority:** Chandler v. Miller, 520 U.S. 305 (1997), decided April 15, 1997, Suspicionless testing of candidates; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Chandler v. Miller, 520 U.S. 305 (1997), decided April 15, 1997, Suspicionless testing of candidates; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Chandler v. Miller, 520 U.S. 305 (1997), April 15, 1997.

### Stored pager data and contemporaneous preservation search

**Governing question:** What Fourth Amendment rules govern activation and immediate retrieval of a pager’s stored messages after arrest.

**Current rule:** Activating a pager to display concealed numerical messages is a search distinct from physical seizure. Officers may contemporaneously retrieve the short numerical memory of a pager seized from an arrestee at the place of arrest when retrieval addresses the ordinary risk that incoming pages will overwrite the finite stored sequence. The authority is confined to immediate, limited display serving that preservation function.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not authorize delayed exploration after the preservation justification ends or examination of materially broader, durable, or unrelated data stores.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Ortiz, 84 F.3d 977, decided May 12, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, Activation of stored pager memory and immediate retrieval incident to arrest; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Ortiz, 84 F.3d 977, decided May 12, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, Activation of stored pager memory and immediate retrieval incident to arrest; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Ortiz, 84 F.3d 977, May 12, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date.

### Police-created evidence-destruction exigency

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs police-created evidence-destruction exigency.

**Current rule:** Officers may not justify a warrantless residential entry by an evidence-destruction risk foreseeably produced by their deliberate decision to alert or flush occupants rather than pursue an available warrant. Genuine exigencies not manufactured through that deliberate sequence remain governed by ordinary rules.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Fields, 113 F.3d 313, decided February 9, 1998, Police-created evidence-destruction exigency; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Fields, 113 F.3d 313, decided February 9, 1998, Police-created evidence-destruction exigency; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Fields, 113 F.3d 313, February 9, 1998.

### Intentional means required for a seizure

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs intentional means and the Fourth Amendment.

**Current rule:** A seizure requires termination or restraint of movement through means intentionally applied to stop or control the person. An unintended collision with a pursuing police vehicle is not a seizure when the vehicle was not used as the intentional means of control.

**First controlling authority:** County of Sacramento v. Lewis, 523 U.S. 833 (1998), decided May 26, 1998, Intentional means and the Fourth Amendment; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** County of Sacramento v. Lewis, 523 U.S. 833 (1998), decided May 26, 1998, Intentional means and the Fourth Amendment; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** County of Sacramento v. Lewis, 523 U.S. 833 (1998), May 26, 1998.

### Exclusion in the searching agency’s parole-revocation proceeding

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs exclusion in the searching agency’s revocation proceeding.

**Current rule:** Evidence obtained through an unconstitutional search by parole officers for use in parole supervision and revocation must be excluded from the resulting revocation proceeding.

**First controlling authority:** Pennsylvania Board of Probation & Parole v. Scott, 524 U.S. 357 (1998), decided June 22, 1998, Exclusion in the searching agency’s revocation proceeding; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Pennsylvania Board of Probation & Parole v. Scott, 524 U.S. 357 (1998), decided June 22, 1998, Exclusion in the searching agency’s revocation proceeding; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Pennsylvania Board of Probation & Parole v. Scott, 524 U.S. 357 (1998), June 22, 1998.

### Staleness and particularity for durable digital evidence

**Governing question:** How staleness and particularity apply to warrants for durable downloaded files and computer media.

**Current rule:** Staleness depends on the relationship among the offense, the property sought, and the supported likelihood of continued possession. A substantial interval does not defeat probable cause for durable downloaded files when the affidavit establishes acquisition, durable storage, and a supported likelihood of retention. A warrant may authorize seizure and examination of identified computer equipment and storage media when probable cause extends to the system and the warrant confines examination to evidence and records connected to the specified offense.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Lacy, 119 F.3d 742, decided May 28, 1998, staleness and particularity for durable digital evidence; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Lacy, 119 F.3d 742, decided May 28, 1998, staleness and particularity for durable digital evidence; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Lacy, 119 F.3d 742, May 28, 1998.

### Privacy of short-term invited occupants

**Governing question:** When a short-term invited occupant may invoke the Fourth Amendment in a residence.

**Current rule:** A short-term invited occupant admitted by the resident to use a substantially screened residential interior for private activity possesses a personal Fourth Amendment privacy interest in that interior even when the visit is commercial and brief. Overnight residence is sufficient but not necessary when the host has admitted the person to share the privacy of the screened home interior.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not protect a person lacking permission, a person who does not share the interior, or an area held open to general observation. No controlling rule governs deliberate observation through a narrow aperture from a lawful public location.

**First controlling authority:** Minnesota v. Carter, 525 U.S. 83 (1998), decided December 1, 1998, personal privacy of short-term invited occupants; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Minnesota v. Carter, 525 U.S. 83 (1998), decided December 1, 1998, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Minnesota v. Carter, 525 U.S. 83 (1998), December 1, 1998.

### Citation-only stops and search incident to arrest

**Governing question:** Whether issuance of a traffic citation without custodial arrest permits a full search incident to arrest.

**Current rule:** Issuance of a traffic citation, without a custodial arrest, does not authorize a full search of the driver or vehicle as a search incident to arrest.

**Exceptions and limits:** Consent, probable cause, particularized protective grounds, and other recognized search authority remain available. Officers may take fitted protective measures upon reasonable suspicion of danger.

**First controlling authority:** Knowles v. Iowa, 525 U.S. 113 (1998), decided December 8, 1998, full search following a citation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Knowles v. Iowa, 525 U.S. 113 (1998), decided December 8, 1998, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Knowles v. Iowa, 525 U.S. 113 (1998), December 8, 1998.

### Scope of automobile probable-cause searches

**Governing question:** Which containers may be searched when officers have probable cause to search a vehicle for a particular object.

**Current rule:** The automobile exception permits inspection of every container found in the vehicle that is capable of concealing the object of the probable cause, including a passenger-owned container. A passenger’s independent privacy interest in a closed container does not prevent application of the exception.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not authorize a search of the passenger’s person. The object of the probable cause and the places capable of concealing it define the permissible scope.

**First controlling authority:** Wyoming v. Houghton, 526 U.S. 295 (1999), decided April 5, 1999, scope of vehicle probable cause as to passenger-owned containers; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Wyoming v. Houghton, 526 U.S. 295 (1999), decided April 5, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Wyoming v. Houghton, 526 U.S. 295 (1999), April 5, 1999.

### Garbage at the boundary of protected residential space

**Governing question:** When police may inspect closed garbage containers intended for collection without a warrant.

**Current rule:** The no-privacy rule for refuse exposed for collection does not categorically extend to a closed garbage container located within, or at a disputed boundary of, the home’s curtilage. A court must first determine whether police entered or exploited constitutionally protected residential space to obtain access. Future intent to surrender garbage to an authorized collector and limited practical access by private persons do not alone eliminate protection when police use curtilage or another protected residential area.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** Determine the container’s relationship to curtilage or other protected residential space before applying the exposed-for-collection rule. Curtilage remains governed by proximity, enclosure, use, and protection from observation or access.

**Exceptions and limits:** No warrant is required when police use no protected space and the refuse is genuinely exposed for collection outside that space.

**First controlling authority:** Redmon v. United States, 138 F.3d 1109, decided May 10, 1999, reach of the exposed-garbage rule at the home’s boundary; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Redmon v. United States, 138 F.3d 1109, decided May 10, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Redmon v. United States, 138 F.3d 1109, May 10, 1999.

### Warrantless public-place seizure of a forfeitable automobile

**Governing question:** Whether police need a warrant to seize an automobile from a public place when the vehicle itself is forfeitable.

**Current rule:** Police may seize an automobile from a public place without a warrant when they have probable cause to believe that the vehicle itself is forfeitable contraband under governing law.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not govern an immovable home, entry into protected private premises, or seizure without probable cause establishing the Government’s lawful claim to the vehicle itself.

**First controlling authority:** Florida v. White, 526 U.S. 559 (1999), decided May 17, 1999, warrantless seizure of a forfeitable automobile; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Florida v. White, 526 U.S. 559 (1999), decided May 17, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Florida v. White, 526 U.S. 559 (1999), May 17, 1999.

### Unrelated private observers during residential warrant execution

**Governing question:** Whether officers executing a warrant may bring private observers into a home.

**Current rule:** Officers violate the Fourth Amendment when they bring a private observer into a home during warrant execution and the observer serves no function reasonably related to lawful execution. Conduct inside the home must remain connected to an execution need authorized by the warrant and objective reasonableness.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not bar persons whose presence is reasonably related to identification, safety, technical assistance, custody, or another lawful execution function.

**First controlling authority:** Wilson v. Layne, 526 U.S. 603 (1999), decided May 24, 1999, unrelated media presence during home entry; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Wilson v. Layne, 526 U.S. 603 (1999), decided May 24, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Wilson v. Layne, 526 U.S. 603 (1999), May 24, 1999.

### Emergency entry and crime-scene searches

**Governing question:** What search authority remains after officers lawfully enter a suspected crime scene for emergency or protective purposes.

**Current rule:** The Fourth Amendment recognizes no general crime-scene or murder-scene exception. Officers may enter to provide immediate aid, search promptly for victims or a dangerous person, secure the premises, preserve evidence against imminent loss, and seek a warrant. Once emergency and protective purposes have ended, an extended evidentiary search and the opening of a closed container require a warrant or an independently recognized exception.

**Exceptions and limits:** Consent, another recognized warrant exception, and harmless error remain available when properly preserved; the seriousness of the suspected crime alone supplies no evidentiary-search authority.

**First controlling authority:** Mincey v. Arizona, 437 U.S. 385 (1978), decided June 21, 1978, absence of a general murder-scene exception; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Mincey v. Arizona, 437 U.S. 385 (1978), decided June 21, 1978, supplies the no-murder-scene-exception rule and the emergency-purpose boundary. Flippo v. West Virginia, 528 U.S. 11 (1999), decided October 18, 1999, precedential summary disposition, applies that rule to a prolonged secured-scene evidentiary search and the opening of a closed container.

**Present operation effective:** Mincey v. Arizona, 437 U.S. 385 (1978), June 21, 1978.

**Current wording authority:** Flippo v. West Virginia, 528 U.S. 11 (1999), October 18, 1999, supplies the present expression without changing the operation established by Mincey.

### Reliability of anonymous tips for investigatory stops

**Governing question:** When an anonymous accusation supplies reasonable suspicion for an investigatory stop.

**Current rule:** An anonymous accusation supports a *Terry* stop only when police possess corroborated circumstances indicating the informant’s reliability or a reliable basis for the asserted illegality. Corroboration of readily observable identity and location shows only that the informant can identify the person and is insufficient by itself. There is no categorical firearm exception to the reliability requirement.

**Exceptions and limits:** Predictive information corroborated by police, a traceable source, an explained firsthand basis, independently observed threatening conduct, an emergency, or comparable circumstances may supply the required reliability. The rule does not prescribe the response to a report of an immediate emergency presenting materially different danger and reliability considerations.

**First controlling authority:** *Florida v. J. L.*, 529 U.S. 266, decided March 28, 2000, anonymous accusation of firearm possession; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Florida v. J. L.*, 529 U.S. 266, decided March 28, 2000, supplies the reliability requirement, the identity-and-location limit, and rejection of a categorical firearm exception.

**Present operation effective:** *Florida v. J. L.*, March 28, 2000.

### Physical attachment to an effect for information gathering

**Governing question:** Whether deliberate governmental attachment of a tracking device to a person’s vehicle or other protected effect for the purpose of obtaining information is a search or seizure.

**Current rule:** Government conducts a search when it deliberately physically attaches a tracking device to a person’s protected effect for the purpose of obtaining information. The attachment constitutes a seizure only if it meaningfully interferes with the person’s possession, control, or use of the effect.

**Exceptions and limits:** Lawful visual observation of an exposed surface does not authorize physical occupation for information gathering. The rule does not decide consensual installation before transfer, establish that attachment without meaningful interference is a seizure, or make short-term monitoring of movements exposed on public roads independently a search.

**First controlling authority:** *United States v. McIver*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 13, 2000, physical attachment of an electronic tracker to a vehicle for information gathering; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *United States v. McIver*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 13, 2000, by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg for the search component and by all nine participating Justices for the seizure component, supplies the physical-attachment search rule and meaningful-interference seizure boundary.

**Present operation effective:** *United States v. McIver*, June 13, 2000.

## Federalism and State Sovereign Immunity

### Eleventh Amendment status of a bistate compact entity

**Governing question:** When a bistate compact entity qualifies as an arm of a State for Eleventh Amendment immunity.

**Current rule:** Statutory characterization is relevant but not controlling. Arm-of-State status turns on legal and fiscal reality, including responsibility for judgments, operational independence, ownership and control of revenues, responsibility for debts, the degree of state control, and the legal identity of the obligation being enforced. A bistate entity created under the Compact Clause is not presumed to share the compacting States’ immunity unless the compact and implementing law supply good reason to conclude that the States structured it as their own alter ego and Congress concurred.

**Exceptions and limits:** State-treasury responsibility is highly probative but not categorically dispositive. Commercial activity alone neither establishes nor defeats arm-of-State status. A materially different compact that makes the entity a direct state alter ego, subjects state funds to its judgments, or identifies its obligations as state obligations may produce a different result.

**First controlling authority:** Hess v. Port Authority Trans-Hudson Corp., 513 U.S. 30, decided November 14, 1994, Eleventh Amendment status of a bistate compact entity; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Hess v. Port Authority Trans-Hudson Corp., 513 U.S. 30, decided November 14, 1994, Eleventh Amendment status of a bistate compact entity; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Hess v. Port Authority Trans-Hudson Corp., 513 U.S. 30, November 14, 1994.

**Related current rules:** Commercial state enterprises in interstate markets. Arm-of-State status asks whether the entity is legally the State; the separate commercial-enterprise rule determines the remedies applicable to qualifying state enterprises operating in an interstate market.

### Article I duties, remedial autonomy, and federal fallback

**Governing question:** What obligations and private remedies Congress may impose on a nonconsenting State under Article I, and when state remedial autonomy yields to a federal fallback.

**Current rule:** A valid exercise of Article I may impose substantive federal obligations on States. Congress may unmistakably authorize prospective enforcement directly against a State when it acts within an enumerated Article I power, identifies the State as defendant, creates the cause of action, and supplies a constitutionally permissible remedy. For a private retrospective compensatory monetary claim, a nonconsenting State may channel enforcement through an adequate state remedial system but may not extinguish the federal entitlement. When the State supplies no constitutionally and practically adequate substitute and Congress has clearly authorized the monetary remedy, the federal remedial action may proceed.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** The court first identifies a valid Article I duty and the remedy Congress clearly enacted. Prospective direct-State enforcement may proceed within the enacted authorization. For retrospective compensatory relief, the court determines whether the State supplies an adequate remedial forum and remedy. An adequate system controls the remedial channel; the clearly authorized federal action remains available when no adequate substitute exists.

**Exceptions and limits:** Article I does not itself create a cause of action, and statutory clarity remains indispensable. Valid Reconstruction Amendment enforcement, unequivocal state consent, federal public enforcement, lawful prospective officer relief, and commercial-enterprise liability remain independent routes. Seminole Tribe did not decide the treatment of retrospective compensatory relief because IGRA required no treasury award for accrued liability.

**First controlling authority:** Seminole Tribe of Florida v. Florida, 517 U.S. 44, decided March 27, 1996, expressly authorized prospective Article I action under IGRA; established the prospective component of the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Seminole Tribe of Florida v. Florida, 517 U.S. 44, decided March 27, 1996, supplies the Article I source, statutory-clarity, and prospective direct-State requirements. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board v. College Savings Bank, 527 U.S. 627 (1999), decided June 23, 1999, supplies remedial autonomy and federal fallback for a clearly enacted Patent Clause obligation. College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board, 527 U.S. 666 (1999), decided June 23, 1999, applies the same remedial structure to a clearly enacted Lanham Act obligation and supplies the separate commercial-enterprise rule. Alden v. Maine, 527 U.S. 706 (1999), decided June 23, 1999, supplies the general Article I remedial-autonomy rule and its state-court operation.

**Present operation effective:** Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board v. College Savings Bank, 527 U.S. 627 (1999); College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board, 527 U.S. 666 (1999); and Alden v. Maine, 527 U.S. 706 (1999), June 23, 1999.

**Related current rules:** Adequate state remedial forums for federal monetary rights; State-court nondiscrimination and federal remedial channeling; Commercial state enterprises in interstate markets; Prospective officer relief for ongoing federal-law violations. The first two rules define an adequate state channel and the obligation of a competent state court; the latter two supply independent remedial routes.

**Development:**
- March 27, 1996, Seminole Tribe of Florida v. Florida, 517 U.S. 44: Union Gas was followed insofar as it permits Congress, acting under an enumerated Article I power and with unmistakable statutory language, to make a State the defendant in a prospective statutory action. The treatment of retrospective compensatory relief remained open because IGRA supplied no accrued treasury award.
- June 23, 1999, Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board v. College Savings Bank, 527 U.S. 627; College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board, 527 U.S. 666; and Alden v. Maine, 527 U.S. 706: Each decision was measured against the same controlling-law baseline in force at the start of the day. Together they establish that States may channel clearly imposed Article I monetary duties through adequate state remedies but may not extinguish the federal entitlement, and that the clearly authorized federal remedy remains available when no adequate substitute exists. The same-day decisions preserve the prospective direct-State route and identify commercial-enterprise liability as an independent rule.

### Waiver of state sovereign immunity

**Governing question:** What constitutes a State’s waiver of sovereign immunity, including whether regulated commercial activity or acceptance of federal program funds supplies consent.

**Current rule:** Waiver requires an unequivocal and voluntary manifestation of the State’s own consent. A State does not waive immunity merely by engaging in interstate commercial activity after Congress announces that private suit will follow; regulatory coverage and Congress’s stated consequence do not substitute for the State’s voluntary legal act. A State does consent to program-specific federal enforcement when, with clear advance notice, it knowingly accepts federal funds subject to a separately valid condition unmistakably identifying private federal suit and the program duties and remedies to be enforced.

**Exceptions and limits:** Express consent and voluntary invocation of federal jurisdiction remain distinct waiver routes. A funding condition must be prospective, clearly stated before acceptance, related to the funded program, and leave a genuinely voluntary choice; acceptance binds the State only within the identified program and enforcement condition. Commercial activity may determine regulatory coverage and substantive liability without fictionally constituting consent.

**First controlling authority:** *College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board*, 527 U.S. 666 (1999), decided June 23, 1999, constructive waiver through commercial activity; established the voluntary-act requirement carried in this Register.

**Controlling authorities:** *College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board*, 527 U.S. 666 (1999), decided June 23, 1999, rejects constructive waiver from regulated market participation and preserves acceptance of a separately valid, clearly stated funding condition as a distinct consent route. *Bradley v. Arkansas Department of Education*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 29, 2000, supplies knowing acceptance of a prospective, program-related, clearly stated, noncoercive federal-suit condition as program-specific consent.

**Present operation effective:** *Bradley v. Arkansas Department of Education*, June 29, 2000.

**Related current rules:** `Commercial state enterprises in interstate markets`, under Federalism and State Sovereign Immunity, rests on market operation and fiscal structure rather than constructive consent. `Article I duties, remedial autonomy, and federal fallback`, under Federalism and State Sovereign Immunity, governs direct congressional remedial authority rather than a State’s voluntary acceptance of a funding condition.

**Development:**
- June 29, 2000, *Bradley v. Arkansas Department of Education*: Before *Bradley*, the entry rejected constructive waiver through commerce while preserving a separately valid and clearly stated funding condition as a possible consent route. After *Bradley*, knowing acceptance of a prospective, program-related, unmistakable, and genuinely voluntary federal-suit condition constitutes program-specific consent. The rejection of constructive commercial waiver remains unchanged.

### Prospective officer relief for ongoing federal-law violations

**Governing question:** When a federal court may grant prospective declaratory or injunctive relief against a responsible state officer to end an ongoing violation of federal law.

**Current rule:** A responsible state officer may be subjected to properly confined prospective declaratory or injunctive relief to end an ongoing violation of controlling federal law. The complaint must identify an officer with a genuine enforcement connection and seek relief directed to future conduct. The character or importance of the State’s underlying governmental, regulatory, or property interest does not itself defeat the action, even when the court must resolve a disputed title question to determine whether future official conduct is unlawful.

**Exceptions and limits:** The officer action may not award damages, retrospective compensation, an accounting payable by the State, execution against state property, or a decree requiring the State itself to convey title.

**First controlling authority:** Idaho v. Coeur d’Alene Tribe of Idaho, 521 U.S. 261 (1997), decided June 23, 1997, prospective officer relief for an ongoing federal-law violation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Idaho v. Coeur d’Alene Tribe of Idaho, 521 U.S. 261 (1997), decided June 23, 1997, supplies the general prospective officer-action rule and its limits as applied to disputed tribal property rights.

**Present operation effective:** Idaho v. Coeur d’Alene Tribe of Idaho, 521 U.S. 261 (1997), June 23, 1997.

**Related current rules:** Article I duties, remedial autonomy, and federal fallback. Prospective officer relief remains an independent enforcement route and does not depend on the monetary-remedial analysis governing a direct action against the State.

### Anti-commandeering of state executive officers

**Governing question:** When federal law impermissibly requires state or local executive officers to administer a federal regulatory program and when it instead directly regulates state activity.

**Current rule:** Outside a constitutionally assigned source-specific exception, Congress may not command state or local executive officers, as officers of their States, to administer or enforce a federal regulatory program. Congress may directly regulate a State’s own activity within an enumerated power, including the State’s conduct as an owner or supplier in an interstate market; administrative steps necessary for the State to comply with that direct rule do not convert regulation into commandeering. Congress may also regulate private actors, preempt state law, create federal offices, enact generally applicable rules reaching state employees as persons, contract with willing States, obtain voluntary assistance, and attach lawful funding conditions. It may not transfer federal administration or enforcement against third parties to unwilling state executives by direct command.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not affect state-court obligations under the Supremacy Clause, valid preemption, voluntary agreements, information sharing, lawful spending conditions, or duties expressly authorized by another constitutional provision. Direct regulation still requires an enumerated-power foundation and does not authorize Congress to command state legislation or conscript state officers to enforce federal law against others.

**Open question:** The Court does not decide when a financial condition becomes so coercive that purported state participation ceases to be genuinely voluntary.

**First controlling authority:** Printz v. United States; Mack v. United States, 521 U.S. 898 (1997), decided June 27, 1997, mandatory state-executive administration; established the anti-commandeering component.

**Controlling authorities:** Printz v. United States; Mack v. United States, 521 U.S. 898 (1997), decided June 27, 1997, supplies the prohibition on compelled state administration and enforcement. Reno v. Condon, 528 U.S. 141 (2000), decided January 12, 2000, supplies the distinction between commandeering and direct federal regulation of a State’s own activity as an owner or supplier of information in interstate commerce.

**Present operation effective:** Reno v. Condon, 528 U.S. 141 (2000), January 12, 2000.

**Development:**
- January 12, 2000, Reno v. Condon, 528 U.S. 141: *Printz* previously supplied the prohibition on compelled state administration of federal law. The rule now expressly distinguishes a valid federal command regulating the State’s own market activity from a command to legislate or enforce federal law against others; compliance administration incidental to direct regulation is not commandeering.

### Commercial state enterprises in interstate markets

**Governing question:** When Congress may subject a state-owned enterprise operating in an interstate commercial market to the ordinary compensatory remedies governing that market.

**Current rule:** When Congress clearly regulates an interstate commercial market, it may subject a state-owned enterprise to the ordinary compensatory remedies governing that market if the enterprise voluntarily engages in commercial sales or services in competition with private actors and possesses sufficient operational and fiscal separation that liability runs primarily against enterprise revenues or assets rather than functioning as an ordinary charge on the State’s general governmental treasury.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** The court determines the enterprise’s legal and fiscal structure and the character of the challenged activity. Relevant considerations include separate legal identity; ownership and control of operating revenues; responsibility for debts and judgments; commercial sales, services, or licensing; competition with private firms; authority to sue, contract, and hold assets; the source from which a judgment will be paid; the degree of direct state control; and whether the challenged conduct is commercial rather than an exercise of regulatory or coercive sovereign authority.

**Exceptions and limits:** Market participation does not constitute waiver. When the entity lacks the required operational and fiscal separation or the remedy would function as an ordinary charge on the general treasury, the ordinary remedial-autonomy rule governs. The rule does not convert a State’s regulatory command into voluntary market participation.

**First controlling authority:** College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board, 527 U.S. 666 (1999), decided June 23, 1999, compensatory liability of commercial state enterprises; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board, 527 U.S. 666 (1999), decided June 23, 1999, supplies the current rule and its distinction from constructive waiver.

**Present operation effective:** College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board, 527 U.S. 666 (1999), June 23, 1999.

**Related current rules:** Eleventh Amendment status of a bistate compact entity; Waiver of state sovereign immunity; Routing within a municipally purchased service, under Commerce Clause. Arm-of-State status remains a distinct entity question, while the market-participant rule distinguishes voluntary commercial transactions from coercive governmental regulation.

### Adequate state remedial forums for federal monetary rights

**Governing question:** When a state remedial system is adequate to channel a private claim seeking retrospective compensation for a federal monetary right.

**Current rule:** A state remedial system is adequate when it provides a neutral adjudicator, reasonable access, a reasonably timely determination, authority to award substantially the full federal compensatory entitlement, interest and fee treatment sufficient to prevent practical destruction of the claim when those mechanisms are necessary to enforcement, and an enforceable payment obligation. The forum and remedy may not discriminate against the claim because federal law supplies the right.

**Exceptions and limits:** A State may use ordinary limitations periods, reasonable notice rules, claims commissions, administrative tribunals, specialized state courts, and other neutral procedural requirements. The system is not adequate if it materially caps the federal entitlement, leaves payment to unreviewable legislative discretion, imposes a practically impossible deadline, assigns adjudication to a tribunal controlled by the defendant agency, or withholds a remedy because the right is federal.

**First controlling authority:** Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board v. College Savings Bank, 527 U.S. 627 (1999); College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board, 527 U.S. 666 (1999); and Alden v. Maine, 527 U.S. 706 (1999), all decided June 23, 1999, adequate state remedial substitutes for federal monetary rights; jointly established the current rule against the common start-of-day baseline.

**Controlling authorities:** Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board v. College Savings Bank, 527 U.S. 627 (1999), and College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board, 527 U.S. 666 (1999), both decided June 23, 1999, supply the adequacy requirement for state compensation procedures and the federal fallback when no adequate procedure exists. Alden v. Maine, 527 U.S. 706 (1999), decided June 23, 1999, supplies the general minimum features of an adequate state remedial system.

**Present operation effective:** Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board v. College Savings Bank, 527 U.S. 627 (1999); College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board, 527 U.S. 666 (1999); and Alden v. Maine, 527 U.S. 706 (1999), June 23, 1999.

**Related current rules:** Article I duties, remedial autonomy, and federal fallback; State-court nondiscrimination and federal remedial channeling. Adequacy determines whether the State’s chosen channel controls or the clearly authorized federal remedy remains available.

### State-court nondiscrimination and federal remedial channeling

**Governing question:** When a State may channel a federal monetary claim and when a state court must hear the federal action.

**Current rule:** A nonconsenting State may channel a private retrospective monetary claim into an adequate state remedial system. If Congress unmistakably creates the private compensatory remedy and the State supplies no adequate substitute, a state court possessing ordinary jurisdiction over comparable claims must hear the federal action. State courts may apply neutral procedural rules but may not close an otherwise competent forum or withhold an effective remedy merely because federal law supplies the right.

**Exceptions and limits:** A State need not create a court of general jurisdiction or disregard neutral rules governing comparable claims. A genuinely adequate specialized forum or administrative remedy may control the channel.

**First controlling authority:** Alden v. Maine, 527 U.S. 706 (1999), decided June 23, 1999, state-court enforcement and remedial channeling of Article I monetary rights; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Alden v. Maine, 527 U.S. 706 (1999), decided June 23, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Alden v. Maine, 527 U.S. 706 (1999), June 23, 1999.

**Related current rules:** Article I duties, remedial autonomy, and federal fallback; Adequate state remedial forums for federal monetary rights; Removal when one claim encounters state sovereign immunity, under Article III and Federal Courts. The first two rules define the enforceable federal entitlement and an adequate substitute; the removal rule preserves claim-specific adjudication without treating immunity as destruction of jurisdiction over the action.

### In rem jurisdiction over a wreck outside state possession

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs in rem jurisdiction over an unpossessed wreck.

**Current rule:** The Eleventh Amendment does not bar federal in rem admiralty jurisdiction over property outside a State’s possession merely because the State asserts title. Abandonment, application of the Abandoned Shipwreck Act, ownership, and salvage remain separate merits questions.

**First controlling authority:** California v. Deep Sea Research, Inc., 523 U.S. 491 (1998), decided April 22, 1998, In rem jurisdiction over an unpossessed wreck; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** California v. Deep Sea Research, Inc., 523 U.S. 491 (1998), decided April 22, 1998, In rem jurisdiction over an unpossessed wreck; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** California v. Deep Sea Research, Inc., 523 U.S. 491 (1998), April 22, 1998.

## Federalism and Preemption

### State consumer-fraud regulation

**Governing question:** When the Airline Deregulation Act preempts application of a state consumer-fraud law to airline rates, routes, or services.

**Current rule:** The ADA preempts application of the Illinois Consumer Fraud Act to frequent-flyer restrictions directly related to airline rates and services. A State may not impose prescriptive standards governing airline rates, routes, or services through consumer-protection law. A generally applicable statute is preempted when its particular application directs or polices the carrier’s primary economic conduct in those fields.

**First controlling authority:** American Airlines, Inc. v. Wolens, 513 U.S. 219, decided January 18, 1995, State consumer-fraud regulation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** American Airlines, Inc. v. Wolens, 513 U.S. 219, decided January 18, 1995, State consumer-fraud regulation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** American Airlines, Inc. v. Wolens, 513 U.S. 219, January 18, 1995.

### Enforcement of self-imposed contractual obligations

**Governing question:** When the Airline Deregulation Act permits state-law enforcement of an airline’s self-imposed contractual obligations.

**Current rule:** The ADA does not preempt routine enforcement of an airline’s own contractual commitments. A court may determine and enforce the parties’ agreement through ordinary contract law and ordinary contract remedies. It may not enlarge, reform, or supplement the undertaking through state policies external to the bargain.

**Exceptions and limits:** Good faith, implied terms, and related doctrines may be used to identify and enforce the actual agreement, but not to recreate consumer regulation under a contract label.

**First controlling authority:** American Airlines, Inc. v. Wolens, 513 U.S. 219, decided January 18, 1995, Enforcement of self-imposed contractual obligations; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** American Airlines, Inc. v. Wolens, 513 U.S. 219, decided January 18, 1995, Enforcement of self-imposed contractual obligations; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** American Airlines, Inc. v. Wolens, 513 U.S. 219, January 18, 1995.

### Express preemption without a federal standard in force

**Governing question:** Whether an express preemption clause displaces state duties when no federal standard addressing the product or conduct is in force.

**Current rule:** The Safety Act’s express preemption clause does not displace state requirements concerning a vehicle-safety feature when no federal standard governing the same aspect of performance is in effect.

**First controlling authority:** Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick, 514 U.S. 280, decided April 18, 1995, Express preemption without a federal standard in force; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick, 514 U.S. 280, decided April 18, 1995, Express preemption without a federal standard in force; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick, 514 U.S. 280, April 18, 1995.

### Implied conflict preemption

**Governing question:** When state law is impliedly preempted because its legal operation conflicts with an operative federal requirement or a concrete federal objective.

**Current rule:** State law is impliedly preempted when simultaneous compliance with state and federal law is impossible or when the state duty materially obstructs a concrete objective embedded in an enacted federal statute, an operative regulation, or an affirmative and lawfully adopted federal decision. The court must identify the federal objective from the legal regime itself and compare the state duty’s operation with the method federal law selected. Conflict exists when state law would eliminate a range of options federal law deliberately preserved, expand sanctions beyond Congress’s chosen coverage, remove flexibility Congress assigned to a federal decisionmaker, or materially obstruct the federal method for carrying out the enacted policy. Regulatory absence, generalized federal preference, or the mere possibility that state law may influence regulated conduct is insufficient.

**Exceptions and limits:** A savings clause does not preserve a state duty that actually conflicts with the governing federal regime, but it confirms that compatible state duties remain available. Foreign-policy context does not create a free-standing judicial foreign-affairs preemption power; displacement must rest on a concrete conflict with enacted federal law or lawfully operative federal action. A different state duty survives when compliance with both systems is possible and the state rule does not frustrate the identified federal method.

**First controlling authority:** *Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick*, 514 U.S. 280, decided April 18, 1995, implied conflict preemption; established the requirement of an operative federal conflict rather than preemption from regulatory absence.

**Controlling authorities:** *Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick*, 514 U.S. 280, decided April 18, 1995, supplies the impossibility, operative-federal-policy, and regulatory-absence components. *Geier v. American Honda Motor Co.*, 529 U.S. 861 (2000), decided May 22, 2000, supplies obstruction of a deliberately preserved range of regulatory options and the savings-clause boundary. *Crosby v. National Foreign Trade Council*, 530 U.S. 363 (2000), decided June 19, 2000, supplies conflict through expanded state coverage, removal of federally assigned executive flexibility, obstruction of the enacted federal method, and the no-free-standing-foreign-affairs limit.

**Present operation effective:** *Crosby v. National Foreign Trade Council*, 530 U.S. 363 (2000), June 19, 2000.

**Development:**
- May 22, 2000, *Geier v. American Honda Motor Co.*: Before *Geier*, the entry rejected conflict preemption based on regulatory absence or an inadequately demonstrated federal preference. After *Geier*, a state duty also conflicts when it would eliminate a range of options deliberately preserved as the operative means of achieving a concrete regulatory objective; compatible state duties and the requirement of an affirmative federal objective remain unchanged.
- June 19, 2000, *Crosby v. National Foreign Trade Council*: Before *Crosby*, the entry addressed impossibility and obstruction of regulatory objectives. After *Crosby*, it expressly includes state expansion of federally chosen sanctions, removal of executive flexibility, and obstruction of an enacted implementation strategy, while rejecting a free-standing foreign-affairs basis for preemption.

### Common-law requirements and parallel duties

**Governing question:** When common-law product duties are preempted as requirements and when equivalent parallel duties survive.

**Current rule:** The remedial form of a state duty does not determine preemption. The court must compare the conduct required by state law with the binding federal counterpart. A genuinely equivalent state duty is not different from or additional to federal law merely because state law supplies damages.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not create a private federal cause of action, decide tort liability, or determine preemption for a device subjected to full premarket approval or another specific federal condition.

**First controlling authority:** Medtronic, Inc. v. Lohr; Lohr v. Medtronic, Inc., 518 U.S. 470, decided June 26, 1996, Common-law requirements and parallel duties; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Medtronic, Inc. v. Lohr; Lohr v. Medtronic, Inc., 518 U.S. 470, decided June 26, 1996, Common-law requirements and parallel duties; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Medtronic, Inc. v. Lohr; Lohr v. Medtronic, Inc., 518 U.S. 470, June 26, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 26, 1996, Medtronic, Inc. v. Lohr; Lohr v. Medtronic, Inc., 518 U.S. 470: *Myrick* is clarified for an express clause keyed to requirements applicable to a product. The decision adds claim-specific comparison, confirms that common-law duties may count as requirements, and establishes that equivalent parallel duties survive. *Myrick*’s rejection of preemption by regulatory absence or generalized federal policy remains unchanged.

### General manufacturing and labeling rules

**Governing question:** How courts determine whether general federal manufacturing or labeling rules preempt a particular state-law claim.

**Current rule:** Express preemption requires a binding federal requirement applicable to the particular device or the relevant aspect of its performance, a state requirement concerning the same subject, and a material difference or addition between them. Regulatory generality, market permission, or agency inaction does not by itself establish that federal law selected an exclusive device standard.

**First controlling authority:** Medtronic, Inc. v. Lohr; Lohr v. Medtronic, Inc., 518 U.S. 470, decided June 26, 1996, General manufacturing and labeling rules; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Medtronic, Inc. v. Lohr; Lohr v. Medtronic, Inc., 518 U.S. 470, decided June 26, 1996, General manufacturing and labeling rules; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Medtronic, Inc. v. Lohr; Lohr v. Medtronic, Inc., 518 U.S. 470, June 26, 1996.

### McCarran-Ferguson impairment of state insurance law

**Governing question:** When a federal statute invalidates, impairs, or supersedes state insurance law under McCarran-Ferguson Act § 2(b).

**Current rule:** Impairment requires injurious interference with the operation or policy of state insurance law, not merely additional federal liability. A federal claim does not impair state insurance regulation when it targets conduct the State also forbids, leaves the state scheme fully operative, and adds compatible remedies rather than frustrating an exclusive state mechanism.

**Exceptions and limits:** A federal rule that defeats a state prohibition, displaces an exclusive remedial structure, or otherwise frustrates operation of the state insurance regime remains subject to McCarran-Ferguson’s enacted protection.

**First controlling authority:** Humana Inc. v. Forsyth, 525 U.S. 299 (1999), decided January 20, 1999, McCarran-Ferguson reverse preemption; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Humana Inc. v. Forsyth, 525 U.S. 299 (1999), decided January 20, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Humana Inc. v. Forsyth, 525 U.S. 299 (1999), January 20, 1999.

### Warsaw Convention exclusivity within Article 17’s field

**Governing question:** Whether local law may supply passenger-injury recovery when an occurrence falls within Article 17’s field but does not satisfy its liability conditions.

**Current rule:** When a passenger-injury occurrence falls within Article 17’s temporal and substantive field, Article 24 permits an action, however founded, only subject to the Warsaw Convention’s conditions and limits. Local law may measure an established Convention remedy when the Convention permits, but may not create liability solely because the claimant fails a Convention condition.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not govern an occurrence outside Article 17’s temporal or substantive field. Purely psychic injury without qualifying bodily injury does not satisfy Article 17 as then construed.

**First controlling authority:** El Al Israel Airlines, Ltd. v. Tsui Yuan Tseng, 525 U.S. 155 (1999), decided January 12, 1999, exclusivity within Article 17’s field; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** El Al Israel Airlines, Ltd. v. Tsui Yuan Tseng, 525 U.S. 155 (1999), decided January 12, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** El Al Israel Airlines, Ltd. v. Tsui Yuan Tseng, 525 U.S. 155 (1999), January 12, 1999.

## Commerce Clause

### Origin-based charges and compensatory-burden defense

**Governing question:** When a state charge triggered by out-of-state origin survives dormant Commerce Clause review as compensatory.

**Current rule:** A charge triggered expressly by interstate origin is presumptively invalid. A compensatory defense requires identification of an actual intrastate tax or burden imposed on a substantially equivalent in-state event for the same governmental purpose; generalized estimates of additional public costs do not substitute for that corresponding burden.

**Burdens and proof:** The State bears the demanding burden of justifying the facial discrimination and establishing the substantially equivalent intrastate burden.

**Exceptions and limits:** The State may impose uniform charges, regulate genuine health or environmental risks evenhandedly, and recover demonstrable costs through nondiscriminatory measures.

**First controlling authority:** Oregon Waste Systems, Inc. v. Department of Environmental Quality, 511 U.S. 93 (1994), decided April 4, 1994, Origin-based waste surcharge; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Oregon Waste Systems, Inc. v. Department of Environmental Quality, 511 U.S. 93 (1994), decided April 4, 1994, Origin-based waste surcharge; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Oregon Waste Systems, Inc. v. Department of Environmental Quality, 511 U.S. 93 (1994), April 4, 1994.

### Operational discrimination favoring a local private enterprise

**Governing question:** When a formally local routing requirement discriminates against interstate commerce by reserving a market for a favored private enterprise.

**Current rule:** A law is discriminatory when its practical operation directs commerce to a favored local private enterprise, even if disfavored local firms are excluded alongside out-of-state firms. Such a law is invalid unless the government establishes a legitimate local objective that cannot adequately be served through reasonable nondiscriminatory alternatives.

**Burdens and proof:** The government bears the burden of showing both the legitimate local objective and the inadequacy of reasonable nondiscriminatory alternatives.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not condemn evenhanded health and safety regulation or decide a materially different arrangement involving a genuinely public facility and distinct governmental-market circumstances.

**First controlling authority:** C & A Carbone, Inc. v. Town of Clarkstown, 511 U.S. 383 (1994), decided May 16, 1994, Exclusive local processing requirement; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** C & A Carbone, Inc. v. Town of Clarkstown, 511 U.S. 383 (1994), decided May 16, 1994, Exclusive local processing requirement; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** C & A Carbone, Inc. v. Town of Clarkstown, 511 U.S. 383 (1994), May 16, 1994.

### Linked market assessment and local-producer subsidy

**Governing question:** When a facially evenhanded market assessment becomes discriminatory because of a formally linked local-only distribution.

**Current rule:** A State discriminates against interstate commerce when it links an otherwise evenhanded market assessment to distribution of the entire resulting fund exclusively to in-state producers competing in the assessed market, so that the integrated program shifts the economic burden away from local producers and onto their interstate competitors. Formally linked components are evaluated as one economic arrangement.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not invalidate an evenhanded tax merely because revenue enters the general treasury, prohibit local subsidies financed from general revenues, or constitutionalize ordinary public-spending choices.

**First controlling authority:** West Lynn Creamery, Inc. v. Healy, 512 U.S. 186 (1994), decided June 17, 1994, Combined assessment and in-state producer subsidy; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** West Lynn Creamery, Inc. v. Healy, 512 U.S. 186 (1994), decided June 17, 1994, Combined assessment and in-state producer subsidy; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** West Lynn Creamery, Inc. v. Healy, 512 U.S. 186 (1994), June 17, 1994.

### Sales tax on an interstate transportation ticket

**Governing question:** Whether a State may impose a nondiscriminatory sales tax on an interstate transportation ticket sold within the State.

**Current rule:** A conventional sales tax may be measured by the full price of an interstate transportation service when the taxable event is a discrete local sale, the tax is internally consistent, the State’s claim to the sale is externally consistent, interstate and intrastate transactions receive equal treatment, and the tax is fairly related to state-provided services. Apportionment is not required merely because performance of the purchased service later crosses state lines.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not authorize multiple taxation of the same sale, discriminatory origin-based taxes, or unapportioned taxes on receipts or income earned through multistate operations. A tax failing internal or external consistency remains invalid.

**First controlling authority:** Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Jefferson Lines, Inc., 514 U.S. 175, decided April 3, 1995, Sales tax on an interstate transportation ticket; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Jefferson Lines, Inc., 514 U.S. 175, decided April 3, 1995, Sales tax on an interstate transportation ticket; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Jefferson Lines, Inc., 514 U.S. 175, April 3, 1995.

### Interstate commerce, anti-evasion, and material interstate externalities

**Governing question:** Which interstate mechanisms permit Congress to regulate conduct under the Commerce Clause, including intrastate noneconomic conduct.

**Current rule:** Congress may regulate interstate transactions and the channels through which interstate commerce moves; instrumentalities, persons, and things in interstate commerce and direct threats to them; economic activity whose properly defined class substantially affects an actual interstate market; intrastate conduct whose inclusion is reasonably necessary to prevent evasion or material impairment of a valid federal interstate regulatory regime; and intrastate conduct that Congress, on a materially adequate evidentiary basis, reasonably identifies as causing a substantial interstate externality that individual States cannot reasonably internalize. Noneconomic character is relevant but not categorically dispositive. Generalized chains through social consequences, productivity, insurance, consumption, or similar downstream effects are insufficient without an identified interstate mechanism. When Congress relies on an offense-specific commerce element, that element must establish a genuine connection in the particular case; a nominal jurisdictional phrase or remote historical movement that performs no present constitutional work is insufficient.

**Burdens and proof:** Courts independently determine whether the asserted category falls within an enumerated commerce route. Congress’s predictive and empirical judgments receive reasonable respect when supported by an identified interstate mechanism and materially adequate evidence, but legislative findings do not create constitutional authority merely by reciting interstate commerce. The Government must genuinely prove any offense-specific commerce element on which federal authority depends.

**Exceptions and limits:** National importance alone is not an interstate mechanism. Generalized interests in biodiversity, scientific research, tourism, development, environmental quality, or possible future economic value do not establish a material interstate externality without evidence connecting the regulated class to a substantial spillover that individual States cannot reasonably internalize. An intrastate species or noneconomic activity is not automatically beyond federal authority when the anti-evasion, anti-impairment, or externality requirements are satisfied.

**Related current rules:** *Direct protection of an interstate transportation instrumentality*; *Commerce Clause authority over access-directed obstruction*; *Regulation of the post-1986 civilian machinegun market*; *Interstate computer-assisted procurement transactions*; *Interstate support-payment obligations*; *Local commercial robbery under the Hobbs Act*; *A RICO enterprise’s own interstate operations*, under Racketeer Influenced and Corrupt Organizations Act; *Habitat modification that actually kills or injures wildlife*, under Environmental Law. Each specialized entry applies one route of this unified commerce inquiry; the environmental rule separately governs the ESA’s statutory injury and causation requirements.

**First controlling authority:** *United States v. Lopez*, 514 U.S. 549, Supreme Court simulation decided April 26, 1995, interstate-commerce routes and the limit on generalized social-consequence reasoning; established the original rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *United States v. Lopez*, 514 U.S. 549, Supreme Court simulation decided April 26, 1995, supplies the interstate-transaction, channel, instrumentality, substantial-market-effect, anti-evasion, anti-impairment, and material-interstate-externality routes and rejects generalized downstream-consequence reasoning. *National Association of Home Builders v. Babbitt*, 130 F.3d 1041, Supreme Court simulation decided June 8, 1998, clarifies the evidentiary and mechanism requirements for noneconomic intrastate conduct. *Fraternal Order of Police v. United States*, 173 F.3d 898 (D.C. Cir. 1999), Supreme Court simulation decided March 13, 2000, supplies the requirement that an offense-specific commerce element perform genuine constitutional work.

**Present operation effective:** *Fraternal Order of Police v. United States*, March 13, 2000.

**Development:**
- June 8, 1998, *National Association of Home Builders v. Babbitt*: *Lopez* already made noneconomic character relevant but noncategorical and required an identified interstate mechanism. *Home Builders* clarified that an intrastate species neither establishes nor defeats federal authority and that further adjudication is required when the record has not resolved a qualifying anti-evasion, anti-impairment, or externality theory. Generalized national interest remained insufficient. Former titles: Federal regulation of noneconomic local possession; Federal regulation of noneconomic local conduct.
- March 13, 2000, *Fraternal Order of Police v. United States*: The prior unified rule recognized that a genuine case-specific commerce connection could support federal regulation but did not state the proof boundary for a jurisdictional element. *Fraternal Order of Police* adds that a nominal phrase or remote historical movement performing no present constitutional work is insufficient. The existing commerce routes and anti-evasion mechanics remain unchanged.

### Direct protection of an interstate transportation instrumentality

**Governing question:** Whether Congress may directly protect an interstate transportation instrumentality from local obstruction or damage.

**Current rule:** The Commerce Clause permits Congress to protect a motor vehicle against a direct intrastate forcible taking when the federal offense requires proof of the particular vehicle’s interstate connection. An offense-specific commerce element must perform real constitutional work rather than add a remote interstate fact to an ordinary local crime. A direct attack on possession and control of a transportation instrumentality, combined with proof that the particular vehicle moved through interstate commerce, falls within Congress’s authority over instrumentalities and things in commerce.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not hold that every local offense involving an object that once crossed a state line is federal, that every crime occurring in or near a car is subject to federal jurisdiction, or that generalized insurance and productivity effects are sufficient. The holding rests on the direct forcible seizure of the transportation instrumentality and the statute’s vehicle-specific interstate requirement.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Harris, 25 F.3d 1275, decided April 27, 1995, Direct protection of an interstate transportation instrumentality; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Harris, 25 F.3d 1275, decided April 27, 1995, Direct protection of an interstate transportation instrumentality; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Harris, 25 F.3d 1275, April 27, 1995.

**Related current rules:** Interstate commerce, anti-evasion, and material interstate externalities. This rule applies the direct-instrumentality route and requires the offense-specific interstate connection to perform constitutional work.

### Commerce Clause authority over access-directed obstruction

**Governing question:** When Congress may regulate conduct directed at obstructing access to services in an interstate market under the Commerce Clause.

**Current rule:** Congress may regulate access-directed conduct whose object and operation are to prevent transactions and services in an interstate market. The constitutional connection must arise from the regulated conduct’s direct interference with the market itself, not from a generalized chain through social costs, health consequences, productivity, or crime.

**Exceptions and limits:** FACE’s access-directed conduct and intent elements must perform real work in every application. The decision does not authorize federal prosecution of every local assault, trespass, protest, or disturbance associated with a medical facility.

**First controlling authority:** American Life League v. Reno, 47 F.3d 642, decided January 29, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Commerce Clause authority over access-directed obstruction; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** American Life League v. Reno, 47 F.3d 642, decided January 29, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Commerce Clause authority over access-directed obstruction; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** American Life League v. Reno, 47 F.3d 642, January 29, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.
