**Related current rules:** Interstate commerce, anti-evasion, and material interstate externalities. Access-directed obstruction is regulated through its direct interference with an actual interstate service market, not through aggregate downstream consequences.

**Development:**
- January 29, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, American Life League v. Reno, 47 F.3d 642: *Lopez* is clarified rather than narrowed. Generalized economic consequences remain insufficient, but conduct directed at preventing provision and receipt of services in an actual interstate market may be regulated because the interference operates on the market itself.

### Criminally enforceable routing to a private facility

**Governing question:** When a municipality’s criminally enforceable routing of waste to a private facility violates the dormant Commerce Clause.

**Current rule:** Governmental financial exposure, planning responsibility, or contractual access to a facility does not convert a coercive legal command into a market purchase. A routing rule backed by fines or imprisonment and applicable to persons outside governmental contractual privity remains regulation.

**Exceptions and limits:** The holding does not govern a genuinely public facility operating under distinct public-service circumstances, an evenhanded health regulation, or a municipality’s own purchase of disposal capacity.

**First controlling authority:** SSC Corp. v. Town of Smithtown; Town of Smithtown v. SSC Corp., 66 F.3d 502, decided June 18, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Criminally enforceable routing to a private facility; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** SSC Corp. v. Town of Smithtown; Town of Smithtown v. SSC Corp., 66 F.3d 502, decided June 18, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Criminally enforceable routing to a private facility; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** SSC Corp. v. Town of Smithtown; Town of Smithtown v. SSC Corp., 66 F.3d 502, June 18, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

### Routing within a municipally purchased service

**Governing question:** When a municipality acts as a market participant in routing waste within a service it purchases, and when contractual conditions exceed that purchased transaction.

**Current rule:** A government buyer may define the suppliers, route, facility, and performance it will purchase within the transaction. Market-participant protection ends when a contractual condition controls independent transactions, noncontracting actors, commercial waste outside the purchased service, or downstream commerce in which government is not participating.

**First controlling authority:** SSC Corp. v. Town of Smithtown; Town of Smithtown v. SSC Corp., 66 F.3d 502, decided June 18, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Routing within a municipally purchased service; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** SSC Corp. v. Town of Smithtown; Town of Smithtown v. SSC Corp., 66 F.3d 502, decided June 18, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Routing within a municipally purchased service; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** SSC Corp. v. Town of Smithtown; Town of Smithtown v. SSC Corp., 66 F.3d 502, June 18, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Related current rules:** Commercial state enterprises in interstate markets, under Federalism and State Sovereign Immunity. Both rules distinguish voluntary participation in a commercial transaction from coercive governmental regulation, although the sovereign-immunity rule separately examines fiscal structure and the source of payment.

**Development:**
- June 18, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, SSC Corp. v. Town of Smithtown; Town of Smithtown v. SSC Corp., 66 F.3d 502: Simulated *Carbone* is applied and clarified. Its private-enterprise discrimination rule governs Smithtown’s ordinance, while its reservation of genuinely public and governmental-market arrangements remains intact. The decision adds a recurring boundary between contractual participation in a purchased service and coercive regulation of commerce outside contractual privity.

### Regulation of the post-1986 civilian machinegun market

**Governing question:** When Congress may include local possession within regulation of a defined interstate product market.

**Current rule:** Congress may include local possession within a valid regulation of a defined interstate product market when possession and transfer are functionally inseparable and excluding possession would permit concealment, retention, or acquisition that defeats the market prohibition. Generalized effects of violent crime, insurance costs, fear, or lost productivity remain insufficient.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not establish a general federal police power over firearms, validate every possession statute, or hold that any object remains federally regulable forever merely because it once crossed a state line. The anti-evasion relationship to a direct market regime must perform real work.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Hale, 978 F.2d 1016, decided June 5, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Regulation of the post-1986 civilian machinegun market; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Hale, 978 F.2d 1016, decided June 5, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Regulation of the post-1986 civilian machinegun market; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Hale, 978 F.2d 1016, June 5, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Related current rules:** Interstate commerce, anti-evasion, and material interstate externalities. This rule applies the anti-evasion and anti-impairment route to a defined interstate product-market prohibition.

**Development:**
- June 5, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, United States v. Hale, 978 F.2d 1016: *Lopez* is clarified. Its rejection of attenuated aggregate-effects reasoning remains intact, while a direct product-market prohibition may include possession that is reasonably necessary to prevent evasion of the regulated transfer market.

### Interstate computer-assisted procurement transactions

**Governing question:** When Congress may regulate a commercial transaction completed through an interstate computer transmission.

**Current rule:** Congress regulates interstate commerce itself when it prohibits a knowing interstate computer transmission made as an operative part of a commercial transaction to procure an interest in another State’s lottery. Aggregate-effects analysis is unnecessary when the regulated conduct is the cross-border transaction or transmission itself. The offense-specific interstate element must perform genuine work.

**Exceptions and limits:** A nominal jurisdictional element, generalized moral concern, or purely local gambling is insufficient.

**First controlling authority:** Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno, 76 F.3d 1294, decided April 28, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, Interstate lottery procurement; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno, 76 F.3d 1294, decided April 28, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, Interstate lottery procurement; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno, 76 F.3d 1294, April 28, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date.

**Related current rules:** Interstate commerce, anti-evasion, and material interstate externalities. This rule applies the direct interstate-transaction and transmission route.

### Congressional authorization of state restraints on interstate commerce

**Governing question:** When Congress may permit a state restriction otherwise barred only by the dormant Commerce Clause.

**Current rule:** Congress may unmistakably authorize state restrictions that would otherwise violate the dormant Commerce Clause. The authorization must be clear in the federal enactment and may alter only dormant-commerce limitations, not an independent constitutional prohibition.

**Exceptions and limits:** Authorization does not validate a materially different state restriction outside the federal structure or permit violation of another constitutional guarantee.

**First controlling authority:** Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno, 76 F.3d 1294, decided April 28, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, Interstate-agreement exception; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno, 76 F.3d 1294, decided April 28, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, Interstate-agreement exception; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno, 76 F.3d 1294, April 28, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date.

### Residency-based charitable tax exemptions

**Governing question:** When a charitable property-tax exemption discriminates against interstate commerce.

**Current rule:** A State facially discriminates against interstate commerce when it denies a charitable tax exemption because an institution principally serves nonresidents while granting the exemption to comparable charities serving residents.

**Exceptions and limits:** States need not subsidize charitable activity everywhere and may use neutral criteria based on nonprofit status, property use, finances, or regulatory compliance. Direct resident public benefits present a different question.

**First controlling authority:** Camps Newfound/Owatonna, Inc. v. Town of Harrison, 520 U.S. 564 (1997), decided May 19, 1997, Residency-based charitable tax treatment; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Camps Newfound/Owatonna, Inc. v. Town of Harrison, 520 U.S. 564 (1997), decided May 19, 1997, Residency-based charitable tax treatment; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Camps Newfound/Owatonna, Inc. v. Town of Harrison, 520 U.S. 564 (1997), May 19, 1997.

### Interstate support-payment obligations

**Governing question:** When Congress may criminalize willful nonpayment of a state-created child-support obligation.

**Current rule:** Congress may directly protect a legally fixed child-support obligation owed between persons residing in different States when the statute requires the different-state relationship, an existing lawful state order, and willful default. The interstate element must identify an obligation whose legally required performance calls for value to pass between States; generalized social and economic consequences are insufficient. Direct regulation of the private obligor neither commandeers a State nor transfers domestic-relations adjudication to the federal court.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not create a federal domestic-relations code, reach purely intrastate nonpayment, permit revision of custody or support terms, or dispense with willfulness. Federal prosecution may punish only post-enactment failures.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Mussari, 95 F.3d 787, decided June 30, 1997, Commerce power over an interstate support-payment obligation and Tenth Amendment boundary; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Mussari, 95 F.3d 787, decided June 30, 1997, Commerce power over an interstate support-payment obligation and Tenth Amendment boundary; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Mussari, 95 F.3d 787, June 30, 1997.

**Related current rules:** Interstate commerce, anti-evasion, and material interstate externalities. This rule applies the direct interstate-transaction route to a legally fixed obligation requiring value to pass between States.

### Local commercial robbery under the Hobbs Act

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs local commercial robbery and the Hobbs Act.

**Current rule:** Congress may apply the Hobbs Act to robbery that directly depletes the operating assets of a business participating in interstate commerce. The effect of the individual robbery may be slight when the regulated class is economic, the statutory commerce element is actually proved, and the class substantially affects interstate commerce.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Robinson, 119 F.3d 1205, decided March 23, 1998, Local commercial robbery and the Hobbs Act; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Robinson, 119 F.3d 1205, decided March 23, 1998, Local commercial robbery and the Hobbs Act; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Robinson, 119 F.3d 1205, March 23, 1998.

**Related current rules:** Interstate commerce, anti-evasion, and material interstate externalities. This rule applies the substantial-market-effects route to an economic class and requires actual proof of the statutory commerce element.

### Evenhanded routing to a genuinely public facility

**Governing question:** When municipal routing to a public facility and a diversion fee discriminate against interstate commerce.

**Current rule:** A municipality does not facially discriminate against interstate commerce by directing locally generated waste to a genuinely municipally owned facility performing a traditional public service when local private processors and out-of-state processors receive equal treatment. A uniform fee based on diversion from the public system is likewise not facially discriminatory when liability does not turn on the destination’s State. Compensatory-charge analysis follows classification of the measure as discriminatory.

**First controlling authority:** Waste Management, Inc. of Tennessee v. Metropolitan Government of Nashville, 130 F.3d 731, decided May 27, 1998, evenhanded routing and fees for a public facility; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Waste Management, Inc. of Tennessee v. Metropolitan Government of Nashville, 130 F.3d 731, decided May 27, 1998, evenhanded routing and fees for a public facility; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Waste Management, Inc. of Tennessee v. Metropolitan Government of Nashville, 130 F.3d 731, May 27, 1998.

## Separation of Powers

### Legislative reopening of final Article III judgments

**Governing question:** When legislation governing an existing federal judgment impermissibly reopens a completed Article III disposition and when it permissibly changes the continuing operation of prospective relief.

**Current rule:** Congress violates Article III when it commands federal courts to reopen final judgments in private civil actions and replace them with dispositions required by subsequently enacted law. Congress may change substantive law and apply it to pending or nonfinal cases, regulate future conduct, alter the standards governing the continuing force of prospective equitable relief, and attach temporary statutory consequences while a court applies the new standards. Once a private judgment has become final within the Article III hierarchy, Congress may not direct a federal court to reopen that completed judgment and decide it differently under unchanged law.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** A statutory automatic stay may temporarily suspend prospective relief before final adjudication of a termination motion, but the statute may not be administered through a timetable that deprives the Article III court of a meaningful opportunity to determine whether the existing relief satisfies the newly enacted standards.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not prevent new law from governing pending cases, lawful prospective legislation, modification or termination of continuing injunctions under changed governing law, waiver of federal claims or defenses, or generally applicable reopening rules consistent with Article III. It does not permit Congress to prescribe factual findings or a result under unchanged law, reopen a final damages judgment, or deny the court a meaningful opportunity to adjudicate the new standard.

**First controlling authority:** *Plaut v. Spendthrift Farm, Inc.*, 514 U.S. 211, decided April 18, 1995, legislative reopening of final Article III judgments; established the completed-judgment boundary.

**Controlling authorities:** *Plaut v. Spendthrift Farm, Inc.*, 514 U.S. 211, decided April 18, 1995, supplies the prohibition on legislatively reopening a completed private judgment and preserves changes governing future or continuing relief. *Miller v. French; United States v. French*, 530 U.S. 327 (2000), decided June 19, 2000, supplies Congress’s authority to change the continuing standards for prospective relief, the facial validity of temporary automatic-stay consequences, and the meaningful-opportunity limit on the adjudicative timetable.

**Present operation effective:** *Miller v. French; United States v. French*, 530 U.S. 327 (2000), June 19, 2000.

**Development:**
- June 19, 2000, *Miller v. French; United States v. French*: Before *Miller*, *Plaut* prohibited reopening completed private judgments while preserving legislation governing future conduct and continuing equitable relief. After *Miller*, Congress may change the substantive standards for continuing prospective relief and attach a temporary automatic stay, but the statutory timetable must preserve a meaningful judicial opportunity to apply the new standards. The prohibition on reopening a completed damages judgment remains unchanged.

### Congressional influence and legally operative control

**Governing question:** When congressional participation becomes legally operative control over executive or operational decisions.

**Current rule:** Congressional officers or agents do not violate separation of powers merely by receiving information, attending meetings, advising, observing, reporting, or attempting to persuade an executive or operational decisionmaker. Congress crosses the constitutional line when it gives itself or its agents legally operative authority to approve, disapprove, suspend, delay, alter, or condition execution outside the constitutional legislative process. The inquiry turns on the legal consequence of the asserted authority, not solely on the identity of the participant, the presence or absence of a formal vote, or the practical likelihood that political influence will be effective.

**Exceptions and limits:** Congressional affiliation, consultation, advice, observation, political pressure, and ordinary oversight are not independently unconstitutional. Congress may regulate the airports through enacted law.

**First controlling authority:** Metropolitan Washington Airports Authority v. Hechinger, 36 F.3d 97, decided June 5, 1995, Congressional influence and legally operative control; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Metropolitan Washington Airports Authority v. Hechinger, 36 F.3d 97, decided June 5, 1995, Congressional influence and legally operative control; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Metropolitan Washington Airports Authority v. Hechinger, 36 F.3d 97, June 5, 1995.

### Report-and-wait mechanisms and bicameralism

**Governing question:** Which report-and-wait mechanisms comply with bicameralism and presentment.

**Current rule:** Congress may require advance reporting and may enact a fixed waiting period that expires automatically. It may not make the legal effectiveness of an operational decision depend on approval, disapproval, suspension, extension, or another intervention by Congress or its agent outside bicameralism and presentment.

**Exceptions and limits:** Purely informational reports, hearings, recommendations, and an automatic waiting period remain permissible.

**First controlling authority:** Metropolitan Washington Airports Authority v. Hechinger, 36 F.3d 97, decided June 5, 1995, Report-and-wait mechanisms and bicameralism; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Metropolitan Washington Airports Authority v. Hechinger, 36 F.3d 97, decided June 5, 1995, Report-and-wait mechanisms and bicameralism; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Metropolitan Washington Airports Authority v. Hechinger, 36 F.3d 97, June 5, 1995.

### Timely Appointments Clause challenge

**Governing question:** What remedy a litigant receives after timely challenging the appointment of officers adjudicating the litigant’s case.

**Current rule:** A litigant who timely challenges the unconstitutional appointment of officers adjudicating his own case is entitled to a fresh adjudication by lawfully appointed officers. The de facto officer doctrine does not validate the defective panel’s judgment against that litigant. The de facto officer doctrine may protect institutional continuity and settled acts in appropriate circumstances, but it does not defeat relief for a timely, preserved constitutional challenge to the source of an adjudicator’s authority.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not reopen final judgments in which no timely objection was made, invalidate routine ministerial acts, or prescribe relief for a challenge raised only after the ordinary review process ended.

**First controlling authority:** Ryder v. United States, 515 U.S. 177, decided June 12, 1995, Timely Appointments Clause challenge; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Ryder v. United States, 515 U.S. 177, decided June 12, 1995, Timely Appointments Clause challenge; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Ryder v. United States, 515 U.S. 177, June 12, 1995.

### Congressional authorization and presidential implementation

**Governing question:** How Congress and the President must divide the definition and implementation of military capital sentencing.

**Current rule:** Congress must make the fundamental legislative choices by defining the offense, authorizing the available punishment, and confining military rulemaking to the enacted system. Within that boundary, the President may prescribe criteria that narrow and implement capital sentencing. The President may not create a new capital offense or authorize a punishment Congress withheld.

**First controlling authority:** Loving v. United States, 517 U.S. 748, decided June 3, 1996, Congressional authorization and presidential implementation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Loving v. United States, 517 U.S. 748, decided June 3, 1996, Congressional authorization and presidential implementation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Loving v. United States, 517 U.S. 748, June 3, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 3, 1996, Loving v. United States, 517 U.S. 748: The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule allowing bounded presidential implementation of military capital sentencing within offenses and punishments enacted by Congress.

### Constitutional review of census methodology

**Governing question:** What constitutional standard applies to a congressionally authorized census methodology used for apportionment.

**Current rule:** A federal census methodology, including authorized statistical sampling used as part of the decennial enumeration, satisfies the Census Clause when it is directed to determining actual population and bears a reasonable relationship to accomplishing a complete and accurate count for apportionment. A differential effect on racial or socioeconomic groups does not by itself trigger heightened scrutiny or constitutionally compel a competing statistical method; intentional discrimination remains independently reviewable.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not immunize intentional discrimination, a method outside delegated authority, refusal to consider material evidence, or a properly presented statutory or Administrative Procedure Act violation. It does not hold that statistical adjustment is constitutionally forbidden in a later census supported by a materially different record.

**First controlling authority:** Wisconsin v. City of New York; Oklahoma v. City of New York; Department of Commerce v. City of New York, 517 U.S. 1, decided March 20, 1996, Constitutional review of census methodology; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Wisconsin v. City of New York; Oklahoma v. City of New York; Department of Commerce v. City of New York, 517 U.S. 1, decided March 20, 1996, supplies the reasonable-relationship standard. Department of Commerce v. United States House of Representatives; Clinton v. Glavin, 525 U.S. 316 (1999), decided January 25, 1999, supplies its application to authorized statistical sampling directed to actual population.

**Present operation effective:** Department of Commerce v. United States House of Representatives; Clinton v. Glavin, 525 U.S. 316 (1999), January 25, 1999.

**Development:**
- March 20, 1996, Wisconsin v. City of New York; Oklahoma v. City of New York; Department of Commerce v. City of New York, 517 U.S. 1: The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule for constitutional review of federal census methodology. *Department of Commerce v. Montana* and *Franklin v. Massachusetts* are applied and clarified: delegated census choices receive review for constitutional authorization and reasonable relation to the enumeration and apportionment function, not heightened scrutiny borrowed from intrastate districting.
- January 25, 1999, Department of Commerce v. United States House of Representatives; Clinton v. Glavin, 525 U.S. 316 (1999): The reasonable-relationship standard extends to authorized supplementary sampling directed to determining actual population. No categorical rule governs every possible form of apportionment sampling.

### Civil litigation against a sitting President for unofficial conduct

**Governing question:** Whether separation of powers creates temporary presidential immunity from federal civil litigation concerning unofficial pre-office conduct.

**Current rule:** A sitting President has no constitutional temporary immunity from a federal civil action based on unofficial conduct alleged to have occurred before taking office. Separation of powers does not require categorical postponement through the end of the term. Particular proceedings may be postponed or modified only upon a concrete showing of material interference with presidential responsibilities, using an accommodation proportionate to the demonstrated burden.

**Burdens and proof:** The party seeking delay must identify the asserted official burden.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not decide state-court litigation, criminal process against a sitting President, or any particular subpoena, deposition, trial date, or attendance order.

**First controlling authority:** Clinton v. Jones, 520 U.S. 681 (1997), decided May 27, 1997, Temporary immunity and case-specific accommodation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Clinton v. Jones, 520 U.S. 681 (1997), decided May 27, 1997, Temporary immunity and case-specific accommodation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Clinton v. Jones, 520 U.S. 681 (1997), May 27, 1997.

### Legislative branch requirement for bills of attainder

**Governing question:** When the Bill of Attainder Clause applies to governmental action.

**Current rule:** The Bill of Attainder Clause prohibits legislative identification and punishment without judicial trial. The branch requirement is indispensable: an executive or military regulation is not a bill of attainder, although it remains reviewable under any other applicable constitutional or statutory source.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not foreclose a challenge to nominally civil legislation that identifies persons or a closed class and imposes punishment for past conduct. When the challenged act is executive, the Court does not reach whether it otherwise inflicts punishment in the constitutional sense.

**First controlling authority:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, decided March 20, 1995, on the restored simulation date, Bill of attainder; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Ambassador Books & Video, Inc. v. City of Little Rock, 20 F.3d 858, decided March 20, 1995, preserves the legislative-punishment inquiry. Walmer v. Department of Defense, 52 F.3d 851, decided January 13, 1997, supplies the branch-specific executive-regulation boundary.

**Present operation effective:** Walmer v. Department of Defense, 52 F.3d 851, January 13, 1997.

### Post-enactment presidential cancellation of statutory provisions

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs post-enactment cancellation of statutory provisions.

**Current rule:** After a provision becomes law through bicameralism and presentment, the President may not use independent policy judgment to make that provision cease to have legal force.

**First controlling authority:** Clinton v. City of New York, 524 U.S. 417 (1998), decided June 25, 1998, Post-enactment cancellation of statutory provisions; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Clinton v. City of New York, 524 U.S. 417 (1998), decided June 25, 1998, Post-enactment cancellation of statutory provisions; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Clinton v. City of New York, 524 U.S. 417 (1998), June 25, 1998.

### Limits on judicial-council reassignment authority

**Governing question:** What case-management and reassignment measures 28 U.S.C. § 332 permits a circuit judicial council to impose.

**Current rule:** Section 332 permits neutral administration of judicial business, including prospective assignment rules, docket equalization, emergency management, lawful recusal, and expressly authorized discipline. It does not permit a judicial council to punish an Article III judge by removing pending cases because of disagreement with merits-related conduct, nullifying findings, or substituting administrative supervision for appellate review.

**First controlling authority:** Judicial Council v. McBryde, 117 F.3d 208, decided June 10, 1998, limits on judicial-council reassignment authority; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Judicial Council v. McBryde, 117 F.3d 208, decided June 10, 1998, limits on judicial-council reassignment authority; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Judicial Council v. McBryde, 117 F.3d 208, June 10, 1998.

## Constitutional Attribution

### Government-created and controlled corporations

**Governing question:** When a federally created and controlled corporation counts as the Government for constitutional-rights purposes.

**Current rule:** A corporation created by special federal law to advance governmental objectives and permanently controlled through federal appointment of a majority of its directors is part of the Government for purposes of constitutional rights, notwithstanding a statutory declaration that it is not an agency. Governmental creation, governmental objectives, and permanent governmental control establish constitutional responsibility. Funding, regulation, chartering, or temporary participation alone does not.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not convert every federally chartered, subsidized, or heavily regulated corporation into a governmental actor. Nor does it decide whether the billboard was a public forum or whether Amtrak’s particular restriction survives First Amendment review.

**First controlling authority:** Lebron v. National Railroad Passenger Corp., 513 U.S. 374, decided February 21, 1995, Government-created and controlled corporations; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Lebron v. National Railroad Passenger Corp., 513 U.S. 374, decided February 21, 1995, Government-created and controlled corporations; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Lebron v. National Railroad Passenger Corp., 513 U.S. 374, February 21, 1995.

### Attribution of independently exercised private choices

**Governing question:** When a private actor’s use of a government-authorized option is attributable to the State.

**Current rule:** Governmental authorization, regulation, or availability of an option does not make an independently exercised private choice state action. Attribution requires a close nexus to the particular challenged act through coercion, joint administration, legal compulsion, delegated exclusive public function, selective governmental enforcement, or another recognized basis.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not foreclose attribution when government directs, jointly administers, compels, selectively enforces, or delegates exclusive governmental authority over the challenged act.

**First controlling authority:** American Manufacturers Mutual Insurance Co. v. Sullivan, 526 U.S. 40 (1999), decided March 3, 1999, attribution of a private insurer’s withholding decision; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** American Manufacturers Mutual Insurance Co. v. Sullivan, 526 U.S. 40 (1999), decided March 3, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** American Manufacturers Mutual Insurance Co. v. Sullivan, 526 U.S. 40 (1999), March 3, 1999.

## Property and Takings

### Essential nexus and rough proportionality for adjudicative property exactions

**Governing question:** What relationship must exist between an adjudicative permit exaction of a property interest and a proposed development’s impacts.

**Current rule:** A permit condition compelling dedication of a property interest must bear an essential nexus and rough proportionality, in nature and extent, to impacts attributable to the proposed development. Government must make an individualized determination connecting the demanded property to the development’s attributable impact; mathematical precision and least-restrictive-means analysis are not required.

**Burdens and proof:** The government bears the obligation to justify the compelled dedication through the required individualized connection.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule governs adjudicative exactions of property interests, not an ordinary permit denial compelling no dedication, ordinary zoning restrictions, or generally applicable taxes and fees that do not function as compelled conveyances.

**First controlling authority:** Dolan v. City of Tigard, 512 U.S. 374 (1994), decided June 24, 1994, Nexus and proportionality of compelled land dedications; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Dolan v. City of Tigard, 512 U.S. 374 (1994), decided June 24, 1994, supplies the nexus and rough-proportionality rule. City of Monterey v. Del Monte Dunes at Monterey, Ltd., 526 U.S. 687 (1999), decided May 24, 1999, supplies the boundary for an ordinary permit denial compelling no dedication.

**Present operation effective:** Dolan v. City of Tigard, 512 U.S. 374 (1994), June 24, 1994.

**Current wording authority:** City of Monterey v. Del Monte Dunes at Monterey, Ltd., 526 U.S. 687 (1999), May 24, 1999.

### Jury allocation in § 1983 regulatory-taking damages actions

**Governing question:** Whether the Seventh Amendment entitles a plaintiff to a jury determination of liability on a § 1983 claim alleging a completed regulatory taking.

**Current rule:** A § 1983 action seeking legal damages for an asserted completed regulatory taking carries a Seventh Amendment jury right. The judge states the governing constitutional law and determines pure legal questions; under proper instructions, the jury may resolve disputed historical application facts and determine legal damages.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** The judge supplies the governing regulatory-taking standard, rules on pure questions of law, instructs the jury, and retains ordinary legal-sufficiency and postverdict review. The jury determines disputed historical application facts and the amount of legal damages.

**Burdens and proof:** The ordinary burdens governing the underlying § 1983 regulatory-taking claim remain unchanged; the Seventh Amendment allocates decisionmaking and does not create a new substantive takings standard.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not authorize a jury to formulate zoning policy, extend *Dolan* to ordinary permit denials, or establish a free-standing regulatory-taking test. It governs legal damages actions and leaves purely equitable proceedings and pure questions of law to the court.

**First controlling authority:** City of Monterey v. Del Monte Dunes at Monterey, Ltd., 526 U.S. 687 (1999), decided May 24, 1999, Seventh Amendment allocation for regulatory-taking liability; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** City of Monterey v. Del Monte Dunes at Monterey, Ltd., 526 U.S. 687 (1999), decided May 24, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** City of Monterey v. Del Monte Dunes at Monterey, Ltd., 526 U.S. 687 (1999), May 24, 1999.

### General monetary liability and identified property

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs general monetary liability and the Takings Clause.

**Current rule:** A statutory obligation to pay money from a regulated party’s general assets does not effect a taking of identified property when the law creates no lien, appropriation of a specified fund, physical invasion, or transfer of a particular asset.

**First controlling authority:** Eastern Enterprises v. Apfel, 524 U.S. 498 (1998), decided June 25, 1998, General monetary liability and the Takings Clause; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Eastern Enterprises v. Apfel, 524 U.S. 498 (1998), decided June 25, 1998, General monetary liability and the Takings Clause; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Eastern Enterprises v. Apfel, 524 U.S. 498 (1998), June 25, 1998.

### Property generated only through mandatory pooled accounts

**Governing question:** Whether an increment created only by mandatory pooling is the individual principal owner’s private property.

**Current rule:** An increment generated only through a mandatory pooled IOLTA arrangement is not the individual client’s private property when governing banking and program rules create the only circumstance in which the interest can exist and deny the client any lawful route to generate or receive it individually. The maxim that interest follows principal does not create title to an increment that would not exist for the claimant apart from the challenged arrangement.

**Exceptions and limits:** Webb’s Fabulous Pharmacies is distinguished; Roth continues to require identification through complete background property law. Absence of net value is not independently dispositive.

**First controlling authority:** Phillips v. Washington Legal Foundation, 524 U.S. 156 (1998), decided June 15, 1998, property status of mandatory IOLTA interest; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Phillips v. Washington Legal Foundation, 524 U.S. 156 (1998), decided June 15, 1998, property status of mandatory IOLTA interest; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Phillips v. Washington Legal Foundation, 524 U.S. 156 (1998), June 15, 1998.

## Bankruptcy

### Reasonably equivalent value after mortgage foreclosure

**Governing question:** How a bankruptcy court determines reasonably equivalent value after a regularly conducted mortgage foreclosure.

**Current rule:** A regularly conducted, noncollusive foreclosure sale is substantial evidence of value under forced-sale conditions, but the sale price and compliance with state foreclosure law do not conclusively establish reasonably equivalent value under 11 U.S.C. § 548(a)(2). The court must conduct a transaction-specific inquiry using relevant evidence such as the price, forced-sale conditions, exposure to purchasers, procedural regularity, and secured debt discharged, without double counting equivalent consideration.

**Burdens and proof:** The party seeking avoidance retains the ordinary burden of proof. No presumption or shifted burden of production arises from the foreclosure sale alone.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule prescribes no fixed percentage, automatic avoidance rule, mandatory valuation sequence, or alteration of state foreclosure procedure.

**First controlling authority:** BFP v. Resolution Trust Corp., 511 U.S. 531 (1994), decided May 23, 1994, Meaning of reasonably equivalent value after foreclosure; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** BFP v. Resolution Trust Corp., 511 U.S. 531 (1994), decided May 23, 1994, Meaning of reasonably equivalent value after foreclosure; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** BFP v. Resolution Trust Corp., 511 U.S. 531 (1994), May 23, 1994.

### Categorical equitable subordination

**Governing question:** What claim-specific showing permits equitable subordination in bankruptcy.

**Current rule:** Equitable subordination must rest on the circumstances of the particular claim and recognized equitable principles. A court may not reorder an entire statutory class because it disagrees with the distributive consequences Congress selected.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide whether creditor misconduct is invariably required or foreclose subordination based on fraud, insider abuse, inequitable conduct, or another recognized case-specific ground.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Noland, 517 U.S. 535, decided May 13, 1996, Categorical equitable subordination; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Noland, 517 U.S. 535, decided May 13, 1996, Categorical equitable subordination; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Noland, 517 U.S. 535, May 13, 1996.

**Development:**
- May 13, 1996, United States v. Noland, 517 U.S. 535: The decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule governing categorical equitable subordination. It leaves the content of recognized case-specific equitable grounds open.

### Temporary administrative hold and the automatic stay

**Governing question:** When a creditor’s temporary administrative hold becomes a setoff prohibited by the automatic stay.

**Current rule:** A setoff occurs when a creditor decides to settle mutual obligations and takes action giving that settlement legal and accounting effect. A temporary refusal to pay that preserves the disputed amount while the creditor promptly seeks stay relief, without a permanent reduction or bookkeeping application, is not itself the completed setoff prohibited by § 362(a)(7).

**Exceptions and limits:** The holding does not authorize an indefinite freeze, an amount exceeding the asserted setoff, unreasonable delay in seeking relief, or an accounting entry that actually applies the deposit to the loan.

**First controlling authority:** Citizens Bank of Maryland v. Strumpf, 516 U.S. 16, decided October 31, 1995, Temporary administrative hold and the automatic stay; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Citizens Bank of Maryland v. Strumpf, 516 U.S. 16, decided October 31, 1995, Temporary administrative hold and the automatic stay; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Citizens Bank of Maryland v. Strumpf, 516 U.S. 16, October 31, 1995.

**Development:**
- October 31, 1995, Citizens Bank of Maryland v. Strumpf, 516 U.S. 16: The decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule distinguishing a temporary status-preserving account hold from a completed setoff. It does not alter the separate rule requiring obedience to a bankruptcy injunction pending authorized review.

### Valuation of retained collateral in Chapter 13 cram down

**Governing question:** How § 506(a) values collateral a Chapter 13 debtor retains and uses.

**Current rule:** When a debtor retains and uses collateral, the secured claim is valued by the cost of obtaining like property for the same proposed use, adjusted for age and condition, rather than by net foreclosure proceeds. Replacement value is not invariably retail value or the cost of a new item and must exclude warranties, services, accessories, or components the retained collateral does not provide.

**Exceptions and limits:** No single pricing source governs every asset, and the rule does not apply when the debtor surrenders, abandons, or proposes a materially different use.

**First controlling authority:** Associates Commercial Corp. v. Rash, 520 U.S. 953 (1997), decided June 16, 1997, Valuation of retained collateral; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Associates Commercial Corp. v. Rash, 520 U.S. 953 (1997), decided June 16, 1997, Valuation of retained collateral; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Associates Commercial Corp. v. Rash, 520 U.S. 953 (1997), June 16, 1997.

### Willful and malicious injury under § 523(a)(6)

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs willful and malicious injury.

**Current rule:** A debt arising from negligently or recklessly inflicted injury does not satisfy § 523(a)(6)’s willfulness requirement. The provision requires a deliberate or intentional injury, not merely a deliberate act that happens to cause harm.

**First controlling authority:** Kawaauhau v. Geiger, 523 U.S. 57 (1998), decided March 3, 1998, Willful and malicious injury; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Kawaauhau v. Geiger, 523 U.S. 57 (1998), decided March 3, 1998, Willful and malicious injury; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Kawaauhau v. Geiger, 523 U.S. 57 (1998), March 3, 1998.

### Exclusive new-value opportunities under the absolute-priority rule

**Governing question:** When old equity receives property on account of its prior interest through an exclusive new-value opportunity in a cramdown plan.

**Current rule:** A plan violates the absolute-priority rule when, over an impaired senior class’s objection, old equity receives because of its former position an exclusive opportunity to contribute new capital and obtain ownership without market exposure demonstrating that the opportunity carries no residual value.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule neither recognizes nor rejects every new-value plan. A market-tested opportunity or a plan arising after exclusivity ends remains governed by its own terms and the complete statutory priority structure.

**First controlling authority:** Bank of America National Trust & Savings Ass’n v. 203 North LaSalle Street Partnership, 526 U.S. 434 (1999), decided May 3, 1999, old equity’s exclusive new-value opportunity; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Bank of America National Trust & Savings Ass’n v. 203 North LaSalle Street Partnership, 526 U.S. 434 (1999), decided May 3, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Bank of America National Trust & Savings Ass’n v. 203 North LaSalle Street Partnership, 526 U.S. 434 (1999), May 3, 1999.

### Burden of proof for claims in bankruptcy

**Governing question:** Whether bankruptcy reallocates the burden of proof assigned by the substantive law creating a creditor’s claim.

**Current rule:** Unless the Bankruptcy Code expressly provides otherwise, the burden of proof governing a claim in bankruptcy remains where the substantive nonbankruptcy law creating the claim places it. Federal claim-allowance procedure does not silently reallocate that burden.

**Exceptions and limits:** An express Bankruptcy Code provision may supply a different allocation. The rule concerns the burden attached to the validity of the underlying claim and does not determine every procedural burden arising solely under bankruptcy law.

**First controlling authority:** *Raleigh v. Illinois Department of Revenue*, 530 U.S. 15, decided May 30, 2000, state-law burden governing a tax claim in bankruptcy; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Raleigh v. Illinois Department of Revenue*, 530 U.S. 15, decided May 30, 2000, supplies the nonbankruptcy-law allocation and express-Code-exception rule.

**Present operation effective:** *Raleigh v. Illinois Department of Revenue*, May 30, 2000.

## Arbitration

### Choice of law and arbitral punitive damages

**Governing question:** Whether a general choice-of-law clause withdraws punitive damages from a broad arbitration agreement.

**Current rule:** A general clause selecting New York law does not clearly withdraw punitive damages from a broad arbitration agreement that otherwise authorizes the arbitrators to resolve the controversy and award available relief. A general choice-of-law clause ordinarily selects the substantive rules governing the parties’ rights. A state rule specially allocating decisionmaking authority between courts and arbitrators is incorporated only when the agreement expresses that limitation clearly. The contract must be read as a whole, and unresolved ambiguity in a standard form is construed against the drafter.

**Exceptions and limits:** An express exclusion of punitive damages or a clear reservation of that remedy to courts would control. The decision does not authorize a remedy forbidden by the governing substantive law.

**First controlling authority:** Mastrobuono v. Shearson Lehman Hutton, Inc., 514 U.S. 52, decided March 6, 1995, Choice of law and arbitral punitive damages; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Mastrobuono v. Shearson Lehman Hutton, Inc., 514 U.S. 52, decided March 6, 1995, Choice of law and arbitral punitive damages; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Mastrobuono v. Shearson Lehman Hutton, Inc., 514 U.S. 52, March 6, 1995.

### Commerce coverage under FAA § 2

**Governing question:** What transactional connection to commerce brings an arbitration agreement within FAA § 2.

**Current rule:** Section 2 reaches contracts evidencing transactions within the full extent of Congress’s lawful commerce authority, and coverage depends on the transaction’s actual commercial operation rather than whether the parties contemplated an interstate connection. The phrase involving commerce invokes the breadth of Congress’s commerce power. A court determines coverage from commerce in fact, while preserving generally applicable grounds for revoking the contract under § 2’s saving clause.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision defines the statutory reach of § 2 but does not define the outer constitutional boundary of the Commerce Clause. It does not hold that an otherwise local transaction becomes federal merely because an incidental object crossed a state line at some earlier point.

**First controlling authority:** Allied-Bruce Terminix Cos. v. Dobson, 513 U.S. 265, decided April 26, 1995, Commerce coverage under FAA § 2; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Allied-Bruce Terminix Cos. v. Dobson, 513 U.S. 265, decided April 26, 1995, Commerce coverage under FAA § 2; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Allied-Bruce Terminix Cos. v. Dobson, 513 U.S. 265, April 26, 1995.

### Agreement to arbitrate arbitrability

**Governing question:** Who decides arbitrability and what evidence establishes delegation to the arbitrator.

**Current rule:** Courts independently determine whether parties agreed to submit arbitrability to an arbitrator unless clear and unmistakable evidence establishes that delegation. Ordinary state-law principles governing contract formation and consent determine whether the parties delegated arbitrability. Silence, ambiguity, or defensive presentation of a jurisdictional objection to the arbitrators does not supply the required clear agreement.

**First controlling authority:** First Options of Chicago, Inc. v. Kaplan, 514 U.S. 938, decided May 22, 1995, Agreement to arbitrate arbitrability; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** First Options of Chicago, Inc. v. Kaplan, 514 U.S. 938, decided May 22, 1995, Agreement to arbitrate arbitrability; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** First Options of Chicago, Inc. v. Kaplan, 514 U.S. 938, May 22, 1995.

### Judicial review

**Governing question:** What standard an appellate court uses to review a district court’s determination of arbitrability.

**Current rule:** An appellate court applies ordinary standards when reviewing a district court’s judicial determination of arbitrability. Legal conclusions receive independent review, while underlying factual findings receive the review ordinarily governing such findings.

**Exceptions and limits:** A clear delegation clause may assign arbitrability to the arbitrator. When the parties validly submit a merits dispute, judicial review of the resulting merits award remains deferential under the Federal Arbitration Act.

**First controlling authority:** First Options of Chicago, Inc. v. Kaplan, 514 U.S. 938, decided May 22, 1995, Judicial review; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** First Options of Chicago, Inc. v. Kaplan, 514 U.S. 938, decided May 22, 1995, Judicial review; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** First Options of Chicago, Inc. v. Kaplan, 514 U.S. 938, May 22, 1995.

### Arbitration-specific contract notice

**Governing question:** Whether a State may impose arbitration-specific notice or form requirements beyond generally applicable contract defenses.

**Current rule:** States may apply generally applicable defenses such as fraud, duress, unconscionability, lack of assent, or neutral conspicuousness rules. They may not single out arbitration clauses for special legends, placement, form, or enforceability burdens.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not approve hidden terms, waive ordinary formation requirements, displace generally applicable unconscionability law, or require arbitration of a dispute the parties did not agree to submit.

**First controlling authority:** Doctor’s Associates, Inc. v. Casarotto, 517 U.S. 681, decided May 20, 1996, Arbitration-specific contract notice; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Doctor’s Associates, Inc. v. Casarotto, 517 U.S. 681, decided May 20, 1996, Arbitration-specific contract notice; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Doctor’s Associates, Inc. v. Casarotto, 517 U.S. 681, May 20, 1996.

**Development:**
- May 20, 1996, Doctor’s Associates, Inc. v. Casarotto, 517 U.S. 681: *Allied-Bruce*, *Mastrobuono*, and *First Options* are applied without alteration. The decision adds a recurring equal-treatment application: a State may regulate contract notice neutrally, but it may not impose a special notice condition on arbitration alone.

## Evidence

### Prior consistent statements and the premotive requirement

**Governing question:** When Rule 801(d)(1)(B) admits a prior consistent statement and what premotive timing it requires.

**Current rule:** Rule 801(d)(1)(B) admits a prior consistent statement to rebut recent fabrication or improper influence or motive only when the statement was made before the alleged fabrication, influence, or motive arose. A statement satisfying the premotive requirement may be admitted substantively under Rule 801(d)(1)(B). A postmotive statement does not qualify under that provision merely because it has rehabilitative relevance.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide whether any statement is admissible under another hearsay exception, the residual exception, or for a legitimate nonhearsay purpose accompanied by an adequate limiting instruction.

**First controlling authority:** Tome v. United States, 513 U.S. 150, decided January 10, 1995, Prior consistent statements and the premotive requirement; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Tome v. United States, 513 U.S. 150, decided January 10, 1995, Prior consistent statements and the premotive requirement; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Tome v. United States, 513 U.S. 150, January 10, 1995.

### Psychotherapist-patient privilege

**Governing question:** What confidential treatment communications Rule 501 protects through the psychotherapist-patient privilege.

**Current rule:** Rule 501 protects confidential communications made for diagnosis or treatment to a licensed psychiatrist, psychologist, or clinical social worker, including treatment notes reflecting those communications. The privilege belongs to the patient and is not overcome by ad hoc judicial balancing of the evidentiary need asserted in a particular case. Waiver and any recognized exception remain separate inquiries.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not define waiver, communications made for a nontherapeutic forensic purpose, threats of imminent harm, or other possible exceptions not presented by the record.

**First controlling authority:** Jaffee v. Redmond, 518 U.S. 1, decided June 13, 1996, Psychotherapist-patient privilege; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Jaffee v. Redmond, 518 U.S. 1, decided June 13, 1996, Psychotherapist-patient privilege; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Jaffee v. Redmond, 518 U.S. 1, June 13, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 13, 1996, Jaffee v. Redmond, 518 U.S. 1: The decision creates the first controlling simulated psychotherapist-patient privilege under Rule 501. Its categorical protection includes licensed clinical social workers but leaves the privilege’s unpresented exceptions and waiver rules unresolved.

### Conceded status elements and Rule 403 alternatives

**Governing question:** When Rule 403 requires consideration of an admission proving a formal legal-status element.

**Current rule:** When a defendant offers an unconditional admission to a complete prior-felony status element and government identifies no other permissible purpose, Rule 403 requires comparison with that equally conclusive alternative. The prior judgment’s name and nature must be excluded when their incremental probative value is substantially outweighed by unfair propensity prejudice.

**Exceptions and limits:** A defendant may not stipulate away the narrative of the charged event, compel acceptance of a qualified admission, or exclude prior acts offered for a genuinely disputed nonpropensity purpose.

**First controlling authority:** Old Chief v. United States, 519 U.S. 172 (1997), decided January 7, 1997, Rule 403 and a conceded status element; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Old Chief v. United States, 519 U.S. 172 (1997), decided January 7, 1997, Rule 403 and a conceded status element; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Old Chief v. United States, 519 U.S. 172 (1997), January 7, 1997.

### Posthumous attorney-client privilege

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs posthumous attorney-client privilege.

**Current rule:** The federal attorney-client privilege ordinarily survives the client’s death and does not yield to an ad hoc balancing test based on the asserted need for evidence in a later criminal investigation.

**First controlling authority:** Swidler & Berlin v. United States, 524 U.S. 399 (1998), decided June 25, 1998, Posthumous attorney-client privilege; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Swidler & Berlin v. United States, 524 U.S. 399 (1998), decided June 25, 1998, Posthumous attorney-client privilege; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Swidler & Berlin v. United States, 524 U.S. 399 (1998), June 25, 1998.

### Rule 702 rulings and unsupported analytical gaps

**Governing question:** How an appellate court reviews a Rule 702 ruling and when a district court may reject the fit between expert data and opinion.

**Current rule:** Case-specific decisions admitting or excluding expert evidence under Rule 702 are reviewed for abuse of discretion, including when exclusion is outcome determinative. The trial judge’s reasonable selection of reliability considerations for the particular expertise and record receives the same review. Interpretation of Rule 702 remains independently reviewable. A district court may exclude an opinion when the underlying data do not reliably support the inference required by the conclusion; the expert’s assertion alone does not bridge the analytical gap.

**First controlling authority:** General Electric Co. v. Joiner, 522 U.S. 136 (1997), decided December 15, 1997, Rule 702 review and analytical gaps; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** General Electric Co. v. Joiner, 522 U.S. 136 (1997), decided December 15, 1997, supplies abuse-of-discretion review and the analytical-gap rule. Kumho Tire Co. v. Carmichael, 526 U.S. 137 (1999), decided March 23, 1999, supplies abuse-of-discretion review of the reasonable selection of reliability considerations.

**Present operation effective:** Kumho Tire Co. v. Carmichael, 526 U.S. 137 (1999), March 23, 1999.

**Development:**
- March 23, 1999, Kumho Tire Co. v. Carmichael, 526 U.S. 137 (1999): Abuse-of-discretion review now expressly covers the trial judge’s reasonable selection of reliability considerations for the expertise, methodology, issue, and record. Independent review of Rule 702’s legal meaning and the unsupported-gap rule remain unchanged.

### Rule 702 gatekeeping for specialized expert knowledge

**Governing question:** What gatekeeping obligation applies to scientific, technical, and other specialized expert testimony.

**Current rule:** The trial judge must assess the reliability and fit of all expert knowledge offered under Rule 702, whether scientific, technical, or otherwise specialized. The considerations identified in Daubert are flexible and nonexclusive; the judge uses those reasonably pertinent to the particular expertise, methodology, issue, and record rather than applying a fixed checklist.

**Exceptions and limits:** No Daubert consideration is invariably required or dispositive. The inquiry concerns reliability and fit, not substitution of the judge’s view for a sufficiently reliable expert conclusion.

**First controlling authority:** Kumho Tire Co. v. Carmichael, 526 U.S. 137 (1999), decided March 23, 1999, gatekeeping beyond scientific testimony; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Kumho Tire Co. v. Carmichael, 526 U.S. 137 (1999), decided March 23, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Kumho Tire Co. v. Carmichael, 526 U.S. 137 (1999), March 23, 1999.

### No protective-function privilege for grand-jury testimony

**Governing question:** Whether Rule 501 recognizes a Secret Service protective-function privilege against compelled testimony before a federal grand jury.

**Current rule:** Rule 501 does not recognize a protective-function privilege, on the controlling record, for operational information obtained by Secret Service personnel while protecting the President when that information is sought by a federal grand jury. Established relational privileges do not automatically extend to operational observations lacking a demonstrated predicate for categorical confidentiality.

**Exceptions and limits:** No absolute or qualified overcoming standard exists because the asserted privilege was not recognized. The rule does not decide whether a materially different privilege claim could operate in another type of proceeding.

**First controlling authority:** In re Sealed Case, 148 F.3d 1073, decided April 13, 1999, recognition of a protective-function privilege; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** In re Sealed Case, 148 F.3d 1073, decided April 13, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** In re Sealed Case, 148 F.3d 1073, April 13, 1999.

### Preservation after anticipatory disclosure under Rule 609

**Governing question:** Whether a criminal defendant forfeits appellate review of a Rule 609 ruling by disclosing the prior conviction first on direct examination.

**Current rule:** A defendant who timely objects to a definitive adverse Rule 609 ruling and then testifies does not forfeit appellate review merely by disclosing the conviction first on direct examination to reduce its prejudicial force.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** The defendant must obtain a definitive ruling, make a timely objection, and testify. Anticipatory disclosure after those steps preserves the claim; the appellate court reviews the ruling and, if it finds error, applies the governing harmless-error analysis.

**Exceptions and limits:** A defendant who does not testify remains governed by *Luce v. United States*. Preservation does not establish evidentiary error and does not entitle the defendant to an automatic new trial.

**First controlling authority:** *Ohler v. United States*, 529 U.S. 753, corrected simulation disposition decided May 22, 2000, preservation after defensive disclosure; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Ohler v. United States*, 529 U.S. 753, corrected simulation disposition decided May 22, 2000, supplies the timely-objection, definitive-ruling, testimony, anticipatory-disclosure, and harmless-error components.

**Present operation effective:** *Ohler v. United States*, May 22, 2000.

## Criminal Law and Sentencing

### Willfulness in the federal anti-structuring offense

**Governing question:** What mental state the willfulness requirement adds to the federal anti-structuring offense.

**Current rule:** Conviction under 31 U.S.C. §§ 5322 and 5324 requires proof that the defendant knew the structuring was unlawful, in addition to proof that the defendant intended to evade the financial institution’s reporting duty. The Government need not prove knowledge of the precise statutory citation or every detail of the reporting scheme.

**Burdens and proof:** The Government must prove the required knowledge of unlawfulness beyond a reasonable doubt.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule is tied to the text and context of the currency-reporting subchapter and creates no general ignorance-of-law defense.

**First controlling authority:** Ratzlaf v. United States, 510 U.S. 135 (1994), decided January 11, 1994, Willfulness in the anti-structuring offense; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Ratzlaf v. United States, 510 U.S. 135 (1994), decided January 11, 1994, Willfulness in the anti-structuring offense; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Ratzlaf v. United States, 510 U.S. 135 (1994), January 11, 1994.

### Mens rea for operative facts distinguishing lawful conduct from serious crime

**Governing question:** When statutory silence leaves uncertain whether mens rea reaches the physical characteristic or operative fact that separates familiar or potentially lawful conduct from a serious crime.

**Current rule:** When otherwise familiar or potentially lawful conduct becomes seriously criminal only because of a particular physical characteristic or operative fact, statutory silence does not displace the traditional mens rea presumption. The Government must prove knowledge of the characteristic or fact that makes the conduct unlawful, though not knowledge of the statute, legal classification, or precise legal boundary unless the enacted text separately requires it.

**Burdens and proof:** The Government must prove the required knowledge beyond a reasonable doubt.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not govern unmistakable contraband or conduct whose unlawful character is evident from possession or commission. It does not create a general ignorance-of-law defense.

**First controlling authority:** Staples v. United States, 511 U.S. 600 (1994), decided May 23, 1994, Mens rea for the regulated characteristic; established knowledge of the physical characteristic making an otherwise familiar firearm a machinegun.

**Controlling authorities:** Staples v. United States, 511 U.S. 600 (1994), decided May 23, 1994, supplies the physical-characteristic component. United States v. X-Citement Video, Inc., 513 U.S. 64, decided November 29, 1994, Scienter for the facts making a depiction unlawful, extends the presumption across statutory clauses to operative facts distinguishing lawful material from a serious crime.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. X-Citement Video, Inc., 513 U.S. 64, November 29, 1994.

**Development:**
- May 23, 1994, Staples v. United States: The Court required knowledge of the physical characteristic that made an otherwise familiar firearm a machinegun, while leaving broader operative-fact applications unresolved.
- November 29, 1994, United States v. X-Citement Video, Inc.: The Court extended the same mens-rea principle across intervening statutory clauses to require knowledge that a depiction was sexually explicit and involved a minor. The rule remains limited to facts that distinguish familiar or potentially lawful conduct from serious crime.

### Collateral attack on Armed Career Criminal Act predicates

**Governing question:** Which constitutional defects in an outstanding state conviction may be litigated during federal ACCA sentencing.

**Current rule:** A defendant may challenge an ACCA predicate during federal sentencing when the conviction was obtained after a complete denial of the Gideon right to counsel. Other constitutional challenges to an outstanding state conviction, including ineffective assistance and involuntary-plea claims, must first succeed through an authorized direct or collateral proceeding.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** The federal sentencing court determines whether an existing conviction qualifies; fact-intensive collateral challenges proceed in the convicting jurisdiction unless Congress supplies a broader sentencing-stage review mechanism.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule leaves open the procedure and disposition when a predicate is later vacated.

**First controlling authority:** Custis v. United States, 511 U.S. 485 (1994), decided May 23, 1994, Collateral attack on ACCA predicates; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Custis v. United States, 511 U.S. 485 (1994), decided May 23, 1994, Collateral attack on ACCA predicates; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Custis v. United States, 511 U.S. 485 (1994), May 23, 1994.

### Use of valid uncounseled fine-only misdemeanors in later sentencing

**Governing question:** When a valid uncounseled misdemeanor that resulted in no imprisonment may affect a later custodial sentence.

**Current rule:** A sentencing scheme may consider a valid fine-only uncounseled misdemeanor when the defendant has a meaningful opportunity to challenge the information’s accuracy and sentencing significance and the court has authority to correct a criminal-history category that materially overstates the defendant’s conduct or risk. The decision does not validate automatic, irrebuttable, or conclusive custodial effect.

**Burdens and proof:** The defendant must invoke the available individualized correction mechanism to preserve a claim that the resulting category materially overstates the record.

**Exceptions and limits:** *Baldasar* is not categorically overruled. This rule does not decide whether a valid uncounseled fine-only conviction may receive automatic, irrebuttable, or conclusive custodial effect under a scheme lacking meaningful rebuttal and individualized correction.

**First controlling authority:** Nichols v. United States, 511 U.S. 738 (1994), decided June 6, 1994, Use of Nichols’s prior conviction under the federal Guidelines; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Nichols v. United States, 511 U.S. 738 (1994), decided June 6, 1994, Use of Nichols’s prior conviction under the federal Guidelines; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Nichols v. United States, 511 U.S. 738 (1994), June 6, 1994.

### Overt acts under 21 U.S.C. § 846

**Governing question:** Whether a narcotics conspiracy under § 846 requires proof of an overt act in addition to agreement and knowing participation.

**Current rule:** Section 846 does not require the Government to allege or prove an overt act in furtherance of the conspiracy. A narcotics conspiracy under § 846 is complete upon formation of the unlawful agreement. The Government must prove the agreement and the defendant’s knowing participation beyond a reasonable doubt, but no additional overt act is an element.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not lessen the Government’s burden to prove an unlawful agreement and knowing participation. It does not determine the elements of differently worded conspiracy statutes.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Shabani, 513 U.S. 10, decided November 1, 1994, Overt acts under 21 U.S.C. § 846; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Shabani, 513 U.S. 10, decided November 1, 1994, Overt acts under 21 U.S.C. § 846; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Shabani, 513 U.S. 10, November 1, 1994.

### Mens rea and fair notice under the then-operative § 2119

**Governing question:** What mental state and knowledge the then-operative § 2119 required for carjacking.

**Current rule:** Section 2119 requires knowing participation in the taking of a covered vehicle by force, violence, or intimidation. Under the version containing the intent-to-cause-death-or-serious-bodily-harm element, the defendant must have made, at the moment of the demand or taking, a real decision to kill or cause serious bodily harm if that violence becomes necessary to complete the taking. The statute does not require knowledge that the vehicle moved through interstate commerce or a separate intent permanently to deprive the owner.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not construe materially different statutory language or remove mens rea from a fact Congress makes part of the wrongful conduct itself.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Harris, 25 F.3d 1275, decided April 27, 1995, Mens rea and fair notice under the then-operative § 2119; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Harris, 25 F.3d 1275, decided April 27, 1995, supplies the knowing-participation, interstate-commerce, and permanent-deprivation components. Holloway v. United States, 526 U.S. 1 (1999), decided March 2, 1999, supplies the conditional-intent component.

**Present operation effective:** Holloway v. United States, 526 U.S. 1 (1999), March 2, 1999.

**Development:**
- March 2, 1999, Holloway v. United States, 526 U.S. 1 (1999): A genuine decision to cause death or serious bodily harm if necessary satisfies the statute’s intent element when held at the moment of the demand or taking. The knowledge-of-commerce and permanent-deprivation rules remain unchanged.

### Federal courts under 18 U.S.C. § 1001

**Governing question:** Whether false statements in a federal judicial proceeding fall within § 1001’s department-or-agency jurisdiction.

**Current rule:** A federal court is neither a department nor an agency within § 1001, and the statute therefore does not criminalize false statements on the theory that a judicial proceeding is a matter within the jurisdiction of a federal department or agency. The terms department and agency in § 1001 are governed by their ordinary executive-branch meaning and the definitions applicable throughout Title 18. A federal court does not change character depending on whether it performs adjudicative, administrative, or housekeeping work.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not protect false statements made to executive agencies merely because litigation is pending. Congress may enact a different rule expressly covering the Judicial and Legislative Branches and may define exceptions appropriate to adversarial proceedings.

**First controlling authority:** Hubbard v. United States, 514 U.S. 695, decided May 15, 1995, Federal courts under 18 U.S.C. § 1001; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Hubbard v. United States, 514 U.S. 695, decided May 15, 1995, Federal courts under 18 U.S.C. § 1001; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Hubbard v. United States, 514 U.S. 695, May 15, 1995.

### Nexus between an obstructive act and a pending proceeding

**Governing question:** What nexus and knowledge § 1503 requires between an alleged obstructive act and a pending judicial or grand-jury proceeding.

**Current rule:** False statements to investigators do not violate § 1503 absent proof that the defendant knew the statements were likely to reach and obstruct a pending judicial or grand-jury proceeding. The alleged endeavor must have a relationship in time, causation, or logic to the proceeding. The defendant must know that the conduct is likely to affect that proceeding, and the conduct must possess the natural and probable effect of interference. An intent to mislead an investigation that remains independent of the court or grand jury is insufficient.

**Exceptions and limits:** Direct lies to a grand jury, destruction of subpoenaed material, witness tampering, or statements knowingly routed to a pending proceeding remain within § 1503 when the other elements are proved.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Aguilar, 515 U.S. 593, decided June 21, 1995, Nexus between an obstructive act and a pending proceeding; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Aguilar, 515 U.S. 593, decided June 21, 1995, Nexus between an obstructive act and a pending proceeding; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Aguilar, 515 U.S. 593, June 21, 1995.

### Disclosure after expiration of a wiretap authorization

**Governing question:** Whether § 2232(c) reaches a disclosure made after the identified wiretap authorization expires and what purpose it requires.

**Current rule:** Section 2232(c) reaches a disclosure made after the identified authorization expires when the defendant knew of the application or authorization, gave notice of the possible interception, and acted with the specific purpose of obstructing the interception. The statute does not require the application or authorization to remain formally pending at the moment of disclosure. “Such interception” identifies the interception associated with the application or authorization known to the defendant. “Possible interception” accounts for uncertainty about whether interception will occur.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Government must prove both knowledge and the specific obstructive purpose. Disclosure unrelated to an application, authorization, or possible interception is not covered.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Aguilar, 515 U.S. 593, decided June 21, 1995, Disclosure after expiration of a wiretap authorization; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Aguilar, 515 U.S. 593, decided June 21, 1995, Disclosure after expiration of a wiretap authorization; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Aguilar, 515 U.S. 593, June 21, 1995.

### Active employment of a firearm

**Governing question:** What conduct constitutes active employment of a firearm under § 924(c).

**Current rule:** Active employment includes firing or attempting to fire, brandishing, displaying, striking with, bartering, or referring to a firearm in a manner calculated to affect another person.
