Possession, concealment, storage, strategic placement, proximity, or availability may show why a firearm was kept, but those facts do not themselves constitute use.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not define the full scope of “carry,” decide constructive-possession rules under other statutes, or address later amendments using broader language. A court may not sustain either judgment on a carry theory unless the charging instrument, trial procedure, preservation, and evidence independently permit that disposition.

**First controlling authority:** Bailey v. United States; Robinson v. United States, 516 U.S. 137, decided December 6, 1995, Active employment of a firearm; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Bailey v. United States; Robinson v. United States, 516 U.S. 137, decided December 6, 1995, Active employment of a firearm; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Bailey v. United States; Robinson v. United States, 516 U.S. 137, December 6, 1995.

**Development:**
- December 6, 1995, Bailey v. United States; Robinson v. United States, 516 U.S. 137: The decision supplies the first controlling simulated construction of firearm “use” under § 924(c)(1). The existing mens rea rules from *Staples* and *X-Citement Video* are applied as principles of element preservation but are not altered.

### Appellate review of Guidelines departures

**Governing question:** How appellate courts divide legal, factual, and discretionary review of Sentencing Guidelines departures.

**Current rule:** Whether a factor is categorically forbidden, encouraged, discouraged, already accounted for, or unmentioned is a legal question subject to independent review. Historical findings receive clear-error review. Whether a legally available factor exists to an exceptional degree and places the case outside the applicable heartland is reviewed for abuse of discretion.

**First controlling authority:** Koon v. United States; Powell v. United States, 518 U.S. 81, decided June 13, 1996, Appellate review of Guidelines departures; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Koon v. United States; Powell v. United States, 518 U.S. 81, decided June 13, 1996, Appellate review of Guidelines departures; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Koon v. United States; Powell v. United States, 518 U.S. 81, June 13, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 13, 1996, Koon v. United States; Powell v. United States, 518 U.S. 81: The decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule for appellate review of Guidelines departures. *Ornelas* is distinguished: its independent constitutional review does not displace § 3742’s express due-deference command for sentencing judgments. Legal exclusions remain independently reviewable, historical facts receive clear-error review, and heartland application receives abuse-of-discretion review.

### Resentencing after mixed departure grounds

**Governing question:** When a sentence based on both valid and invalid departure factors requires resentencing.

**Current rule:** Resentencing is required because legally invalid factors contributed to the integrated departure and the record does not establish that the district court would have imposed the same sentences using only permissible considerations.

**First controlling authority:** Koon v. United States; Powell v. United States, 518 U.S. 81, decided June 13, 1996, Resentencing after mixed departure grounds; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Koon v. United States; Powell v. United States, 518 U.S. 81, decided June 13, 1996, Resentencing after mixed departure grounds; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Koon v. United States; Powell v. United States, 518 U.S. 81, June 13, 1996.

### Rule 11(f) and statutory authority for forfeiture

**Governing question:** What Rule 11(f) and § 853 require before criminal forfeiture may be imposed following a guilty plea.

**Current rule:** Criminal forfeiture under § 853 is punishment imposed following guilt, not a separate substantive charge to which another guilty plea is entered. Rule 11(f) therefore does not require a second guilty-plea factual-basis colloquy directed specifically to forfeiture. Section 853 independently limits forfeiture to property bearing the statutory relationship to the offense or qualifying as substitute property. A plea agreement does not enlarge the court’s statutory authority. The indictment, trial evidence, plea admissions, sentencing materials, and district court’s findings supplied adequate support for the forfeiture entered here.

**Exceptions and limits:** A bare omnibus forfeiture clause unsupported by admissions, evidence, or findings would not authorize a judgment exceeding § 853.

**First controlling authority:** Libretti v. United States, 516 U.S. 29, decided November 7, 1995, Rule 11(f) and statutory authority for forfeiture; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Libretti v. United States, 516 U.S. 29, decided November 7, 1995, Rule 11(f) and statutory authority for forfeiture; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Libretti v. United States, 516 U.S. 29, November 7, 1995.

**Development:**
- November 7, 1995, Libretti v. United States, 516 U.S. 29: Rule 11(f) does not create a second plea inquiry for punishment imposed through criminal forfeiture, while § 853’s statutory nexus remains an independent limit on the property forfeitable.

### Waiver of the Rule 31(e) jury determination

**Governing question:** When a defendant validly waives the former Rule 31(e) jury determination of forfeiture.

**Current rule:** The former Rule 31(e) special-verdict right is statutory and waivable. Libretti validly waived it by knowingly ending the ongoing jury trial, agreeing to forfeiture, and submitting the matter directly to sentencing, even though the court did not separately recite Rule 31(e)’s number and text.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide whether a materially different forfeiture statute making a fact alter the legally authorized maximum would present an independent constitutional jury question.

**First controlling authority:** Libretti v. United States, 516 U.S. 29, decided November 7, 1995, Waiver of the Rule 31(e) jury determination; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Libretti v. United States, 516 U.S. 29, decided November 7, 1995, Waiver of the Rule 31(e) jury determination; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Libretti v. United States, 516 U.S. 29, November 7, 1995.

### Acquitted conduct at Guidelines sentencing

**Governing question:** Whether a general acquittal categorically bars consideration of the underlying conduct at sentencing.

**Current rule:** A general acquittal does not categorically prevent a sentencing court from considering underlying conduct when reliable evidence proves it by a preponderance and the resulting sentence remains within punishment legally authorized by the conviction. The court must comply with the Guidelines, Rule 32, ordinary reliability requirements, and the governing burden. An accusation is not evidence, and the judge may not remove an offense element from the jury or exceed conviction-authorized punishment.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not decide unresolved reliability objections, require special verdicts, or excuse Rule 32 noncompliance.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Watts; United States v. Putra, 519 U.S. 148 (1997), decided January 6, 1997, Acquitted conduct at Guidelines sentencing; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Watts; United States v. Putra, 519 U.S. 148 (1997), decided January 6, 1997, Acquitted conduct at Guidelines sentencing; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Watts; United States v. Putra, 519 U.S. 148 (1997), January 6, 1997.

### Electronic transmission of obscenity and receiving-community venue

**Governing question:** How § 1465 applies to controlled interstate electronic delivery of obscene image files and where venue lies.

**Current rule:** A generally worded statute covering interstate transport or distribution of obscene pictures, images, and electrical transcriptions applies to later-developed electronic transmission when the conduct falls within the enacted terms. When an operator screens a subscriber, knows and controls the recipient’s district, approves access, and knowingly transmits obscene files there, venue is proper in the receiving district and that receiving community supplies Miller’s geographic standards.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not decide open websites, anonymous access, unavoidable routing, publishers unable to know or control recipient location, or statutes confined to specifically enumerated physical media. It creates no national cyberspace obscenity standard.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Thomas, 74 F.3d 701, decided May 19, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, Section 1465, electronic image files, venue, and receiving-community standards; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Thomas, 74 F.3d 701, decided May 19, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date, Section 1465, electronic image files, venue, and receiving-community standards; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Thomas, 74 F.3d 701, May 19, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date.

### False denials to federal investigators

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs false denial to federal investigators.

**Current rule:** Section 1001 applies to a knowingly false, material negative answer given to federal investigators. The statute contains no judicially created exculpatory-no exception.

**First controlling authority:** Brogan v. United States, 522 U.S. 398 (1998), decided January 26, 1998, False denial to federal investigators; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Brogan v. United States, 522 U.S. 398 (1998), decided January 26, 1998, False denial to federal investigators; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Brogan v. United States, 522 U.S. 398 (1998), January 26, 1998.

### Active commercial use under the federal arson statute

**Governing question:** When a building is “used in interstate or foreign commerce or in any activity affecting interstate or foreign commerce” within 18 U.S.C. § 844(i).

**Current rule:** Section 844(i) asks what function the building itself was actively serving when the arson occurred. Rental property, a business, or a materially mixed-use property may satisfy the statute because the building is actively employed in commercial activity. An owner-occupied dwelling used only for ordinary family life, or a church devoted to worship, is not covered merely because ownership or operation entails purchases, utilities, insurance, employment, donations, mail, a mortgage, or national affiliation.

**Controlling construction:** “Used” requires active employment of the building in commerce or commerce-affecting activity, not passive, past, or ordinary incidents of ownership.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not exclude rental, business, or materially mixed-use property and does not decide coverage from labels alone. Courts examine the building’s actual function at the relevant time.

**First controlling authority:** *United States v. Hinds*, 125 F.3d 849, Supreme Court simulation decided April 6, 1998, noncommercial use of a church building; established the original rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *United States v. Hinds*, 125 F.3d 849, Supreme Court simulation decided April 6, 1998, supplies the rule for a worship building and rejects ordinary operational connections as sufficient. *Jones v. United States*, 529 U.S. 848, decided May 22, 2000, supplies the active-use construction, the owner-occupied-home exclusion, and the rental, business, and mixed-use boundaries.

**Present operation effective:** *Jones v. United States*, May 22, 2000.

**Development:**
- May 22, 2000, *Jones v. United States*: The prior rule held that a worship building was not covered merely because of ordinary operational connections to commerce. *Jones* adopts the broader controlling construction that “used” requires active commercial employment of the building, excludes an owner-occupied home devoted to ordinary family life, and preserves coverage for rental, business, and materially mixed-use property. Former title: Commercial use under the federal arson statute.

### Illegal gratuities and specific official acts

**Governing question:** What connection 18 U.S.C. § 201(c)(1)(A) requires between a thing of value and official action.

**Current rule:** An illegal gratuity requires proof that the thing of value was given for or because of a specific official act. The Government need not prove the corrupt agreement required for bribery, but generalized goodwill toward an officeholder or a gift given solely because of official status is insufficient.

**Burdens and proof:** The Government must identify the specific official act and prove the required link beyond a reasonable doubt.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Sun-Diamond Growers of California, 526 U.S. 398 (1999), decided April 27, 1999, specific-official-act requirement for an illegal gratuity; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Sun-Diamond Growers of California, 526 U.S. 398 (1999), decided April 27, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Sun-Diamond Growers of California, 526 U.S. 398 (1999), April 27, 1999.

### Excess imprisonment and commencement of supervised release

**Governing question:** Whether excess imprisonment automatically reduces a term of supervised release and how that custody may be considered.

**Current rule:** Under 18 U.S.C. § 3624(e), supervised release begins when the person is actually released from imprisonment and does not run during imprisonment. Excess custody therefore does not automatically shorten the term. A district court may consider excess incarceration when exercising its authority under 18 U.S.C. § 3583(e) to modify conditions or, after the statutory minimum, terminate supervision early.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** The actual-release date determines commencement. Equitable consequences of excess custody are addressed by the sentencing court through § 3583(e), not by automatic credit against the term.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Johnson, 529 U.S. 53 (2000), decided March 1, 2000, excess imprisonment and supervised release; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Johnson, 529 U.S. 53 (2000), decided March 1, 2000, supplies the actual-release construction of § 3624(e) and the modification and early-termination routes under § 3583(e).

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Johnson, 529 U.S. 53 (2000), March 1, 2000.

### Federal program benefits under 18 U.S.C. § 666(b)

**Governing question:** When federal payments to an organization constitute “benefits” received under a federal program for purposes of 18 U.S.C. § 666(b).

**Current rule:** An organization receives federal program benefits when continuing federal payments materially support its operations and advance an ongoing federal programmatic purpose. The inquiry does not turn solely on traceability of federal dollars or on whether another person is the program’s primary beneficiary.

**Exceptions and limits:** Federal origin alone does not convert ordinary procurement, a one-time market transaction, or payments lacking a continuing programmatic relationship into benefits. Satisfaction of § 666(b) does not eliminate the offense’s remaining elements.

**First controlling authority:** *Fischer v. United States*, 529 U.S. 667, decided May 15, 2000, federal program benefits received by a participating organization; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Fischer v. United States*, 529 U.S. 667, decided May 15, 2000, supplies the operational-support and ongoing-programmatic-purpose requirements and the ordinary-transaction limit.

**Present operation effective:** *Fischer v. United States*, May 15, 2000.

## Criminal Procedure

### Objective custody under Miranda

**Governing question:** What role an officer’s undisclosed suspicions or communicated views play in determining Miranda custody.

**Current rule:** Miranda custody turns on the objective circumstances manifested to the person questioned and whether those circumstances impose restraint comparable to formal arrest. An officer’s undisclosed belief that the person is or is not a suspect is legally irrelevant. Communicated suspicion is relevant only insofar as it would affect a reasonable person’s understanding of the freedom to end the encounter and leave, and it is not automatically dispositive.

**Exceptions and limits:** An undisclosed view may be used to test credibility concerning what occurred, but it does not itself enter the custody standard.

**First controlling authority:** Stansbury v. California, 511 U.S. 318 (1994), decided April 26, 1994, Objective custody under Miranda; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Stansbury v. California, 511 U.S. 318 (1994), decided April 26, 1994, Objective custody under Miranda; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Stansbury v. California, 511 U.S. 318 (1994), April 26, 1994.

### Neutral clarification of an ambiguous request for counsel

**Governing question:** What officers must do when a reasonable officer would understand a suspect’s words as a genuine but ambiguous possible request for counsel after a Miranda waiver.

**Current rule:** Substantive interrogation must pause while officers ask neutral questions directed solely to whether counsel is being requested. A clear invocation ends interrogation under Edwards; a clear disclaimer permits substantive questioning to resume. Clarification may not become persuasion, argument, discouragement of counsel, or questioning about the offense.

**Exceptions and limits:** A passing reference that no reasonable officer would understand as a possible request does not activate the clarification requirement.

**First controlling authority:** Davis v. United States, 512 U.S. 452 (1994), decided June 24, 1994, Ambiguous possible request for counsel after waiver; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Davis v. United States, 512 U.S. 452 (1994), decided June 24, 1994, Ambiguous possible request for counsel after waiver; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Davis v. United States, 512 U.S. 452 (1994), June 24, 1994.

### Civil and criminal classification of contempt fines

**Governing question:** When a contempt fine is civil rather than criminal and what process a serious criminal fine requires.

**Current rule:** A contempt fine is civil when it compensates proved loss or coerces present compliance while leaving the contemnor a meaningful opportunity to avoid or purge the sanction. A fixed, determinate, noncompensatory, nonpurgeable fine for completed misconduct is punitive and therefore criminal. Serious criminal contempt fines may be imposed only through criminal proceedings that include the right to a jury trial.

**Exceptions and limits:** Courts retain summary authority over petty direct contempt necessary to maintain courtroom order and may use genuinely purgeable coercive sanctions or compensation tied to proved loss.

**First controlling authority:** International Union, United Mine Workers of America v. Bagwell, 512 U.S. 821 (1994), decided June 30, 1994, Classification and procedure for serious contempt fines; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** International Union, United Mine Workers of America v. Bagwell, 512 U.S. 821 (1994), decided June 30, 1994, Classification and procedure for serious contempt fines; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** International Union, United Mine Workers of America v. Bagwell, 512 U.S. 821 (1994), June 30, 1994.

### Waiver of the plea-statement exclusions

**Governing question:** When a pre-proffer agreement validly waives Rule 410 and Rule 11(e)(6) protections for impeachment use.

**Current rule:** Rules 410 and 11(e)(6) do not categorically prohibit enforcement of a pre-proffer agreement permitting plea-discussion statements to be used to impeach the defendant’s inconsistent testimony. Evidentiary protections are ordinarily waivable. An impeachment waiver is enforceable unless the defendant establishes an affirmative basis for concluding that the agreement resulted from fraud, coercion, lack of understanding, or involuntariness.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide waivers authorizing use in the Government’s case in chief, at sentencing, or in response to defense evidence or argument when the defendant has not personally testified.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Mezzanatto, 513 U.S. 196, decided January 18, 1995, Waiver of the plea-statement exclusions; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Mezzanatto, 513 U.S. 196, decided January 18, 1995, Waiver of the plea-statement exclusions; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Mezzanatto, 513 U.S. 196, January 18, 1995.

### Cumulative Brady materiality and prosecution-team responsibility

**Governing question:** How courts aggregate suppressed favorable evidence and assign prosecution-team responsibility in Brady materiality review.

**Current rule:** The State violates *Brady* when favorable evidence suppressed by the prosecution team, considered collectively in the context of the complete record, creates a reasonable probability of a different result sufficient to undermine confidence in the verdict. The suppressed evidence in Kyles’s case satisfies that standard. Materiality does not require proof by a preponderance that disclosure would have produced acquittal, and it is not a sufficiency-of-the-remaining-evidence inquiry. Courts must evaluate the net effect of all suppressed favorable evidence together. The prosecutor bears responsibility for favorable evidence known to police and other government actors participating in the investigation, whether or not those actors communicated it to the trial prosecutor. Once *Brady* materiality is established, no separate harmless-error inquiry follows.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not require open-file discovery, make every nondisclosure structural error, or relieve the defense of ordinary responsibility for evidence already known and available to it.

**First controlling authority:** Kyles v. Whitley, 514 U.S. 419, decided April 19, 1995, Cumulative Brady materiality and prosecution-team responsibility; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Kyles v. Whitley, 514 U.S. 419, decided April 19, 1995, Cumulative Brady materiality and prosecution-team responsibility; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Kyles v. Whitley, 514 U.S. 419, April 19, 1995.

### Charging and jury determination of maximum-increasing elements

**Governing question:** What charging and jury protections apply to a fact that increases the maximum punishment authorized by the charged offense.

**Current rule:** A fact that increases the maximum punishment authorized by the charged offense is an element of the aggravated offense, including a prior-conviction fact used to increase that maximum. The element must be alleged in the indictment and either admitted as an element of that charged offense or proved to a jury beyond a reasonable doubt. A later admission that the fact exists does not cure an indictment that charged only the lesser offense. A sentencing judge may find facts under ordinary sentencing rules only within the punishment already authorized by the conviction. When materiality is an offense element, the judge supplies its legal definition and the jury determines beyond a reasonable doubt whether the proved statement had the required tendency or capacity to influence.

**Exceptions and limits:** Simulated United States v. Watts remains limited to factfinding within conviction-authorized punishment. Custis remains limited to collateral attacks on outstanding convictions. Old Chief remains controlling on admission or stipulation to a prior-conviction element without prejudicial detail.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Gaudin, 515 U.S. 506, decided June 19, 1995, jury determination of an offense element; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Gaudin, 515 U.S. 506, decided June 19, 1995; Almendarez-Torres v. United States, 523 U.S. 224 (1998), decided March 24, 1998, maximum-increasing aggravated-felony element; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Almendarez-Torres v. United States, 523 U.S. 224 (1998), March 24, 1998.

**Development:**
- March 24, 1998, Almendarez-Torres v. United States: Gaudin extends from a fact already classified as an element to a fact whose maximum-increasing effect makes it an element. McMillan remains applicable only to genuine sentencing considerations that do not raise conviction-authorized punishment. Former title: Jury determination of materiality.

### Source of discovery authority

**Governing question:** What legal source authorizes discovery of prosecutorial charging files for a selective-prosecution claim.

**Current rule:** Federal Rule of Criminal Procedure 16(a)(1)(C) does not itself authorize discovery of prosecutorial charging files to prepare a selective-prosecution claim.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide Rule 16’s treatment of every affirmative or collateral defense.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Armstrong, 517 U.S. 456, decided May 13, 1996, Source of discovery authority; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Armstrong, 517 U.S. 456, decided May 13, 1996, Source of discovery authority; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Armstrong, 517 U.S. 456, May 13, 1996.

### Direct-fruit just cause in trial contempt

**Governing question:** When an intercepted speaker has just cause to refuse trial questions derived from unlawful surveillance.

**Current rule:** When trial questions are direct fruits of unlawful interception of the witness’s own calls, and no independent source, attenuation, objectively reasonable reliance, or other lawful basis permits the questions, refusal constitutes just cause under 28 U.S.C. § 1826(a). Derivation is determined question by question and does not create general testimonial immunity.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule is confined to direct-fruit use against the intercepted speaker at trial and does not decide ordinary grand-jury witnesses, remote derivative evidence, or independently sourced questions.

**First controlling authority:** In re Askin, 47 F.3d 100, decided December 9, 1996, Just cause and civil contempt; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** In re Askin, 47 F.3d 100, decided December 9, 1996, Just cause and civil contempt; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** In re Askin, 47 F.3d 100, December 9, 1996.

### Facially incriminating redaction of a codefendant confession

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs facially incriminating redacted confession.

**Current rule:** A nontestifying codefendant’s confession remains within *Bruton* when a conspicuous blank, deletion, symbol, or equivalent substitution directly identifies the defendant from the statement and its immediate context. A limiting instruction does not cure the resulting confrontation violation.

**First controlling authority:** Gray v. Maryland, 523 U.S. 185 (1998), decided March 9, 1998, Facially incriminating redacted confession; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Gray v. Maryland, 523 U.S. 185 (1998), decided March 9, 1998, Facially incriminating redacted confession; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Gray v. Maryland, 523 U.S. 185 (1998), March 9, 1998.

### Self-incrimination at a voluntary clemency interview

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs compulsion at the voluntary interview.

**Current rule:** An optional clemency interview does not compel self-incrimination merely because a prisoner must choose between remaining silent and presenting information that may improve the clemency request.

**First controlling authority:** Ohio Adult Parole Authority v. Woodard, 523 U.S. 272 (1998), decided March 25, 1998, Compulsion at the voluntary interview; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Ohio Adult Parole Authority v. Woodard, 523 U.S. 272 (1998), decided March 25, 1998, Compulsion at the voluntary interview; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Ohio Adult Parole Authority v. Woodard, 523 U.S. 272 (1998), March 25, 1998.

### Categorical exclusion of polygraph evidence

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs categorical exclusion of polygraph evidence.

**Current rule:** Military Rule of Evidence 707 does not violate the constitutional right to present a defense. A neutral evidentiary rule may categorically exclude a class of disputed scientific evidence when the exclusion is reasonably related to legitimate reliability concerns and does not substantially disable presentation of the accused’s underlying factual defense.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Scheffer, 523 U.S. 303 (1998), decided March 31, 1998, Categorical exclusion of polygraph evidence; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Scheffer, 523 U.S. 303 (1998), decided March 31, 1998, Categorical exclusion of polygraph evidence; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Scheffer, 523 U.S. 303 (1998), March 31, 1998.

### Judicial entry into closed court-martial deliberations

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs judicial entry into closed deliberations.

**Current rule:** A military judge violates Article 39(b) by entering the members’ closed deliberative session without the accused and counsel, but the resulting presumption of prejudice may be rebutted when a complete record establishes that the contact was exclusively ministerial, concerned findings already reached, and affected no substantial right.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Thompson, 47 M.J. 378, decided May 21, 1998, Judicial entry into closed deliberations; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Thompson, 47 M.J. 378, decided May 21, 1998, Judicial entry into closed deliberations; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Thompson, 47 M.J. 378, May 21, 1998.

### Self-incrimination and independent foreign prosecution

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs fear of prosecution by an independent foreign sovereign.

**Current rule:** A reasonable fear of prosecution by an independent foreign sovereign, without more, does not permit a witness to refuse testimony under the Fifth Amendment.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Balsys, 524 U.S. 666 (1998), decided June 25, 1998, Fear of prosecution by an independent foreign sovereign; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Balsys, 524 U.S. 666 (1998), decided June 25, 1998, Fear of prosecution by an independent foreign sovereign; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Balsys, 524 U.S. 666 (1998), June 25, 1998.

### Standing to challenge racial exclusion from a charging grand jury

**Governing question:** Who may challenge racial discrimination in selection of the grand jury that exercises charging authority.

**Current rule:** A criminal defendant may assert his own due-process objection when alleged racial discrimination affected the grand jury exercising charging authority over him. A defendant outside the excluded race may also assert excluded citizens’ equal-protection rights when he is directly injured by the selection process, has the required close litigation relationship, and practical barriers hinder separate suits.

**First controlling authority:** Campbell v. Louisiana, 523 U.S. 392 (1998), decided April 21, 1998, standing to challenge racial exclusion from a charging grand jury; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Campbell v. Louisiana, 523 U.S. 392 (1998), decided April 21, 1998, standing to challenge racial exclusion from a charging grand jury; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Campbell v. Louisiana, 523 U.S. 392 (1998), April 21, 1998.

### Venue for an offense incorporating a continuing predicate

**Governing question:** Where venue lies when a charged offense incorporates commission of a continuing predicate crime as essential conduct.

**Current rule:** Venue lies in every district where the incorporated continuing predicate offense occurs, even when another conduct component occurs only elsewhere. For a § 924(c) charge predicated on continuing kidnapping, the firearm offense’s essential conduct includes use or carriage during and in relation to the kidnapping, so venue lies in every district through which the unlawful confinement and transportation continue.

**Exceptions and limits:** Venue may not rest on effects alone. The district must contain an essential conduct element of the charged offense or its incorporated continuing predicate.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Rodriguez-Moreno, 526 U.S. 275 (1999), decided March 30, 1999, venue tied to a continuing predicate offense; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Rodriguez-Moreno, 526 U.S. 275 (1999), decided March 30, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Rodriguez-Moreno, 526 U.S. 275 (1999), March 30, 1999.

### Self-incrimination at sentencing after a guilty plea

**Governing question:** Whether a guilty plea waives the Fifth Amendment privilege as to disputed sentencing facts and whether silence supports an adverse inference.

**Current rule:** A guilty plea does not waive the privilege at sentencing with respect to disputed facts bearing on punishment. The sentencing court may not draw an adverse factual inference from the defendant’s silence when determining the circumstances and details of the offense that affect punishment.

**Exceptions and limits:** A defendant may separately waive the privilege through voluntary testimony or another legally sufficient act. The rule does not govern an optional executive-clemency interview after conviction and sentence.

**First controlling authority:** Mitchell v. United States, 526 U.S. 314 (1999), decided April 5, 1999, effect of a guilty plea and sentencing silence; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Mitchell v. United States, 526 U.S. 314 (1999), decided April 5, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Mitchell v. United States, 526 U.S. 314 (1999), April 5, 1999.

### Jury unanimity on continuing-series violations

**Governing question:** What unanimity 21 U.S.C. § 848 requires for the violations constituting a continuing series.

**Current rule:** Each violation necessary to constitute the continuing series is an offense element. The jury must unanimously identify the same qualifying violations beyond a reasonable doubt, although jurors need not agree on every evidentiary detail or means of committing an agreed predicate.

**First controlling authority:** Richardson v. United States, 526 U.S. 813 (1999), decided June 1, 1999, unanimity on continuing-series violations; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Richardson v. United States, 526 U.S. 813 (1999), decided June 1, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Richardson v. United States, 526 U.S. 813 (1999), June 1, 1999.

### Harmless-error review of an omitted offense element

**Governing question:** When complete withdrawal of an unadmitted offense element from the jury may be harmless.

**Current rule:** Complete withdrawal of an unadmitted element from the jury is constitutional error. The error may be harmless when facts the jury actually and validly found necessarily embrace the omitted element. An appellate conclusion that the element was uncontested and supported by overwhelming evidence is not, standing alone, sufficient; harmlessness must rest on the constitutional adjudicative record rather than a hypothesized guilty verdict.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not treat every element omission as structural and does not permit an appellate court to make a genuinely disputed elemental finding the jury never necessarily made.

**First controlling authority:** Neder v. United States, 527 U.S. 1 (1999), decided June 10, 1999, consequence of withholding an offense element; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Neder v. United States, 527 U.S. 1 (1999), decided June 10, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Neder v. United States, 527 U.S. 1 (1999), June 10, 1999.

### Blame-shifting custodial accomplice confessions

**Governing question:** When a nontestifying accomplice’s custodial confession shifting blame to the accused may be admitted consistently with the Confrontation Clause.

**Current rule:** A custodial accomplice confession whose portions used against the accused minimize the declarant’s responsibility and assign the charged crime to the accused is not admissible merely as a statement against penal interest. Without prior cross-examination, admission requires particularized guarantees of trustworthiness arising from the statement’s own circumstances; corroborating trial evidence cannot supply those guarantees.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** Separate genuinely self-inculpatory portions from collateral or blame-shifting accusations, then assess any residual trustworthiness from the circumstances of the statement itself.

**Exceptions and limits:** The statement-against-interest category is not firmly rooted for blame-shifting accomplice accusations. The rule does not decide an accusation previously subjected to adequate cross-examination.

**First controlling authority:** Lilly v. Virginia, 527 U.S. 116 (1999), decided June 10, 1999, blame-shifting custodial confession; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Lilly v. Virginia, 527 U.S. 116 (1999), decided June 10, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Lilly v. Virginia, 527 U.S. 116 (1999), June 10, 1999.

### Equivalent no-merit procedure on a first appeal as of right

**Governing question:** What an alternative to the precise Anders procedure must preserve when appointed counsel finds no nonfrivolous issue on a first appeal as of right.

**Current rule:** A State may use a procedure different from the precise Anders format, but a constitutionally equivalent procedure must preserve appointed counsel’s active advocate role and focused identification of issues that might arguably support the appeal. Counsel may not withhold every potentially supportive issue and shift the issue-spotting function to the appellate court or the indigent appellant. Independent judicial review is a safeguard, not a substitute for counsel’s adversarial presentation.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Constitution does not require one filing label or withdrawal mechanism. A failure to identify a genuinely nonfrivolous issue may also present an ineffective-assistance question, but a procedure that authorizes wholesale substitution of judicial screening for advocacy is itself inadequate.

**First controlling authority:** Smith v. Robbins, 528 U.S. 259 (2000), decided January 19, 2000, equivalent advocacy under an alternative to Anders; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Smith v. Robbins, 528 U.S. 259 (2000), decided January 19, 2000, supplies the active-advocate requirement, the focused-identification requirement, and the limit on judicial substitution.

**Present operation effective:** Smith v. Robbins, 528 U.S. 259 (2000), January 19, 2000.

### Curative use of an allotted peremptory challenge

**Governing question:** Whether using an allotted peremptory challenge to cure an erroneous denial of a for-cause challenge violates due process or Federal Rule of Criminal Procedure 24.

**Current rule:** A defendant who preserves the for-cause objection, uses an allotted peremptory challenge to remove the juror, receives the full number of challenges prescribed by Rule 24, and is tried by an impartial jury suffers no constitutional or Rule 24 deprivation merely because the remaining challenges are exhausted. Rule 24 guarantees the prescribed number of challenges, not that none will be used to correct a for-cause error.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not approve the for-cause error, require a defendant to cure it, or govern a biased seated juror, denial of the prescribed allotment, deliberate manipulation of for-cause rulings, or an independent Batson or J.E.B. violation. The Court leaves open a claim involving curative use plus identification of another juror the defendant otherwise would have struck.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Martinez-Salazar, 528 U.S. 304 (2000), decided January 19, 2000, curative use of an allotted peremptory challenge; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Martinez-Salazar, 528 U.S. 304 (2000), decided January 19, 2000, supplies the current rule and its limits.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Martinez-Salazar, 528 U.S. 304 (2000), January 19, 2000.

### Counsel’s duty to consult about an appeal

**Governing question:** What Strickland standard governs counsel’s failure to file a notice of appeal when the defendant gave no clear instruction to appeal or not to appeal.

**Current rule:** Counsel must follow an express instruction to file an appeal and respect an express and competent decision not to appeal. Without a clear instruction, counsel must reasonably consult when a rational defendant would want an appeal or the particular defendant has demonstrated an interest in appealing. Consultation requires advice about the advantages and disadvantages of appeal and a reasonable effort to determine the client’s wishes.

**Burdens and proof:** Deficient performance is determined from all relevant circumstances, including whether conviction followed trial or plea, whether nonfrivolous grounds were apparent, whether the defendant received the expected sentence, and whether the defendant expressed interest in review. Prejudice requires a reasonable probability that, but for deficient performance, the defendant would have taken a timely appeal; the defendant need not separately prove that the lost appeal probably would have succeeded.

**Exceptions and limits:** There is no automatic duty to file or consult in every case when the defendant gave no instruction. The rule governs the antecedent decision whether to appeal and does not alter the active-advocacy requirements after a first appeal begins.

**First controlling authority:** Roe v. Flores-Ortega, 528 U.S. 470 (2000), decided February 23, 2000, failure to consult or file a notice of appeal; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Roe v. Flores-Ortega, 528 U.S. 470 (2000), decided February 23, 2000, supplies the duty-to-consult trigger, consultation content, and lost-appeal prejudice inquiry.

**Present operation effective:** Roe v. Flores-Ortega, 528 U.S. 470 (2000), February 23, 2000.

**Related current rules:** Equivalent no-merit procedure on a first appeal as of right. That rule governs advocacy after a first appeal exists; this rule governs counsel’s antecedent duty concerning whether to initiate it.

### Immunized testimonial production of documents

**Governing question:** When compelled production of documents is testimonial and what use-and-derivative-use immunity requires.

**Current rule:** Compliance with a broad document subpoena is testimonial when the witness must identify, locate, select, and assemble responsive documents and the Government did not already know the documents’ existence, location, and possession with reasonable particularity. When that act of production is immunized, the Government may not use the testimonial act or any direct or indirect fruits to investigate or prosecute the witness.

**Burdens and proof:** To prosecute after immunized testimonial production, the Government bears the burden to prove that every item of evidence it proposes to use came from a source wholly independent of the compelled act.

**Exceptions and limits:** The voluntarily prepared contents of existing documents are not protected merely because the documents are later compelled. The rule does not alter collective-entity doctrine or bar prosecution supported wholly by genuinely independent evidence. The foregone-conclusion boundary applies only when the Government possessed the required prior knowledge with reasonable particularity.

**First controlling authority:** *United States v. Hubbell*, 530 U.S. 27 (2000), decided June 5, 2000, testimonial document production and use-and-derivative-use immunity; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *United States v. Hubbell*, 530 U.S. 27 (2000), decided June 5, 2000, supplies the reasonable-particularity threshold, testimonial-production rule, independent-source burden, and document-content and collective-entity limits.

**Present operation effective:** *United States v. Hubbell*, 530 U.S. 27 (2000), June 5, 2000.

### Miranda safeguards and congressional replacement

**Governing question:** Whether Congress may replace the constitutional safeguards governing custodial interrogation with a voluntariness-only admissibility rule.

**Current rule:** *Miranda* and its constitutional progeny govern the admissibility in state and federal courts of statements obtained through custodial interrogation. Congress may supplement that framework or provide genuinely equivalent safeguards, but it may not replace the framework with voluntariness as the sole rule for admission in the prosecution’s case in chief.

**Controlling construction:** 18 U.S.C. § 3501 is inoperative to the extent it makes voluntariness alone sufficient to admit a custodial statement obtained without the governing constitutional safeguards.

**Exceptions and limits:** Existing rules governing custody, interrogation, waiver, genuinely equivalent safeguards, recognized exceptions, and uses outside the prosecution’s case in chief remain intact.

**First controlling authority:** *Dickerson v. United States*, 530 U.S. 428 (2000), decided June 26, 2000, constitutional status of *Miranda* and congressional replacement through § 3501; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Dickerson v. United States*, 530 U.S. 428 (2000), decided June 26, 2000, supplies the constitutional-status, state-and-federal operation, equivalent-safeguards, and § 3501 replacement components.

**Present operation effective:** *Dickerson v. United States*, 530 U.S. 428 (2000), June 26, 2000.

## Habeas Corpus

### Actual innocence as a gateway to defaulted conviction claims

**Governing question:** What factual-innocence showing permits review of a defaulted conviction claim, including a challenge to a guilty plea.

**Current rule:** A defaulted guilty-plea or trial challenge may pass through the actual-innocence gateway when, on the whole admissible record, it is more likely than not that no reasonable juror would convict under the correct elements. The showing must concern factual innocence, not merely legal insufficiency. For a guilty plea, the Government may use admissible evidence and require innocence of a genuinely more serious charge actually forgone, but may not substitute an uncharged alternative theory merely because it might fit the facts.

**First controlling authority:** Schlup v. Delo, 513 U.S. 298, decided January 23, 1995, actual-innocence gateway for defaulted trial claims; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Schlup v. Delo, 513 U.S. 298, decided January 23, 1995; Bousley v. United States, 523 U.S. 614 (1998), decided May 18, 1998, guilty-plea application; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Bousley v. United States, 523 U.S. 614 (1998), May 18, 1998.

**Development:**
- May 18, 1998, Bousley v. United States: Schlup extends from defaulted trial claims to defaulted guilty-plea challenges; perceived futility alone remains insufficient cause. Former title: Actual innocence as a gateway to defaulted trial claims.

### Grave doubt under the federal habeas harmless-error standard

**Governing question:** How a federal habeas court treats constitutional error when the record leaves the judge in grave doubt under Brecht.

**Current rule:** When a federal habeas court finds constitutional trial error and remains in grave doubt about whether the error had a substantial and injurious effect or influence on the verdict, the error is not harmless. Grave doubt exists when, after a conscientious examination of the complete record, the reviewing judge is in virtual equipoise about the error’s effect on the verdict. The rule does not permit a court to avoid making a difficult judgment when the evidence supports a reasoned conclusion one way or the other.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not shift the burden on every habeas issue, alter the substantive *Brecht* standard, decide structural error, or establish that O’Neal’s instruction was unconstitutional.

**First controlling authority:** O’Neal v. McAninch, 513 U.S. 432, decided February 21, 1995, Grave doubt under the federal habeas harmless-error standard; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** O’Neal v. McAninch, 513 U.S. 432, decided February 21, 1995, Grave doubt under the federal habeas harmless-error standard; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** O’Neal v. McAninch, 513 U.S. 432, February 21, 1995.

### Custody under consecutive sentences

**Governing question:** When a prisoner remains in custody under an earlier conviction in a consecutive-sentence sequence.

**Current rule:** A prisoner serving consecutive sentences remains in custody under the conviction supporting an earlier sentence already served in sequence when invalidating that conviction would advance the date of release from the aggregate term. Consecutive sentences are treated as a continuous stream of custody for purposes of 28 U.S.C. § 2254. Formal completion of one segment does not defeat jurisdiction while that conviction continues to postpone release from the undischarged aggregate confinement.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not revive a challenge after all custody has fully ended when the petitioner relies solely on collateral consequences insufficient under otherwise governing law.

**First controlling authority:** Garlotte v. Fordice, 515 U.S. 39, decided May 30, 1995, Custody under consecutive sentences; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Garlotte v. Fordice, 515 U.S. 39, decided May 30, 1995, Custody under consecutive sentences; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Garlotte v. Fordice, 515 U.S. 39, May 30, 1995.

### Rule-governed disposition of a first federal petition

**Governing question:** Which rule-governed doctrines may dispose of a delayed first federal petition, and whether general disapproval of delay suffices.

**Current rule:** Delay may have the consequences assigned by Rule 9, exhaustion, procedural default, adverse evidentiary rules, or another applicable doctrine. A court must identify and apply the governing source rather than convert general disapproval of filing conduct into an independent ground of dismissal.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court decides no habeas claim, exhaustion issue, procedural default, Rule 9 defense, or abuse question not resolved below.

**First controlling authority:** Lonchar v. Thomas, 517 U.S. 314, decided April 1, 1996, Rule-governed disposition of a first federal petition; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Lonchar v. Thomas, 517 U.S. 314, decided April 1, 1996, Rule-governed disposition of a first federal petition; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Lonchar v. Thomas, 517 U.S. 314, April 1, 1996.

**Development:**
- April 1, 1996, Lonchar v. Thomas, 517 U.S. 314: The current rule governing capital habeas counsel and prepetition stay authority is clarified. A stay remains neither automatic nor indefinite, but a court cannot permit execution to moot a first petition that has not been lawfully dismissed.

### Original habeas jurisdiction and AEDPA

**Governing question:** Whether AEDPA repealed the Supreme Court’s original habeas jurisdiction.

**Current rule:** AEDPA did not repeal the Supreme Court’s original habeas jurisdiction.

**First controlling authority:** Felker v. Turpin, 518 U.S. 651, decided June 28, 1996, Original habeas jurisdiction and AEDPA; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Felker v. Turpin, 518 U.S. 651, decided June 28, 1996, Original habeas jurisdiction and AEDPA; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Felker v. Turpin, 518 U.S. 651, June 28, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 28, 1996, Felker v. Turpin, 518 U.S. 651: AEDPA’s gatekeeping provisions do not repeal the Supreme Court’s original habeas jurisdiction; the surviving jurisdiction remains governed by its own demanding exercise requirements.

### Successive claims and suspension of the writ

**Governing question:** What restrictions a petitioner must satisfy when seeking the Supreme Court’s original successive writ.

**Current rule:** A state prisoner seeking an original successive writ must satisfy the restrictions governing repetitive or newly presented claims and must also establish the exceptional circumstances required for the Supreme Court’s exercise of original habeas authority.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide a case in which a reliably demonstrated innocence claim would otherwise lack every lawful forum, or whether Congress could abolish all Supreme Court habeas review.

**First controlling authority:** Felker v. Turpin, 518 U.S. 651, decided June 28, 1996, Successive claims and suspension of the writ; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Felker v. Turpin, 518 U.S. 651, decided June 28, 1996, Successive claims and suspension of the writ; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Felker v. Turpin, 518 U.S. 651, June 28, 1996.

### Supreme Court review of appellate AEDPA gatekeeping rulings

**Governing question:** Which appellate AEDPA rulings are reviewable by the Supreme Court.

**Current rule:** Section 2244(b)(3)(E) bars appeal and certiorari from a court of appeals’ actual decision granting or denying authorization to file a successive application. It does not bar certiorari review of a ruling that no second or successive application exists. A certificate-of-appealability application adjudicated by a circuit judge or court-of-appeals panel is a case in the court of appeals reviewable under § 1254(1).

**First controlling authority:** Felker v. Turpin, 518 U.S. 651, decided June 28, 1996, bar on review of actual authorization decisions; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Felker v. Turpin, 518 U.S. 651, decided June 28, 1996; Stewart v. Martinez-Villareal, 523 U.S. 637 (1998), decided May 18, 1998; Hohn v. United States, 524 U.S. 236 (1998), decided June 15, 1998; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Hohn v. United States, 524 U.S. 236 (1998), June 15, 1998.

**Development:**
- May 18, 1998, Stewart v. Martinez-Villareal: The review bar is confined to genuine authorization decisions and does not cover an antecedent ruling that no successive application exists.
- June 15, 1998, Hohn v. United States: A certificate denial is reviewable under § 1254(1); House v. Mayo is overruled in part. Former title: Bar on review of successive-application authorization decisions.

### Standard of federal habeas review for Miranda custody

**Governing question:** How a federal habeas court separates historical facts from the ultimate custody determination under Miranda.

**Current rule:** The reviewing court first determines what happened, including the location, duration, statements, restrictions, transportation, and freedom communicated to the suspect. Those are historical facts. It then asks whether a reasonable person in that position would have understood the restraint as comparable to formal arrest. That ultimate application is a mixed constitutional question reviewed independently.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide whether Thompson was in custody or whether admission of the confession was harmless.

**First controlling authority:** Thompson v. Keohane, 516 U.S. 99, decided November 29, 1995, Standard of federal habeas review for Miranda custody; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Thompson v. Keohane, 516 U.S. 99, decided November 29, 1995, Standard of federal habeas review for Miranda custody; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Thompson v. Keohane, 516 U.S. 99, November 29, 1995.

**Development:**
- November 29, 1995, Thompson v. Keohane, 516 U.S. 99: *Stansbury* remains the governing objective custody standard and is unchanged. This decision adds the recurring federal-habeas rule separating deferential review of historical facts from independent review of the ultimate custody conclusion.

### Effect of a surviving aggravating circumstance

**Governing question:** Whether a surviving aggravating circumstance automatically cures a capital-sentencing error affecting evidence and assistance.

**Current rule:** A valid aggravating circumstance establishes continued death eligibility, but it does not automatically cure a constitutional error that altered the evidence, rebuttal, mitigation, and assistance available to the sentencer.

**First controlling authority:** Tuggle v. Netherland, 516 U.S. 10, decided October 30, 1995, Effect of a surviving aggravating circumstance; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Tuggle v. Netherland, 516 U.S. 10, decided October 30, 1995, Effect of a surviving aggravating circumstance; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Tuggle v. Netherland, 516 U.S. 10, October 30, 1995.

**Development:**
- October 30, 1995, Tuggle v. Netherland, 516 U.S. 10: *O’Neal* is applied without alteration. *Zant* is clarified: survival of an eligibility factor does not replace harmless-error review of a constitutional defect affecting the evidence and defense presented during capital selection.

### Federal habeas prejudice review

**Governing question:** How a federal habeas court assesses prejudice from a conceded Ake violation under Brecht and O’Neal.

**Current rule:** The conceded *Ake* violation is reviewed under *Brecht* by examining the complete penalty record and the denied expert’s possible functions in rebuttal, mitigation, and counsel preparation. A court in genuine grave doubt about the error’s effect must grant relief under *O’Neal*.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not presume what a defense expert would have testified, declare *Ake* error structural, or decide prejudice on the present appellate record.

**First controlling authority:** Tuggle v. Netherland, 516 U.S. 10, decided October 30, 1995, Federal habeas prejudice review; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Tuggle v. Netherland, 516 U.S. 10, decided October 30, 1995, Federal habeas prejudice review; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Tuggle v. Netherland, 516 U.S. 10, October 30, 1995.

### Discovery good cause for concealed judicial bias

**Governing question:** What showing establishes good cause for habeas discovery into a concealed judicial-bias claim.

**Current rule:** A petitioner need not prove the concealed constitutional fact before receiving discovery necessary to investigate it. Good cause requires a legally sufficient claim and specific facts giving reason to believe that properly developed evidence may establish entitlement to relief. The district court may confine discovery to that connection.

**Exceptions and limits:** Unrelated judicial misconduct, appearance alone, or speculation does not establish good cause. The rule does not determine default, prejudice, or ultimate relief.

**First controlling authority:** Bracy v. Gramley, 520 U.S. 899 (1997), decided June 9, 1997, Good cause for judicial-bias discovery; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Bracy v. Gramley, 520 U.S. 899 (1997), decided June 9, 1997, Good cause for judicial-bias discovery; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Bracy v. Gramley, 520 U.S. 899 (1997), June 9, 1997.

### Watershed treatment of the Simmons parole-ineligibility rule

**Governing question:** When the Simmons instruction rule falls within Teague’s watershed exception.

**Current rule:** Simmons announced a new procedural rule. It falls within Teague’s watershed exception only when the State seeks death through future dangerousness, the only lawful alternative sentence makes parole legally impossible, and the court refuses an accurate instruction necessary to correct the jury’s false release premise.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not assign watershed status to capital-sentencing rules generally, cases without a future-dangerousness theory, cases where parole remains legally possible, practical release estimates, or cases with an accurate instruction.

**First controlling authority:** O’Dell v. Netherland, 521 U.S. 151 (1997), decided June 19, 1997, New-rule classification and watershed exception in the precise Simmons setting; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** O’Dell v. Netherland, 521 U.S. 151 (1997), decided June 19, 1997, New-rule classification and watershed exception in the precise Simmons setting; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** O’Dell v. Netherland, 521 U.S. 151 (1997), June 19, 1997.

### Temporal reach of AEDPA chapter 153 and the certificate regime

**Governing question:** Which federal habeas applications are governed by AEDPA’s chapter 153 merits amendments and which appeals are governed by § 2253(c)’s certificate-of-appealability provisions.

**Current rule:** AEDPA’s chapter 153 merits amendments do not govern a noncapital federal habeas application filed before April 24, 1996; the filing of the federal application supplies that boundary. Section 2253(c), by contrast, governs an appeal initiated after AEDPA’s effective date even when the underlying district-court habeas proceeding began earlier. The district-court merits stage and the later appellate-certificate stage attach to their respective statutory provisions.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** Determine chapter 153’s merits regime from the date the federal application was filed. Determine the governing certificate regime separately from the date the prisoner initiated the appellate proceeding.

**Exceptions and limits:** Chapter 154 follows its own express pending-case instruction. Successive applications and enactments carrying another temporal command remain governed by their specific provisions.

**First controlling authority:** *Lindh v. Murphy*, 521 U.S. 320, Supreme Court simulation decided June 23, 1997, temporal reach of chapter 153; established the application-filing boundary.

**Controlling authorities:** *Lindh v. Murphy*, 521 U.S. 320, Supreme Court simulation decided June 23, 1997, supplies the district-court application-filing boundary. *Slack v. McDaniel*, 529 U.S. 473, decided April 26, 2000, Part II joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas, and Ginsburg, supplies the separate post-effective-date appellate-certificate boundary.

**Present operation effective:** *Slack v. McDaniel*, April 26, 2000.

**Development:**
- April 26, 2000, *Slack v. McDaniel*: *Lindh* fixed chapter 153’s merits regime by the filing date of the federal application but did not resolve a later appeal initiated after AEDPA. *Slack* adds that § 2253(c) governs the separately initiated post-effective-date appellate stage even when the district-court case began earlier. Former title: Temporal reach of AEDPA chapter 153.

### Stay preserving adjudication of a first federal petition

**Governing question:** When execution must be stayed so a first federal habeas petition can be lawfully adjudicated.

**Current rule:** When a first federal petition cannot lawfully be dismissed before execution, a court abuses its discretion by denying a stay to produce the same terminal result indirectly. The stay remains neither automatic nor indefinite and lasts only as reasonably necessary for lawful adjudication.

**First controlling authority:** Lonchar v. Thomas, 517 U.S. 314, decided April 1, 1996, Stay preserving adjudication; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Lonchar v. Thomas, 517 U.S. 314, decided April 1, 1996, Stay preserving adjudication; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Lonchar v. Thomas, 517 U.S. 314, April 1, 1996.

### Plea intelligence after authoritative statutory construction

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs plea intelligence and Bailey.

**Current rule:** A guilty plea is not intelligent when the defendant was materially misinformed about an essential element of the charged offense. An authoritative construction of the substantive criminal statute supplies the controlling elements on collateral review and is not a new procedural rule.

**First controlling authority:** Bousley v. United States, 523 U.S. 614 (1998), decided May 18, 1998, Plea intelligence and Bailey; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Bousley v. United States, 523 U.S. 614 (1998), decided May 18, 1998, Plea intelligence and Bailey; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Bousley v. United States, 523 U.S. 614 (1998), May 18, 1998.

### Renewal after nonmerits dismissal of a first federal habeas claim

**Governing question:** When renewal after a nonmerits dismissal remains part of the prisoner’s first federal habeas effort rather than becoming a second or successive application.

**Current rule:** A materially identical claim timely raised in the first federal petition and dismissed solely as premature is not second or successive when renewed at its first ripening. A petition refiled after a mixed petition was dismissed without prejudice solely to permit exhaustion likewise continues the prisoner’s first federal habeas effort and is not second or successive.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not excuse timeliness, procedural default, deliberate withholding, failure to comply with dismissal conditions, abuse of the writ, or another independently valid barrier. It does not govern a claim that received a merits disposition or a genuinely new application attacking the judgment on a different ground.

**First controlling authority:** *Stewart v. Martinez-Villareal*, 523 U.S. 637, Supreme Court simulation decided May 18, 1998, renewal of a previously premature claim; established the original rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Stewart v. Martinez-Villareal*, 523 U.S. 637, Supreme Court simulation decided May 18, 1998, supplies the premature-claim route. *Slack v. McDaniel*, 529 U.S. 473, decided April 26, 2000, Part III joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies the mixed-petition exhaustion route and preserves independent barriers.

**Present operation effective:** *Slack v. McDaniel*, April 26, 2000.

**Development:**
- April 26, 2000, *Slack v. McDaniel*: The prior rule treated first-ripening renewal of a claim dismissed solely as premature as part of the first federal petition. *Slack* extends the same non-successive treatment to a properly refiled petition after without-prejudice dismissal of a mixed petition solely for exhaustion, while preserving independent procedural barriers. Former title: Renewal of a first-petition claim dismissed as premature.

### Recall of a completed first-application habeas mandate

**Governing question:** When AEDPA permits a court of appeals to recall a completed mandate in a first federal habeas proceeding.

**Current rule:** AEDPA does not eliminate a court of appeals’ authority to recall its mandate when the court acts solely on a prisoner’s first federal habeas application; a genuine second or successive application remains governed by § 2244(b). Recall is extraordinary and reviewable for abuse of discretion, but a court may use it to correct a genuine malfunction in its own decisional process and prevent execution under a judgment the court concludes is unreliable. The power is not categorically confined to fraud, nullity, or evidence independently satisfying the Schlup actual-innocence gateway.

**Exceptions and limits:** Felker remains controlling for genuine successive applications. Schlup remains an innocence gateway but is not the exclusive permissible basis for correcting a decisional malfunction. Lonchar’s source-governed finality principles continue to make recall extraordinary.

**First controlling authority:** Calderon v. Thompson, 523 U.S. 538 (1998), decided April 29, 1998, recall of a completed first-application mandate; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Calderon v. Thompson, 523 U.S. 538 (1998), decided April 29, 1998, recall of a completed first-application mandate; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Calderon v. Thompson, 523 U.S. 538 (1998), April 29, 1998.

### One complete round of state appellate review

**Governing question:** What state appellate presentation federal habeas exhaustion ordinarily requires.

**Current rule:** A state prisoner ordinarily must fairly present each federal claim through one complete round of the State’s established appellate process. That round includes an available discretionary petition to the State’s highest court when the petition is an ordinary part of the review system.

**Exceptions and limits:** A clear state rule declaring the discretionary step unnecessary controls. Extraordinary collateral remedies that are not an ordinary available appellate step are not added to the exhaustion sequence by this rule.

**First controlling authority:** O’Sullivan v. Boerckel, 526 U.S. 838 (1999), decided June 7, 1999, one complete round of state review; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** O’Sullivan v. Boerckel, 526 U.S. 838 (1999), decided June 7, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** O’Sullivan v. Boerckel, 526 U.S. 838 (1999), June 7, 1999.

### Cause from suppressed evidence and reasonable open-file reliance

**Governing question:** When government suppression establishes cause for procedural default of a Brady claim.

**Current rule:** Governmental suppression of favorable evidence, combined with reasonable defense reliance on the prosecution’s representation that its file was open and complete, establishes cause when that combination made the factual basis of the Brady claim unavailable for earlier presentation.

**Exceptions and limits:** Cause does not establish Brady materiality or prejudice, which remain separate. The rule does not excuse default when the defense already possessed or reasonably could obtain the factual basis despite the representation.

**First controlling authority:** Strickler v. Greene, 527 U.S. 263 (1999), decided June 17, 1999, cause for procedural default of a suppressed-evidence claim; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Strickler v. Greene, 527 U.S. 263 (1999), decided June 17, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Strickler v. Greene, 527 U.S. 263 (1999), June 17, 1999.

### Contrary-to and unreasonable-application review under § 2254(d)(1)

**Governing question:** When a state-court merits adjudication is “contrary to” or “an unreasonable application of” clearly established federal law under 28 U.S.C. § 2254(d)(1).

**Current rule:** A state decision is contrary to clearly established Supreme Court law when it applies a rule opposite to the governing rule or reaches a different result on materially indistinguishable facts. A state court unreasonably applies clearly established law when it identifies the correct rule but applies it in an objectively unreasonable manner. An incorrect application alone does not authorize habeas relief.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** Identify the Supreme Court holdings constituting clearly established law at the time of the state adjudication; determine separately whether the state decision is contrary to that law; if not, determine whether its application of the correct rule was objectively unreasonable.

**Exceptions and limits:** Clearly established law consists of holdings, not dicta, of this Court. Objective unreasonableness is not measured by whether every reasonable jurist would agree, and federal habeas review is not ordinary de novo correction of legal error.

**First controlling authority:** *Williams v. Taylor* (Terry Williams), 529 U.S. 362, decided April 18, 2000, construction of § 2254(d)(1)’s two clauses; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Williams v. Taylor* (Terry Williams), 529 U.S. 362, decided April 18, 2000, O’Connor’s controlling Part II, supplies the opposite-rule, materially-indistinguishable-facts, and objectively-unreasonable-application standards.

**Present operation effective:** *Williams v. Taylor* (Terry Williams), April 18, 2000.

### Prisoner-attributable failure to develop facts under § 2254(e)(2)

**Governing question:** When a state prisoner has “failed to develop” the factual basis of a claim in state court so that 28 U.S.C. § 2254(e)(2)’s heightened conditions govern a federal evidentiary hearing.

**Current rule:** The opening clause of § 2254(e)(2) is triggered only when lack of diligence or greater fault in developing the facts is attributable to the prisoner or counsel. The absence of a completed state-court factual record by itself is insufficient. Diligence ordinarily requires a reasonable and timely effort, in light of the information available, to investigate and present the claim through the state procedures provided.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** Determine first whether prisoner-attributable lack of diligence or greater fault caused the undeveloped factual basis. Only if that threshold is met do § 2254(e)(2)’s further statutory conditions restrict a federal hearing.

**Exceptions and limits:** State prevention of factual development despite reasonable efforts does not activate the heightened bar. When available investigative leads were not reasonably pursued, the failure is attributable to the defense, and a hearing is unavailable unless the statute’s further conditions are satisfied.

**First controlling authority:** *Williams v. Taylor* (Michael Wayne Williams), 529 U.S. 420, decided April 18, 2000, meaning of “failed to develop” in § 2254(e)(2); established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Williams v. Taylor* (Michael Wayne Williams), 529 U.S. 420, decided April 18, 2000, supplies the fault-based threshold, diligence measure, and required sequence.

**Present operation effective:** *Williams v. Taylor* (Michael Wayne Williams), April 18, 2000.

### Certificate of appealability after a procedural dismissal

**Governing question:** What showing is required for a certificate of appealability when a district court dismisses a habeas petition on procedural grounds without reaching the constitutional claim.

**Current rule:** A certificate should issue only if reasonable jurists could debate both whether the petition states a valid constitutional claim and whether the district court’s procedural ruling was correct.

**Burdens and proof:** The applicant must make both threshold showings. Failure on either component defeats the certificate.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** The reviewing court may address first whichever component is more apparent from the record and arguments, but a certificate requires reasonable debatability on both.

**Exceptions and limits:** The inquiry is a threshold certificate determination, not a merits adjudication of either component. A certificate following a merits disposition is governed by the otherwise applicable substantial-showing standard.

**First controlling authority:** *Slack v. McDaniel*, 529 U.S. 473, decided April 26, 2000, certificate after a nonmerits procedural dismissal; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Slack v. McDaniel*, 529 U.S. 473, decided April 26, 2000, Part IV joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies the dual reasonable-debatability requirement and flexible sequence.

**Present operation effective:** *Slack v. McDaniel*, April 26, 2000.

### Section 2255 savings clause and intervening statutory innocence

**Governing question:** When the remedy under 28 U.S.C. § 2255 is inadequate or ineffective so that a federal prisoner may invoke § 2241 through the savings clause.

**Current rule:** Section 2255 is not inadequate or ineffective merely because AEDPA bars a successive motion, an earlier § 2255 motion failed, or the prisoner omitted a claim that could reasonably have been presented. The savings clause permits a § 2241 route when an intervening substantive interpretation establishes that the criminal statute does not reach the prisoner’s conduct and the prisoner had no earlier reasonable opportunity to present that legal claim.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** Determine whether the claim rests on an intervening substantive construction making the conduct noncriminal and whether a reasonable earlier opportunity to present that claim existed. Only if both savings-clause predicates are satisfied may the prisoner proceed through § 2241, subject to otherwise applicable collateral-review requirements.

**Exceptions and limits:** Procedural failure, poor presentation, a claim available from the existing record, or the successive-motion bar alone does not open the savings clause. The rule does not displace ordinary procedural-default and actual-innocence requirements when a lawful collateral route exists.

**First controlling authority:** *United States v. Barrett*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 14, 2000, § 2255 savings clause and intervening statutory innocence; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *United States v. Barrett*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 14, 2000, supplies the inadequacy threshold, intervening-substantive-construction route, no-earlier-reasonable-opportunity predicate, and successive-bar and omitted-claim limits.

**Present operation effective:** *United States v. Barrett*, June 14, 2000.

## Ex Post Facto
