### Retrospective changes to parole-hearing frequency

**Governing question:** When a retrospective change to parole-hearing frequency creates an unconstitutional risk of increased punishment.

**Current rule:** A retrospective parole-scheduling change violates the Ex Post Facto Clause when its design or practical administration creates a significant, nonspeculative risk of prolonged confinement. Neither a longer interval nor the existence of nominal administrative discretion is conclusive by itself. A merely conceivable, speculative, or attenuated possibility of increased punishment is insufficient.

**Burdens and proof:** When the rule does not reveal a significant risk on its face, the prisoner may establish the risk through evidence of the rule’s practical implementation, including actual advancement practices, access to earlier review, and the comparative likelihood of longer confinement.

**Exceptions and limits:** A meaningful mechanism for advancing review may reduce the risk, but nominal discretion does not defeat a claim without regard to its practical availability and administration. A law that materially delays eligibility, removes meaningful reconsideration, or predictably lengthens confinement on a demonstrated record may violate the Clause.

**First controlling authority:** *California Department of Corrections v. Morales*, 514 U.S. 499, decided April 25, 1995, retrospective parole-hearing deferral; established the original risk rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *California Department of Corrections v. Morales*, 514 U.S. 499, decided April 25, 1995, supplies the rejection of speculative and attenuated risk. *Garner v. Jones*, 529 U.S. 244, decided March 28, 2000, supplies the significant-nonspeculative-risk formulation, practical-administration inquiry, and treatment of advancement discretion.

**Present operation effective:** *Garner v. Jones*, March 28, 2000.

**Development:**
- March 28, 2000, *Garner v. Jones*: *Morales* rejected a speculative and attenuated possibility of increased punishment. *Garner* states the controlling threshold as a significant, nonspeculative risk shown by design or practical administration and makes actual operation relevant when the text is inconclusive. Neither a longer interval nor nominal advancement discretion is conclusive alone.

### Retroactive cancellation of accrued release credits

**Governing question:** When cancellation of release credits increases punishment for ex post facto purposes.

**Current rule:** A State violates the Ex Post Facto Clause when it retrospectively cancels release credits already awarded and used to release an inmate, and the cancellation lengthens actual confinement. Objective operation, not the administrative motive for creating the credits, controls.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not protect credits never awarded or unavailable under the law governing the offense.

**First controlling authority:** Lynce v. Mathis, 519 U.S. 433 (1997), decided February 19, 1997, Retroactive cancellation of release credits; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Lynce v. Mathis, 519 U.S. 433 (1997), decided February 19, 1997, Retroactive cancellation of release credits; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Lynce v. Mathis, 519 U.S. 433 (1997), February 19, 1997.

### Criminal-punishment classification of nominally civil measures

**Governing question:** When a legislative civil designation is displaced and a nominally civil measure is treated as criminal punishment.

**Current rule:** A legislative civil designation controls unless the clearest proof shows that the measure’s combined design and actual operation are so punitive that it must be treated as criminal punishment. Severity, deterrence, occupational restriction, monetary magnitude, and overlap with criminal conduct may be relevant, but no one feature, and sanction-specific disproportionality alone, displaces the classification. Only after the measure is classified as criminal punishment does a court apply the same-offense, Ex Post Facto, or other punishment-specific consequence.

**First controlling authority:** Kansas v. Hendricks, 521 U.S. 346 (1997), decided June 23, 1997, punitive classification of civil commitment; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Kansas v. Hendricks, 521 U.S. 346 (1997), decided June 23, 1997; Hudson v. United States, 522 U.S. 93 (1997), decided December 10, 1997, general civil-or-criminal classification; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Hudson v. United States, 522 U.S. 93 (1997), December 10, 1997.

**Development:**
- December 10, 1997, Hudson v. United States: The prior commitment-specific clearest-proof inquiry becomes a generally applicable civil-or-criminal classification rule. Halper no longer permits sanction-specific severity or disproportionality alone to control; Ursery remains governing. Former title: Punitive operation of nominally civil commitment.

### Retroactive reduction in the evidence required for conviction

**Governing question:** Whether a retrospective change reducing the evidence legally sufficient to convict violates the Ex Post Facto Clause.

**Current rule:** A State may not retrospectively make conviction possible on a lesser quantum of evidence than the law required when the offense occurred. A change eliminating a corroboration, timely-outcry, or comparable condition that previously made the remaining proof legally insufficient falls within the prohibition.

**Exceptions and limits:** A rule changing witness competency or expanding admissibility without reducing the quantum of evidence legally necessary for conviction is distinct. The court examines the legal sufficiency consequence, not the legislature’s label for the rule.

**First controlling authority:** *Calder v. Bull*, 3 U.S. (3 Dall.) 386 (1798), fourth category; current application and boundary supplied by *Carmell v. Texas*, 529 U.S. 513, decided May 1, 2000.

**Controlling authorities:** *Calder v. Bull*, 3 U.S. (3 Dall.) 386 (1798), supplies the prohibition on retrospective reduction of required evidence. *Carmell v. Texas*, 529 U.S. 513, decided May 1, 2000, supplies the distinction between a legal-sufficiency condition and changes to competency or admissibility.

**Present operation effective:** *Carmell v. Texas*, May 1, 2000.

## Double Jeopardy

### Punitive taxes imposed after criminal punishment

**Governing question:** When an assessment denominated a tax constitutes a second punishment for Double Jeopardy purposes.

**Current rule:** A tax designation receives substantial but not conclusive weight. A levy constitutes punishment when its legal design and practical operation depart sharply from ordinary revenue collection and tie the assessment to a completed criminal offense and prosecution. A separate punitive assessment may not follow criminal punishment for the same conduct.

**Exceptions and limits:** No single unusual feature is dispositive. The rule does not regulate ordinary tax rates, bar taxation of unlawful businesses, or classify remedial charges, interest, investigation costs, or conventional civil assessments as punishment merely because they follow misconduct.

**First controlling authority:** Department of Revenue of Montana v. Kurth Ranch, 511 U.S. 767 (1994), decided June 6, 1994, Punitive post-conviction drug tax; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Department of Revenue of Montana v. Kurth Ranch, 511 U.S. 767 (1994), decided June 6, 1994, Punitive post-conviction drug tax; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Department of Revenue of Montana v. Kurth Ranch, 511 U.S. 767 (1994), June 6, 1994.

### Consecutive punishment under § 924(c)

**Governing question:** Whether Congress authorized a consecutive § 924(c) punishment in addition to the sentence for carjacking under the then-operative § 2119.

**Current rule:** Congress authorized a consecutive § 924(c) sentence in addition to the sentence for carjacking under the then-operative § 2119, so the cumulative punishment imposed in this single prosecution does not violate the Double Jeopardy Clause.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide a charging pair for which Congress supplied no clear cumulative-punishment instruction.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Harris, 25 F.3d 1275, decided April 27, 1995, Consecutive punishment under § 924(c); established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Harris, 25 F.3d 1275, decided April 27, 1995, Consecutive punishment under § 924(c); supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Harris, 25 F.3d 1275, April 27, 1995.

## Eighth Amendment

### Deliberate indifference to a substantial custodial risk

**Governing question:** What knowledge and response establish an Eighth Amendment violation based on a serious risk of harm in custody.

**Current rule:** A prison official violates the Eighth Amendment when the official knows that an inmate faces an objectively substantial risk of serious harm and consciously disregards it by failing to take reasonable protective measures. Actual awareness may be proved directly or circumstantially, and a factfinder may infer knowledge from an obvious risk, but obviousness is evidence rather than an irrebuttable presumption.

**Burdens and proof:** The claimant must prove the objectively substantial risk, each defendant’s actual awareness, conscious disregard, causation, and individual role.

**Exceptions and limits:** An official who responds reasonably is not liable merely because the measures fail, and negligence or a classification mistake alone is insufficient.

**First controlling authority:** Farmer v. Brennan, 511 U.S. 825 (1994), decided June 6, 1994, Knowledge and disregard of a serious custodial risk; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Farmer v. Brennan, 511 U.S. 825 (1994), decided June 6, 1994, Knowledge and disregard of a serious custodial risk; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Farmer v. Brennan, 511 U.S. 825 (1994), June 6, 1994.

### Gross disproportionality of a punitive forfeiture

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs gross disproportionality of the full forfeiture.

**Current rule:** A punitive forfeiture is a fine and violates the Excessive Fines Clause when grossly disproportional to the gravity of the offense. Gross disproportionality, not strict proportionality, supplies the measure.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Bajakajian, 524 U.S. 321 (1998), decided June 22, 1998, Gross disproportionality of the full forfeiture; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Bajakajian, 524 U.S. 321 (1998), decided June 22, 1998, Gross disproportionality of the full forfeiture; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Bajakajian, 524 U.S. 321 (1998), June 22, 1998.

## Capital Sentencing

### Meaningful opportunity to answer capital-sentencing evidence

**Governing question:** When the State’s disclosure and presentation of evidence at a capital penalty proceeding deny the defendant due process.

**Current rule:** A capital defendant must receive a meaningful opportunity to deny or explain evidence the State asks the sentencer to use in choosing death. When the prosecution affirmatively and materially narrows its announced penalty presentation in a manner that reasonably induces defense reliance, it may not, after the guilt verdict, introduce a materially different and highly prejudicial evidentiary presentation without affording a practical opportunity to investigate, rebut, and seek effective relief.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule creates no constitutional entitlement to a witness list, itemized discovery, or advance disclosure of every piece of nonstatutory sentencing evidence. An inaccurate prediction, ordinary change in strategy, or late disclosure is not automatically unconstitutional. Timely disclosure, a meaningful continuance, or another procedure that provides a practical opportunity to answer the changed presentation may satisfy due process.

**First controlling authority:** Gray v. Netherland, 518 U.S. 152, decided June 20, 1996, Meaningful opportunity to answer capital-sentencing evidence; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Gray v. Netherland, 518 U.S. 152, decided June 20, 1996, Meaningful opportunity to answer capital-sentencing evidence; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Gray v. Netherland, 518 U.S. 152, June 20, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 20, 1996, Gray v. Netherland, 518 U.S. 152: *Gardner v. Florida* is applied and clarified. Its requirement that a capital defendant be able to deny or explain sentencing information extends to an affirmative prosecutorial assurance followed by a late material expansion that makes effective response impracticable, without creating a general item-by-item discovery right.

### Exclusion of another sentencer’s irrelevant death judgment

**Governing question:** Whether a capital jury independently choosing life or death may be told that another jury already sentenced the defendant to death.

**Current rule:** When a capital jury must independently choose between life and death, the State may not introduce the fact that another jury already imposed a death sentence when that prior sentence is irrelevant to a valid aggravating issue. The prior conviction and admissible underlying conduct may be proved, but another sentencer’s ultimate life-or-death judgment may not be used as aggravating evidence merely to suggest that execution has already been authoritatively approved.

**Consequence or recurring remedy:** Admission over objection requires a new penalty proceeding when it undermines the reliability and independent responsibility of the sentencing decision.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule leaves open a case in which the prior sentence itself bears on a valid statutory issue.

**First controlling authority:** Romano v. Oklahoma, 512 U.S. 1 (1994), decided June 13, 1994, Prior death sentence in an independent capital sentencing; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Romano v. Oklahoma, 512 U.S. 1 (1994), decided June 13, 1994, Prior death sentence in an independent capital sentencing; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Romano v. Oklahoma, 512 U.S. 1 (1994), June 13, 1994.

### Parole-ineligibility instruction when future dangerousness is argued

**Governing question:** When due process requires accurate information that the noncapital sentence carries no possibility of parole.

**Current rule:** When the prosecution places future dangerousness at issue and the only noncapital sentence makes the defendant legally ineligible for parole, due process requires accurate information that a life sentence carries no possibility of parole. The consequence is legally fixed for this purpose when an outstanding guilty verdict, absent a genuine unresolved substantive defect, will automatically create parole ineligibility upon entry of judgment and the prosecution relies on that verdict as substantively reliable evidence of future dangerousness; formal entry of judgment is not indispensable.

**Consequence or recurring remedy:** Failure to provide accurate parole information requires a new penalty proceeding when the jury was left with a materially false implication about eventual release.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not require predictions about discretionary parole when legal eligibility remains genuinely possible or contingent, does not treat a verdict with a genuine unresolved substantive defect as final, and does not dictate the weight the jury must assign to parole ineligibility.

**First controlling authority:** *Simmons v. South Carolina*, 512 U.S. 154 (1994), decided June 17, 1994, parole-ineligibility instruction when future dangerousness is argued; established the current rule’s core.

**Controlling authorities:** *Simmons v. South Carolina*, 512 U.S. 154 (1994), decided June 17, 1994, supplies the future-dangerousness trigger and accurate parole-ineligibility requirement. *Ramdass v. Angelone*, 530 U.S. 156 (2000), decided June 12, 2000, supplies the outstanding-verdict, automatic-consequence, no-substantive-defect, prosecution-reliance, and no-formal-judgment components.

**Present operation effective:** *Ramdass v. Angelone*, 530 U.S. 156 (2000), June 12, 2000.

**Development:**
- June 12, 2000, *Ramdass v. Angelone*: Before *Ramdass*, the entry required an instruction when future dangerousness was argued and the noncapital sentence made parole legally unavailable. After *Ramdass*, formal judgment is not indispensable when an outstanding verdict without a genuine unresolved substantive defect will automatically create ineligibility and the prosecution already treats it as reliable proof. Genuine legal contingency and discretionary parole remain outside the rule.

### Eligibility narrowing and understandable selection factors

**Governing question:** What clarity and guidance the Eighth Amendment requires of factors used after a defendant has been made death eligible.

**Current rule:** Eligibility factors must genuinely narrow the class exposed to death and provide a principled boundary. Once eligibility has lawfully been established, selection factors may direct individualized consideration of relevant characteristics of the offense and offender. A selection factor must possess a common-sense core ordinary jurors can understand, but it need not yield a yes-or-no answer or carry predetermined aggravating or mitigating weight.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not approve a nonnarrowing eligibility factor, an instruction that forecloses relevant mitigation, or a label lacking an understandable core. Another sentencer’s irrelevant ultimate judgment remains governed by the separate rule excluding a prior death sentence.

**First controlling authority:** Tuilaepa v. California / Proctor v. California, 512 U.S. 967 (1994), decided June 30, 1994, Clarity and function of capital selection factors; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Tuilaepa v. California / Proctor v. California, 512 U.S. 967 (1994), decided June 30, 1994, Clarity and function of capital selection factors; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Tuilaepa v. California / Proctor v. California, 512 U.S. 967 (1994), June 30, 1994.

**Related current rules:** Exclusion of another sentencer’s irrelevant death judgment, under Capital Sentencing, governs use of a prior jury’s ultimate death decision.

### Weight assigned to an advisory life recommendation

**Governing question:** What constitutional weight a trial judge must give an advisory jury recommendation of life in capital sentencing.

**Current rule:** The Eighth Amendment does not require a State to prescribe a numerical weight, fixed verbal formula, or Florida-style limitation governing a trial judge’s consideration of an advisory jury recommendation of life. A State may place final capital-sentencing responsibility in the trial judge, require consideration of the jury’s recommendation, and rely on written findings and appellate review to guard against arbitrary sentencing. The Constitution does not make the recommendation binding or dictate the weight it must receive.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not permit refusal to consider relevant mitigation, reliance on an invalid aggravating circumstance, or a sentence unsupported by the findings and record. It does not decide a scheme in which the advisory verdict is disregarded altogether.

**First controlling authority:** Harris v. Alabama, 513 U.S. 504, decided February 22, 1995, Weight assigned to an advisory life recommendation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Harris v. Alabama, 513 U.S. 504, decided February 22, 1995, Weight assigned to an advisory life recommendation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Harris v. Alabama, 513 U.S. 504, February 22, 1995.

### Additional Penry instruction for mitigating evidence

**Governing question:** When capital jurors require an additional instruction to give presented mitigating evidence its full effect.

**Current rule:** The defendant need not establish a formal diagnosis, a uniquely severe permanent disability, or a rigid causal nexus between the mitigation and the offense. The inquiry is whether there is a reasonable likelihood that the existing questions prevent jurors from giving the actually presented evidence its full mitigating effect.

**Exceptions and limits:** No supplemental instruction is required for evidence never introduced or when the mitigating force of the evidence can be expressed through the existing questions.

**First controlling authority:** Allridge v. Scott, 41 F.3d 213, decided January 15, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, When an additional Penry instruction is required; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Allridge v. Scott, 41 F.3d 213, decided January 15, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, When an additional Penry instruction is required; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Allridge v. Scott, 41 F.3d 213, January 15, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

### Clarifying a capital jury’s life-or-death authority

**Governing question:** What a trial court must do when a capital jury asks whether finding an aggravating circumstance makes death mandatory.

**Current rule:** When a capital jury expressly asks whether proof of an aggravating circumstance makes death mandatory, the trial court must respond with sufficient clarity that life remains an authorized sentence and that mitigating evidence may justify it. Referring the jury back to existing language is sufficient only when that language clearly answers the demonstrated confusion; repeating language that does not unambiguously resolve the question violates the requirement of an individualized capital-selection decision.

**Exceptions and limits:** No particular wording is required. The rule does not invalidate a reference to existing instructions that clearly answers the jury’s actual question.

**First controlling authority:** Weeks v. Angelone, 528 U.S. 225 (2000), decided January 19, 2000, express jury confusion over life-or-death discretion; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Weeks v. Angelone, 528 U.S. 225 (2000), decided January 19, 2000, supplies the response requirement and distinguishes facially sufficient general instructions from an unanswered question demonstrating actual confusion.

**Present operation effective:** Weeks v. Angelone, 528 U.S. 225 (2000), January 19, 2000.

**Related current rules:** Grave doubt under the federal habeas harmless-error standard, under Habeas Corpus. That rule governs the federal habeas consequence when the instructional error leaves grave doubt about substantial and injurious effect.

### Strickland prejudice from omitted capital mitigation

**Governing question:** How a court determines prejudice when deficient capital-sentencing representation omitted mitigating evidence.

**Current rule:** The court evaluates the totality of the mitigating evidence that competent investigation and presentation would have made available against the aggravating evidence before the sentencer and asks whether there is a reasonable probability of a different sentencing result. Substantial omitted mitigation must be assessed cumulatively and as part of the defendant’s altered sentencing profile.

**Exceptions and limits:** *Lockhart v. Fretwell* does not displace ordinary *Strickland* prejudice when deficient performance deprived the defendant of mitigating evidence the law permitted the sentencer to consider. The rule does not presume prejudice from deficient investigation or require a new sentence whenever additional mitigating evidence exists.

**First controlling authority:** *Strickland v. Washington*, 466 U.S. 668 (1984), prejudice inquiry; current omitted-capital-mitigation operation supplied by *Williams v. Taylor* (Terry Williams), 529 U.S. 362, decided April 18, 2000.

**Controlling authorities:** *Strickland v. Washington*, 466 U.S. 668 (1984), supplies the reasonable-probability and totality framework. *Williams v. Taylor* (Terry Williams), 529 U.S. 362, decided April 18, 2000, supplies the cumulative reweighing instruction and confirms that *Fretwell* does not replace ordinary prejudice in this setting.

**Present operation effective:** *Williams v. Taylor* (Terry Williams), April 18, 2000.

## Labor and Employment

### Title VII hostile work environment

**Governing question:** What discriminatory conduct and injury establish a Title VII hostile-work-environment claim, including same-sex harassment.

**Current rule:** Title VII does not categorically exclude harassment because the alleged harasser and employee are of the same sex. The employee must establish that sex caused the treatment, that the environment was objectively and subjectively severe or pervasive under the totality of circumstances, and that the conduct is attributable to the employer under governing liability rules. Serious psychological injury is not required.

**Exceptions and limits:** Ordinary incivility is not actionable merely because it is offensive; causation by sex and employer attribution remain required.

**First controlling authority:** Harris v. Forklift Systems, Inc., 510 U.S. 17 (1993), decided November 9, 1993, severe-or-pervasive hostile environment; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Harris v. Forklift Systems, Inc., 510 U.S. 17 (1993), decided November 9, 1993; Oncale v. Sundowner Offshore Services, Inc., 523 U.S. 75 (1998), decided March 4, 1998, same-sex harassment; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Oncale v. Sundowner Offshore Services, Inc., 523 U.S. 75 (1998), March 4, 1998.

**Development:**
- March 4, 1998, Oncale v. Sundowner Offshore Services, Inc.: The existing severe-or-pervasive rule now expressly governs same-sex harassment; causation by sex and employer attribution remain required. Former title: Title VII hostile work environment without serious psychological injury.

### Professional direction versus statutory supervisory authority

**Governing question:** When skilled direction of other workers makes a professional employee a supervisor under NLRA § 2(11).

**Current rule:** Work does not become supervisory merely because it benefits the enterprise or includes skilled direction of less-experienced employees. The Board may distinguish professional or technical direction incidental to the employee’s own skilled service from genuine labor-management authority aligning the employee with management, including independent authority over hiring, discharge, discipline, promotion, compensation, grievances, or comparable matters.

**Exceptions and limits:** Professionals may qualify as supervisors when they independently exercise genuine management prerogatives; no profession is categorically exempt.

**First controlling authority:** NLRB v. Health Care & Retirement Corp. of America, 511 U.S. 571 (1994), decided May 23, 1994, Professional direction and statutory supervisory authority; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** NLRB v. Health Care & Retirement Corp. of America, 511 U.S. 571 (1994), decided May 23, 1994, Professional direction and statutory supervisory authority; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** NLRB v. Health Care & Retirement Corp. of America, 511 U.S. 571 (1994), May 23, 1994.

### NLRA preemption of state withdrawal of generally available protections

**Governing question:** Whether a State may withhold an otherwise available, nonwaivable labor protection solely because an employee is covered by a collective-bargaining agreement.

**Current rule:** The NLRA preempts a state policy that categorically withholds otherwise available, nonwaivable wage protections solely because an employee is covered by a collective-bargaining agreement containing an arbitration clause. A State may administer neutral enforcement priorities and decline a claim genuinely displaced by federal law, but it may not make exercise of federally protected collective bargaining the condition for losing a generally available enforcement service.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not preempt every state distinction involving union representation or invalidate a statute that can be administered neutrally.

**First controlling authority:** Livadas v. Bradshaw, 512 U.S. 107 (1994), decided June 13, 1994, Federal preemption of the nonenforcement policy; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Livadas v. Bradshaw, 512 U.S. 107 (1994), decided June 13, 1994, Federal preemption of the nonenforcement policy; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Livadas v. Bradshaw, 512 U.S. 107 (1994), June 13, 1994.

### Section 301 interpretation-versus-consultation boundary

**Governing question:** When LMRA § 301 preempts an independent state-law claim that refers to a collective-bargaining agreement.

**Current rule:** Section 301 does not preempt an independent state-law claim when liability arises from state law, no contractual term is genuinely disputed, and the agreement is consulted only for an undisputed factual input such as a wage rate. Preemption may apply when liability arises from the agreement or resolution requires interpretation rather than mere reference.

**Exceptions and limits:** The existence of a collective-bargaining agreement alone does not trigger preemption.

**First controlling authority:** Livadas v. Bradshaw, 512 U.S. 107 (1994), decided June 13, 1994, Section 301 and the independent wage-penalty claim; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Livadas v. Bradshaw, 512 U.S. 107 (1994), decided June 13, 1994, Section 301 and the independent wage-penalty claim; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Livadas v. Bradshaw, 512 U.S. 107 (1994), June 13, 1994.

### After-acquired evidence and ADEA remedies

**Governing question:** How after-acquired employee misconduct affects ADEA liability and remedies, and what the employer must prove.

**Current rule:** After-acquired evidence of employee wrongdoing does not bar ADEA liability for an earlier discriminatory discharge, but misconduct that would independently have caused lawful termination materially limits the available remedy. The employer bears the burden of proving that the wrongdoing was sufficiently serious and that the employee in fact would have been discharged upon its discovery. When that showing is made, reinstatement and front pay are ordinarily unavailable. Backpay generally runs from the unlawful discharge until the employer discovered the misconduct, subject to extraordinary equitable circumstances.

**Exceptions and limits:** Minor wrongdoing, newly invented standards, or pretextual assertions do not shorten relief. The district court retains equitable authority to address extraordinary circumstances and other remedies authorized by the statute.

**First controlling authority:** McKennon v. Nashville Banner Publishing Co., 513 U.S. 352, decided January 23, 1995, After-acquired evidence and ADEA remedies; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** McKennon v. Nashville Banner Publishing Co., 513 U.S. 352, decided January 23, 1995, After-acquired evidence and ADEA remedies; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** McKennon v. Nashville Banner Publishing Co., 513 U.S. 352, January 23, 1995.

### Ordinary drawbar misalignment

**Governing question:** When drawbar misalignment constitutes failure of the statutory automatic-coupler duty.

**Current rule:** The Act imposes an absolute duty to provide and maintain couplers that couple automatically by impact when properly set for coupling. Liability follows from failure of that required function, not merely from the need to realign a drawbar that moved laterally during ordinary operation.

**Exceptions and limits:** A recurrent misalignment caused by defective maintenance, improper setup, damaged equipment, or failure after proper positioning may present a different case.

**First controlling authority:** Norfolk & Western Railway Co. v. Hiles, 516 U.S. 400, decided February 27, 1996, Ordinary drawbar misalignment; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Norfolk & Western Railway Co. v. Hiles, 516 U.S. 400, decided February 27, 1996, Ordinary drawbar misalignment; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Norfolk & Western Railway Co. v. Hiles, 516 U.S. 400, February 27, 1996.

**Development:**
- February 27, 1996, Norfolk & Western Railway Co. v. Hiles, 516 U.S. 400: The decision supplies the first controlling simulated distinction between failure of an automatic coupler’s required function and ordinary operational misalignment. The Act’s absolute-duty character remains intact within the former category.

### Postimpasse implementation within multiemployer bargaining

**Governing question:** When the nonstatutory labor exemption continues to protect coordinated multiemployer implementation after bargaining impasse.

**Current rule:** The exemption extends beyond the formal moment of impasse when coordinated employer conduct concerns the same mandatory subject, the same bargaining parties and unit, and the immediate implementation labor law permits after good-faith bargaining. Impasse does not automatically transfer supervision of the employment relationship from labor law to treble-damages antitrust litigation.

**Exceptions and limits:** The exemption does not cover combinations involving nonlabor markets, nonparties, nonmandatory subjects, or conduct sufficiently distant in time or circumstance from collective bargaining that antitrust review would no longer interfere materially with labor policy.

**First controlling authority:** Brown v. Pro Football, Inc., 518 U.S. 231, decided June 20, 1996, Postimpasse implementation within multiemployer bargaining; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Brown v. Pro Football, Inc., 518 U.S. 231, decided June 20, 1996, Postimpasse implementation within multiemployer bargaining; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Brown v. Pro Football, Inc., 518 U.S. 231, June 20, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 20, 1996, Brown v. Pro Football, Inc., 518 U.S. 231: The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule defining the nonstatutory labor exemption after bargaining impasse. It leaves the legality of the implementation under the NLRA to labor law and does not immunize employer coordination outside the bargaining relationship.

### Repudiation based on concealed preacceptance doubt

**Governing question:** Whether an employer may repudiate an accepted collective-bargaining agreement based on doubt it concealed before acceptance.

**Current rule:** An employer possessing the facts supporting its asserted doubt before contract formation must raise the issue through lawful procedures before acceptance, withdraw the offer, or remain bound after the union accepts. The rule does not govern genuinely new postacceptance evidence.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not govern a new postcontract loss of majority support, fraud in obtaining acceptance, expiration of the agreement, or a timely authorized representation proceeding.

**First controlling authority:** Auciello Iron Works, Inc. v. NLRB, 517 U.S. 781, decided June 3, 1996, Repudiation based on concealed preacceptance doubt; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Auciello Iron Works, Inc. v. NLRB, 517 U.S. 781, decided June 3, 1996, Repudiation based on concealed preacceptance doubt; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Auciello Iron Works, Inc. v. NLRB, 517 U.S. 781, June 3, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 3, 1996, Auciello Iron Works, Inc. v. NLRB, 517 U.S. 781: The decision establishes the first controlling simulated rule concerning an employer’s use of concealed preacceptance doubt to repudiate an accepted collective-bargaining agreement. *Town & Country Electric* and *Holly Farms* remain unchanged because they concern employee classification rather than bargaining stability and contract formation.

### Employee status of paid union organizers

**Governing question:** Whether paid union organizers qualify as employees despite their union compensation and organizing purpose.

**Current rule:** Simultaneous union compensation and organizing purpose do not create a categorical exclusion from employee status. Whether an applicant genuinely intended to work, performed assigned duties, or engaged in misconduct remains governed by the Act’s ordinary factual and remedial rules.

**Exceptions and limits:** The holding does not protect an applicant who never intended to work, a refusal to perform assigned duties, sabotage, theft, or independently unlawful conduct.

**First controlling authority:** NLRB v. Town & Country Electric, Inc., 516 U.S. 85, decided November 28, 1995, Employee status of paid union organizers; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** NLRB v. Town & Country Electric, Inc., 516 U.S. 85, decided November 28, 1995, Employee status of paid union organizers; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** NLRB v. Town & Country Electric, Inc., 516 U.S. 85, November 28, 1995.

**Development:**
- November 28, 1995, NLRB v. Town & Country Electric, Inc., 516 U.S. 85: The decision creates a new recurring employee-status rule for paid organizers. *Health Care & Retirement Corp.* is applied without changing its separate supervisor test; both decisions require statutory labor status to follow actual functions rather than status labels.

### Material disadvantage in Title VII retaliation

**Governing question:** What consequence must accompany hostile response to protected discrimination activity for Title VII retaliation liability.

**Current rule:** Opposition, ridicule, hostile references to a complaint, or conditional litigation rhetoric does not complete a retaliation claim when the claimant suffers no material employment, membership, representation, or comparable disadvantage. The standard is not confined to ultimate employment decisions; threats, harassment, or public shaming may qualify on a record showing material deterrence.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not foreclose nontraditional acts that materially deter protected activity.

**First controlling authority:** DeAngelis v. El Paso Municipal Police Officers’ Association, 51 F.3d 591, decided January 6, 1997, Retaliation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** DeAngelis v. El Paso Municipal Police Officers’ Association, 51 F.3d 591, decided January 6, 1997, Retaliation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** DeAngelis v. El Paso Municipal Police Officers’ Association, 51 F.3d 591, January 6, 1997.

### Salary-basis practical exposure and correction

**Governing question:** When an employee is subject to salary reduction and when an employer may preserve exempt status.

**Current rule:** An employee is subject to salary reduction when an actual practice of inconsistent deductions exists or a clear and particularized policy creates a significant practical likelihood of such deductions. General theoretical disciplinary authority or an isolated deduction is insufficient. An employer may preserve exempt status by reimbursing an improper deduction and committing to future compliance when the deduction was inadvertent or made for a reason other than lack of work.

**Exceptions and limits:** A recurring deduction practice or policy specifically threatening salary reductions for ordinary work variations may defeat salary status.

**First controlling authority:** Auer v. Robbins, 519 U.S. 452 (1997), decided February 19, 1997, Salary status, correction, and duties; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Auer v. Robbins, 519 U.S. 452 (1997), decided February 19, 1997, Salary status, correction, and duties; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Auer v. Robbins, 519 U.S. 452 (1997), February 19, 1997.

### Public-employee religious accommodation and resource use

**Governing question:** How Title VII accommodation applies to a public employee’s workplace religious practices.

**Current rule:** A public employer must reasonably accommodate private religious practices unless accommodation would impose more than a de minimis operational burden under the then-governing Title VII standard. It need not permit use of subordinate labor or public resources for personal religious activity, official worship, coercion, harassment, or disruption. It may not treat voluntary private religious expression as an undue hardship merely because coworkers object or because the expression is religious.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not protect conduct attributable to government, compulsory participation, or misuse of public authority or resources.

**First controlling authority:** Brown v. Polk County, 61 F.3d 650, decided March 17, 1997, Title VII accommodation and public resources; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Brown v. Polk County, 61 F.3d 650, decided March 17, 1997, Title VII accommodation and public resources; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Brown v. Polk County, 61 F.3d 650, March 17, 1997.

### Predeath beneficiary status under LHWCA § 33(g)

**Governing question:** When a potential survivor is a person entitled to compensation for purposes of the LHWCA settlement-approval forfeiture.

**Current rule:** Entitlement is assessed when the third-party settlement is made. A spouse is not a person entitled to death compensation before the worker dies from a qualifying cause; a contingent possibility of future widowhood is not present entitlement. A predeath settlement therefore does not trigger § 33(g) forfeiture of a later death-benefit claim.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not govern settlements made after death, vested disability benefits, or offsets expressly authorized elsewhere.

**First controlling authority:** Ingalls Shipbuilding, Inc. v. Director, Office of Workers’ Compensation Programs, 519 U.S. 248 (1997), decided February 18, 1997, Predeath spouse and § 33(g); established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Ingalls Shipbuilding, Inc. v. Director, Office of Workers’ Compensation Programs, 519 U.S. 248 (1997), decided February 18, 1997, Predeath spouse and § 33(g); supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Ingalls Shipbuilding, Inc. v. Director, Office of Workers’ Compensation Programs, 519 U.S. 248 (1997), February 18, 1997.

### Director of OWCP as appellate respondent

**Governing question:** When the Director of OWCP may participate as respondent in review of a Benefits Review Board order.

**Current rule:** After a private party invokes appellate jurisdiction to review a Benefits Review Board order, the Secretary may designate the Director of OWCP to appear as the Department of Labor’s respondent and state the Department’s position, including disagreement with the Board. Respondent status does not give the Director an independent right to initiate review.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not decide whether the Director alone can preserve an Article III controversy after all private adversity ends.

**First controlling authority:** Ingalls Shipbuilding, Inc. v. Director, Office of Workers’ Compensation Programs, 519 U.S. 248 (1997), decided February 18, 1997, Director as respondent; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Ingalls Shipbuilding, Inc. v. Director, Office of Workers’ Compensation Programs, 519 U.S. 248 (1997), decided February 18, 1997, Director as respondent; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Ingalls Shipbuilding, Inc. v. Director, Office of Workers’ Compensation Programs, 519 U.S. 248 (1997), February 18, 1997.

### Effect of a noncompliant ADEA release

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs effect of a noncompliant ADEA release.

**Current rule:** An ADEA release that fails the OWBPA’s mandatory requirements is unenforceable as an ADEA waiver. Retention of severance payments does not cure the defect through ratification, and tendering the payments back is not a precondition to filing the ADEA action.

**First controlling authority:** Oubre v. Entergy Operations, Inc., 522 U.S. 422 (1998), decided January 26, 1998, Effect of a noncompliant ADEA release; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Oubre v. Entergy Operations, Inc., 522 U.S. 422 (1998), decided January 26, 1998, Effect of a noncompliant ADEA release; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Oubre v. Entergy Operations, Inc., 522 U.S. 422 (1998), January 26, 1998.

### Safeguarded employer polling of union support

**Governing question:** What threshold the NLRB may require before an employer polls employees about an incumbent union’s continuing majority support.

**Current rule:** The NLRB may require a good-faith objective reasonable doubt concerning an incumbent union’s continuing majority support before an employer conducts a safeguarded poll. The standard is permissible, though not uniquely required, and the union’s presumption becomes rebuttable after the protected conclusive period.

**First controlling authority:** Allentown Mack Sales & Service, Inc. v. NLRB, 522 U.S. 359 (1998), decided January 26, 1998, safeguarded employer polling; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Allentown Mack Sales & Service, Inc. v. NLRB, 522 U.S. 359 (1998), decided January 26, 1998, safeguarded employer polling; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Allentown Mack Sales & Service, Inc. v. NLRB, 522 U.S. 359 (1998), January 26, 1998.

### Duty of fair representation and NLRA-only claims

**Governing question:** When alleged noncompliance with the NLRA establishes a union’s breach of the duty of fair representation and which tribunal initially resolves the claim.

**Current rule:** A union breaches the duty of fair representation only through arbitrary, discriminatory, or bad-faith representation. Use of statutory union-security terminology without restating every later judicial limitation is not itself a breach. A claim alleging only that a union-security arrangement violates the NLRA remains initially within the NLRB’s jurisdiction unless the operative facts independently support a duty-of-fair-representation violation. A federal court may resolve an NLRA issue when it arises collaterally within a properly supported duty-of-fair-representation claim.

**First controlling authority:** Marquez v. Screen Actors Guild, Inc., 525 U.S. 33 (1998), decided November 3, 1998, statutory union-security language and NLRB jurisdiction; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Marquez v. Screen Actors Guild, Inc., 525 U.S. 33 (1998), decided November 3, 1998, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Marquez v. Screen Actors Guild, Inc., 525 U.S. 33 (1998), November 3, 1998.

### Collective-bargaining waiver of a federal statutory forum

**Governing question:** When a collective-bargaining agreement requires arbitration of an employee’s independent federal statutory claim.

**Current rule:** A collective-bargaining agreement requires arbitration of an employee’s federal antidiscrimination claim only when it clearly and unmistakably identifies that statutory claim or an equivalent class of independent federal rights. General grievance or arbitration language does not surrender the independent federal right and judicial forum.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not decide enforceability of an agreement that expressly and unmistakably submits the federal statutory claim to arbitration or an independently lawful ground disposing of the claim.

**First controlling authority:** Wright v. Universal Maritime Service Corp., 525 U.S. 70 (1998), decided November 16, 1998, waiver of the federal statutory forum; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Wright v. Universal Maritime Service Corp., 525 U.S. 70 (1998), decided November 16, 1998, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Wright v. Universal Maritime Service Corp., 525 U.S. 70 (1998), November 16, 1998.

### Inspector General examinations and employee representation

**Governing question:** Whether an agency Office of Inspector General investigator is a representative of the agency for 5 U.S.C. § 7114(a)(2)(B).

**Current rule:** An investigator employed by an agency’s Office of Inspector General is a representative of the agency when examining an agency employee within § 7114(a)(2)(B). Statutory independence from ordinary management does not erase the agency relationship or the employee’s representation right.

**Exceptions and limits:** The representation right does not transfer control of the interview to the representative. Its remaining statutory predicates must still be satisfied.

**First controlling authority:** National Aeronautics and Space Administration v. Federal Labor Relations Authority, 527 U.S. 229 (1999), decided June 17, 1999, Inspector General investigator as an agency representative; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** National Aeronautics and Space Administration v. Federal Labor Relations Authority, 527 U.S. 229 (1999), decided June 17, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** National Aeronautics and Space Administration v. Federal Labor Relations Authority, 527 U.S. 229 (1999), June 17, 1999.

### Title VII punitive damages and good-faith compliance

**Governing question:** What mental state permits Title VII punitive damages and when a managerial employee’s conduct is attributed to the employer.

**Current rule:** Punitive damages may be available when a managerial actor intentionally discriminates with malice or reckless indifference to the claimant’s federally protected rights. Independently egregious conduct is not required, and intentional discrimination alone is insufficient. Qualifying managerial conduct within the scope of employment is ordinarily attributable to the employer, but the employer avoids vicarious punitive liability when it proves that the act was contrary to meaningful good-faith compliance efforts reasonably designed and actually implemented.

**Burdens and proof:** The claimant must establish the statutory punitive mental state. The employer bears the burden of proving the bounded good-faith-compliance defense.

**First controlling authority:** Kolstad v. American Dental Association, 527 U.S. 526 (1999), decided June 22, 1999, punitive-damages mental state, managerial attribution, and good-faith compliance; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Kolstad v. American Dental Association, 527 U.S. 526 (1999), decided June 22, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Kolstad v. American Dental Association, 527 U.S. 526 (1999), June 22, 1999.

### Direction of accrued public-sector compensatory time

**Governing question:** Whether Fair Labor Standards Act § 207(o) gives a public employee a veto over an employer’s direction to use accrued compensatory time.

**Current rule:** Section 207(o) creates no employee veto over a public employer’s direction to use accrued compensatory time when no statute, valid regulation, or binding agreement prohibits the policy. The employer’s scheduling direction does not eliminate the statutory value of the accrued time or the employee’s independently protected rights to use or receive payment for it.

**Exceptions and limits:** A valid regulation, binding agreement, or express statutory protection may restrict compelled use. The rule does not permit forfeiture of accrued value or impairment of an employee-requested use right the statute independently protects.

**First controlling authority:** *Christensen v. Harris County*, 529 U.S. 576, decided May 1, 2000, employer-directed use of public-sector compensatory time; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Christensen v. Harris County*, 529 U.S. 576, decided May 1, 2000, supplies the no-veto rule and preserves contrary statutes, valid regulations, binding agreements, and independently protected accrued-time rights.

**Present operation effective:** *Christensen v. Harris County*, May 1, 2000.

### Prima facie proof, pretext, and judgment as a matter of law

**Governing question:** What evidentiary and review rules apply after an employment-discrimination plaintiff establishes a prima facie case and offers evidence that the employer’s stated reason is false.

**Current rule:** Once the employer produces a lawful explanation, the intermediate burden-of-production presumption disappears, but the evidence establishing the prima facie case remains part of the record. A prima facie case combined with evidence permitting rejection of the employer’s stated reason may support a finding of intentional discrimination; rejection of the reason permits but does not compel that ultimate inference.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** On a motion for judgment as a matter of law, the court must review the complete record, draw reasonable inferences for the verdict winner, refrain from credibility determinations and reweighing, and disregard moving-party evidence the jury was not required to believe.

**Exceptions and limits:** Proof of pretext does not automatically establish liability in every case. Judgment as a matter of law remains available when the complete record, viewed under the stated standard, does not permit a reasonable finding of intentional discrimination.

**First controlling authority:** *Reeves v. Sanderson Plumbing Products, Inc.*, 530 U.S. 133 (2000), decided June 12, 2000, evidentiary effect of a prima facie case and pretext and Rule 50 review; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Reeves v. Sanderson Plumbing Products, Inc.*, 530 U.S. 133 (2000), decided June 12, 2000, supplies the continuing evidentiary force of the prima facie case, the permissible pretext inference, the no-automatic-liability limit, and the complete-record, inference, credibility, weighing, and moving-party-evidence rules for judgment as a matter of law.

**Present operation effective:** *Reeves v. Sanderson Plumbing Products, Inc.*, 530 U.S. 133 (2000), June 12, 2000.

## Housing and Civil Rights

### Family composition and the maximum-occupancy exemption

**Governing question:** When a family-composition zoning rule qualifies for the Fair Housing Act’s reasonable maximum-occupancy exemption.

**Current rule:** The Fair Housing Act’s exemption for reasonable maximum-occupancy restrictions does not cover a zoning rule that permits an unlimited number of related occupants while imposing a numerical limit on unrelated occupants. A maximum-occupancy restriction covered by 42 U.S.C. § 3607(b)(1) limits the number of persons who may occupy a dwelling to address crowding, safety, or comparable conditions. A rule whose operation turns on the occupants’ legal or familial relationships rather than a uniform numerical ceiling remains subject to the Act’s ordinary provisions.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not invalidate single-family zoning, establish that Edmonds violated the Fair Housing Act, decide Oxford House’s accommodation claim, or prevent evenhanded occupancy limits genuinely directed to health and safety.

**First controlling authority:** City of Edmonds v. Oxford House, Inc., 514 U.S. 725, decided May 15, 1995, Family composition and the maximum-occupancy exemption; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** City of Edmonds v. Oxford House, Inc., 514 U.S. 725, decided May 15, 1995, Family composition and the maximum-occupancy exemption; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** City of Edmonds v. Oxford House, Inc., 514 U.S. 725, May 15, 1995.

### Private enforcement of Title VI disparate-impact regulations

**Governing question:** Whether a claimant may privately enforce a valid Title VI § 602 regulation prohibiting unjustified disparate impact and what proof the regulatory claim requires.

**Current rule:** A valid regulation issued under Title VI § 602 that prohibits unjustified disparate impact is privately enforceable through the longstanding direct equitable action and through 42 U.S.C. § 1983 when it gives concrete content to a right anchored in Title VI and Congress has not displaced § 1983 through an incompatible comprehensive remedial scheme. A claimant seeking prospective relief under the regulation need not prove the discriminatory purpose required for a direct § 601 claim but must prove disparate impact and every other element the valid regulation requires.

**Exceptions and limits:** An agency regulation cannot manufacture a free-standing right outside rights-creating statutory text. The rule is confined to valid regulations and prospective equitable enforcement unless another source independently authorizes damages or a different remedy. Section 601 itself continues to require intentional discrimination.

**First controlling authority:** *Powell v. Ridge*, 189 F.3d 387 (3d Cir. 1999), Supreme Court simulation decided April 24, 2000, private enforcement of valid Title VI § 602 disparate-impact regulations; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Powell v. Ridge*, 189 F.3d 387 (3d Cir. 1999), Supreme Court simulation decided April 24, 2000, supplies direct equitable enforcement, § 1983 availability, the no-purpose component of the regulatory claim, and the prospective-remedy limit.

**Present operation effective:** *Powell v. Ridge*, April 24, 2000.

**Related current rules:** *Identification of statutory rights enforceable through § 1983*, under Remedies, governs whether the regulation gives concrete content to a right anchored in the statute. *Congressional displacement of § 1983*, under Remedies, governs whether the statutory remedial scheme forecloses that additional remedy.

## Education

### IDEA reimbursement for a parent-selected private placement

**Governing question:** When parents may obtain reimbursement after rejecting an inadequate public special-education program.

**Current rule:** IDEA permits equitable reimbursement when the public agency failed to offer a free appropriate public education and the parents obtained an education otherwise appropriate under the Act. A private school’s lack of blanket state approval and failure to satisfy procedures written for public-agency placements do not categorically bar relief.

**Burdens and proof:** Parents who act unilaterally bear the financial risk and must establish both the public violation and the appropriateness of the private education.

**Consequence or recurring remedy:** The court retains equitable discretion to reduce or deny an unreasonable amount.

**Exceptions and limits:** Reimbursement is unavailable for a placement that is merely preferred, educationally inappropriate, or unreasonably expensive.

**First controlling authority:** Florence County School District Four v. Carter, 510 U.S. 7 (1993), decided November 9, 1993, Reimbursement for a parent-selected private placement; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Florence County School District Four v. Carter, 510 U.S. 7 (1993), decided November 9, 1993, Reimbursement for a parent-selected private placement; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Florence County School District Four v. Carter, 510 U.S. 7 (1993), November 9, 1993.

### Title IX participation opportunities

**Governing question:** How an intercollegiate athletics program may establish effective accommodation of the interests and abilities of both sexes.

**Current rule:** An institution may demonstrate effective accommodation through substantial proportionality, a continuing practice of responsive program expansion, or full and effective accommodation of the underrepresented sex’s demonstrated interests and abilities. Under the third route, the inquiry is whether actual, competitively viable unmet interests and abilities are fully accommodated. Aggregate comparisons of male and female interest do not excuse proved unmet demand, and numerical imbalance alone establishes neither liability nor a quota.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule preserves no particular team and requires no unsupported, noncompetitive, or infeasible program. After liability, the institution ordinarily receives a fair opportunity to choose a lawful compliance method before more specific judicial relief is imposed.

**First controlling authority:** Kelley v. Board of Trustees of the University of Illinois, 35 F.3d 265, decided May 22, 1995, Title IX participation opportunities; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Kelley v. Board of Trustees of the University of Illinois, 35 F.3d 265, decided May 22, 1995, supplies the three alternative routes. Cohen v. Brown University, 101 F.3d 155, decided June 23, 1997, defines full accommodation under the third route and the ordinary remedial sequence.

**Present operation effective:** Cohen v. Brown University, 101 F.3d 155, June 23, 1997, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

**Development:**
- June 23, 1997, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Cohen v. Brown University, 101 F.3d 155: The third route requires full accommodation of actual, competitively viable unmet interests and abilities of the underrepresented sex; comparative aggregate interest and numerical imbalance do not independently decide liability.

### IDEA neighborhood-school placement

**Governing question:** Whether IDEA creates a presumption that a child must be educated in the neighborhood school.

**Current rule:** IDEA creates no independent rebuttable neighborhood-school presumption. Education with nondisabled children to the maximum extent appropriate remains controlling. The responsible team must consider proximity, place the child as close to home as practicable, and ordinarily use the school the child would attend if nondisabled when the IEP can be implemented there. Those are mandatory placement considerations, not a burden-shifting rule overriding an individualized IEP requiring another arrangement.

**Exceptions and limits:** Convenience alone does not justify transfer; travel distance and supplementary aids remain relevant.

**First controlling authority:** Tyler Murray v. Montrose County School District RE-1J, 51 F.3d 921, decided December 2, 1996, Neighborhood-school placement under IDEA; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Tyler Murray v. Montrose County School District RE-1J, 51 F.3d 921, decided December 2, 1996, Neighborhood-school placement under IDEA; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Tyler Murray v. Montrose County School District RE-1J, 51 F.3d 921, December 2, 1996.

### Voluntary school access to nonprescription preventive items

**Governing question:** Whether voluntary availability of a nonprescription preventive item in a public school violates parental liberty or free exercise without notice or opt out.

**Current rule:** A voluntary school program that merely makes a nonprescription preventive item available does not directly or coercively interfere with parental instruction or substantially burden religious exercise when students need not obtain the item, receive counseling, affirm a message, or suffer any penalty for refusal. The Constitution does not require notice or an opt out solely because the option exists within a compulsory-school setting.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not decide confidential medical treatment, prescription medication, compelled sex education, distribution to a child legally unable to consent, or penalties for following parental instruction.

**First controlling authority:** Elizabeth G. Curtis v. School Committee of Falmouth, 420 Mass. 749, decided January 21, 1997, Parental liberty, familial privacy, and free exercise; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Elizabeth G. Curtis v. School Committee of Falmouth, 420 Mass. 749, decided January 21, 1997, Parental liberty, familial privacy, and free exercise; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Elizabeth G. Curtis v. School Committee of Falmouth, 420 Mass. 749, January 21, 1997.

### Compulsory curricular presentations and parental liberty

**Governing question:** Whether mandatory attendance at a public-school curricular presentation creates a parental veto or free-exercise exemption.

**Current rule:** Mandatory attendance at a curricular presentation, without compelled affirmation, participation, disclosure, bodily intrusion, or religious conduct, does not create a parental right to veto individual curricular content or a substantial free-exercise burden. A procedural-due-process claim fails when no protected liberty is deprived.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not authorize compelled affirmation, disclosure, bodily intrusion, religious exercise, or punishment for protected parental instruction.

**First controlling authority:** Brown v. Hot, Sexy & Safer Productions, Inc., 68 F.3d 525, decided February 24, 1997, Parental liberty, student privacy, procedural due process, and free exercise; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Brown v. Hot, Sexy & Safer Productions, Inc., 68 F.3d 525, decided February 24, 1997, Parental liberty, student privacy, procedural due process, and free exercise; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Brown v. Hot, Sexy & Safer Productions, Inc., 68 F.3d 525, February 24, 1997.

### Recipient fault for Title IX damages

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs recipient fault for Title IX damages.

**Current rule:** Title IX does not impose respondeat superior for every employee wrong. A funding recipient may nevertheless be liable in damages when school-conferred authority materially enables the discrimination and the recipient’s own actual knowledge or willfully blind, deliberately indifferent reporting failure causes the discrimination to continue; actual notice to an official with corrective authority is sufficient but not exclusive. For student-on-student harassment, the known conduct must be severe, pervasive, and objectively offensive, occur in a context substantially controlled by the recipient, and deprive the victim of equal access to educational opportunities or benefits.

**First controlling authority:** Gebser v. Lago Vista Independent School District, 524 U.S. 274 (1998), decided June 22, 1998, Recipient fault for Title IX damages; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Gebser v. Lago Vista Independent School District, 524 U.S. 274 (1998), decided June 22, 1998, supplies the recipient-fault, knowledge, and causation framework. Davis v. Monroe County Board of Education, 526 U.S. 629 (1999), decided May 24, 1999, supplies the peer-harassment, substantial-control, and equal-access components.

**Present operation effective:** Davis v. Monroe County Board of Education, 526 U.S. 629 (1999), May 24, 1999.

**Development:**
- May 24, 1999, Davis v. Monroe County Board of Education, 526 U.S. 629 (1999): The recipient-fault and causation framework now reaches known peer harassment under the recipient’s substantial control when the harassment is severe, pervasive, objectively offensive, and denies equal educational access. The existing knowledge routes remain intact.

### IDEA coverage of necessary nonphysician health services

**Governing question:** When IDEA requires a school district to provide a health service necessary for a child to attend and benefit from special education.

**Current rule:** IDEA requires a nonphysician health service necessary for the child to attend and benefit from special education even when the service is continuous, individualized, and costly. The medical-services exclusion covers services that must be performed by a physician, apart from evaluation and diagnosis.

**Exceptions and limits:** Duration, complexity, and cost do not independently remove a necessary nonphysician service from coverage. Services that must be performed by a physician remain within the statutory exclusion except as the Act provides for evaluation and diagnosis.

**First controlling authority:** Cedar Rapids Community School District v. Garret F., 526 U.S. 66 (1999), decided March 3, 1999, required school-day nursing services; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Cedar Rapids Community School District v. Garret F., 526 U.S. 66 (1999), decided March 3, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Cedar Rapids Community School District v. Garret F., 526 U.S. 66 (1999), March 3, 1999.

### Title IX recipient status through intermediary payments

**Governing question:** When payments from federally funded institutions make an association a recipient of federal financial assistance under Title IX.

**Current rule:** Ordinary membership dues paid from a recipient institution’s general funds do not, without more, make the receiving association a recipient of federal financial assistance. Indirect receipt requires assistance extended through an intermediary or earmarked for the entity, rather than ordinary payments from a recipient’s general resources.

**Exceptions and limits:** Direct federal assistance and delegated-control theories remain governed by their own facts and were not decided by this rule.

**First controlling authority:** National Collegiate Athletic Association v. Smith, 525 U.S. 459 (1999), decided February 23, 1999, recipient status based on membership dues; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** National Collegiate Athletic Association v. Smith, 525 U.S. 459 (1999), decided February 23, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** National Collegiate Athletic Association v. Smith, 525 U.S. 459 (1999), February 23, 1999.

## Health-Care Programs

### Medicare prohibition on redistributed educational costs

**Governing question:** Whether 42 C.F.R. § 413.85(c) permits a patient-care institution to obtain Medicare reimbursement for educational costs previously borne by an affiliated educational institution.

**Current rule:** Section 413.85(c), independently interpreted, bars Medicare reimbursement for educational costs previously borne by an affiliated educational institution and later shifted to the patient-care institution. The regulation’s anti-redistribution language controls even when the underlying education may otherwise be reimbursable.

**Open question:** The decision left open what controlling weight, if any, an agency interpretation receives when its own regulation remains genuinely ambiguous after ordinary interpretation.

**First controlling authority:** Thomas Jefferson University v. Shalala, 512 U.S. 504 (1994), decided June 24, 1994, Meaning of the anti-redistribution regulation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Thomas Jefferson University v. Shalala, 512 U.S. 504 (1994), decided June 24, 1994, Meaning of the anti-redistribution regulation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Thomas Jefferson University v. Shalala, 512 U.S. 504 (1994), June 24, 1994.

### Fault and causation under 38 U.S.C. § 1151

**Governing question:** What causation, additional disability, and fault showing § 1151 requires for a claim arising from VA treatment.

**Current rule:** Section 1151 requires a causal connection between VA hospitalization or treatment and additional disability. It does not require negligence, accident, malpractice, or other fault. A claimant must establish additional disability resulting from VA treatment. The VA may exclude disability attributable to the natural progression of the underlying disease and consequences that were certain or intended incidents of treatment to which the veteran consented, but it may not impose a fault prerequisite Congress omitted.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not make the VA an insurer against the underlying disease or against disabilities that treatment did not cause.

**First controlling authority:** Brown v. Gardner, 513 U.S. 115, decided December 12, 1994, Fault and causation under 38 U.S.C. § 1151; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Brown v. Gardner, 513 U.S. 115, decided December 12, 1994, Fault and causation under 38 U.S.C. § 1151; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Brown v. Gardner, 513 U.S. 115, December 12, 1994.

### EMTALA’s stabilization duty

**Governing question:** What stabilization or transfer EMTALA requires after a participating hospital identifies an emergency medical condition.

**Current rule:** A participating hospital that determines an individual has an emergency respiratory condition must provide treatment within its capabilities necessary to prevent material deterioration of that condition, or effect an appropriate transfer, even when an underlying condition makes the treatment incapable of producing a long-term cure. The stabilization duty is activated by the emergency medical condition actually presented. It is not displaced by a physician’s broader judgment that treatment is futile for an incurable underlying condition. The statute contains no independent medical-futility exception.

**Exceptions and limits:** EMTALA does not require indefinite intensive treatment after stabilization, prescribe nonemergency care, cure the underlying condition, or establish a general federal right to every medically requested treatment.

**First controlling authority:** In re Baby K, 16 F.3d 590, decided December 12, 1994, EMTALA’s stabilization duty; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** In re Baby K, 16 F.3d 590, decided December 12, 1994, EMTALA’s stabilization duty; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** In re Baby K, 16 F.3d 590, December 12, 1994.

### State law and conflict preemption

**Governing question:** When EMTALA preempts state medical law authorizing a hospital to withhold required stabilization.

**Current rule:** State law cannot authorize a participating hospital to withhold stabilization that EMTALA requires. State medical law remains operative outside the direct conflict.

**First controlling authority:** In re Baby K, 16 F.3d 590, decided December 12, 1994, State law and conflict preemption; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** In re Baby K, 16 F.3d 590, decided December 12, 1994, State law and conflict preemption; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** In re Baby K, 16 F.3d 590, December 12, 1994.

### Medicare reimbursement and generally accepted accounting principles

**Governing question:** Whether Medicare reimbursement must follow generally accepted accounting principles for the timing of a hospital’s defeasance loss.

**Current rule:** The Medicare statute and regulations do not require immediate recognition of the hospital’s defeasance loss merely because generally accepted accounting principles would recognize the loss in the refinancing year. Generally accepted accounting principles may inform Medicare cost accounting, but they do not conclusively determine reimbursement. Program rules may spread a financing loss across the periods to which the refinanced debt and reimbursable costs relate.

**First controlling authority:** Shalala v. Guernsey Memorial Hospital, 514 U.S. 87, decided March 6, 1995, Medicare reimbursement and generally accepted accounting principles; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Shalala v. Guernsey Memorial Hospital, 514 U.S. 87, decided March 6, 1995, Medicare reimbursement and generally accepted accounting principles; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Shalala v. Guernsey Memorial Hospital, 514 U.S. 87, March 6, 1995.

## Employee Benefits

### Component-specific ERISA treatment of mixed guaranteed-benefit contracts

**Governing question:** How ERISA’s guaranteed-benefit-policy exclusion applies to a mixed insurance or annuity contract.

**Current rule:** Each materially distinct component of a mixed insurance contract is examined separately. A component falls within ERISA’s guaranteed-benefit exclusion only to the extent the insurer guarantees an aggregate amount of benefits and assumes the corresponding investment risk. Nonexcluded accumulation or free-fund components remain plan assets, and functional fiduciary status follows to the extent the insurer exercises authority or control over them.

**Exceptions and limits:** Insurer status and commingling in a general account do not alone establish the exclusion, and fiduciary status does not attach categorically to every aspect of the insurance contract.

**First controlling authority:** John Hancock Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Harris Trust & Savings Bank, 510 U.S. 86 (1993), decided December 13, 1993, Guaranteed-benefit exclusion and fiduciary control over free funds; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** John Hancock Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Harris Trust & Savings Bank, 510 U.S. 86 (1993), decided December 13, 1993, Guaranteed-benefit exclusion and fiduciary control over free funds; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** John Hancock Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Harris Trust & Savings Bank, 510 U.S. 86 (1993), December 13, 1993.

### Interest under the withdrawal-liability amortization formula

**Governing question:** When interest begins accruing under the MPPAA withdrawal-liability amortization formula.

**Current rule:** For purposes of the MPPAA installment calculation, interest begins accruing on the first day of the plan year following the plan year in which withdrawal occurs. Section 1399(c)(1)(A)(i) requires the schedule to be calculated as though the first payment were made on that date. Interest therefore runs over the subsequent amortization period, not during the withdrawal year or from the preceding valuation date.

**Exceptions and limits:** The holding concerns calculation of the statutory installment schedule. It does not alter separate rules governing assessment, delinquency, default, or interest on payments actually made late.

**First controlling authority:** Milwaukee Brewery Workers’ Pension Plan v. Jos. Schlitz Brewing Co., 513 U.S. 414, decided February 21, 1995, Interest under the withdrawal-liability amortization formula; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Milwaukee Brewery Workers’ Pension Plan v. Jos. Schlitz Brewing Co., 513 U.S. 414, decided February 21, 1995, Interest under the withdrawal-liability amortization formula; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Milwaukee Brewery Workers’ Pension Plan v. Jos. Schlitz Brewing Co., 513 U.S. 414, February 21, 1995.

### Indirect economic influence on ERISA plans

**Governing question:** When a generally applicable state law or tax has an impermissible connection with an ERISA plan.
