**Current rule:** ERISA does not preempt a generally applicable law merely because it increases a plan’s costs, influences purchasing decisions, or directly taxes a facility owned and operated by the plan. Preemption requires ERISA-specific reference, regulation of benefits or plan administration, an alternative enforcement mechanism, or economic force equivalent to a direct command concerning plan structure or choices.

**Exceptions and limits:** A law singling out ERISA plans, dictating benefits or administration, or operating as an alternative enforcement device may be preempted.

**First controlling authority:** New York State Conference of Blue Cross & Blue Shield Plans v. Travelers Insurance Co., 514 U.S. 645, decided April 26, 1995, Indirect economic influence on ERISA plans; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** New York State Conference of Blue Cross & Blue Shield Plans v. Travelers Insurance Co., 514 U.S. 645, decided April 26, 1995, supplies the indirect-cost rule. De Buono v. NYSA-ILA Medical and Clinical Services Fund, 520 U.S. 806 (1997), decided June 2, 1997, confirms the rule for a generally applicable tax on a plan-owned treatment facility.

**Present operation effective:** De Buono v. NYSA-ILA Medical and Clinical Services Fund, 520 U.S. 806 (1997), June 2, 1997.

**Development:**
- June 2, 1997, De Buono v. NYSA-ILA Medical and Clinical Services Fund, 520 U.S. 806 (1997): The Travelers rule does not turn on whether the plan purchases the taxed service or owns the taxed facility and pays the assessment directly.

### Fiduciary status in communications about plan benefits

**Governing question:** When an employer acts as an ERISA fiduciary while communicating about plan benefits.

**Current rule:** An employer does not act as a fiduciary merely because a business decision affects a plan. It does act as a fiduciary when, exercising plan-administration authority, it communicates about benefits for the purpose of guiding beneficiaries’ plan choices. Deliberate material deception in that capacity violates the duty of loyalty.

**First controlling authority:** Varity Corp. v. Howe, 516 U.S. 489, decided March 19, 1996, Fiduciary status in communications about plan benefits; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Varity Corp. v. Howe, 516 U.S. 489, decided March 19, 1996, Fiduciary status in communications about plan benefits; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Varity Corp. v. Howe, 516 U.S. 489, March 19, 1996.

**Development:**
- March 19, 1996, Varity Corp. v. Howe, 516 U.S. 489: *John Hancock* is applied and clarified: fiduciary status follows the function exercised, including plan-administration communications, and does not attach to all employer conduct. *Peacock* is distinguished. *Peacock* involved a new action against a person not liable for an ERISA violation; *Varity* involves equitable relief against the fiduciary that committed the proved breach.

### Individualized equitable relief under § 502(a)(3)

**Governing question:** What violation, defendant, and form of relief a claimant may pursue under ERISA § 502(a)(3), and when that relief is duplicative.

**Current rule:** A claimant must establish a violation of ERISA or the plan and seek relief traditionally available in equity. Section 502(a)(3) authorizes historically equitable relief against a nonfiduciary party in interest that receives plan property through a transaction prohibited by § 406(a); the defendant’s nonfiduciary status is not a categorical bar. The claimant must establish the prohibited transaction, the transferee’s actual or constructive knowledge of the circumstances making the transaction unlawful, and the basis for tracing the property or proceeds into the requested equitable remedy.

**Burdens and proof:** The claimant bears the stated violation, knowledge, tracing, and remedial showings. The defendant may invoke traditional equitable defenses applicable to the particular form of relief.

**Exceptions and limits:** Section 502(a)(3) remains unavailable for duplicative recovery, legal damages relabeled as equity, or circumvention of a more specific provision that fully remedies the same injury. The rule does not impose fiduciary duties on every transaction counterparty, authorize compensatory or punitive damages, or establish liability merely from receipt of plan property.

**First controlling authority:** *Varity Corp. v. Howe*, 516 U.S. 489, decided March 19, 1996, individualized equitable relief under § 502(a)(3); established the violation, equitable-form, and nonduplication components.

**Controlling authorities:** *Varity Corp. v. Howe*, 516 U.S. 489, decided March 19, 1996, supplies the individualized-equitable-relief and nonduplication components. *Harris Trust and Savings Bank v. Salomon Smith Barney Inc.*, 530 U.S. 238 (2000), decided June 12, 2000, supplies the nonfiduciary transferee route and the prohibited-transaction, knowledge, tracing, and traditional-equitable-defense components.

**Present operation effective:** *Harris Trust and Savings Bank v. Salomon Smith Barney Inc.*, 530 U.S. 238 (2000), June 12, 2000.

**Development:**
- June 12, 2000, *Harris Trust and Savings Bank v. Salomon Smith Barney Inc.*: Before *Harris Trust*, the entry required an ERISA violation and traditionally equitable, nonduplicative relief. After *Harris Trust*, nonfiduciary status does not categorically bar historically equitable relief against a party in interest receiving plan property through a prohibited transaction, subject to knowledge, tracing, and equitable defenses. The prohibition on legal damages and duplicate recovery remains unchanged.

### Interference with attainment of welfare-plan rights under ERISA § 510

**Governing question:** Whether § 510 protects rights under a welfare plan that are not statutorily vested.

**Current rule:** ERISA § 510 reaches discharge or other prohibited action intended to interfere with attainment of rights under an employee welfare benefit plan even when the benefits are not subject to statutory vesting. The claimant must identify a plan right that may be attained and prove specific intent, prohibited action, causation, proper defendant, and the other statutory elements.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not vest welfare benefits, freeze plan terms, find prohibited intent, or impose liability on an entity outside § 510’s proper reach.

**First controlling authority:** Inter-Modal Rail Employees Association v. Atchison, Topeka & Santa Fe Railway Co., 520 U.S. 510 (1997), decided May 12, 1997, Section 510 and nonvesting welfare benefits; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Inter-Modal Rail Employees Association v. Atchison, Topeka & Santa Fe Railway Co., 520 U.S. 510 (1997), decided May 12, 1997, Section 510 and nonvesting welfare benefits; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Inter-Modal Rail Employees Association v. Atchison, Topeka & Santa Fe Railway Co., 520 U.S. 510 (1997), May 12, 1997.

### Plan amendment as settlor conduct

**Governing question:** Whether an employer acts as an ERISA fiduciary when deciding whether and how to amend a pension plan.

**Current rule:** Fiduciary status follows the function performed. Plan design and amendment are settlor functions; implementation and administration of the adopted plan may separately involve fiduciary duties.

**First controlling authority:** Lockheed Corp. v. Spink, 517 U.S. 882, decided June 10, 1996, Plan amendment as settlor conduct; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Lockheed Corp. v. Spink, 517 U.S. 882, decided June 10, 1996, Plan amendment as settlor conduct; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Lockheed Corp. v. Spink, 517 U.S. 882, June 10, 1996.

### Defined-benefit accrued rights and actuarial surplus

**Governing question:** What interests participants possess in a defined-benefit plan’s promised benefits and actuarial surplus.

**Current rule:** Participants in a defined-benefit plan have enforceable rights to their accrued benefits, not individual ownership of the plan’s actuarial surplus. A plan amendment that leaves accrued benefits intact does not violate the anti-cutback rule merely because it changes contribution structures or uses surplus to support new plan terms.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not authorize reduction of accrued benefits or decide ownership under a materially different plan structure that gives participants a specific interest in identified assets.

**First controlling authority:** Hughes Aircraft Co. v. Jacobson, 525 U.S. 432 (1999), decided January 25, 1999, participant interests in surplus and accrued benefits; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Hughes Aircraft Co. v. Jacobson, 525 U.S. 432 (1999), decided January 25, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Hughes Aircraft Co. v. Jacobson, 525 U.S. 432 (1999), January 25, 1999.

### Partial termination of a defined-benefit plan

**Governing question:** When a continuing defined-benefit plan is partially terminated.

**Current rule:** A continuing defined-benefit plan is not partially terminated merely because the sponsor changes contribution structures and benefit options while the plan continues accepting participants and paying promised benefits.

**Exceptions and limits:** A genuine cessation, exclusion of a material participant group, or failure to preserve accrued benefits remains governed by the statutory partial-termination rule on its own facts.

**First controlling authority:** Hughes Aircraft Co. v. Jacobson, 525 U.S. 432 (1999), decided January 25, 1999, partial termination; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Hughes Aircraft Co. v. Jacobson, 525 U.S. 432 (1999), decided January 25, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Hughes Aircraft Co. v. Jacobson, 525 U.S. 432 (1999), January 25, 1999.

### Insurance notice-prejudice rules and ERISA

**Governing question:** Whether a state notice-prejudice rule is saved from ERISA preemption and may govern a benefits action.

**Current rule:** A state rule requiring an insurer to prove actual prejudice before enforcing a late-notice forfeiture regulates insurance and is saved from ERISA preemption. It may supply the rule of decision in an action under ERISA § 502(a)(1)(B) without creating a new federal remedy.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not save a state command that reallocates core plan-administration responsibilities or conflicts with ERISA’s exclusive remedial structure.

**First controlling authority:** UNUM Life Insurance Co. of America v. Ward, 526 U.S. 358 (1999), decided April 20, 1999, California notice-prejudice rule; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** UNUM Life Insurance Co. of America v. Ward, 526 U.S. 358 (1999), decided April 20, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** UNUM Life Insurance Co. of America v. Ward, 526 U.S. 358 (1999), April 20, 1999.

### State assignment of claim-notice agency under ERISA

**Governing question:** Whether a State may categorically deem an employer-plan administrator the insurer’s agent for receipt of every claim notice.

**Current rule:** A State may not categorically assign the employer-plan administrator as the insurer’s agent for receipt of every claim notice. That command regulates core plan administration and is preempted by ERISA.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not prevent agency from arising under plan terms or ordinary fact-specific agency principles consistent with ERISA.

**First controlling authority:** UNUM Life Insurance Co. of America v. Ward, 526 U.S. 358 (1999), decided April 20, 1999, employer as insurer’s categorical agent; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** UNUM Life Insurance Co. of America v. Ward, 526 U.S. 358 (1999), decided April 20, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** UNUM Life Insurance Co. of America v. Ward, 526 U.S. 358 (1999), April 20, 1999.

### Fiduciary status for mixed treatment-and-benefit decisions

**Governing question:** Whether a treating physician in an ERISA-covered health maintenance organization acts as an ERISA fiduciary when making a decision inseparably combining medical treatment and benefit eligibility.

**Current rule:** A treating HMO physician does not act as an ERISA fiduciary when making an inseparably mixed decision that determines both what medical treatment the patient should receive and whether the plan will provide that treatment. The result does not change merely because the compensation arrangement gives the physician a financial incentive to limit care.

**Exceptions and limits:** Fiduciary status remains function specific. The rule does not govern a separable plan-administration decision, excuse deception in a fiduciary plan communication, determine medical-malpractice liability, or immunize an HMO or physician from otherwise applicable law.

**First controlling authority:** *Pegram v. Herdrich*, 530 U.S. 211 (2000), decided June 12, 2000, fiduciary status for an inseparably mixed treatment-and-benefit decision; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Pegram v. Herdrich*, 530 U.S. 211 (2000), decided June 12, 2000, supplies the treating-physician, inseparably mixed decision, incentive, and separable-administration boundaries.

**Present operation effective:** *Pegram v. Herdrich*, 530 U.S. 211 (2000), June 12, 2000.

**Related current rules:** `Fiduciary status in communications about plan benefits`, under Employee Benefits, governs plan-administration communications rather than an inseparably mixed medical-treatment and benefit decision.

## Consumer Protection

### Attorneys engaged in consumer-debt litigation

**Governing question:** When a lawyer collecting consumer debts through litigation qualifies as an FDCPA debt collector.

**Current rule:** The FDCPA applies to lawyers who regularly attempt to collect consumer debts through litigation. A lawyer qualifies as a debt collector when the lawyer regularly collects or attempts to collect consumer debts owed to another, whether the collection method is correspondence, negotiation, filing suit, or litigation activity. The plaintiff must still establish a specific practice prohibited by the Act, and all express statutory exceptions remain operative.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not cover a lawyer who does not regularly collect consumer debts. It does not make ordinary advocacy automatically false, misleading, abusive, or unfair, and it does not decide whether Heintz’s particular demand was unlawful.

**First controlling authority:** Heintz v. Jenkins, 514 U.S. 291, decided April 18, 1995, Attorneys engaged in consumer-debt litigation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Heintz v. Jenkins, 514 U.S. 291, decided April 18, 1995, Attorneys engaged in consumer-debt litigation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Heintz v. Jenkins, 514 U.S. 291, April 18, 1995.

## Banking and Financial Regulation

### National-bank authority to broker annuities

**Governing question:** Whether the National Bank Act permits national banks to broker fixed and variable annuities, and what deference the Comptroller receives.

**Current rule:** The Comptroller reasonably construed the National Bank Act to permit national banks to act as agents in the sale of fixed and variable annuities. When the National Bank Act leaves the precise boundary of an incidental banking power unresolved, the Comptroller’s permissible construction receives controlling weight so long as it remains within the statutory design.

**First controlling authority:** NationsBank of North Carolina, N.A. v. Variable Annuity Life Insurance Co. / Ludwig v. Variable Annuity Life Insurance Co., 513 U.S. 251, decided January 19, 1995, National-bank authority to broker annuities; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** NationsBank of North Carolina, N.A. v. Variable Annuity Life Insurance Co. / Ludwig v. Variable Annuity Life Insurance Co., 513 U.S. 251, decided January 19, 1995, National-bank authority to broker annuities; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** NationsBank of North Carolina, N.A. v. Variable Annuity Life Insurance Co. / Ludwig v. Variable Annuity Life Insurance Co., 513 U.S. 251, January 19, 1995.

### Treatment of annuities under 12 U.S.C. § 92

**Governing question:** Whether annuities count as insurance or investment products under 12 U.S.C. § 92.

**Current rule:** The Comptroller may reasonably classify fixed and variable annuities as investment products rather than insurance for purposes of § 92 when they serve investment and retirement functions rather than conventional indemnity against loss. Fixed annuities’ debt-like features and the permissibility of classifying fixed and variable products together support that treatment.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not authorize national banks to underwrite insurance generally or displace state regulation preserved by federal law.

**First controlling authority:** NationsBank of North Carolina, N.A. v. Variable Annuity Life Insurance Co. / Ludwig v. Variable Annuity Life Insurance Co., 513 U.S. 251, decided January 19, 1995, Treatment of annuities under 12 U.S.C. § 92; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** NationsBank of North Carolina, N.A. v. Variable Annuity Life Insurance Co. / Ludwig v. Variable Annuity Life Insurance Co., 513 U.S. 251, decided January 19, 1995, Treatment of annuities under 12 U.S.C. § 92; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** NationsBank of North Carolina, N.A. v. Variable Annuity Life Insurance Co. / Ludwig v. Variable Annuity Life Insurance Co., 513 U.S. 251, January 19, 1995.

### Federal interbank liability and jurisdiction

**Governing question:** When Regulation CC and § 4010 authorize a federal interbank liability claim and jurisdiction.

**Current rule:** When § 4010(f) authorizes interbank liability standards, Regulation CC supplies those standards, and § 4010(d) authorizes federal or state adjudication of any action under the section, a regulated bank may bring a federal interbank claim. Recognition of the claim and jurisdiction does not establish breach or damages.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not determine whether Midwest violated Regulation CC, what damages are recoverable, or whether state law is displaced in circumstances presenting no actual conflict.

**First controlling authority:** Bank One Chicago, N.A. v. Midwest Bank & Trust Co., 516 U.S. 264, decided January 17, 1996, Federal interbank liability and jurisdiction; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Bank One Chicago, N.A. v. Midwest Bank & Trust Co., 516 U.S. 264, decided January 17, 1996, Federal interbank liability and jurisdiction; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Bank One Chicago, N.A. v. Midwest Bank & Trust Co., 516 U.S. 264, January 17, 1996.

**Development:**
- January 17, 1996, Bank One Chicago, N.A. v. Midwest Bank & Trust Co., 516 U.S. 264: The decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule recognizing a federal interbank claim under the EFAA and Regulation CC. It preserves the existing distinction among statutory duty, judicial cause of action, jurisdiction, and ultimate liability.

### State standards and § 1821(k) officer-director liability

**Governing question:** What law governs the duty of care for officers and directors of a federally chartered savings institution in an FDIC receivership action.

**Current rule:** State law ordinarily governs traditional corporate duties. Section 1821(k) establishes gross negligence as a federal minimum and preserves stricter applicable state standards, including simple negligence. Federal chartering, insurance, supervision, receivership convenience, or a desire to increase recoveries does not itself authorize a federal common-law ordinary-negligence rule.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not determine a State’s business-judgment doctrine or a distinct federal enforcement action based on duties created by federal law.

**First controlling authority:** Atherton v. Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, 519 U.S. 213 (1997), decided January 14, 1997, Governing law and § 1821(k); established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Atherton v. Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, 519 U.S. 213 (1997), decided January 14, 1997, Governing law and § 1821(k); supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Atherton v. Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, 519 U.S. 213 (1997), January 14, 1997.

### Occupational common bond for federal credit unions

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs meaning of the occupational common bond.

**Current rule:** The Federal Credit Union Act does not permit one occupational credit union to aggregate multiple unrelated employee groups whose only connection is membership in the same credit union.

**First controlling authority:** National Credit Union Administration v. First National Bank & Trust Co., 522 U.S. 479 (1998), decided February 25, 1998, Meaning of the occupational common bond; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** National Credit Union Administration v. First National Bank & Trust Co., 522 U.S. 479 (1998), decided February 25, 1998, Meaning of the occupational common bond; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** National Credit Union Administration v. First National Bank & Trust Co., 522 U.S. 479 (1998), February 25, 1998.

## Securities Regulation

### Private liability for culpable assistance to a § 10(b) violation

**Governing question:** When a private § 10(b) plaintiff may impose liability on a defendant who materially assists another actor’s completed securities fraud.

**Current rule:** A private § 10(b) plaintiff may pursue aiding-and-abetting liability by proving a completed primary violation, the secondary defendant’s knowledge of that violation or conscious recklessness approaching actual knowledge, and substantial assistance that materially facilitated it. An omission qualifies only when the defendant had a defined, transaction-specific responsibility to act.

**Burdens and proof:** The plaintiff retains the ordinary burdens of proving the primary violation, the secondary defendant’s state of mind and material assistance, transaction causation, loss causation, and damages.

**Exceptions and limits:** Professional status, negligence, proximity to the transaction, passive association, or silence unconnected to a defined responsibility is insufficient.

**First controlling authority:** Central Bank of Denver, N.A. v. First Interstate Bank of Denver, N.A., 511 U.S. 164 (1994), decided April 19, 1994, Limited private liability for culpable assistance; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Central Bank of Denver, N.A. v. First Interstate Bank of Denver, N.A., 511 U.S. 164 (1994), decided April 19, 1994, Limited private liability for culpable assistance; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Central Bank of Denver, N.A. v. First Interstate Bank of Denver, N.A., 511 U.S. 164 (1994), April 19, 1994.

### Section 12(2) and a bespoke private sale of an operating company

**Governing question:** Whether Section 12(2) applies to an individually negotiated sale of an operating company between parties with direct access to the enterprise.

**Current rule:** Section 12(2) does not apply to this individually negotiated sale of substantially an entire business through a contract allocating disclosure and valuation risks between parties with direct access to the enterprise.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not restrict § 17(a), § 10(b), ordinary fraud, contract remedies, or another provision whose text reaches private transactions. It leaves standardized offerings to multiple institutional investors unresolved.

**First controlling authority:** Gustafson v. Alloyd Co., 513 U.S. 561, decided February 28, 1995, Section 12(2) and a bespoke private sale of an operating company; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Gustafson v. Alloyd Co., 513 U.S. 561, decided February 28, 1995, Section 12(2) and a bespoke private sale of an operating company; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Gustafson v. Alloyd Co., 513 U.S. 561, February 28, 1995.

### Source-directed misappropriation under § 10(b)

**Governing question:** When secret use of entrusted information constitutes primary securities fraud.

**Current rule:** A trader commits primary fraud under § 10(b) when, with scienter, the trader secretly uses material nonpublic information entrusted by a source participating in an actual or contemplated securities transaction, breaches a defined duty of fidelity or confidentiality to that source, and trades securities whose value is materially affected by the source’s transaction. Trading must consummate or monetize the concealed breach. Disclosure of the intended use to the source before trading removes the deception required by this theory.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule creates no general parity-of-information duty, reaches no innocent receipt, and does not federalize every misuse of confidential information followed by trading.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. O’Hagan, 521 U.S. 642 (1997), decided June 25, 1997, Primary § 10(b) liability for deceptive misappropriation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. O’Hagan, 521 U.S. 642 (1997), decided June 25, 1997, Primary § 10(b) liability for deceptive misappropriation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. O’Hagan, 521 U.S. 642 (1997), June 25, 1997.

### Rule 14e-3 tender-offer trading prohibition

**Governing question:** What preventive trading rule § 14(e) authorizes for material nonpublic tender-offer information.

**Current rule:** Section 14(e) authorizes Rule 14e-3(a)’s bounded prohibition on trading while possessing material nonpublic tender-offer information obtained from the offeror, issuer, or persons acting for them, when the trader knows or has reason to know the information came from one of those sources. The express preventive delegation permits a prophylactic rule extending beyond proof of completed common-law fraud, but the rule must remain fitted to the tender-offer domain and its source, information, and knowledge limits.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not authorize regulation of all confidential-information trading or dispense with the regulation’s source and knowledge requirements.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. O’Hagan, 521 U.S. 642 (1997), decided June 25, 1997, Rule 14e-3(a) and the preventive tender-offer delegation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. O’Hagan, 521 U.S. 642 (1997), decided June 25, 1997, Rule 14e-3(a) and the preventive tender-offer delegation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. O’Hagan, 521 U.S. 642 (1997), June 25, 1997.

## Trademark

### Color as a protectable trademark

**Governing question:** When color alone can function as a protectable, nonfunctional trademark under the Lanham Act.

**Current rule:** The Lanham Act permits a color, standing alone, to serve as a trademark when the color has acquired source-identifying significance and is not functional. A claimant seeking protection for color alone must establish that consumers understand the color to identify the source of the goods. Protection is unavailable when the color is essential to the product’s use or purpose, affects cost or quality, or is competitively necessary. Ordinary likelihood-of-confusion requirements remain applicable.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not protect color in the abstract, functional or competitively necessary colors, or colors lacking secondary meaning. It does not eliminate ordinary proof of likely confusion.

**First controlling authority:** Qualitex Co. v. Jacobson Products Co., 514 U.S. 159, decided March 28, 1995, Color as a protectable trademark; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Qualitex Co. v. Jacobson Products Co., 514 U.S. 159, decided March 28, 1995, Color as a protectable trademark; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Qualitex Co. v. Jacobson Products Co., 514 U.S. 159, March 28, 1995.

### Secondary meaning for product-design trade dress

**Governing question:** What distinctiveness showing is required to protect product design as unregistered trade dress under Lanham Act § 43(a).

**Current rule:** Product design cannot be inherently distinctive. A claimant seeking product-design trade-dress protection must prove secondary meaning—that the design’s primary significance to consumers is identification of source rather than the product itself—and must separately prove nonfunctionality, likely confusion, and the remaining statutory elements.

**Exceptions and limits:** The categorical secondary-meaning requirement concerns product design. It does not eliminate the distinct treatment of product packaging or other trade dress that consumers may immediately understand as source identifying, and secondary meaning does not excuse functionality or confusion proof.

**First controlling authority:** *Wal-Mart Stores, Inc. v. Samara Brothers, Inc.*, 529 U.S. 205, decided March 22, 2000, secondary meaning for product-design trade dress; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Wal-Mart Stores, Inc. v. Samara Brothers, Inc.*, 529 U.S. 205, decided March 22, 2000, supplies the product-design classification, secondary-meaning requirement, and preservation of the other statutory elements.

**Present operation effective:** *Wal-Mart Stores, Inc. v. Samara Brothers, Inc.*, March 22, 2000.

## Copyright

### Commercial parody under the fair-use factors

**Governing question:** How the four statutory fair-use factors apply to a commercial work that comments on the copyrighted original.

**Current rule:** A commercial parody may qualify as fair use. The four factors must be weighed together in light of copyright’s purposes. Transformative use reduces the force of commerciality; the amount copied is judged in relation to the need to make the target recognizable; and market injury concerns substitution in a market copyright law protects, not lost demand caused by criticism or ridicule. Commerciality and market harm are not presumptively dispositive.

**Exceptions and limits:** Humor alone does not establish parody. The new work must reasonably be perceived as commenting on the original and remains subject to the complete statutory inquiry.

**First controlling authority:** Campbell v. Acuff-Rose Music, Inc., 510 U.S. 569 (1994), decided March 7, 1994, Commercial parody under the statutory fair-use inquiry; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Campbell v. Acuff-Rose Music, Inc., 510 U.S. 569 (1994), decided March 7, 1994, Commercial parody under the statutory fair-use inquiry; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Campbell v. Acuff-Rose Music, Inc., 510 U.S. 569 (1994), March 7, 1994.

### Evenhanded discretionary attorney-fee awards under § 505

**Governing question:** What legal standard governs a Copyright Act attorney-fee request by a prevailing plaintiff or defendant.

**Current rule:** Prevailing copyright plaintiffs and defendants are evaluated under the same discretionary standard. Neither side is presumptively entitled to fees, and a prevailing defendant need not prove frivolousness or bad faith as a categorical prerequisite. Courts may consider objective unreasonableness, frivolousness, motivation, compensation, deterrence, and other circumstances faithful to copyright’s purposes, applying all considerations evenhandedly.

**Exceptions and limits:** The considerations are nonexclusive, no single one is mandatory, and § 505 does not establish automatic prevailing-party fee shifting.

**First controlling authority:** Fogerty v. Fantasy, Inc., 510 U.S. 517 (1994), decided March 1, 1994, Evenhanded discretionary awards under § 505; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Fogerty v. Fantasy, Inc., 510 U.S. 517 (1994), decided March 1, 1994, Evenhanded discretionary awards under § 505; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Fogerty v. Fantasy, Inc., 510 U.S. 517 (1994), March 1, 1994.

### First sale and reimported domestic copies

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs first sale and reimported domestic copies.

**Current rule:** Section 602(a) does not revive an exhausted § 106(3) distribution right when a copy made lawfully in the United States is sold abroad with the copyright owner’s authorization and later reimported by its lawful owner.

**First controlling authority:** Quality King Distributors, Inc. v. L’anza Research International, Inc., 523 U.S. 135 (1998), decided March 9, 1998, First sale and reimported domestic copies; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Quality King Distributors, Inc. v. L’anza Research International, Inc., 523 U.S. 135 (1998), decided March 9, 1998, First sale and reimported domestic copies; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Quality King Distributors, Inc. v. L’anza Research International, Inc., 523 U.S. 135 (1998), March 9, 1998.

### Jury determination of copyright statutory damages

**Governing question:** Who determines factual issues and the amount of copyright statutory damages after a timely jury demand.

**Current rule:** Section 504(c) does not itself confer a statutory jury right. On a timely demand, however, the Seventh Amendment assigns to the jury all factual issues pertinent to copyright statutory damages, including willfulness and selection of the amount within the lawful statutory range. Lawful judicial review of the award remains available.

**First controlling authority:** Feltner v. Columbia Pictures Television, Inc., 523 U.S. 340 (1998), decided March 31, 1998, jury determination of copyright statutory damages; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Feltner v. Columbia Pictures Television, Inc., 523 U.S. 340 (1998), decided March 31, 1998, jury determination of copyright statutory damages; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Feltner v. Columbia Pictures Television, Inc., 523 U.S. 340 (1998), March 31, 1998.

## Racketeer Influenced and Corrupt Organizations Act

### No economic-motive element in RICO

**Governing question:** Whether RICO requires the enterprise or alleged predicate acts to pursue profit or another economic objective.

**Current rule:** RICO contains no economic-motive element. Ideological purpose neither establishes nor defeats liability; a civil plaintiff must prove the enacted enterprise, pattern, qualifying predicates, injury to business or property, and required causal connection.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not decide whether particular conduct satisfies the predicates or pattern, and protected advocacy, association, picketing, and persuasion do not become racketeering merely because they accompany a political movement.

**First controlling authority:** National Organization for Women, Inc. v. Scheidler, 510 U.S. 249 (1994), decided January 24, 1994, Economic motive under RICO; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** National Organization for Women, Inc. v. Scheidler, 510 U.S. 249 (1994), decided January 24, 1994, Economic motive under RICO; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** National Organization for Women, Inc. v. Scheidler, 510 U.S. 249 (1994), January 24, 1994.

### A RICO enterprise’s own interstate operations

**Governing question:** What direct interstate operations make a RICO enterprise engaged in interstate commerce under 18 U.S.C. § 1962(a).

**Current rule:** An enterprise is engaged in interstate commerce under 18 U.S.C. § 1962(a) when its own continuing operations include interstate purchasing, interstate recruitment or movement of labor, interstate shipment of output, or comparable direct interstate activity. When that direct commerce is proved, no separate substantial-effects analysis is necessary.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide whether a purely intrastate enterprise with only remote economic consequences satisfies § 1962(a).

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Robertson, 514 U.S. 669, decided May 1, 1995, A RICO enterprise’s own interstate operations; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Robertson, 514 U.S. 669, decided May 1, 1995, A RICO enterprise’s own interstate operations; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Robertson, 514 U.S. 669, May 1, 1995.

**Related current rules:** Interstate commerce, anti-evasion, and material interstate externalities, under Commerce Clause. Direct interstate operations place the enterprise within interstate commerce itself without reliance on aggregate downstream effects.

### Separate accrual without revival of stale civil RICO injuries

**Governing question:** How discovery of the alleged pattern and later predicate acts affect limitations for earlier and later civil RICO injuries.

**Current rule:** The four-year civil RICO limitations period does not await discovery of the complete racketeering pattern. A later predicate act does not revive damages for an older time-barred injury. A later act causing a genuinely new and independent injury may support a separately accruing claim limited to that injury and resulting damages. Each injury must be connected to the act causing it; a timely new injury does not reset the period for completed older harm merely because both acts are part of one alleged pattern.

**Exceptions and limits:** The universal civil RICO accrual trigger remains unresolved, as does accrual when an injury occurs before the alleged pattern is legally complete. Fraudulent-concealment tolling remains governed by the separate concealment-and-diligence rule.

**First controlling authority:** Klehr v. A.O. Smith Corp., 521 U.S. 179 (1997), decided June 19, 1997, separate accrual and no revival of stale injuries; established the no-revival and new-injury components.

**Controlling authorities:** Klehr v. A.O. Smith Corp., 521 U.S. 179 (1997), decided June 19, 1997, supplies separate accrual and the prohibition on reviving stale injuries through later predicates. Rotella v. Wood, 528 U.S. 549 (2000), decided February 23, 2000, supplies the rule that discovery of the complete pattern is not an additional accrual prerequisite and preserves the unresolved universal trigger.

**Present operation effective:** Rotella v. Wood, 528 U.S. 549 (2000), February 23, 2000.

**Related current rules:** Fraudulent-concealment diligence in civil RICO. That rule supplies the distinct equitable tolling route preserved by *Rotella*.

**Development:**
- February 23, 2000, Rotella v. Wood, 528 U.S. 549: *Klehr* already barred revival of an earlier stale injury through a later predicate and allowed separate accrual for a genuinely new injury. The current rule now also rejects discovery of the complete pattern as an additional accrual prerequisite, while leaving the universal trigger and an injury predating a legally complete pattern unresolved.

### Fraudulent-concealment diligence in civil RICO

**Governing question:** What a civil RICO plaintiff must prove to toll limitations through fraudulent concealment.

**Current rule:** A plaintiff relying on fraudulent concealment must prove concealment and reasonable diligence in attempting to discover the cause of action. Concealment does not toll the period when reasonable investigation would have disclosed the claim.

**Burdens and proof:** The claimant bears persuasion on concealment and reasonable diligence under the governing equitable doctrine.

**Exceptions and limits:** No universal form of investigation is prescribed, and the rule does not decide concealment that could not be overcome through reasonable diligence.

**First controlling authority:** Klehr v. A.O. Smith Corp., 521 U.S. 179 (1997), decided June 19, 1997, Fraudulent concealment; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Klehr v. A.O. Smith Corp., 521 U.S. 179 (1997), decided June 19, 1997, Fraudulent concealment; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Klehr v. A.O. Smith Corp., 521 U.S. 179 (1997), June 19, 1997.

## Taxation

### Income from repurchase agreements involving federal securities

**Governing question:** Whether § 3124(a) exempts income from repurchase agreements collateralized by federal securities.

**Current rule:** Section 3124(a) does not exempt the income because the mutual funds receive interest on private loans to seller-borrowers, not interest on obligations of the United States. Federal securities used as collateral do not transform the return on an independent private lending obligation into federally exempt interest. The transaction is classified according to its legal and economic operation rather than the parties’ sale-and-repurchase labels.

**Exceptions and limits:** A transaction conveying genuine beneficial ownership of federal securities and the accompanying federal coupon or discount income may present a different question.

**First controlling authority:** Nebraska Department of Revenue v. Loewenstein, 513 U.S. 123, decided December 12, 1994, Income from repurchase agreements involving federal securities; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Nebraska Department of Revenue v. Loewenstein, 513 U.S. 123, decided December 12, 1994, Income from repurchase agreements involving federal securities; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Nebraska Department of Revenue v. Loewenstein, 513 U.S. 123, December 12, 1994.

### ADEA settlements under § 104(a)(2)

**Governing question:** Whether ADEA backpay and liquidated damages are excluded from gross income under § 104(a)(2).

**Current rule:** ADEA backpay and liquidated damages are not excluded from gross income under § 104(a)(2). Exclusion requires both a cause of action based on tort or tort-type rights and damages received on account of personal injuries or sickness. Each component of a recovery must satisfy the statutory causal requirement.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide damages expressly compensating physical injury, medical injury, or an emotional injury under a statute and record satisfying the enacted exclusion.

**First controlling authority:** Commissioner v. Schleier, 515 U.S. 323, decided June 14, 1995, ADEA settlements under § 104(a)(2); established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Commissioner v. Schleier, 515 U.S. 323, decided June 14, 1995, ADEA settlements under § 104(a)(2); supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Commissioner v. Schleier, 515 U.S. 323, June 14, 1995.

### Tax on insurance inseparable from export transit

**Governing question:** Whether § 4371 may tax premiums for insurance covering goods in export transit under the Export Clause.

**Current rule:** Section 4371 may not be applied to premiums paid for insurance covering goods in export transit because that application imposes a tax within the Export Clause’s categorical prohibition.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not exempt exporters from income, property, payroll, or other generally applicable taxes imposed on independent events unrelated to export transit. The Court does not decide every possible tax on a service connected more remotely to exports.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. International Business Machines Corp., 517 U.S. 843, decided June 10, 1996, Tax on insurance inseparable from export transit; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. International Business Machines Corp., 517 U.S. 843, decided June 10, 1996, Tax on insurance inseparable from export transit; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. International Business Machines Corp., 517 U.S. 843, June 10, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 10, 1996, United States v. International Business Machines Corp., 517 U.S. 843: The decision creates the first controlling simulated Export Clause rule. A tax inseparably incident to the export transit of goods remains forbidden even when generally applicable and nondiscriminatory; taxes on legally independent events remain outside the holding.

### Export tax or compensatory user fee

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs export tax or compensatory user fee.

**Current rule:** A charge on exports violates the Export Clause when its ad valorem measure does not fairly approximate governmental services, facilities, or benefits used by the exporter. General applicability and nondiscrimination do not cure a tax laid on exports.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. United States Shoe Corp., 523 U.S. 360 (1998), decided March 31, 1998, Export tax or compensatory user fee; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. United States Shoe Corp., 523 U.S. 360 (1998), decided March 31, 1998, Export tax or compensatory user fee; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. United States Shoe Corp., 523 U.S. 360 (1998), March 31, 1998.

### Nondiscriminatory taxation of federal compensation

**Governing question:** When a state or local levy measured by federal compensation may be imposed on federal officers.

**Current rule:** Under 4 U.S.C. § 111, a nondiscriminatory levy measured by compensation may be collected from federal officers, including federal judges, when it neither discriminates because of the federal source of pay nor regulates performance of the federal function. Taxation of compensation does not by itself impose an unconstitutional burden on the Federal Government.

**Exceptions and limits:** Source-based discrimination and a levy operating as regulation of official federal functions remain prohibited.

**First controlling authority:** Jefferson County v. Acker, 527 U.S. 423 (1999), decided June 21, 1999, occupational tax on federal judges; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Jefferson County v. Acker, 527 U.S. 423 (1999), decided June 21, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Jefferson County v. Acker, 527 U.S. 423 (1999), June 21, 1999.

### State-created control and federal tax liens

**Governing question:** When a state-created legal interest constitutes property or a right to property under 26 U.S.C. § 6321.

**Current rule:** State law defines the legal interests a taxpayer possesses, but federal law determines whether those interests constitute property or rights to property for the federal tax lien. A legally protected and economically valuable power to accept property or redirect it to another is reached by a preexisting federal lien; a state relation-back, exemption, or disclaimer label cannot erase control already acquired or bind the federal classification.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not govern a disclaimer completed before the taxpayer acquires legally cognizable control.

**First controlling authority:** Drye v. United States, 528 U.S. 49 (1999), decided December 7, 1999, inheritance-control interest under § 6321; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Drye v. United States, 528 U.S. 49 (1999), decided December 7, 1999, supplies the allocation between state-law interest definition and federal classification and the treatment of state disclaimer and relation-back rules.

**Present operation effective:** Drye v. United States, 528 U.S. 49 (1999), December 7, 1999.

### Attribution of expenses to constitutionally exempt income

**Governing question:** When a State may limit a nondomiciliary corporation’s expense deduction by reference to constitutionally exempt nonunitary income.

**Current rule:** A State may not disallow interest or another expense by reference to constitutionally exempt nonunitary income unless it establishes a fair attribution between the expense and that income. It may disallow expenses actually attributable to exempt income through a tracing, ratio-based, or other supported allocation method that rationally reflects how the income and expense were generated.

**Exceptions and limits:** The amount of exempt income cannot serve automatically as a dollar-for-dollar proxy for expenses of the taxable unitary business. The rule does not prevent a supported attribution of expenses actually connected to exempt activity.

**First controlling authority:** Hunt-Wesson, Inc. v. Franchise Tax Board of California, 528 U.S. 458 (2000), decided February 22, 2000, expense attribution to nonunitary income; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Hunt-Wesson, Inc. v. Franchise Tax Board of California, 528 U.S. 458 (2000), decided February 22, 2000, supplies the fair-attribution requirement and the supported-allocation boundary.

**Present operation effective:** Hunt-Wesson, Inc. v. Franchise Tax Board of California, 528 U.S. 458 (2000), February 22, 2000.

### Deemed-payment dates for refund lookback

**Governing question:** When wage withholding and estimated-tax remittances are treated as paid for the federal refund lookback rule.

**Current rule:** For the refund lookback rule, wage withholding and estimated-tax remittances are deemed paid on the statutory dates assigned by 26 U.S.C. § 6513(b), not when the taxpayer later files a delinquent return or when the Service assesses the liability. General deposit principles do not override an express statutory payment date.

**Controlling construction:** Sections 6513(b)(1) and (2) provide the payment dates used in the lookback calculation under § 6511.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not decide a remittance outside § 6513 or the consequence of a timely administrative claim.

**First controlling authority:** Baral v. United States, 528 U.S. 431 (2000), decided February 22, 2000, deemed-payment dates for withholding and estimated tax; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Baral v. United States, 528 U.S. 431 (2000), decided February 22, 2000, supplies the statutory payment-date construction and distinguishes Rosenman v. United States where no statute fixed the payment date.

**Present operation effective:** Baral v. United States, 528 U.S. 431 (2000), February 22, 2000.

## Federal Indian Law

### Legal incidence of the motor-fuels tax

**Governing question:** How courts determine the legal incidence of a state motor-fuels tax and when a tax on a Tribe or member in Indian country is valid.

**Current rule:** When the legal incidence of a state tax falls directly on a Tribe or tribal member inside Indian country, the tax is invalid absent clear congressional authorization. Courts determine legal incidence from the enacted liability, payment, reporting, and pass-through provisions rather than from economic burden alone.

**Exceptions and limits:** Oklahoma may prospectively redesign the tax within federal limits. The Court does not decide a tax whose incidence is lawfully placed on a nontribal distributor or consumer.

**First controlling authority:** Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Chickasaw Nation, 515 U.S. 450, decided June 14, 1995, Legal incidence of the motor-fuels tax; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Chickasaw Nation, 515 U.S. 450, decided June 14, 1995, Legal incidence of the motor-fuels tax; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Chickasaw Nation, 515 U.S. 450, June 14, 1995.

### Income earned from the Tribe

**Governing question:** When a State may tax wages paid by a Tribe to a tribal member based on the member’s residence.

**Current rule:** A State may not tax wages paid by a Tribe to a tribal member residing in Indian country. It may tax the wages of a member residing outside Indian country absent federal law providing an exemption. Reservation residence and tribal-source income trigger the territorial protection; tribal employment alone does not carry that protection to an off-reservation residence.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not govern nonmember employees, income from nontribal sources, or an express federal alteration of state taxing authority.

**First controlling authority:** Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Chickasaw Nation, 515 U.S. 450, decided June 14, 1995, Income earned from the Tribe; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Chickasaw Nation, 515 U.S. 450, decided June 14, 1995, Income earned from the Tribe; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Chickasaw Nation, 515 U.S. 450, June 14, 1995.

### Dependent Indian community status

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs dependent Indian community status.

**Current rule:** Land is a dependent Indian community under 18 U.S.C. § 1151(b) only when the Federal Government set it aside for Indian use and maintains it under federal superintendence. Both requirements must be satisfied.

**First controlling authority:** Alaska v. Native Village of Venetie Tribal Government, 522 U.S. 520 (1998), decided February 25, 1998, Dependent Indian community status; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Alaska v. Native Village of Venetie Tribal Government, 522 U.S. 520 (1998), decided February 25, 1998, Dependent Indian community status; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Alaska v. Native Village of Venetie Tribal Government, 522 U.S. 520 (1998), February 25, 1998.

### Tribal immunity for off-reservation commercial contracts

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs tribal immunity for off-reservation commercial contracts.

**Current rule:** An Indian tribe retains sovereign immunity from suit on an off-reservation commercial contract unless the tribe clearly waives immunity or Congress unequivocally abrogates it.

**First controlling authority:** Kiowa Tribe of Oklahoma v. Manufacturing Technologies, Inc., 523 U.S. 751 (1998), decided May 26, 1998, Tribal immunity for off-reservation commercial contracts; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Kiowa Tribe of Oklahoma v. Manufacturing Technologies, Inc., 523 U.S. 751 (1998), decided May 26, 1998, Tribal immunity for off-reservation commercial contracts; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Kiowa Tribe of Oklahoma v. Manufacturing Technologies, Inc., 523 U.S. 751 (1998), May 26, 1998.

### Clear abrogation of treaty-reserved use rights

**Governing question:** What federal action extinguishes treaty-reserved hunting, fishing, or gathering rights and what effect statehood has.

**Current rule:** Abrogation requires clear federal action and may not be inferred from general policy, changed circumstances, a later treaty lacking clear relinquishment, or a State’s admission to the Union. Continuing treaty-reserved rights remain enforceable subject to necessary and nondiscriminatory conservation measures.

**Exceptions and limits:** Clear statutory or treaty language relinquishing or abrogating the particular right controls according to its terms. The rule does not prevent fitted conservation regulation.

**First controlling authority:** Minnesota v. Mille Lacs Band of Chippewa Indians, 526 U.S. 172 (1999), decided March 24, 1999, continuing force and abrogation of 1837 Treaty rights; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Minnesota v. Mille Lacs Band of Chippewa Indians, 526 U.S. 172 (1999), decided March 24, 1999, supplies the current rule and overrules Ward v. Race Horse to the extent statehood alone terminated federal treaty rights.

**Present operation effective:** Minnesota v. Mille Lacs Band of Chippewa Indians, 526 U.S. 172 (1999), March 24, 1999.

## Environmental Law

### Scope of Clean Water Act § 401 certification conditions

**Governing question:** What project conditions a State may impose once an activity that may result in a discharge triggers Clean Water Act § 401 certification.

**Current rule:** A potential discharge determines whether certification is required. Once triggered, § 401(d) authorizes conditions on the applicant or licensed activity that are necessary to assure compliance with applicable federal and state water-quality requirements, including adopted designated uses supported by the administrative record.

**Exceptions and limits:** Certification is not a general license to regulate conduct unrelated to water quality or the licensed activity. A flow condition must remain tied to an adopted designated use or other applicable water-quality requirement and have record support.

**First controlling authority:** PUD No. 1 of Jefferson County v. Washington Department of Ecology, 511 U.S. 700 (1994), decided May 31, 1994, Scope of conditions under § 401(d); established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** PUD No. 1 of Jefferson County v. Washington Department of Ecology, 511 U.S. 700 (1994), decided May 31, 1994, Scope of conditions under § 401(d); supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** PUD No. 1 of Jefferson County v. Washington Department of Ecology, 511 U.S. 700 (1994), May 31, 1994.

### Habitat modification that actually kills or injures wildlife

**Governing question:** When habitat modification constitutes a harm take under the ESA and what injury and causation the Government must prove.

**Current rule:** The ESA permits the Secretary to define ‘harm’ as significant habitat modification or degradation that actually kills or injures protected wildlife, including injury caused by material impairment of essential behavioral patterns. Habitat alteration alone is insufficient. The Government must establish actual death or injury to protected wildlife and the ordinary causal connection between the modification and that injury. Speculative, conjectural, or merely potential ecological effects do not constitute a completed take.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule is statutory and applies only where Congress possesses constitutional authority. National Association of Home Builders v. Babbitt leaves the definition of harm and its actual-injury and causation requirements intact while requiring a separate determination whether intrastate habitat modification falls within a valid interstate regulatory regime or produces a sufficiently supported material interstate externality.

**Related current rules:** Interstate commerce, anti-evasion, and material interstate externalities, under Commerce Clause. The commerce inquiry determines federal authority; this entry separately determines whether habitat modification constitutes a statutory take.

**First controlling authority:** Babbitt v. Sweet Home Chapter of Communities for a Great Oregon, 515 U.S. 687, decided June 29, 1995, habitat modification as harm; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Babbitt v. Sweet Home Chapter of Communities for a Great Oregon, 515 U.S. 687, decided June 29, 1995, habitat modification as harm; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Babbitt v. Sweet Home Chapter of Communities for a Great Oregon, 515 U.S. 687, June 29, 1995.

### Parent-corporation liability under CERCLA

**Governing question:** When a parent corporation is derivatively or directly liable for a subsidiary facility’s pollution under CERCLA.

**Current rule:** A parent corporation is derivatively liable for a subsidiary’s CERCLA obligations only when ordinary veil-piercing law permits attribution. Ownership, appointment of directors, general policies, and ordinary oversight do not suffice. A parent is directly liable as an operator when the parent itself manages, directs, or conducts facility operations specifically related to pollution, hazardous-waste disposal, leakage, or environmental compliance. Direct liability turns on operation of the facility, not general control of the subsidiary.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Bestfoods, 524 U.S. 51 (1998), decided June 8, 1998, parent-corporation CERCLA liability; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Bestfoods, 524 U.S. 51 (1998), decided June 8, 1998, parent-corporation CERCLA liability; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Bestfoods, 524 U.S. 51 (1998), June 8, 1998.

### Current ownership or control as a surface-mining permit block

**Governing question:** When ownership or control of a violating surface-mining operation blocks an applicant’s permit under the Surface Mining Control and Reclamation Act.

**Current rule:** The ordinary ownership-and-control permit block under SMCRA § 510(c) applies only while the applicant possesses the legally relevant ownership or control of an operation that is currently in violation. Once that relationship ends, former ownership or control does not continue the ordinary block.

**Exceptions and limits:** Former control may support a permit consequence through another enacted provision when that provision’s own requirements are satisfied, including a demonstrated pattern of willful violations. The rule does not immunize a current violation or invalidate independently authorized enforcement.

**Controlling construction:** Section 510(c)’s reference to an operation “owned or controlled by the applicant” that “is currently in violation” requires the legally relevant ownership-or-control relationship and violation to be current for the ordinary permit block.

**First controlling authority:** *National Mining Association v. United States Department of the Interior*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 7, 2000, permit blocking after ownership or control ends; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *National Mining Association v. United States Department of the Interior*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 7, 2000, supplies the current-relationship construction, termination of the ordinary permit block, and independently enacted-provision limit.

**Present operation effective:** *National Mining Association v. United States Department of the Interior*, June 7, 2000.

**Related current rules:** `Retroactive administrative disabilities and reopening final determinations`, under Administrative Law, governs a new permit disability attached to completed pre-rule conduct or an attempt to reopen a final agency determination.

## Telecommunications

### FCC implementation authority under the 1996 Act

**Governing question:** How the Telecommunications Act of 1996 allocates implementation of 47 U.S.C. §§ 251 and 252 between the FCC and state commissions.

**Current rule:** The FCC may issue national rules implementing the federal duties in §§ 251 and 252. State commissions retain the agreement, arbitration, approval, and rate functions Congress specifically assigned to them. The presence of state implementation responsibilities does not withdraw the FCC’s authority over the federal statutory duties.

**First controlling authority:** AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board, 525 U.S. 366 (1999), decided January 25, 1999, FCC implementation authority; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board, 525 U.S. 366 (1999), decided January 25, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board, 525 U.S. 366 (1999), January 25, 1999.

### Necessary-and-impair limits on unbundling

**Governing question:** What 47 U.S.C. § 251(d)(2) requires before the FCC orders access to unbundled network elements.

**Current rule:** The FCC must give independent constraining effect to the statutory necessary-and-impair requirements and make element-specific judgments. A rule making unbundling available without those statutory determinations is invalid.

**First controlling authority:** AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board, 525 U.S. 366 (1999), decided January 25, 1999, necessary-and-impair limits; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board, 525 U.S. 366 (1999), decided January 25, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board, 525 U.S. 366 (1999), January 25, 1999.

### Leasing network elements already maintained in combination

**Governing question:** Whether 47 U.S.C. § 251(c)(3) requires an incumbent carrier to separate network elements it already maintains in combined form before leasing them.

**Current rule:** Section 251(c)(3) does not require an incumbent to separate network elements already maintained in combined form before leasing them to a requesting carrier. An implementing rule preserving the existing combination is a permissible construction.

**First controlling authority:** AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board, 525 U.S. 366 (1999), decided January 25, 1999, separation of already combined elements; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board, 525 U.S. 366 (1999), decided January 25, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board, 525 U.S. 366 (1999), January 25, 1999.

### Adoption of individual terms from approved interconnection agreements

**Governing question:** What 47 U.S.C. § 252(i) permits a requesting carrier to adopt from another approved agreement.

**Current rule:** A requesting carrier may adopt individual qualifying interconnection, service, or network-element terms from an approved agreement on the same conditions, subject to statutory cost, feasibility, and comparability distinctions. The statute does not require adoption of the entire agreement as an indivisible package.

**First controlling authority:** AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board, 525 U.S. 366 (1999), decided January 25, 1999, § 252(i) pick-and-choose rule; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board, 525 U.S. 366 (1999), decided January 25, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board, 525 U.S. 366 (1999), January 25, 1999.

## Administrative Law

### Delegated power to modify does not authorize elimination

**Governing question:** How far an agency may alter a statutory requirement under a delegated power to modify it.

**Current rule:** A delegated power to modify permits bounded alteration, including adjustments to form, content, timing, and genuinely limited exceptions. It does not authorize an agency to eliminate a central statutory requirement for nearly an entire regulated sector or replace the enacted regime with a fundamentally different model. Deference does not extend authority beyond a meaning the statutory text can bear.

**Exceptions and limits:** The agency may make incremental adjustments and grant genuinely limited waivers consistent with the statutory delegation.

**First controlling authority:** MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. American Telephone & Telegraph Co., 512 U.S. 218 (1994), decided June 17, 1994, Scope of the power to modify tariff-filing requirements; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. American Telephone & Telegraph Co., 512 U.S. 218 (1994), decided June 17, 1994, Scope of the power to modify tariff-filing requirements; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. American Telephone & Telegraph Co., 512 U.S. 218 (1994), June 17, 1994.

### Provider Reimbursement Manual § 233

**Governing question:** Whether Provider Reimbursement Manual § 233 is an interpretive rule exempt from notice and comment or creates a new binding obligation.

**Current rule:** Section 233 is an interpretive rule that explains the application of existing reasonable-cost principles to advance refunding and did not require notice-and-comment rulemaking. An agency guideline is interpretive when it explains how existing statutory and regulatory standards apply to a recurring transaction without creating a new binding obligation inconsistent with those standards. A manual may not be used to amend the governing law or impose an obligation outside the statute and regulations.

**First controlling authority:** Shalala v. Guernsey Memorial Hospital, 514 U.S. 87, decided March 6, 1995, Provider Reimbursement Manual § 233; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Shalala v. Guernsey Memorial Hospital, 514 U.S. 87, decided March 6, 1995, Provider Reimbursement Manual § 233; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Shalala v. Guernsey Memorial Hospital, 514 U.S. 87, March 6, 1995.

### Deference to authoritative interpretations of genuinely ambiguous regulations

**Governing question:** What weight a court gives an agency’s interpretation of its own regulation.

**Current rule:** After using ordinary interpretive tools, if an agency regulation remains genuinely ambiguous, the agency’s authoritative interpretation controls unless plainly erroneous or inconsistent with the regulation, provided it reflects fair and considered judgment rather than a convenient post hoc rationalization. An interpretation stated in an amicus brief may qualify when it expresses the agency’s established authoritative position and is not manufactured for the litigation.

**Exceptions and limits:** Deference cannot make regulatory text bear an unreasonable meaning and does not apply to an inconsistent, secret, plainly erroneous, or merely convenient interpretation.

**First controlling authority:** Auer v. Robbins, 519 U.S. 452 (1997), decided February 19, 1997, Interpretation of an agency’s own regulation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Auer v. Robbins, 519 U.S. 452 (1997), decided February 19, 1997, Interpretation of an agency’s own regulation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Auer v. Robbins, 519 U.S. 452 (1997), February 19, 1997.

### Substantial-evidence review under the agency’s announced standard

**Governing question:** How substantial-evidence review accounts for the substantive standard an agency publicly announces.

**Current rule:** Substantial-evidence review tests an agency finding against the substantive standard the agency publicly announces and the record as a whole. An agency may not preserve a nominal standard while applying a materially more demanding evidentiary rule in adjudicative practice. The record must contain enough evidence for a reasonable factfinder applying the announced standard.

**First controlling authority:** Allentown Mack Sales & Service, Inc. v. NLRB, 522 U.S. 359 (1998), decided January 26, 1998, safeguarded employer polling; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Allentown Mack Sales & Service, Inc. v. NLRB, 522 U.S. 359 (1998), decided January 26, 1998, safeguarded employer polling; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Allentown Mack Sales & Service, Inc. v. NLRB, 522 U.S. 359 (1998), January 26, 1998.

### Judicial weight for agency statutory interpretations

**Governing question:** What weight a reviewing court gives an agency’s interpretation of a statute it administers.

**Current rule:** The court first uses ordinary interpretive tools to identify and enforce any fixed statutory boundary. If genuine ambiguity or evaluative space remains and the agency adopted its interpretation through an exercise of delegated authority carrying the force of law, a reasonable construction controls. An interpretation in an informal opinion letter, litigation position, or other format lacking delegated legal force receives weight only to the extent its reasoning, consistency, expertise, and power to persuade warrant. An agency does not lose delegated authority merely because it reasonably changes an earlier position, but it must acknowledge and justify the change.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** First determine the statute’s fixed meaning. Second determine whether Congress delegated authority to act with the force of law and whether the interpretation was issued through that authority. Third, apply controlling deference within genuine ambiguity when those predicates are met; otherwise evaluate the interpretation for persuasive weight. Review agency reasoning and record support under the otherwise applicable administrative standards.

**Exceptions and limits:** Deference cannot enlarge an agency’s authority beyond enacted text or resolve a question ordinary interpretation settles. Informal guidance lacking the force of law does not receive controlling statutory deference. Deference to an agency’s interpretation of its own genuinely ambiguous regulation remains governed by *Auer v. Robbins* and does not apply to an unambiguous regulation or an interpretation inconsistent with its text.

**First controlling authority:** *Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc.*, 467 U.S. 837 (1984), statutory ambiguity and reasonable agency construction; established the core rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc.*, 467 U.S. 837 (1984), supplies the fixed-meaning-first and reasonable-construction framework. *INS v. Aguirre-Aguirre*, 526 U.S. 415, decided May 3, 1999, supplies the requirement to enforce fixed statutory boundaries before deferring within evaluative space and preserves review of reasoning and record support. *FDA v. Brown & Williamson Tobacco Corp.*, 529 U.S. 120, simulation disposition decided March 21, 2000, confirms controlling deference within an existing delegation and supplies the reasoned-change boundary. *Christensen v. Harris County*, 529 U.S. 576, decided May 1, 2000, Part III joined by Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, and Souter, supplies the force-of-law threshold and persuasion-based weight for informal interpretations.

**Present operation effective:** *Christensen v. Harris County*, May 1, 2000.

**Related current rules:** *Later legislation and an existing statutory delegation*, under Statutory Interpretation, governs whether later legislation has narrowed or withdrawn the delegation before the deference inquiry begins. *Deference to authoritative interpretations of genuinely ambiguous regulations* governs the distinct interpretation of an agency’s own regulation.

**Development:**
- March 21, 2000, *FDA v. Brown & Williamson Tobacco Corp.*: The prior entry required fixed statutory boundaries to be enforced before deference within genuine ambiguity. *Brown & Williamson* confirms that a justified agency change of position does not itself eliminate otherwise delegated authority and that later legislation alters the delegation only under the separate enacted-law rule. The fixed-boundary requirement remains.
- May 1, 2000, *Christensen v. Harris County*: The prior entry did not distinguish interpretations carrying delegated legal force from informal guidance. *Christensen* adds that controlling statutory deference requires a force-of-law exercise and that informal guidance receives only persuasion-based weight. Former title: Statutory boundary before deference to immigration adjudication.

### APA review of Patent and Trademark Office factfinding

**Governing question:** What standard governs direct Federal Circuit review of PTO factual findings.

**Current rule:** Direct review of PTO factfinding uses the applicable court-agency standard in APA § 706 on the whole record, not the clearly erroneous standard governing appellate review of district-court findings. The reviewing court must identify and apply the proper § 706 category.

**Exceptions and limits:** Pre-APA patent-review terminology does not displace the APA without a sufficiently clear statutory preservation.

**First controlling authority:** Dickinson v. Zurko, 527 U.S. 150 (1999), decided June 10, 1999, APA review of PTO factual findings; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Dickinson v. Zurko, 527 U.S. 150 (1999), decided June 10, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Dickinson v. Zurko, 527 U.S. 150 (1999), June 10, 1999.

### Issue exhaustion in nonadversarial agency review

**Governing question:** When a claimant who completes a prescribed administrative-review route forfeits judicial review by failing to identify a particular issue in a request for discretionary agency review.

**Current rule:** Completion of an administrative-review route does not itself create issue exhaustion. A claimant in a nonadversarial agency process does not forfeit judicial review of an issue omitted from a request for discretionary review when no statute or valid regulation requires issue exhaustion and the governing procedure neither assigns adversarial issue development to the claimant nor gives fair notice that every later judicial issue must be listed.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** Determine first whether enacted law or a valid regulation imposes issue exhaustion. If neither does, examine the character of the administrative process and the notice supplied by its procedures before applying a judicial issue-exhaustion rule.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not excuse failure to complete the prescribed administrative-review route, decide preservation at an adversarial agency stage, or bar a statute or valid regulation from expressly requiring issue exhaustion.

**First controlling authority:** *Sims v. Apfel*, 530 U.S. 103 (2000), decided June 5, 2000, issue exhaustion in a request for discretionary Appeals Council review; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Sims v. Apfel*, 530 U.S. 103 (2000), decided June 5, 2000, supplies the enacted-law, valid-regulation, nonadversarial-process, procedural-notice, and completed-route components.

**Present operation effective:** *Sims v. Apfel*, 530 U.S. 103 (2000), June 5, 2000.

### Retroactive administrative disabilities and reopening final determinations

**Governing question:** When an agency may attach a new regulatory disability to completed conduct or reopen a final agency determination.

**Current rule:** An agency may not attach a new legal disability to a completed pre-rule transaction or reopen a final agency determination on that basis unless enacted law clearly authorizes the retroactive consequence or the reopening. Characterizing the new disability as a condition on a present application does not avoid the rule when eligibility turns on completed conduct to which the prior law attached no such consequence.

**Exceptions and limits:** An agency may regulate a present relationship or application under law that validly governs current conduct, and an independently enacted provision may impose a consequence when its own predicates are satisfied. The rule does not prevent correction or reopening through authority that enacted law validly and clearly supplies.
