**First controlling authority:** *National Mining Association v. United States Department of the Interior*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 7, 2000, retroactive permit disabilities and reopening final determinations; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *National Mining Association v. United States Department of the Interior*, citation and docket not supplied, decided June 7, 2000, supplies the completed-transaction, new-disability, clear-authorization, and final-agency-determination components.

**Present operation effective:** *National Mining Association v. United States Department of the Interior*, June 7, 2000.

**Related current rules:** `Presumption against retroactive legal consequences`, under Statutory Interpretation, governs the temporal reach of enacted statutes; this entry governs an agency’s authority to impose retroactive administrative consequences or reopen its final determinations.

## Statutory Interpretation

### No implied claim-ending sanction for missed statutory timing directives

**Governing question:** When a court may attach dismissal or loss of governmental authority to violation of a statutory timing command.

**Current rule:** Mandatory timing language imposes duties on responsible officials, but a court does not create a claim-ending dismissal sanction or loss of governmental authority that Congress omitted, particularly when Congress separately enacted an express limitations period. The absence of that sanction does not make the timing directives optional or foreclose independently preserved constitutional objections to delay.

**Exceptions and limits:** A different result follows when Congress expressly supplies dismissal, loss of authority, or another consequence.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. James Daniel Good Real Property, 510 U.S. 43 (1993), decided December 13, 1993, Effect of the statutory timing directives; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. James Daniel Good Real Property, 510 U.S. 43 (1993), decided December 13, 1993, Effect of the statutory timing directives; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. James Daniel Good Real Property, 510 U.S. 43 (1993), December 13, 1993.

### Presumption against retroactive legal consequences

**Governing question:** How a court determines the temporal reach of a statute enacted while a case is pending.

**Current rule:** A court first asks whether Congress clearly prescribed temporal reach. If not, it determines whether application would attach new legal consequences to completed conduct, including increased liability, impairment of an existing right, imposition of a new duty, or creation or revival of a private action previously unavailable. A jurisdictional label does not avoid retroactivity analysis when the amendment changes whether a private cause of action exists rather than merely reallocating an existing action among tribunals. Statutory structure, including an express pending-case instruction in one companion chapter and its omission from another, may supply the temporal answer.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** Identify a controlling temporal command first. If none exists, determine retroactive legal effect. Apply a more specific enacted structural answer when one is available.

**Exceptions and limits:** Genuinely procedural or forum-allocating provisions may apply to pending matters when they do not alter substantive exposure or completed legal consequences. Congress may prescribe retroactive operation clearly.

**First controlling authority:** Landgraf v. USI Film Products, 511 U.S. 244 (1994), decided April 26, 1994, Temporal reach of newly enacted remedies; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Landgraf v. USI Film Products, 511 U.S. 244 (1994), decided April 26, 1994, supplies the general sequence. Rivers v. Roadway Express, Inc., 511 U.S. 298 (1994), applies it to expanded conduct coverage. Hughes Aircraft Co. v. United States ex rel. Schumer, 520 U.S. 939 (1997), decided June 16, 1997, supplies the private-action and jurisdictional-label boundary. Lindh v. Murphy, 521 U.S. 320 (1997), decided June 23, 1997, supplies the companion-chapter structural inference.

**Present operation effective:** Lindh v. Murphy, 521 U.S. 320 (1997), June 23, 1997.

**Development:**
- June 16, 1997, Hughes Aircraft Co. v. United States ex rel. Schumer, 520 U.S. 939 (1997): A jurisdictional label does not remove an amendment from retroactivity analysis when it creates or revives a private action.
- June 23, 1997, Lindh v. Murphy, 521 U.S. 320 (1997): An express pending-case instruction in one simultaneously enacted chapter and its omission from another may establish different temporal operation.

### Supplementary sampling in the decennial census

**Governing question:** Whether 13 U.S.C. §§ 141(a) and 195 categorically prohibit supplementary sampling because resulting population totals are used for apportionment.

**Current rule:** Sections 141(a) and 195 authorize statistical procedures that operate as supplementary methods within the decennial enumeration. Section 195 does not categorically prohibit such a method merely because the resulting population totals are used for apportionment.

**Exceptions and limits:** No categorical rule governs every possible form of apportionment sampling. The method must remain within delegated statutory authority and directed to the actual enumeration.

**First controlling authority:** Department of Commerce v. United States House of Representatives; Clinton v. Glavin, 525 U.S. 316 (1999), decided January 25, 1999, statutory authorization for supplementary sampling; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Department of Commerce v. United States House of Representatives; Clinton v. Glavin, 525 U.S. 316 (1999), decided January 25, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Department of Commerce v. United States House of Representatives; Clinton v. Glavin, 525 U.S. 316 (1999), January 25, 1999.

### Temporal reach of AEDPA § 440(d)

**Governing question:** Whether AEDPA § 440(d)’s categorical restrictions govern a former § 212(c) application already pending on AEDPA’s effective date.

**Current rule:** Section 440(d) does not govern a former § 212(c) application already pending when AEDPA took effect. Congress supplied no clear pending-application command, and the surrounding structure does not attach the new categorical ineligibility rule to that existing application.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not govern an application filed after enactment or a provision carrying a different express temporal instruction.

**First controlling authority:** Reno v. Goncalves, 144 F.3d 110, decided June 3, 1999, temporal reach of AEDPA § 440(d); established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Reno v. Goncalves, 144 F.3d 110, decided June 3, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Reno v. Goncalves, 144 F.3d 110, June 3, 1999.

### Temporal reach of the PLRA attorney-fee cap

**Governing question:** Which legal services in a case pending on the PLRA’s effective date are governed by the new attorney-fee cap.

**Current rule:** The cap does not govern legal services completed before enactment because Congress supplied no clear retroactive command and application would reduce compensation for completed work. It does govern services performed after enactment even when the litigation was filed earlier. The relevant conduct is the performance of the compensated service, not the filing date of the action.

**Exceptions and limits:** Congress may prescribe a different temporal rule clearly. The rule does not determine the fee amount or entitlement under provisions other than the PLRA cap.

**First controlling authority:** Martin v. Hadix, 527 U.S. 343 (1999), decided June 21, 1999, temporal reach of the PLRA fee cap; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Martin v. Hadix, 527 U.S. 343 (1999), decided June 21, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Martin v. Hadix, 527 U.S. 343 (1999), June 21, 1999.

### Section 230 publisher treatment and temporal operation

**Governing question:** When § 230(c)(1) bars publisher-based liability for third-party content and which actions its temporal provision governs.

**Current rule:** Notice-based distributor liability treats an interactive computer service as the publisher of information supplied by another information-content provider and is barred by § 230(c)(1). The rule does not protect the original author, content created or materially developed by the service, conduct outside the statutory definitions, or duties genuinely independent of publisher status. Then-§ 230(d)(3) governs actions brought and liabilities imposed after its effective date, including claims based on earlier service-provider conduct.

**First controlling authority:** Zeran v. America Online, Inc., 129 F.3d 327, decided June 15, 1998, Section 230 publisher treatment and temporal operation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Zeran v. America Online, Inc., 129 F.3d 327, decided June 15, 1998, Section 230 publisher treatment and temporal operation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Zeran v. America Online, Inc., 129 F.3d 327, June 15, 1998.

### Later legislation and an existing statutory delegation

**Governing question:** When later legislation narrows or withdraws authority already delegated by an earlier statute.

**Current rule:** Later legislation narrows or withdraws an existing statutory delegation only through enacted text, statutory structure, or a clear incompatibility between the later regime and the earlier authority. Legislative silence, failed or rejected bills, and assumptions reflected only in political practice do not amend the earlier statute. A later statute may supplement regulation of a subject without making itself the exclusive source of authority.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not preserve earlier authority when Congress expressly withdraws it or when the two enacted regimes cannot operate together. It does not authorize an agency to exceed the bounds of either statute.

**First controlling authority:** *FDA v. Brown & Williamson Tobacco Corp.*, 529 U.S. 120, simulation disposition decided March 21, 2000, effect of later subject-specific enactments on an existing agency delegation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *FDA v. Brown & Williamson Tobacco Corp.*, 529 U.S. 120, simulation disposition decided March 21, 2000, supplies the enacted-text, structure, clear-incompatibility, and legislative-silence boundaries.

**Present operation effective:** *FDA v. Brown & Williamson Tobacco Corp.*, March 21, 2000.

**Related current rules:** *Judicial weight for agency statutory interpretations*, under Administrative Law, governs the distinct question whether an agency interpretation within an existing delegation controls or persuades.

### State defendants in private False Claims Act qui tam actions

**Governing question:** Whether “person” in the False Claims Act’s private-relator liability provision includes a sovereign State or state agency.

**Current rule:** The False Claims Act does not authorize a private relator to impose qui tam liability on a State or state agency because the statute contains no affirmative indication overcoming the presumption that “person” excludes a sovereign State in this private-suit setting.

**Controlling construction:** “Any person” in the FCA’s operative private-relator liability provision does not include a sovereign State or its agency absent an affirmative statutory indication.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not bar an action brought by the United States, determine whether a municipality is a person, resolve a retaliation claim, govern differently drafted legislation, or decide state sovereign immunity.

**First controlling authority:** *Vermont Agency of Natural Resources v. United States ex rel. Stevens*, 529 U.S. 765, decided May 22, 2000, State as a liable person in a private FCA qui tam action; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Vermont Agency of Natural Resources v. United States ex rel. Stevens*, 529 U.S. 765, decided May 22, 2000, supplies the controlling construction and the reserved boundaries.

**Present operation effective:** *Vermont Agency of Natural Resources v. United States ex rel. Stevens*, May 22, 2000.

## Remedies

### Section 1983 remedy for state interference with a concrete federal labor right

**Governing question:** When state interference with a federal labor right is actionable under 42 U.S.C. § 1983.

**Current rule:** Section 1983 is available against an appropriate state official when federal labor law protects a concrete individual interest against state abridgment, the governmental duty is judicially administrable, and Congress has not displaced the remedy with an exclusive alternative enforcement scheme. Federal preemption by itself does not automatically create an individual § 1983 cause of action.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not waive state sovereign immunity, authorize damages against the State, or predetermine the proper defendant and form of relief.

**First controlling authority:** Livadas v. Bradshaw, 512 U.S. 107 (1994), decided June 13, 1994, Section 1983 remedy for state interference with the federal labor right; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Livadas v. Bradshaw, 512 U.S. 107 (1994), decided June 13, 1994, Section 1983 remedy for state interference with the federal labor right; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Livadas v. Bradshaw, 512 U.S. 107 (1994), June 13, 1994.

### Municipal liability

**Governing question:** Whether a municipality can be liable on a policy claim when the underlying shooting is constitutionally reasonable and no distinct municipal injury exists.

**Current rule:** A municipal policy claim cannot rest solely on conduct that caused no constitutional injury. When the underlying use of force was constitutionally reasonable and no distinct constitutional injury is attributable to municipal policy, the municipal claim fails.

**First controlling authority:** Plakas v. Drinski, 19 F.3d 1143, decided January 9, 1995, Municipal liability; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Plakas v. Drinski, 19 F.3d 1143, decided January 9, 1995, Municipal liability; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Plakas v. Drinski, 19 F.3d 1143, January 9, 1995.

### Relief limited to represented public-employee plaintiffs

**Governing question:** How far relief may extend beyond a represented class of public employees to nonparty officials.

**Current rule:** Relief may protect the represented class, but it may not extend to senior Executive Branch officials who are not parties or represented members.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. National Treasury Employees Union, 513 U.S. 454, decided February 22, 1995, Scope of the injunction; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. National Treasury Employees Union, 513 U.S. 454, decided February 22, 1995, Scope of the injunction; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. National Treasury Employees Union, 513 U.S. 454, February 22, 1995.

### Reliance and retroactive application of a federal constitutional rule

**Governing question:** Whether reliance on an invalidated state statute permits a state court to withhold a controlling federal constitutional rule from a nonfinal case.

**Current rule:** A state court may not deny the ordinary retroactive application of a controlling federal constitutional rule to a nonfinal case merely because a party relied on the invalidated state statute. Once this Court applies a federal rule to the parties before it, that rule governs other cases still open on direct review. A different outcome remains possible when an independently valid legal doctrine supplies it, such as an alternative cure, a preexisting remedial rule, qualified immunity, res judicata, or a doctrine limiting retroactivity itself. Simple reliance on the former law does not create a free-standing exception.

**Exceptions and limits:** Ordinary rules concerning final judgments, statutes of repose, waiver, harmless error, qualified immunity, and other independently valid remedial doctrines remain intact.

**First controlling authority:** Reynoldsville Casket Co. v. Hyde, 514 U.S. 749, decided May 15, 1995, Reliance and retroactive application of a federal constitutional rule; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Reynoldsville Casket Co. v. Hyde, 514 U.S. 749, decided May 15, 1995, Reliance and retroactive application of a federal constitutional rule; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Reynoldsville Casket Co. v. Hyde, 514 U.S. 749, May 15, 1995.

### Unmistakability, sovereign acts, and impossibility

**Governing question:** Whether the unmistakability, sovereign-acts, reserved-powers, or impossibility doctrines bar contract damages after a regulatory change.

**Current rule:** When authorized federal contracts allocate the financial risk that later law will prevent promised regulatory treatment, the later law remains fully effective but does not extinguish the Government’s obligation to pay damages for breach. Because damages preserve sovereign authority while enforcing the financial consequence of the bargain, the unmistakability, sovereign-acts, reserved-powers, and impossibility doctrines do not bar recovery merely because lawful sovereign action prevented performance.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Government does not insure against every later legal change, and agencies may not bargain away constitutional or nondelegable sovereign powers. Respondents remain subject to ordinary contract limitations on causation, foreseeability, mitigation, and proof of loss.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Winstar Corp., 518 U.S. 839, decided July 1, 1996, Unmistakability, sovereign acts, and impossibility; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Winstar Corp., 518 U.S. 839, decided July 1, 1996, Unmistakability, sovereign acts, and impossibility; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Winstar Corp., 518 U.S. 839, July 1, 1996.

### Effect of an express severability command

**Governing question:** When a federal court must honor an express state severability command.

**Current rule:** A federal court must give effect to an express state severability command when the surviving text can function independently and severance neither rewrites the provision nor creates a statutory scheme the legislature did not enact. Judicial speculation about the legislature’s preferred comprehensive bargain cannot override its enacted instruction.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court expresses no view on § 302(3)’s emergency provisions, gestational operation, undue-burden consequences, facial validity, or enforceability in any particular application.

**First controlling authority:** Leavitt v. Jane L., 518 U.S. 137, decided June 17, 1996, Effect of an express severability command; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Leavitt v. Jane L., 518 U.S. 137, decided June 17, 1996, Effect of an express severability command; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Leavitt v. Jane L., 518 U.S. 137, June 17, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 17, 1996, Leavitt v. Jane L., 518 U.S. 137: The decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule for an express state severability command. The abortion standards established in simulated *Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer* and applied earlier this term in *Planned Parenthood v. Miller* remain unchanged and govern only the constitutional review reserved for remand.

### Federal immunity from compensatory damages

**Governing question:** What statutory language is required to waive the United States’ immunity from compensatory damages.

**Current rule:** A substantive statutory command applicable to a federal agency does not authorize a money judgment against the United States unless enacted text expresses that consent unambiguously. Remedial cross-references or plausible structural inferences cannot supply a waiver when the statutory language supports competing readings.

**Exceptions and limits:** The holding does not diminish § 504’s substantive prohibition, disturb Lane’s reinstatement, foreclose prospective relief, or determine damages against a nonfederal recipient under a separately authorized action.

**First controlling authority:** Lane v. Peña, 518 U.S. 187, decided June 20, 1996, Federal immunity from compensatory damages; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Lane v. Peña, 518 U.S. 187, decided June 20, 1996, Federal immunity from compensatory damages; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Lane v. Peña, 518 U.S. 187, June 20, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 20, 1996, Lane v. Peña, 518 U.S. 187: *FDIC v. Meyer* is applied and clarified. Waiver of immunity does not follow from the existence of a right or an available cause of action, and a right applicable to the Federal Government does not itself authorize payment from the Treasury.

### State substantive limit on compensatory damages

**Governing question:** When a federal diversity court must apply a substantive state limit on compensatory damages and what mechanism federal law controls.

**Current rule:** A state rule that materially narrows the damages available on a state-created claim must be given effect in diversity when disregarding it would alter outcomes substantially and encourage forum selection. Federal law continues to govern the judicial mechanism and allocation through which the state limit is applied.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not import New York’s full appellate structure, determine the proper damages figure, or establish a federal numerical cap.

**First controlling authority:** Gasperini v. Center for Humanities, Inc., 518 U.S. 415, decided June 24, 1996, State substantive limit on compensatory damages; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Gasperini v. Center for Humanities, Inc., 518 U.S. 415, decided June 24, 1996, State substantive limit on compensatory damages; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Gasperini v. Center for Humanities, Inc., 518 U.S. 415, June 24, 1996.

**Development:**
- June 24, 1996, Gasperini v. Center for Humanities, Inc., 518 U.S. 415: Simulated *Honda Motor Co. v. Oberg* remains unchanged. It concerns constitutionally adequate review of punitive damages and supplies no Erie or Seventh Amendment allocation rule for state compensatory damages. *Gasperini* creates the first controlling simulated rules for applying a state damages-tolerance standard in federal diversity court.

### Representative standing for statutory damages

**Governing question:** What Article III and germaneness showings permit an association to seek statutory damages for its members.

**Current rule:** Article III requires at least one represented member to possess injury, causation, and redressability. Germaneness assures a genuine representative stake. Once those requirements are met, Congress may authorize an association to litigate common liability and seek member relief even though individual participation is needed to establish the amount due.

**Exceptions and limits:** The association may not recover for persons who are not members or injured, eliminate individual defenses, or dispense with proof of each claimant’s compensable loss.

**First controlling authority:** United Food & Commercial Workers Union Local 751 v. Brown Group, Inc., 517 U.S. 544, decided May 13, 1996, Representative standing for statutory damages; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United Food & Commercial Workers Union Local 751 v. Brown Group, Inc., 517 U.S. 544, decided May 13, 1996, Representative standing for statutory damages; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** United Food & Commercial Workers Union Local 751 v. Brown Group, Inc., 517 U.S. 544, May 13, 1996.

**Development:**
- May 13, 1996, United Food & Commercial Workers Union Local 751 v. Brown Group, Inc., 517 U.S. 544: The decision clarifies *Hunt*. Member standing remains constitutional; the individual-participation prong is an administrability rule subject to congressional alteration. The Court creates no general rule permitting representative damages suits without statutory authorization.

### Territorial limits on punitive authority

**Governing question:** What territorial conduct a State may punish through punitive damages without imposing its policy in other States.

**Current rule:** A State may punish conduct occurring within its lawful regulatory interest, but it may not use punitive damages to impose its policy throughout the Nation or punish transactions lawful in other States as independent offenses.

**Exceptions and limits:** The decision does not prevent consideration of other conduct when independently relevant under lawful evidentiary rules, but the State may not punish the defendant for lawful out-of-state transactions themselves.

**First controlling authority:** BMW of North America, Inc. v. Gore, 517 U.S. 559, decided May 20, 1996, Territorial limits on punitive authority; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** BMW of North America, Inc. v. Gore, 517 U.S. 559, decided May 20, 1996, Territorial limits on punitive authority; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** BMW of North America, Inc. v. Gore, 517 U.S. 559, May 20, 1996.

### Identification of statutory rights enforceable through § 1983

**Governing question:** What a claimant must identify before invoking § 1983 to enforce a federal spending-program right, including a right whose concrete content is specified by a valid agency regulation.

**Current rule:** The claimant must identify a particular statutory provision intended to benefit the claimant, imposing a binding obligation, and defining an interest sufficiently concrete for judicial enforcement. The inquiry proceeds provision by provision; aggregate program standards and an entire statutory title cannot be treated as one indivisible entitlement. A valid agency regulation may give concrete content to a right anchored in rights-creating statutory text, but it cannot manufacture a free-standing right outside the statute.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not foreclose enforcement of a specific rights-creating provision, itself establish a structural remedy, or permit enforcement of a regulation untethered to a statutory right. Remedy availability remains subject to congressional displacement and any claim-specific limits.

**First controlling authority:** *Blessing v. Freestone*, 520 U.S. 329, Supreme Court simulation decided April 21, 1997, identification of enforceable federal spending-program rights; established the original rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Blessing v. Freestone*, 520 U.S. 329, Supreme Court simulation decided April 21, 1997, supplies the intended-beneficiary, binding-obligation, concrete-interest, and provision-by-provision requirements. *Powell v. Ridge*, 189 F.3d 387 (3d Cir. 1999), Supreme Court simulation decided April 24, 2000, supplies the rule that a valid regulation may specify the content of a right anchored in the statute but may not create a right outside it.

**Present operation effective:** *Powell v. Ridge*, April 24, 2000.

**Related current rules:** *Congressional displacement of § 1983* governs whether Congress foreclosed § 1983 after a concrete federal right is identified. *Private enforcement of Title VI disparate-impact regulations*, under Housing and Civil Rights, applies the statutory-right and regulation-content rules to valid Title VI § 602 regulations.

**Development:**
- April 24, 2000, *Powell v. Ridge*: *Blessing* required identification of a concrete right in a particular statutory provision. *Powell* adds that a valid regulation may give operational content to that statutory right but cannot create a free-standing right outside rights-creating statutory text. The provision-by-provision inquiry and congressional-displacement step remain unchanged.

### Congressional displacement of § 1983

**Governing question:** When a federal statute displaces the presumptive § 1983 remedy for a concrete federal right.

**Current rule:** After a concrete federal right is identified, § 1983 remains presumptively available unless Congress expressly foreclosed it or created a remedial scheme so comprehensive and incompatible that individual enforcement cannot coexist with the statutory design. General audits, corrective action, and funding reductions do not categorically displace every individual action.

**Exceptions and limits:** A specific remedial provision may displace § 1983 for a particular claim. The rule creates no substantive right by itself.

**First controlling authority:** Blessing v. Freestone, 520 U.S. 329 (1997), decided April 21, 1997, Statutory displacement of § 1983; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Blessing v. Freestone, 520 U.S. 329 (1997), decided April 21, 1997, Statutory displacement of § 1983; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Blessing v. Freestone, 520 U.S. 329 (1997), April 21, 1997.

### Consent-decree termination and Rule 52 findings

**Governing question:** What findings a court must make before dissolving an institutional consent decree under a substantial-compliance condition.

**Current rule:** Substantial compliance does not require perfection or literal satisfaction of every numerical criterion. It requires performance equivalent to the decree’s essential purposes with only immaterial deviations. Before dissolution, the court must identify the governing obligations, explain whether departures materially impair the decree’s objectives, state how quantitative and qualitative evidence was evaluated, and make subsidiary findings sufficient for meaningful appellate review under Rule 52(a).

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not expand the decree beyond its terms, require perfect compliance, or permit continued federal control after the written termination condition is established.

**First controlling authority:** New Mexico Department of Human Services v. Joseph A. and Josephine A., 69 F.3d 1081, decided April 21, 1997, Substantial compliance and Rule 52 findings; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** New Mexico Department of Human Services v. Joseph A. and Josephine A., 69 F.3d 1081, decided April 21, 1997, Substantial compliance and Rule 52 findings; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** New Mexico Department of Human Services v. Joseph A. and Josephine A., 69 F.3d 1081, April 21, 1997.

### Single final-policymaker hiring liability

**Governing question:** When a municipality may be liable under § 1983 for a final policymaker’s single hiring decision.

**Current rule:** A single hire may constitute municipal policy, but attribution alone does not establish fault or causation. Liability requires an underlying constitutional violation, final policymaking authority in the relevant field, conscious disregard of a substantial and highly predictable applicant-specific risk that conferring the particular authority will produce the kind of injury that occurs, and a direct causal connection. Negligent screening, favoritism, general unsuitability, or unrelated misconduct is insufficient.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not constitutionalize state hiring qualifications, impose respondeat superior, or decide training, supervision, or retention after later notice.

**First controlling authority:** Board of County Commissioners of Bryan County v. Brown, 520 U.S. 397 (1997), decided April 28, 1997, Municipal liability for a final policymaker’s single hire; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Board of County Commissioners of Bryan County v. Brown, 520 U.S. 397 (1997), decided April 28, 1997, Municipal liability for a final policymaker’s single hire; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Board of County Commissioners of Bryan County v. Brown, 520 U.S. 397 (1997), April 28, 1997.

### Rule 60(b)(5) and still-controlling precedent

**Governing question:** When doctrinal erosion permits relief from a prospective injunction under Rule 60(b)(5).

**Current rule:** Doctrinal tension or erosion does not by itself make continued enforcement inequitable when the injunction rests on a Supreme Court precedent that has not been overruled. A lower court must apply the still-controlling precedent. Rule 60(b)(5) relief requires an actual change in controlling law, a material change in facts, or another legally sufficient basis making continued prospective enforcement inequitable.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not prevent relief after controlling law actually changes or material factual conditions change. It does not authorize reopening completed liability merely because later decisions cast doubt on the reasoning of still-controlling precedent.

**First controlling authority:** Agostini v. Felton, 521 U.S. 203 (1997), decided June 23, 1997, Rule 60(b)(5) relief while controlling precedent remains intact; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Agostini v. Felton, 521 U.S. 203 (1997), decided June 23, 1997, Rule 60(b)(5) relief while controlling precedent remains intact; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Agostini v. Felton, 521 U.S. 203 (1997), June 23, 1997.

### Qualified immunity for for-profit private prison employees

**Governing question:** Whether guards employed by a competitive, for-profit prison-management company receive qualified immunity.

**Current rule:** Employees of a private, for-profit prison-management company are not entitled to qualified immunity when the firm systematically performs custody for profit in a competitive market, uses ordinary insurance and employment arrangements, and is not integrated into the State’s civil-service, indemnification, or closely supervised public-officer structure. State action remains distinct from immunity.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not govern a person briefly assisting government, a nonprofit or uniquely public institution, a contractor under direct day-to-day governmental control, or a separate good-faith defense.

**First controlling authority:** Richardson v. McKnight, 521 U.S. 399 (1997), decided June 23, 1997, Qualified immunity for guards employed by a for-profit prison firm; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Richardson v. McKnight, 521 U.S. 399 (1997), decided June 23, 1997, Qualified immunity for guards employed by a for-profit prison firm; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Richardson v. McKnight, 521 U.S. 399 (1997), June 23, 1997.

### Scope and design of institutional access-to-courts relief

**Governing question:** How institutional relief must be fitted to proven denial of prisoner access to the courts.

**Current rule:** Structural relief must identify the prisoners, institutions, and legal-access channels affected; cure the proven barrier effectively; omit program details unnecessary to that cure; and leave operational choice to prison officials when multiple lawful solutions exist. Systemwide relief remains available when systemwide or structurally recurring injury is actually proved.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not bar language assistance, trained help, monitoring, institution-wide measures, or retained jurisdiction when those devices are necessary to correct a demonstrated barrier.

**First controlling authority:** Lewis v. Casey, 518 U.S. 343, decided June 24, 1996, Scope and design of institutional relief; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Lewis v. Casey, 518 U.S. 343, decided June 24, 1996, Scope and design of institutional relief; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Lewis v. Casey, 518 U.S. 343, June 24, 1996.

### Desegregation expenditures and race-determinative scholarships

**Governing question:** When a federal desegregation decree may compel institutional expenditures or race-determinative scholarships.

**Current rule:** A federal desegregation court may compel expenditures necessary to eliminate an identified surviving vestige, but may not order general institutional equalization, prestige enhancement, or demographic attraction without the required violation-remedy connection. A race-determinative scholarship independently receives strict scrutiny and must serve a compelling remedial interest while remaining narrowly tailored in necessity, amount, duration, termination, and treatment of workable race-neutral alternatives.

**Exceptions and limits:** A State may voluntarily improve institutions or use race-neutral recruitment and scholarships beyond the federal constitutional minimum. The rule concerns what a federal decree may compel.

**First controlling authority:** National Association for the Advancement of Colored People v. Fordice, 879 F. Supp. 1419, decided March 25, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Funding, facilities, endowments, and scholarships; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** National Association for the Advancement of Colored People v. Fordice, 879 F. Supp. 1419, decided March 25, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Funding, facilities, endowments, and scholarships; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** National Association for the Advancement of Colored People v. Fordice, 879 F. Supp. 1419, March 25, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

### Monitoring and incremental withdrawal of institutional control

**Governing question:** How long monitoring and retained jurisdiction may continue in an institutional desegregation decree.

**Current rule:** Monitoring, retained jurisdiction, administrative funding, and a general injunction may operate only in relation to surviving traceable vestiges and valid remedial measures. Supervision must terminate incrementally when the constitutional work in a separable component is complete.

**Exceptions and limits:** No fixed termination date is required. Different components may remain under supervision for different periods.

**First controlling authority:** National Association for the Advancement of Colored People v. Fordice, 879 F. Supp. 1419, decided March 25, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Monitoring, injunctions, and withdrawal of control; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** National Association for the Advancement of Colored People v. Fordice, 879 F. Supp. 1419, decided March 25, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date, Monitoring, injunctions, and withdrawal of control; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** National Association for the Advancement of Colored People v. Fordice, 879 F. Supp. 1419, March 25, 1996, on a simulation-assigned opinion date.

### Function-specific absolute immunity

**Governing question:** How absolute official immunity turns on the function performed.

**Current rule:** Absolute immunity follows the function performed, not the official’s title. Prosecutorial charging and courtroom advocacy remain absolutely protected, but a prosecutor’s personal oath to historical probable-cause facts performs a complaining-witness function and is not absolutely immune. Local officials receive legislative immunity for introducing, voting for, and signing prospective policy legislation. Judges receive judicial immunity for adjudicative acts, but not for administrative employment or hiring decisions.

**First controlling authority:** Kalina v. Fletcher, 522 U.S. 118 (1997), decided December 10, 1997, complaining-witness immunity; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Kalina v. Fletcher, 522 U.S. 118 (1997), decided December 10, 1997, complaining-witness immunity; Bogan v. Scott-Harris, 523 U.S. 44 (1998), decided March 3, 1998, local legislative immunity; Duffy v. Wolle, 123 F.3d 1026, decided June 3, 1998, administrative judicial employment decisions; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Duffy v. Wolle, 123 F.3d 1026, June 3, 1998.

**Development:**
- March 3, 1998, Bogan v. Scott-Harris: The functional rule extends legislative immunity to local officials performing legislative acts.
- June 3, 1998, Duffy v. Wolle: Administrative judicial employment decisions are outside judicial immunity.

### Proof and procedure for unconstitutional-motive claims

**Governing question:** Whether qualified immunity changes the ordinary proof burden for a § 1983 claim whose constitutional violation turns on motive.

**Current rule:** Qualified immunity does not authorize clear-and-convincing proof or an equivalent special merits barrier merely because unconstitutional motive is an element of a § 1983 claim. Ordinary pleading, causation, discovery, summary-judgment, and immunity rules apply. Courts may sequence and limit discovery under ordinary authority but may not alter the merits burden.

**First controlling authority:** Crawford-El v. Britton, 523 U.S. 574 (1998), decided May 4, 1998, proof and procedure for motive-defined § 1983 claims; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Crawford-El v. Britton, 523 U.S. 574 (1998), decided May 4, 1998, proof and procedure for motive-defined § 1983 claims; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Crawford-El v. Britton, 523 U.S. 574 (1998), May 4, 1998.

### Existing constitutional damages actions and nonstatutory internal procedures

**Governing question:** Whether a court-created internal federal-employment procedure displaces an existing individual-capacity constitutional damages action.

**Current rule:** A court-created internal employment procedure does not displace an existing individual-capacity constitutional damages action absent congressional authorization. The action recognized in Davis v. Passman remains available for intentional sex discrimination by a federal employer, directed against responsible officers rather than the employing institution.

**First controlling authority:** Duffy v. Wolle, 123 F.3d 1026, decided June 3, 1998, administrative judicial employment decisions; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Duffy v. Wolle, 123 F.3d 1026, decided June 3, 1998, administrative judicial employment decisions; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Duffy v. Wolle, 123 F.3d 1026, June 3, 1998.

### At-will employment as injury under § 1985(2)

**Governing question:** Whether retaliatory interference with at-will employment is injury to person or property under 42 U.S.C. § 1985(2).

**Current rule:** Concrete economic loss from a conspiracy to deter or retaliate against federal testimony constitutes injury to person or property even when the employment is at will and the employee has no constitutional entitlement to continued employment.

**Exceptions and limits:** The claimant must still establish the conspiracy, prohibited deterrent or retaliatory purpose, causation, and the statute’s remaining elements.

**First controlling authority:** Haddle v. Garrison, 525 U.S. 121 (1998), decided December 14, 1998, retaliatory interference with at-will employment; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Haddle v. Garrison, 525 U.S. 121 (1998), decided December 14, 1998, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Haddle v. Garrison, 525 U.S. 121 (1998), December 14, 1998.

### APA waiver and equitable liens against federal property

**Governing question:** When 5 U.S.C. § 702 waives federal sovereign immunity for an asserted equitable lien against Government funds or property.

**Current rule:** Section 702 does not waive immunity for an equitable lien when no identifiable retained fund remains and the claimant seeks substitute compensation from general Government property for a private contractor’s default. Characterizing substitute compensation as equitable does not avoid the requirement of an unequivocal waiver for the particular Treasury or property remedy.

**Exceptions and limits:** Enforcement of a specific statutory payment obligation and a claim to an identifiable fund retained by the Government remain distinct. Creditors may not attach Government funds or property without express authority.

**First controlling authority:** Department of the Army v. Blue Fox, Inc., 525 U.S. 255 (1999), decided January 20, 1999, APA waiver for an equitable lien against federal funds; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Department of the Army v. Blue Fox, Inc., 525 U.S. 255 (1999), decided January 20, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Department of the Army v. Blue Fox, Inc., 525 U.S. 255 (1999), January 20, 1999.

### Prejudgment restraint of assets unrelated to the claim

**Governing question:** When a federal court may freeze a defendant’s unencumbered assets before judgment to secure a possible money award.

**Current rule:** At the request of a general unsecured creditor seeking only money damages, a federal court may not freeze unencumbered assets before judgment absent a lien, equitable interest, statutory authorization, or another recognized independent source of prejudgment control. Rule 65 does not itself create a prejudgment attachment remedy.

**Exceptions and limits:** Rule 64 remedies authorized by an independent source, protection of a specific fund or equitable interest within the suit, and expressly authorized statutory restraints remain available.

**First controlling authority:** Grupo Mexicano de Desarrollo, S.A. v. Alliance Bond Fund, Inc., 527 U.S. 308 (1999), decided June 17, 1999, prejudgment restraint of assets unrelated to the claim; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Grupo Mexicano de Desarrollo, S.A. v. Alliance Bond Fund, Inc., 527 U.S. 308 (1999), decided June 17, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Grupo Mexicano de Desarrollo, S.A. v. Alliance Bond Fund, Inc., 527 U.S. 308 (1999), June 17, 1999.

### Same-decision proof and retrospective relief for a discrete governmental decision

**Governing question:** What effect conclusive same-decision proof has on retrospective § 1983 liability for a discrete governmental decision.

**Current rule:** Conclusive proof that the government would have made the same discrete decision through a lawful process defeats retrospective § 1983 liability for that decision, including compensatory and nominal damages.

**Burdens and proof:** The same-decision showing must be conclusive as to the challenged governmental outcome.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not validate the challenged policy, establish that an impermissible classification was used, decide remaining statutory claims, or bar a separately live claim that ongoing unequal competition causes a distinct injury supporting prospective relief.

**First controlling authority:** Texas v. Lesage, 528 U.S. 18 (1999), decided November 29, 1999, precedential summary disposition on same-decision proof and retrospective § 1983 liability; established the current rule at the stated summary-disposition scope.

**Controlling authorities:** Texas v. Lesage, 528 U.S. 18 (1999), decided November 29, 1999, supplies the current rule, applies Mt. Healthy City Board of Education v. Doyle to a discrete equal-protection decision, and distinguishes Carey v. Piphus as to nominal damages.

**Present operation effective:** Texas v. Lesage, 528 U.S. 18 (1999), November 29, 1999.

**Related current rules:** Patronage retaliation against a regular service provider, under First Amendment; Section 5 discriminatory purpose and identical-change proof, under Elections and Voting. The first invokes this general same-decision consequence in the patronage setting; the second uses a distinct preclearance burden and identical-change route rather than this retrospective-liability rule.

### Function-specific governmental status of prosecutors

**Governing question:** Whether a prosecutor acts for a State or a municipality for purposes of an official-capacity or municipal-liability claim.

**Current rule:** Governmental status is determined under governing state law function by function, not from the prosecutor’s title alone. Office-wide training, supervision, record management, and local administrative practices may be municipal functions even when charging and courtroom advocacy in an individual prosecution receive a different state or immunity classification.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** Identify the precise challenged function, then determine under governing state law which government possesses authority and control over that function. Do not transfer the classification of advocacy automatically to office administration or the classification of administration automatically to advocacy.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not itself establish final policymaking authority, an underlying constitutional violation, municipal fault or causation, or the immunity applicable to an individual act of advocacy.

**First controlling authority:** *Carter v. City of Philadelphia*, 181 F.3d 339 (3d Cir. 1999), Supreme Court simulation decided April 10, 2000, governmental status for prosecutorial office administration; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Carter v. City of Philadelphia*, 181 F.3d 339 (3d Cir. 1999), Supreme Court simulation decided April 10, 2000, supplies the function-specific inquiry and the distinction between local office administration and individual advocacy.

**Present operation effective:** *Carter v. City of Philadelphia*, April 10, 2000.

**Related current rules:** *Municipal liability for training and supervision* supplies the fault and causation requirements once the challenged function is attributable to a municipality. *Absolute and qualified official immunity* governs the distinct immunity question for the act performed.

### Municipal liability for training and supervision

**Governing question:** What a claimant must establish to impose municipal liability for a final policymaker’s failure to train or supervise after notice of a recurring constitutional risk.

**Current rule:** Liability requires an underlying constitutional injury, final policymaking authority in the relevant field, a known pattern or an obvious and recurring constitutional risk, deliberate failure by the responsible policymaker to adopt necessary safeguards, and a direct causal connection between that failure and the injury. Attribution alone and respondeat superior are insufficient.

**Burdens and proof:** At the pleading stage, a claimant must plausibly allege each required component but need not identify unavailable internal directives with evidentiary precision before discovery. At adjudication, the claimant must prove the underlying injury, attribution, deliberate indifference, and causation.

**Exceptions and limits:** Negligent administration, generalized assertions of poor training, employment relationship alone, and a risk unrelated to the injury do not suffice. The applicant-specific rule for a final policymaker’s single hiring decision remains distinct.

**First controlling authority:** *Carter v. City of Philadelphia*, 181 F.3d 339 (3d Cir. 1999), Supreme Court simulation decided April 10, 2000, recurring training and supervision after institutional notice; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** *Carter v. City of Philadelphia*, 181 F.3d 339 (3d Cir. 1999), Supreme Court simulation decided April 10, 2000, supplies the underlying-injury, notice or obvious-risk, deliberate-indifference, causation, and pleading-stage components.

**Present operation effective:** *Carter v. City of Philadelphia*, April 10, 2000.

**Related current rules:** *Municipal liability* supplies the rule that no municipal policy claim exists without a constitutional injury. *Single final-policymaker hiring liability* governs the distinct applicant-specific single-hire setting. *Function-specific governmental status of prosecutors* governs attribution when the asserted policymaker is a prosecutor.

## Admiralty and Maritime Law

### State remedies for a nonseafarer’s territorial-water death

**Governing question:** When state remedies supplement or are displaced for a nonseafarer’s death in territorial waters.

**Current rule:** A court must distinguish the general maritime cause of action from status-specific federal statutes, location-specific federal statutes, and supplementary state remedies. Federal displacement follows from an applicable exclusive statute or controlling maritime rule, not from an abstract preference for uniformity.

**Exceptions and limits:** The Court does not decide remedies for seamen, longshore workers, deaths governed by the Death on the High Seas Act, or a state rule that directly conflicts with a specific federal maritime command.

**First controlling authority:** Yamaha Motor Corp., U.S.A. v. Calhoun, 516 U.S. 199, decided January 9, 1996, State remedies for a nonseafarer’s territorial-water death; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Yamaha Motor Corp., U.S.A. v. Calhoun, 516 U.S. 199, decided January 9, 1996, State remedies for a nonseafarer’s territorial-water death; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Yamaha Motor Corp., U.S.A. v. Calhoun, 516 U.S. 199, January 9, 1996.

### Nominal LHWCA compensation for future wage loss

**Governing question:** When a permanent work injury with no present wage loss supports nominal compensation.

**Current rule:** The LHWCA permits nominal compensation when a permanent injury causes no present loss of wage-earning capacity but the claimant proves a significant, nonconjectural potential that the injury will reduce future capacity. The employer seeking termination of actual benefits first bears the burden to prove current earnings fairly represent present capacity; the claimant seeking nominal compensation then bears persuasion by a preponderance on significant future potential.

**Exceptions and limits:** Permanent impairment alone, an abstract possibility of unemployment, or administrative convenience is insufficient.

**First controlling authority:** Metropolitan Stevedore Co. v. Rambo, 521 U.S. 121 (1997), decided June 19, 1997, Nominal compensation and future earning capacity; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Metropolitan Stevedore Co. v. Rambo, 521 U.S. 121 (1997), decided June 19, 1997, Nominal compensation and future earning capacity; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Metropolitan Stevedore Co. v. Rambo, 521 U.S. 121 (1997), June 19, 1997.

### Federal displacement of state tanker standards

**Governing question:** Which state tanker requirements are displaced by the Ports and Waterways Safety Act and related federal maritime law.

**Current rule:** Title II of the Ports and Waterways Safety Act occupies the subjects of general vessel design, equipment, operation, manning, and personnel qualification. Title I leaves room for genuinely local waterway measures addressing demonstrated local conditions when they do not conflict with federal law or an affirmative federal determination. The Oil Pollution Act’s saving clauses preserve state liability, compensation, and additional cleanup authority, not independent systemic vessel standards within the occupied Title II subjects.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** The court identifies the subject regulated by the state provision, determines whether Title II occupies it, and, for a genuine local-waterway measure under Title I, separately tests conflict with enacted federal law, a valid regulation, or an affirmative federal determination.

**Exceptions and limits:** Agency preference, a generalized interest in uniformity, regulatory absence, or federal nonregulation alone does not preempt. State measures addressing distinctive channel depth, narrowness, visibility, traffic, or similar local-waterway conditions remain potentially valid outside an occupied field and absent conflict.

**First controlling authority:** United States v. Locke; International Association of Independent Tanker Owners v. Locke, 529 U.S. 89 (2000), decided March 6, 2000, federal displacement of state tanker rules; established the current consolidated rule.

**Controlling authorities:** United States v. Locke; International Association of Independent Tanker Owners v. Locke, 529 U.S. 89 (2000), decided March 6, 2000, supplies the Title II occupied subjects, the Title I local-measure route, the Oil Pollution Act saving-clause construction, and the requirement of an operative federal source or conflict.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Locke; International Association of Independent Tanker Owners v. Locke, 529 U.S. 89 (2000), March 6, 2000.

**Related current rules:** Implied conflict preemption, under Federalism and Preemption. That general rule supplies conflict principles where this specialized maritime rule leaves room for local regulation.

## Federal Contracts

### Contract formation and allocation of regulatory-change risk

**Governing question:** When authorized federal officials form a binding contract allocating regulatory-change risk and what recurring consequence follows material governmental nonperformance.

**Current rule:** The United States is bound when authorized officials, acting within statutory contracting or assistance powers, make an express, bargained-for commitment that reasonably allocates a defined regulatory risk. The promise need not disable future legislation; it may allocate the financial consequences if lawful sovereign action prevents the promised performance. When the Government contracts to provide a specified approval process under an incorporated legal regime and later refuses to provide that process because of a statutory condition outside the counterparty’s assumed risk, the refusal is a material repudiation.

**Consequence or recurring remedy:** After material governmental repudiation, the injured counterparty may elect restitution of consideration transferred for the promised process without proving that complete performance ultimately would have produced a profit or final regulatory approval. Lawful credits, offsets, and any independent requirement for interest remain applicable.

**Exceptions and limits:** Not every forbearance letter, regulatory statement, agency prediction, or expectation of favorable regulatory action is contractual. Authority cannot be created by promise. The rule does not guarantee approval, insure against every legal change, or award expectancy profits without the ordinary proof required for that remedy; it depends on an authorized, transaction-specific commitment supported by consideration and on risk outside the counterparty’s contractual assumption.

**First controlling authority:** *United States v. Winstar Corp.*, 518 U.S. 839, decided July 1, 1996, contract formation and allocation of regulatory-change risk; established the binding-risk-allocation component.

**Controlling authorities:** *United States v. Winstar Corp.*, 518 U.S. 839, decided July 1, 1996, supplies authorized contract formation and allocation of the financial consequences of later legislation. *Mobil Oil Exploration & Producing Southeast, Inc. v. United States; Marathon Oil Co. v. United States*, 530 U.S. 604 (2000), decided June 26, 2000, supplies material repudiation through refusal of the promised approval process and restitution without proof of ultimate approval or profit.

**Present operation effective:** *Mobil Oil Exploration & Producing Southeast, Inc. v. United States; Marathon Oil Co. v. United States*, 530 U.S. 604 (2000), June 26, 2000.

**Related current rules:** `Unmistakability, sovereign acts, and impossibility`, under Remedies, preserves the effectiveness of later sovereign law while governing defenses to financial liability under an authorized risk-allocating contract.

**Development:**
- June 26, 2000, *Mobil Oil Exploration & Producing Southeast, Inc. v. United States; Marathon Oil Co. v. United States*: Before *Mobil Oil*, the entry established authorized contract formation and financial allocation of regulatory-change risk. After *Mobil Oil*, refusal to provide a specifically promised approval process because of a later condition outside the counterparty’s assumed risk constitutes material repudiation, and restitution of transferred consideration does not depend on proof of final approval or profit. The Government’s sovereign authority to change the law remains intact.

## Fourteenth Amendment Enforcement

### Claim-specific enforcement under Fourteenth Amendment § 5

**Governing question:** When congressional legislation is valid Fourteenth Amendment § 5 enforcement as applied to a claim, including when that authority supports retrospective monetary enforcement against a State or is invoked against private conduct.

**Current rule:** A court first determines whether the plaintiff states the statutory claim on the conduct alleged and whether that same conduct independently violates a Fourteenth Amendment guarantee as this Court has construed it. If the conduct independently violates the guarantee, the statute is valid enforcement as applied to that conduct, the remedy Congress provided attaches to that claim, and the § 5 inquiry ends. If the conduct does not independently violate the guarantee, the statute must satisfy the fitted requirements drawn from its enacted terms and the governing constitutional standard. A statutory prohibition that reaches substantially more rational-basis state conduct than the Fourteenth Amendment forbids and lacks a fitted remedial, evidentiary, coverage, reauthorization, or bailout boundary cannot support retrospective state-treasury liability under § 5. Section 5 does not authorize untethered constitutional damages liability against a private assailant for private conduct.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** First, the operative command must run against a State, its subdivisions, or persons acting under state authority. Second, the statute must be referable to a specific Fourteenth Amendment guarantee, using this Court’s standard for state action in that field as the baseline. Third, a statute reaching only conduct that baseline forbids is valid without more. A statute that also reaches state conduct the Constitution permits is valid only if it allocates proof under the constitutional standard, supplies remedial or compliance machinery attached to that standard, or imposes a broader prohibition while either preserving a defense of constitutional compliance on the same evidence that would defeat a constitutional claim or using an enacted coverage criterion with a stated reauthorization interval or a bailout keyed to present conditions. Fourth, Congress may not supply the rule of decision for whether the Constitution has been violated, direct the standard of review courts apply to a constitutional claim, or legislate so that the constitutional standard governs for no purpose. When the constitutional violation includes a mental-state or remedial-inadequacy element, the plaintiff must plead and prove that element to proceed on the independently unconstitutional conduct. When retrospective state-treasury liability depends on § 5 abrogation, the same claim-specific validity inquiry determines whether § 5 supplies that authority.

**Exceptions and limits:** The volume of congressional findings neither satisfies nor defeats any requirement. Validity is assessed claim by claim and remedy by remedy, and failure as to one application does not invalidate the statute or provision as to others. Validity is measured against the constitutional standard at enactment; a later narrowing operates prospectively. Congress may prevent and remedy unconstitutional conduct by States and state officials and may adopt fitted measures directed to official obstruction or discriminatory administration. The rule does not decide RFRA’s validity as applied to the Federal Government, authority under another enumerated power or a lawful spending condition, or broader state-law protection of religious exercise. Failure of the § 5 route does not by itself erase a valid substantive duty enacted under another constitutional power.

**First controlling authority:** City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997), decided June 25, 1997, claim-specific § 5 enforcement; established the original rule.

**Controlling authorities:** City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997), decided June 25, 1997, supplies the claim-specific sequence, constitutional-baseline requirement, permissible enforcement mechanisms, and limits on congressional alteration of the constitutional rule. Kimel v. Florida Board of Regents; United States v. Florida Board of Regents, 528 U.S. 62 (2000), decided January 11, 2000, supplies the application of that inquiry to § 5 abrogation for retrospective state-treasury liability and the boundary for broad statutory regulation of rational-basis classifications. United States v. Morrison; Brzonkala v. Morrison, 529 U.S. 598 (2000), decided May 15, 2000, supplies the rule that § 5 does not authorize untethered constitutional damages liability against a private assailant for private conduct and preserves fitted remedies directed to state action.

**Present operation effective:** United States v. Morrison; Brzonkala v. Morrison, 529 U.S. 598 (2000), May 15, 2000.

**Related current rules:** Article I duties, remedial autonomy, and federal fallback, under Federalism and State Sovereign Immunity. Failure of § 5 abrogation does not extinguish a separately valid and clearly enacted Article I duty, whose retrospective monetary remedy remains governed by state channeling and the federal fallback.

**Development:**
- January 11, 2000, Kimel v. Florida Board of Regents; United States v. Florida Board of Regents, 528 U.S. 62: The claim-specific § 5 inquiry now also determines whether Congress validly supports retrospective state-treasury liability through § 5 abrogation. A broad prohibition of rational-basis classifications that reaches substantially more conduct than the Constitution forbids and lacks a fitted limiting mechanism does not supply that authority; a separately valid Article I duty remains distinct.
- May 15, 2000, United States v. Morrison; Brzonkala v. Morrison, 529 U.S. 598: Section 5 cannot support untethered constitutional damages liability against a private assailant for private violence. Fitted prevention and remediation directed to unconstitutional state action or sufficiently connected governmental actors remain available.

## Patent Law

### Allocation of patent-claim construction

**Governing question:** Whether the court or jury construes a patent claim, including technical terms of art within the claim.

**Current rule:** Construction of a patent claim, including the meaning of technical terms of art within the claim, is a question for the court. The jury retains its role in deciding infringement and other factual issues under the claim as construed. Expert testimony may inform the court’s interpretation of the patent instrument, but the presence of technical evidence does not transfer ultimate claim construction to the jury.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not remove infringement from the jury, classify every expert-supported dispute as legal, or decide the appellate standard for every possible subsidiary factual determination.

**First controlling authority:** Markman v. Westview Instruments, Inc., 517 U.S. 370, decided April 23, 1996, Allocation of patent-claim construction; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Markman v. Westview Instruments, Inc., 517 U.S. 370, decided April 23, 1996, Allocation of patent-claim construction; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Markman v. Westview Instruments, Inc., 517 U.S. 370, April 23, 1996.

### Doctrine of equivalents and prosecution-history estoppel

**Governing question:** How infringement by equivalents operates by claim element and how unexplained claim amendments affect the inquiry.

**Current rule:** The doctrine of equivalents remains available but operates element by element and may not erase a claim limitation. Courts determine claim meaning and the legal effect of prosecution history; genuine factual equivalence remains for ordinary infringement procedures. A claim amendment made for a substantial reason related to patentability may create estoppel. When the record does not disclose the reason, the patentee bears the burden of establishing a reason that does not surrender the asserted equivalent; failure produces a presumption that the equivalent was relinquished.

**Exceptions and limits:** The doctrine cannot expand a claim beyond its element-specific boundaries or nullify a surrendered limitation.

**First controlling authority:** Warner-Jenkinson Co. v. Hilton Davis Chemical Co., 520 U.S. 17 (1997), decided March 3, 1997, Survival and element-specific operation of equivalents and prosecution-history estoppel; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Warner-Jenkinson Co. v. Hilton Davis Chemical Co., 520 U.S. 17 (1997), decided March 3, 1997, Survival and element-specific operation of equivalents and prosecution-history estoppel; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Warner-Jenkinson Co. v. Hilton Davis Chemical Co., 520 U.S. 17 (1997), March 3, 1997.

### Pre-AIA on-sale bar

**Governing question:** When an invention is on sale under pre-AIA 35 U.S.C. § 102(b).

**Current rule:** Before the critical date, the invention must be the subject of a commercial offer for sale and be ready for patenting. Readiness may be shown by reduction to practice or by drawings or descriptions sufficiently specific to enable a skilled artisan to practice the invention; a completed physical prototype is unnecessary.

**Exceptions and limits:** Experimental activity undertaken to perfect the invention remains distinct from commercial exploitation.

**First controlling authority:** Pfaff v. Wells Electronics, Inc., 525 U.S. 55 (1998), decided November 10, 1998, commercial offer and readiness for patenting; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Pfaff v. Wells Electronics, Inc., 525 U.S. 55 (1998), decided November 10, 1998, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Pfaff v. Wells Electronics, Inc., 525 U.S. 55 (1998), November 10, 1998.

## Civil Procedure

### Inherent authority and civil fugitive disentitlement

**Governing question:** When a court may use inherent authority to bar a fugitive from defending a related civil action.

**Current rule:** An exercise of inherent judicial authority must be a reasonable and proportionate response to an actual problem affecting the proceeding in which the sanction is imposed. Complete loss of the right to defend is unavailable when narrower measures can protect enforcement, evidence, discovery, and the integrity of related litigation.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not protect disobedience of a court order, require unrestricted civil discovery, decide ownership or traceability, or resolve whether Congress may enact a constitutionally adequate rule addressing a specifically defined form of fugitive abuse.

**First controlling authority:** Degen v. United States, 517 U.S. 820, decided June 10, 1996, Inherent authority and civil fugitive disentitlement; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Degen v. United States, 517 U.S. 820, decided June 10, 1996, Inherent authority and civil fugitive disentitlement; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Degen v. United States, 517 U.S. 820, June 10, 1996.

### Federal trial and appellate allocation for state excessiveness review

**Governing question:** How federal trial and appellate courts divide review under a state-law compensatory-damages excessiveness standard.

**Current rule:** The federal district judge applies the state excessiveness standard through the historically grounded Rule 59 new-trial power. Appellate review is limited to abuse of discretion.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not bar appellate correction of legal error or abuse of discretion and does not govern constitutional review of punitive damages.

**First controlling authority:** Gasperini v. Center for Humanities, Inc., 518 U.S. 415, decided June 24, 1996, Federal trial and appellate allocation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Gasperini v. Center for Humanities, Inc., 518 U.S. 415, decided June 24, 1996, Federal trial and appellate allocation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Gasperini v. Center for Humanities, Inc., 518 U.S. 415, June 24, 1996.

### Settlement-only class certification under Rule 23

**Governing question:** What Rule 23 requires when certification is sought only to implement a settlement.

**Current rule:** A settlement-only class must satisfy every applicable Rule 23 requirement. Settlement may eliminate trial-management difficulties, but it does not relax commonality, typicality, predominance, or adequate representation. Predominance fails when individual and state-law questions overwhelm common issues, and one undivided representation structure is inadequate when currently injured and future claimants have materially conflicting interests.

**Exceptions and limits:** Mass-tort settlement classes are not categorically impossible. Narrower claim groupings, separately represented subclasses, and individual settlements remain available.

**First controlling authority:** Amchem Products, Inc. v. Windsor, 521 U.S. 591 (1997), decided June 25, 1997, Rule 23 in a settlement-only class and adequate representation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Amchem Products, Inc. v. Windsor, 521 U.S. 591 (1997), decided June 25, 1997, Rule 23 in a settlement-only class and adequate representation; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Amchem Products, Inc. v. Windsor, 521 U.S. 591 (1997), June 25, 1997.

### Self-transfer by an MDL transferee court

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs self-transfer by an MDL transferee court.

**Current rule:** A multidistrict-litigation transferee court may not retain a transferred action for trial by transferring the case from itself to itself under § 1404(a). Section 1407(a) requires remand when coordinated pretrial proceedings conclude.

**First controlling authority:** Lexecon Inc. v. Milberg Weiss Bershad Hynes & Lerach, 523 U.S. 26 (1998), decided March 3, 1998, Self-transfer by an MDL transferee court; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Lexecon Inc. v. Milberg Weiss Bershad Hynes & Lerach, 523 U.S. 26 (1998), decided March 3, 1998, Self-transfer by an MDL transferee court; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Lexecon Inc. v. Milberg Weiss Bershad Hynes & Lerach, 523 U.S. 26 (1998), March 3, 1998.

### Mandatory limited-fund settlement classes

**Governing question:** What Rule 23(b)(1)(B) and Rule 23(a)(4) require for a mandatory settlement class asserted to involve a limited fund.

**Current rule:** The fund must be independently demonstrated to be fixed at its maximum practical value, insufficient to satisfy the aggregate claims, substantially devoted to the claimant class, and distributed equitably. Settlement does not relax those requirements. Representation must be conflict free, and materially opposed claimant groups, including present and future claimants with divergent payment, latency, proof, or inflation interests, require separately represented subclasses when one representation structure cannot protect each group.

**Burdens and proof:** The proponents of mandatory certification must establish the limited fund and every applicable Rule 23 requirement.

**Exceptions and limits:** The rule does not prohibit individual settlements, narrower opt-out classes, adequately represented subclasses, bankruptcy, or a genuine limited-fund proceeding satisfying the stated requirements.

**First controlling authority:** Ortiz v. Fibreboard Corp., 527 U.S. 815 (1999), decided June 23, 1999, limited fund and adequate representation; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Ortiz v. Fibreboard Corp., 527 U.S. 815 (1999), decided June 23, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Ortiz v. Fibreboard Corp., 527 U.S. 815 (1999), June 23, 1999.

### Appellate judgment after exclusion of trial evidence

**Governing question:** What disposition a court of appeals may enter after excluding erroneously admitted trial evidence and finding the remaining record legally insufficient.

**Current rule:** Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 50 and 28 U.S.C. § 2106 permit a court of appeals to direct judgment as a matter of law after excluding erroneously admitted evidence and finding the remaining properly admitted record legally insufficient. A new trial is not automatic when the party had a fair opportunity to prove the case.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** The appellate court first determines admissibility, then tests legal sufficiency on the properly admitted record. It retains discretion to order a new trial when fairness or a preserved procedural ground warrants one.

**Exceptions and limits:** A new trial may be appropriate after an unexpected legal change, a blocked proffer, a preserved new-trial ground, or another procedural unfairness. Inadmissible evidence cannot support the verdict merely because the trial court initially admitted it.

**First controlling authority:** Weisgram v. Marley Co., 528 U.S. 440 (2000), decided February 22, 2000, appellate judgment after exclusion of proof; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Weisgram v. Marley Co., 528 U.S. 440 (2000), decided February 22, 2000, supplies the Rule 50 and § 2106 authority, the properly-admitted-record requirement, and the retained new-trial discretion.

**Present operation effective:** Weisgram v. Marley Co., 528 U.S. 440 (2000), February 22, 2000.

## Disability Law

### Title II coverage of state prisons

**Governing question:** What recurring rule governs title II coverage of state prisons.

**Current rule:** A state correctional department is a public entity under Title II, and its prison services, programs, and activities fall within the statute’s unqualified coverage. Involuntary custody does not create an exception.

**First controlling authority:** Pennsylvania Department of Corrections v. Yeskey, 524 U.S. 206 (1998), decided June 15, 1998, Title II coverage of state prisons; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Pennsylvania Department of Corrections v. Yeskey, 524 U.S. 206 (1998), decided June 15, 1998, Title II coverage of state prisons; supplies the current rule.
