**Present operation effective:** Pennsylvania Department of Corrections v. Yeskey, 524 U.S. 206 (1998), June 15, 1998.

### Asymptomatic impairment and individualized disability

**Governing question:** When an asymptomatic disease substantially limits a major life activity for disability-law coverage.

**Current rule:** Asymptomatic disease may constitute a physical impairment substantially limiting a major life activity, including reproduction, but coverage remains individualized. The claimant must establish that the impairment substantially limits the particular claimant’s major life activity; the rule does not make every person with the condition disabled through the same activity.

**First controlling authority:** Bragdon v. Abbott, 524 U.S. 624 (1998), decided June 25, 1998, individualized disability and direct-threat assessment; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Bragdon v. Abbott, 524 U.S. 624 (1998), decided June 25, 1998, individualized disability and direct-threat assessment; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Bragdon v. Abbott, 524 U.S. 624 (1998), June 25, 1998.

### Objective individualized direct-threat assessment

**Governing question:** What showing establishes a direct-threat defense under disability law.

**Current rule:** A direct-threat defense requires an individualized assessment of a significant risk grounded in objective medical evidence reasonably available when treatment was refused. The inquiry considers the nature, duration, severity, probability, and avoidability of harm, including whether reasonable precautions could eliminate or reduce the risk. Sincere professional belief does not substitute for objective contemporaneous evidence.

**First controlling authority:** Bragdon v. Abbott, 524 U.S. 624 (1998), decided June 25, 1998, individualized disability and direct-threat assessment; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Bragdon v. Abbott, 524 U.S. 624 (1998), decided June 25, 1998, individualized disability and direct-threat assessment; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Bragdon v. Abbott, 524 U.S. 624 (1998), June 25, 1998.

### SSDI representations and ADA qualification

**Governing question:** What effect an SSDI application or award has on an ADA plaintiff’s proof that the plaintiff is qualified to work with reasonable accommodation.

**Current rule:** An SSDI application or award does not automatically estop the ADA plaintiff because the two statutes ask materially different questions and SSDI does not account for reasonable accommodation in the same way. The plaintiff must nevertheless explain any apparent factual inconsistency sufficiently to permit a reasonable juror to find that the essential job functions could be performed with reasonable accommodation.

**Burdens and proof:** The ADA plaintiff must produce a sufficient reconciliation to support a reasonable finding on qualification.

**First controlling authority:** Cleveland v. Policy Management Systems Corp., 526 U.S. 795 (1999), decided May 24, 1999, effect of SSDI representations on ADA qualification; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Cleveland v. Policy Management Systems Corp., 526 U.S. 795 (1999), decided May 24, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Cleveland v. Policy Management Systems Corp., 526 U.S. 795 (1999), May 24, 1999.

### Actual disability with mitigation and natural adaptation

**Governing question:** How actual disability is measured when the person uses mitigating measures or has naturally adapted to an impairment.

**Current rule:** Actual disability is assessed through the person’s real, present, individualized functioning with the mitigating measures actually used. The assessment includes their benefits and any proved material side effects, burdens, unreliability, dependence, or interruption consequences. Natural adaptation is relevant to actual functioning without being categorically dispositive, and diagnosis alone does not establish substantial limitation.

**Burdens and proof:** The claimant must prove substantial limitation through individualized evidence of practical functioning.

**Exceptions and limits:** The untreated form of a condition does not itself establish disability when the measures actually used leave no proved substantial limitation. Material residual effects and treatment burdens remain part of the inquiry.

**First controlling authority:** Sutton v. United Air Lines, Inc., 527 U.S. 471 (1999), and Murphy v. United Parcel Service, Inc., 527 U.S. 516 (1999), decided June 22, 1999, measurement of actual disability with mitigating measures; Albertson’s, Inc. v. Kirkingburg, 527 U.S. 555 (1999), decided June 22, 1999, individualized treatment of natural adaptation; jointly established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Sutton v. United Air Lines, Inc., 527 U.S. 471 (1999), and Murphy v. United Parcel Service, Inc., 527 U.S. 516 (1999), decided June 22, 1999, supply assessment with mitigating measures and treatment burdens. Albertson’s, Inc. v. Kirkingburg, 527 U.S. 555 (1999), decided June 22, 1999, supplies individualized treatment of natural adaptation.

**Present operation effective:** Sutton v. United Air Lines, Inc., 527 U.S. 471 (1999); Murphy v. United Parcel Service, Inc., 527 U.S. 516 (1999); and Albertson’s, Inc. v. Kirkingburg, 527 U.S. 555 (1999), June 22, 1999.

### Regarded-as limitation in working

**Governing question:** What perceived occupational restriction constitutes a regarded-as substantial limitation in working.

**Current rule:** The perceived restriction must concern a class or broad range of jobs. An employer’s conclusion that a person cannot satisfy one standard for a single specialized position or one licensed job configuration does not establish a regarded-as-working disability.

**First controlling authority:** Sutton v. United Air Lines, Inc., 527 U.S. 471 (1999), decided June 22, 1999, regarded-as limitation in working; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Sutton v. United Air Lines, Inc., 527 U.S. 471 (1999), and Murphy v. United Parcel Service, Inc., 527 U.S. 516 (1999), decided June 22, 1999, supply the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Sutton v. United Air Lines, Inc., 527 U.S. 471 (1999), and Murphy v. United Parcel Service, Inc., 527 U.S. 516 (1999), June 22, 1999.

### Binding qualification standards and experimental waivers

**Governing question:** Whether an experimental waiver program alters a binding federal job-qualification standard or requires a private employer to treat an applicant as presently qualified.

**Current rule:** An experimental waiver program does not amend the operative federal qualification standard unless the governing scheme gives the waiver that effect. Eligibility to seek a waiver does not itself establish present qualification, and an employer may insist on the certification the binding rule demands.

**Exceptions and limits:** An effective individual waiver or a governing rule making the experimental route operative remains controlling according to its terms.

**First controlling authority:** Albertson’s, Inc. v. Kirkingburg, 527 U.S. 555 (1999), decided June 22, 1999, federal vision standard and experimental waiver; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Albertson’s, Inc. v. Kirkingburg, 527 U.S. 555 (1999), decided June 22, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Albertson’s, Inc. v. Kirkingburg, 527 U.S. 555 (1999), June 22, 1999.

### Title II community placement and fundamental alteration

**Governing question:** When Title II requires community placement instead of institutional isolation and when the requested modification would fundamentally alter the State’s service system.

**Current rule:** Unjustified institutional isolation is discrimination when state treatment professionals determine that community placement is appropriate, the qualified person does not oppose community treatment, and the placement can be reasonably accommodated. A State need not make a modification that would fundamentally alter its service system.

**Sequence or decisionmaker:** After the placement predicates are established, the fundamental-alteration inquiry considers available resources, the needs of others receiving disability services, and whether the State maintains a comprehensive, effectively working placement plan with a waiting list moving at a reasonable pace not controlled by efforts to keep institutions fully populated.

**Exceptions and limits:** Title II does not require community placement opposed by the affected person, unsupported by professional judgment, or requiring a fundamental alteration. Placement and justification must be individualized rather than stereotyped.

**First controlling authority:** Olmstead v. L.C., 527 U.S. 581 (1999), decided June 22, 1999, unjustified institutional isolation and fundamental alteration; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** Olmstead v. L.C., 527 U.S. 581 (1999), decided June 22, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** Olmstead v. L.C., 527 U.S. 581 (1999), June 22, 1999.

## Antitrust

### Vertical maximum resale-price agreements

**Governing question:** How Sherman Act § 1 treats an agreement fixing a maximum resale price.

**Current rule:** A vertical agreement fixing a maximum resale price is evaluated under the rule of reason rather than condemned as unlawful per se.

**Exceptions and limits:** Horizontal price fixing, minimum resale-price maintenance, and cartel-facilitation rules remain unaffected.

**First controlling authority:** State Oil Co. v. Khan, 522 U.S. 3 (1997), decided November 4, 1997, maximum vertical resale-price agreements; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** State Oil Co. v. Khan, 522 U.S. 3 (1997), decided November 4, 1997, maximum vertical resale-price agreements; supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** State Oil Co. v. Khan, 522 U.S. 3 (1997), November 4, 1997.

### Vertical supplier substitution and the per se boycott rule

**Governing question:** When a buyer’s substitution of one supplier for another is a per se group boycott under Sherman Act § 1.

**Current rule:** A single buyer’s supplier substitution, without horizontal concerted exclusion, is not a per se group boycott. Fraud, favoritism, and improper motive do not supply the missing horizontal structure. A vertical restraint does not enter the boycott per se category unless it contains the established structural feature supporting categorical condemnation.

**Exceptions and limits:** Coordinated exclusion containing a horizontal component remains governed by the per se rules applicable to that structure. An adequately pleaded monopolization theory remains separate.

**First controlling authority:** NYNEX Corp. v. Discon, Inc., 525 U.S. 128 (1998), decided December 14, 1998, per se treatment of a vertical supplier substitution; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** NYNEX Corp. v. Discon, Inc., 525 U.S. 128 (1998), decided December 14, 1998, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** NYNEX Corp. v. Discon, Inc., 525 U.S. 128 (1998), December 14, 1998.

### Depth of rule-of-reason analysis

**Governing question:** When an antitrust restraint may be condemned through abbreviated review rather than fuller market analysis.

**Current rule:** The depth of rule-of-reason analysis must be proportionate to the restraint’s plausible competitive harms and benefits. An abbreviated quick look is sufficient only when anticompetitive tendency is apparent without substantial market study. When both the asserted harm and plausible consumer or efficiency justification require economic assessment, the court must conduct a fuller, restraint-sensitive inquiry.

**Exceptions and limits:** Established per se categories retain their own rules. Affecting price or information does not by itself make anticompetitive tendency intuitively obvious.

**First controlling authority:** California Dental Association v. Federal Trade Commission, 526 U.S. 756 (1999), decided May 24, 1999, depth of rule-of-reason analysis; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** California Dental Association v. Federal Trade Commission, 526 U.S. 756 (1999), decided May 24, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** California Dental Association v. Federal Trade Commission, 526 U.S. 756 (1999), May 24, 1999.

### FTC jurisdiction over nonprofit associations serving commercial members

**Governing question:** When a nonprofit association carries on business for the profit of its members within the FTC Act.

**Current rule:** Nonprofit form does not defeat FTC jurisdiction when the association’s activities materially advance the commercial operations of profit-seeking members. The inquiry turns on the association’s operation for member profit, not its formal distribution of earnings.

**First controlling authority:** California Dental Association v. Federal Trade Commission, 526 U.S. 756 (1999), decided May 24, 1999, FTC jurisdiction over the Association; established the current rule.

**Controlling authorities:** California Dental Association v. Federal Trade Commission, 526 U.S. 756 (1999), decided May 24, 1999, supplies the current rule.

**Present operation effective:** California Dental Association v. Federal Trade Commission, 526 U.S. 756 (1999), May 24, 1999.

## Rules no longer current: Federalism and State Sovereign Immunity

### Constructive waiver through regulated commercial activity

**Former governing question:** Whether a State waived sovereign immunity by operating in interstate commerce after Congress authorized private suit as a condition of participation.

**Last operative rule:** Parden v. Terminal Railway treated a State’s operation of a railroad in federally regulated interstate commerce as acceptance of Congress’s condition permitting a private action.

**Ceased to govern:** College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board, 527 U.S. 666 (1999), decided June 23, 1999, overruled Parden to the extent it recognized constructive waiver from regulated market participation.

**Successor:** Waiver of state sovereign immunity, under Federalism and State Sovereign Immunity.

**Surviving force:** None as a rule of consent. Commercial activity may establish regulatory coverage or satisfy the separate rule governing commercial state enterprises without constituting waiver.

**Controlling authorities:** Parden v. Terminal Railway of Alabama State Docks Department, 377 U.S. 184 (1964); College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board, 527 U.S. 666 (1999).

## Rules no longer current: Antitrust

### Per se condemnation of vertical maximum resale-price agreements

**Former governing question:** Whether a vertical agreement fixing a maximum resale price was unlawful per se under Sherman Act § 1.

**Last operative rule:** Albrecht v. Herald Co. treated vertical maximum resale-price agreements as unlawful per se.

**Ceased to govern:** State Oil Co. v. Khan, 522 U.S. 3 (1997), decided November 4, 1997, overruled Albrecht and required rule-of-reason review.

**Successor:** Vertical maximum resale-price agreements, under Antitrust.

**Surviving force:** None as a governing per se rule. Rules addressing horizontal price fixing, minimum resale-price maintenance, and cartel facilitation remain unaffected.

**Controlling authorities:** Albrecht v. Herald Co.; State Oil Co. v. Khan, 522 U.S. 3 (1997).

## Rules no longer current: First Amendment

### RFRA review of prospective state and local enforcement

**Former governing question:** Whether RFRA could govern prospective state and local enforcement under Fourteenth Amendment § 5.

**Last operative rule:** RFRA required provision-specific substantial-burden, compelling-interest, and least-restrictive-means review of state and local enforcement.

**Ceased to govern:** City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997), decided June 25, 1997, held that RFRA’s universal application to state and local government exceeded Congress’s § 5 power.

**Successor:** RFRA strict scrutiny when valid federal authority makes RFRA applicable, under First Amendment.

**Surviving force:** The substantive strict-scrutiny formulation survives for any RFRA application resting on a valid source of federal authority. The final judgment in Church of Scientology remains intact.

**Controlling authorities:** Church of Scientology Flag Service Organization, Inc. v. City of Clearwater, 2 F.3d 1514; City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997).

### RFRA exemptions removing third-party antidiscrimination protection

**Former governing question:** Whether RFRA required a state or local commercial-housing exemption that would remove another person’s statutory protection.

**Last operative rule:** When RFRA was treated as applicable to state and local government, government could deny an exemption that would remove an identified applicant’s antidiscrimination protection when no less restrictive accommodation preserved that protection.

**Ceased to govern:** City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997), decided June 25, 1997, displaced RFRA’s state and local application under § 5.

**Successor:** RFRA strict scrutiny when valid federal authority makes RFRA applicable, under First Amendment.

**Surviving force:** The third-party-protection analysis remains relevant only in a RFRA application supported by a valid federal source. The Free Exercise rule for neutral and generally applicable housing laws remains current.

**Controlling authorities:** Swanner v. Anchorage Equal Rights Commission, 874 P.2d 274; City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997).
